Nonautonomous Linear Systems: Exponential Dichotomy and its Applications
Álvaro Castañeda & Gonzalo Robledo

Abstract




Preface

The first purpose of this work is to
provide a friendly introduction to the theory of nonautonomous linear systems of ordinary differential equations, the property of exponential dichotomy and its corresponding spectral theory. The second purpose of this work is disseminate the linearization results carried out by the authors in a nonautonomous framework.

The actual structure of this work is a consequence of several elective courses (2014, 2016, 2019, 2021 and 2023) carried out by the authors for undergraduate and graduated students at the Department of Mathematics of the Universidad de Chile. The monography assumes a good knowledge of multivariate calculus, linear algebra and ordinary differential equations.

Chapter 1
Preliminaries

1.1.
Linear nonautonomous systems

This chapter is devoted to briefly recalling basic results about the linear system of ordinary differential equations

(1.1.1)

\dot{x}=A(t)x

where
x
is a column vector of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
J\ni t\mapsto A(t)\in M_{n}(\mathbb{R})
is a continuous valued matrix function and
J\subseteq\mathbb{R}
is a no degenerate interval. In general, but not always, we will consider intervals as
J=(-\infty,t_{0}]
,
J=[t_{0},+\infty)
or
J=\mathbb{R}
.

This system has been deeply studied in a myriad of classical
cold war
works from both sides of the iron courtain as
[
1
,
43
,
45
,
54
,
62
,
97
]
and
[
15
,
16
,
14
,
36
,
39
,
38
,
44
,
61
,
64
]
. There also exists more recent monographs as
[
19
,
58
,
66
,
68
,
77
,
120
]
. Nevertheless, we will review a short list of essential topics and results in order to self contain this work.

1.1.1.
Fundamental matrix and transition matrix

Definition 1.1.1
.

A
fundamental matrix
of (
1.1.1
) is a matrix valued function

t\mapsto X(t)\in M_{n}(\mathbb{R})
whose columns
\{x_{1}(t),\ldots,x_{n}(t)\}
are a basis of solutions for the system (
1.1.1
) for any
t\in J
.

The study of fundamental matrices implies recalling facts
about matrix valued functions and its derivatives. In fact,
for any matrix function
J\in t\mapsto U(t)
, not necessarily a
fundamental matrix, we will say that
U
is differentiable at
t
if and only if the following limit exists

\dot{U}(t)=\frac{d}{dt}U(t):=\lim\limits_{h\to 0}\frac{1}{h}[U(t+h)-U(t)],

which must be understood as a componentwise limit.

Lemma 1.1.1
.

If a matrix function
J\ni t\to U(t)
is derivable and invertible for any
t\in J
, then its inverse is derivable on
J
with derivate given by

(1.1.2)

\frac{d}{dt}U^{-1}(t)=-U^{-1}(t)\dot{U}(t)U^{-1}(t).

Proof.

If
U
is derivable on
J
, it follows that
U
is continuous on
J
.
This fact, combined with the invertibility of
U
, also implies the continuity
of
U^{-1}
on
J
. Now, we can see that the following limit exists

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}U^{-1}(t)&=&\displaystyle\lim\limits_{h\to 0}\frac{1}{h}\left[U^{-1}(t+h)-U^{-1}(t)\right]\\
\\
&=&\lim\limits_{h\to 0}\frac{1}{h}U^{-1}(t+h)\left[I-U(t+h)U^{-1}(t)\right]\\
\\
&=&\displaystyle\lim\limits_{h\to 0}U^{-1}(t+h)\frac{1}{h}\left[U(t)-U(t+h)\right]U^{-1}(t)\end{array}

and the identity (
1.1.2
) follows.
∎

Remark 1.1.1
.

For any
t\in J
, it follows that any fundamental matrix
X(t)
of (
1.1.1
) verifies the identity:

(1.1.3)

\dot{X}(t)=A(t)X(t).

In fact, we can write
X(t)=[x_{1}(t)\,x_{2}(t)\,\cdots\,x_{n}(t)]

and observe that:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\dot{X}(t)&=&[\dot{x}_{1}(t)\,\dot{x}_{2}(t)\,\cdots\,\dot{x}_{n}(t)]\\
\\
&=&[A(t)x_{1}(t)\,A(t)x_{2}(t)\,\cdots\,A(t)x_{n}(t)]\\
\\
&=&A(t)[x_{1}(t)\,x_{2}(t)\,\cdots\,x_{n}(t)]\\
\\
&=&A(t)X(t).\end{array}

For example, let us consider the linear system

(1.1.4)

\dot{x}=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}-1+\frac{3}{2}\cos^{2}(t)&1-\frac{3}{2}\cos(t)\sin(t)\\
-1-\frac{3}{2}\cos(t)\sin(t)&-1+\frac{3}{2}\sin^{2}(t)\end{array}\right]x,

which have been studied in
[
89
]
. It straightforward to verify that

X(t)=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}e^{\frac{t}{2}}\cos(t)&e^{-t}\sin(t)\\
-e^{\frac{t}{2}}\sin(t)&e^{-t}\cos(t)\end{array}\right]

is a fundamental matrix of the above linear system.

In the autonomous case, any course of ordinary differential equations provides a method to construct
a fundamental matrix
X(t)=e^{At}
by using the eigenvalues and eigenvectors of

A
. Nevertheless, and contrarily to the autonomous case, there is no algorithm applicable to every
linear system (
1.1.1
) that allows finding a basis of solutions. Furthermore, the system (
1.1.4
) is an iconic example illustrating the subtleties and technicalities of the nonautonomous case. In fact, notice that the eigenvalues of its corresponding matrix
A(t)
have negative real part but the first column of
X(t)
is a unstable solution.

Remark 1.1.2
.

If
X(t)
is a fundamental matrix associated to (
1.1.1
), for any
t\in J
it is easy to verify the following facts:

a)

If
C\in M_{n}(\mathbb{R})
is nonsingular, then
Y(t)=X(t)C
is also a fundamental matrix associated to (
1.1.1
).

b)

We always can choose a fundamental matrix
Y(t)
satisfying
Y(0)=I
. In fact, we can use
C=X^{-1}(0)
in the above property. This matrix
Y(t)
is usually called a
normalized
fundamental matrix.

c)

If
J=\mathbb{R}
and
A(-t)=-A(t)
for any
t\in J
, then
Y(t)=X(-t)
is also a fundamental matrix associated to (
1.1.1
).

d)

By its own definition we have that
for any
t\in J
, the fundamental matrix
X(t)
is invertible since its columns are linearly independent vectors of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. As
X(t)
is invertible and derivable, it is followed by Lemma
1.1.1
that
X^{-1}(t)
is also derivable
and (
1.1.2
) combined with (
1.1.3
) implies the identity:

(1.1.5)

\frac{d}{dt}X^{-1}(t)=-X^{-1}(t)A(t).

The next result is a direct consequence of the identity (
1.1.5
):

Lemma 1.1.2
.

The map
J\ni t\mapsto X(t)X^{-1}(t_{0})c
with
t_{0}\in J
and
c\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
is being the unique solution of the
initial value problem:

(1.1.6)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}\dot{x}&=&A(t)x\\
x(t_{0})&=&c.\end{array}\right.

Proof.

It is straightforward to verify that
t\mapsto X(t)X^{-1}(t_{0})c
is a solution of the initial value problem (
1.1.6
). In order to prove its uniqueness let
t\mapsto y(t)
also be a solution of the above problem. Now, let us define the auxiliar function
u(t)=X(t)X^{-1}(t_{0})c-y(t)
and note that
u(t)
verifies:

\dot{u}(t)=A(t)u(t)\quad\textnormal{with $u(t_{0})=0$}.

First of all, let us assume that
t\geq t_{0}
and
integrates the above equation between
t_{0}
and
t
. By using the fact that
u(t_{0})=0
it follows that

u(t)=\int_{t_{0}}^{t}A(s)u(s)\,ds,

and consequently

\begin{array}[]{rcl}|u(t)|&=&\displaystyle\left|\int_{t_{0}}^{t}A(s)u(s)\,ds\right|\\
\\
&\leq&\displaystyle\int_{t_{0}}^{t}|A(s)u(s)|\,ds\\
\\
&\leq&\displaystyle\int_{t_{0}}^{t}||A(s)||\,|u(s)|\,ds.\end{array}

Secondly, let us assume that
t\leq t_{0}
and
integrates the above equation between
t
and
t_{0}
. By reiterating the fact that
u(t_{0})=0
it follows that:

-u(t)=\int_{t}^{t_{0}}A(s)u(s)\,ds,

and consequently

\begin{array}[]{rcl}|u(t)|&\leq&\displaystyle\int_{t}^{t_{0}}||A(s)||\,|u(s)|\,ds\\
\\
\end{array}

Consequently, we have that

|u(t)|\leq\left|\int_{t_{0}}^{t}||A(s)||\,|u(s)|\,ds\right|\leq c_{n}+\left|\int_{t_{0}}^{t}||A(s)||\,|u(s)|\,ds\right|

for any positive sequence
\{c_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
convergent to zero.

By the variation of Gronwall’s Lemma (see Appendix C for details), we have that

|u(t)|\leq c_{n}\exp\left(\left|\int_{t_{0}}^{t}||A(s)||\,ds\right|\right).

Finally, the uniqueness follows by letting
c_{n}\to 0
when
n\to+\infty
.
∎

Definition 1.1.2
.

Let
X(t)
be a fundamental matrix of (
1.1.1
). The
transition matrix
associated to
X(t)
is defined by

X(t,s):=X(t)X^{-1}(s)\quad\textit{where $t,s\in J$}.

We point out that, in the russian literature, the transition matrix is also called as
Cauchy operator
or
matriciant

while in PDE and functional analysis research it is usually called as evolution operator.

Remark 1.1.3
.

By using Remarks
1.1.1
and
1.1.2
, we can see that:

(1.1.7)

\frac{\partial X}{\partial t}(t,s)=A(t)X(t,s)\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\frac{\partial X}{\partial s}(t,s)=-X(t,s)A(s).

Remark 1.1.4
.

Any transition matrix
X(t,s)
associated to (
1.1.1
) verifies the following properties:

a)

For any
t,t_{0},s\in J
it follows that:

X(t,t)=I\quad\textnormal{and}\quad X(t,t_{0})X(t_{0},s)=X(t,s).

b)

Any solution
t\mapsto x(t)
of the initial value problem (
1.1.6
) can be written as

x(t)=X(t,t_{0})c
. By using a) we can see that, for any
t,s\in J
, we have the identity
x(t)=X(t,s)x(s)
, which
is behind the adjective
transition
.

c)

If
A\in M_{n}(\mathbb{R})
is a constant matrix, it can be seen that
e^{At}
is a fundamental matrix of the system (
1.1.1
) when
A(t)
is constant for any
t\in J
. By using Lemma
1.1.2
, we have that:

X(t,t_{0})=e^{A(t-t_{0})}.

The next result provides estimations for the transition matrix and can be found in

[
68
, Lemma 3.3.4]
:

Lemma 1.1.3
.

Given
t,t_{0}\in J
it follows that

(1.1.8)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}e^{-\left|\int_{t_{0}}^{t}||A(s)||\,ds\right|}\leq||X(t,t_{0})||\,\,\,\,\leq e^{\left|\int_{t_{0}}^{t}||A(s)||\,ds\right|},\\
\\
e^{-\left|\int_{t_{0}}^{t}||A(s)||\,ds\right|}\leq||X(t_{0},t)||^{-1}\leq e^{\left|\int_{t_{0}}^{t}||A(s)||\,ds\right|},\end{array}\right.

where
||\cdot||
is a matrix norm satisfying
||I||=1
.

Proof.

By (
1.1.3
) we know that

\frac{\partial X}{\partial t}(t,t_{0})=A(t)X(t,t_{0})\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\frac{\partial X}{\partial t}(t_{0},t)=-X(t_{0},t)A(t).

If
t>t_{0}
, we integrate the above equations, obtaining

X(t,t_{0})=I+\int_{t_{0}}^{t}A(s)X(s,t_{0})\,ds,\quad X(t_{0},t)=I-\int_{t_{0}}^{t}A(s)X(t_{0},s)\,ds,

which leads to

||X(t,t_{0})||\leq 1+\int_{t_{0}}^{t}||A(s)||\,||X(s,t_{0})||\,ds

and

||X(t_{0},t)||\leq 1+\int_{t_{0}}^{t}||A(s)||\,||X(t_{0},s)||\,ds.

By using Gronwall’s Lemma (see Appendix B) we obtain

||X(t,t_{0})||\leq e^{\int_{t_{0}}^{t}||A(s)||\,ds}\quad\textnormal{and}\quad e^{-\int_{t_{0}}^{t}||A(s)||\,ds}\leq||X(t_{0},t)||^{-1}.

By statement 1 from Remark
1.1.4
we have that

1=||I||=||X(t,t_{0})X(t_{0},t)||\leq||X(t,t_{0})||\,||X(t_{0},t)||,

and by using the above estimations, we can deduce that

e^{-\int_{t_{0}}^{t}||A(s)||\,ds}\leq||X(t,t_{0})||\quad\textnormal{and}\quad||X(t_{0},t)||^{-1}\leq e^{\int_{t_{0}}^{t}||A(s)||\,ds}

and the Lemma follows for
t>t_{0}
. The proof for the case
t<t_{0}
can be carried out in a similar way and is left for the reader.
∎

Lemma 1.1.4
.

The initial value problem:

(1.1.9)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}\dot{x}&=&A(t)x+f(t)\\
x(t_{0})&=&x_{0}\end{array}\right.

where
J\ni t\mapsto f(t)\in\mathbb{R}
,
t_{0}\in J
and
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
has a unique solution
t\mapsto x(t)
given by:

(1.1.10)

\displaystyle x(t)=X(t,t_{0})x_{0}+\int_{t_{0}}^{t}X(t,s)f(s)\,ds.

Proof.

Let
J\ni s\mapsto x(s)
be a solution of (
1.1.9
), then we multiply
\dot{x}(s)
by
X^{-1}(s)
obtaining that

X^{-1}(s)\dot{x}(s)-X^{-1}(s)A(s)x(s)=X^{-1}(s)f(s).

Then, by using Remark
1.1.2
we have that the above identity is equivalent to

\frac{d}{ds}\Big\{X^{-1}(s)x(s)\Big\}=X^{-1}(s)f(s),

thereafter, we integrate between
t_{0}
and
t
, then we can deduce that

X^{-1}(t)x(t)=X^{-1}(t_{0})x_{0}+\int_{t_{0}}^{t}X^{-1}(s)f(s)\,ds

and the identity (
1.1.10
) can be deduced easily.

In order to prove the uniqueness, let us consider the solution
J\ni t\mapsto z(t)
and define
u(t)=x(t)-z(t)
. Then, we can see that
t\mapsto u(t)
is the solution of the initial value problem (
1.1.6
) with
u(t_{0})=c=0
. Hereafter, by using Lemma
1.1.2
we have that
u(t)=0
for any
t\in J
and the uniqueness follows.
∎

From the starting point that fundamental and transition matrices are solutions of matrix differential equations such as (
1.1.3
), we can see that it is possible to study
matrix differential equations generalizing the above matrix equation. The following results
are adapted from
[
43
, Ch.3]
.

Lemma 1.1.5
.

Let us consider the continuous matrix valued functions
J\ni t\mapsto A(t),B(t),F(t)\in M_{n}(\mathbb{R})
. The matrix differential equation

(1.1.11)

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\dot{Q}(t)&=&A(t)Q(t)-Q(t)B(t)+F(t)\\
\end{array}

has a solution given by

(1.1.12)

\displaystyle Q(t)=X(t,s)Q(s)Y(s,t)+\int_{s}^{t}X(t,\tau)F(\tau)Y(\tau,t)\,d\tau,

where
X(t)
and
Y(t)
are fundamental matrices associated to the linear systems

\dot{x}=A(t)x
and
\dot{y}=B(t)y
respectively.

Proof.

We multiply (
1.1.11
) by
X(s,\tau)
on the left. Then, by using (
1.1.7
) we obtain

\begin{array}[]{rcl}X(s,\tau)F(\tau)&=&X(s,\tau)\dot{Q}(\tau)-X(s,\tau)A(\tau)Q(\tau)+X(s,\tau)Q(\tau)B(\tau),\\
\\
&=&\displaystyle X(s,\tau)\dot{Q}(\tau)+\frac{d}{d\tau}\left\{X(s,\tau)\right\}Q(\tau)+X(s,\tau)Q(\tau)B(\tau),\\
\\
&=&\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\tau}\left\{X(s,\tau)Q(\tau)\right\}+X(s,\tau)Q(\tau)B(\tau).\end{array}

In this manner, we multiply the above identity by
Y(\tau,s)
on the right, then by using again (
1.1.7
) we obtain

\begin{array}[]{rcl}X(s,\tau)F(\tau)Y(\tau,s)&=&\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\tau}\left\{X(s,\tau)Q(\tau)\right\}Y(\tau,s)+X(s,\tau)Q(\tau)B(\tau)Y(\tau,s),\\
\\
&=&\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\tau}\left\{X(s,\tau)Q(\tau)\right\}Y(\tau,s)+X(s,\tau)Q(\tau)\frac{d}{d\tau}\left\{Y(\tau,s)\right\},\\
\\
&=&\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\tau}\left\{X(s,\tau)Q(\tau)Y(\tau,s)\right\}.\end{array}

By integrating between
s
and
t
we obtain

X(s,t)Q(t)Y(t,s)=Q(s)+\int_{s}^{t}X(s,\tau)F(\tau)Y(\tau,s)\,d\tau,

and (
1.1.12
) can be deduced by multiplying the above identity
by
X(t,s)
and
Y(s,t)
from the left and right respectively.
∎

The above result has limit cases, which are important by themselves:

Corollary 1.1.1
.

The matrix differential equations

(1.1.13a)

\displaystyle\dot{Q}(t)=A(t)Q(t)+F(t),

(1.1.13b)

\displaystyle\dot{Q}(t)=A(t)Q(t)-B(t)Q(t),

where

J\ni t\mapsto A(t),B(t),F(t)\in M_{n}(\mathbb{R})
have solutions respectively given by:

(1.1.14a)

\displaystyle Q(t)=X(t,s)Q(s)+\int_{s}^{t}X(t,\tau)F(\tau)\,d\tau,

(1.1.14b)

\displaystyle Q(t)=X(t,s)Q(s)Y(s,t)

where
X(t)
and
Y(t)
are fundamental matrices associated to the linear systems

\dot{x}=A(t)x
and
\dot{y}=B(t)y
.

Remark 1.1.5
.

The equation (
1.1.13b
) with initial condition
Q(t_{0})=I
at
t_{0}\in J
has a solution

Q(t)=X(t)Y^{-1}(t)
, where these fundamental matrices verify
X(t_{0})=Y(t_{0})=I
. Moreover, as
Q(t)

is non singular, this allows to write
Y(t)=Q^{-1}(t)X(t)
and
X(t)=Q(t)Y(t)
.

The following Remark can be seen as a converse result of the previous ones:

Remark 1.1.6
.

If a fundamental matrix
X(t)
of
\dot{x}=A(t)x
has the decomposition
X(t)=Q(t)V(t)
, where
Q
and
V
are derivable an invertible then

Q
satisfies the matrix differential equation

\dot{Q}(t)=A(t)Q(t)-Q(t)B(t)\quad\textnormal{with}\quad B(t)=\dot{V}(t)V^{-1}(t).

1.1.2.
Adjoint systems

In order to introduce the adjoint of the linear system (
1.1.1
),
let us consider
a particular case of the matrix differential equation (
1.1.13b
) with
B(t)=-A^{T}(t)
:

(1.1.15)

\dot{Q}(t)=A(t)Q(t)+Q(t)A^{T}(t).

A consequence of Corollary
1.1.1
and Remark
1.1.5
is that the matrix differential equation
(
1.1.15
) with initial condition
Q(t_{0})=I
at some
t_{0}\in J
has a solution

Q(t)=X(t)Y^{-1}(t)
, where
X(t)
and
Y(t)
are respectively fundamental matrices
(normalized at
t_{0}\in J
) of (
1.1.1
) and

(1.1.16)

\dot{y}=-A^{T}(t)y,

which is known as the
adjoint system
of (
1.1.1
).

In order to study the relation between
X(t)
and
Y(t)
, notice that:

Y^{T}(t)\dot{X}(t)=Y^{T}(t)A(t)X(t).

Moreover, we integrate by parts between
t_{0}
and
t
with
t,t_{0}\in J
and, by using the identity
\frac{d}{dt}[U(t)]^{T}=\left[\frac{d}{dt}U(t)\right]^{T},

we can deduce that

Y^{T}(t)X(t)=\displaystyle I+\int_{t_{0}}^{t}[\dot{Y}^{T}(\tau)+Y(\tau)^{T}A(\tau)]X(\tau)\,d\tau=I,

since
Y(t)
verifies
\dot{Y}^{T}(t)+Y(t)A(t)=0
for any

t\in J
and we conclude that:

Theorem 1.1.1
.

If
X(t)
and
Y(t)
are fundamental matrices of (
1.1.1
) and its adjoint respectively, both normalized
at
t_{0}
, it follows that

Y(t)=[X^{-1}(t)]^{T}\quad\textnormal{for any $t\in J$}.

We stress that the above result can be obtained by direct computation by using the statement d) from
Remark
1.1.2
. Nevertheless, the Remark
1.1.5
provides us a complementary point of view: to see
the fundamental matrix of (
1.1.16
) as
Y(t)=Q^{-1}(t)X(t)
where
Q(\cdot)
is a solution of (
1.1.15
)
with
Q(t_{0})=X(t_{0})=Y(t_{0})=I
.

The following result is a direct consequence from Theorem
1.1.1
:

Corollary 1.1.2
.

Let
X(t)
be a fundamental matrix of the system (
1.1.1
). Then
Y(t)
is a fundamental matrix of its adjoint system (
1.1.16
) if and only if
the following identity is satisfied

(1.1.17)

Y^{T}(t)X(t)=C\quad\textnormal{for any $t\in J$, where $C$ is invertible.}

Proof.

We first assume that
X(t)
is a fundamental matrix of (
1.1.1
), then

X(t)X^{-1}(t_{0})
is also a fundamental matrix normalized at
t_{0}\in J
.
By Theorem
1.1.1
we know that

Y(t)=[X(t_{0})X^{-1}(t)]^{T}=[X^{-1}(t)]^{T}X^{T}(t_{0}),

which implies that
Y^{T}(t)X(t)=X(t_{0})
and the identity (
1.1.17
) is verified with
C=X^{T}(t_{0})
.

Now, let us assume that the identity (
1.1.17
) is verified. Given
t_{0}\in J
we have that

C=Y^{T}(t_{0})X(t_{0})
and

\tilde{X}(t)=X(t)X^{-1}(t_{0})=[Y^{T}(t)]^{-1}CX^{-1}(t_{0})=[Y^{T}(t)]^{-1}Y^{T}(t_{0})

is a fundamental matrix of (
1.1.1
) normalized at
t_{0}\in J
. By applying Theorem
1.1.1
we can deduce
that

[\tilde{X}^{-1}(t)]^{T}=Y(t)Y^{-1}(t_{0})

is also a fundamental matrix of of (
1.1.16
) normalized at
t_{0}
. Finally, as
Y(t_{0})
is invertible by using Remark
1.1.1

we an conclude that
Y(t)
is a fundamental matrix of (
1.1.16
) and the result follows.
∎

A particular but interesting case of the above Theorems is given by the following result:

Corollary 1.1.3
.

If a linear system (
1.1.1
) is such that
A(t)
is antisymmetric for any
t\in J
, then any fundamental matrix
X(t)
is orthogonal for any
J
.

Proof.

Let
X(t)
be a fundamental matrix of the system (
1.1.1
). As
A(t)=-A^{T}(t)
for any
t\in J
, then the systems (
1.1.1
) and (
1.1.16
) coincides, which implies that
X(t)=[X^{-1}(t)]^{T}
and the orthogonality of
X(t)
follows.
∎

Remark 1.1.7
.

An interesting example of the above Corollary is given by the linear systems

\dot{x}=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}0&e^{t}\\
-e^{t}&0\end{array}\right]x\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\dot{y}=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
-1&0\end{array}\right]y

which have fundamental matrices

X(t)=\left[\begin{array}[]{rc}\cos(e^{t})&\sin(e^{t})\\
-\sin(e^{t})&\cos(e^{t})\end{array}\right]\quad\textnormal{and}\quad Y(t)=\left[\begin{array}[]{rc}\cos(t)&\sin(t)\\
-\sin(t)&\cos(t)\end{array}\right].

We can easily see that
X^{T}(t)=X^{-1}(t)
and
Y^{-1}(t)=Y^{T}(t)
. Note also that
t\mapsto\det X(t)
and
t\mapsto\det Y(t)

are equal to
1
for any
t\in\mathbb{R}
. Finally, as any antisymmetric matrix has null trace, the above examples
provide a good illustration for the topic of the next subsection.

1.1.3.
The Liouville’s formula

Given a linear system (
1.1.1
), the Liouville’s formula relates the determinant of
any fundamental matrix
X(t)
with the trace of its corresponding matrix as follows:

Theorem 1.1.2
.

For any
t,t_{0}\in J
, the determinant of any fundamental matrix
X(t)
of (
1.1.1
)
verifies the identity:

(1.1.18)

\det X(t)=\det X(t_{0})e^{\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\textnormal{Tr}A(s)\,ds}.

Proof.

Note that

\frac{d}{dt}\Big(\det X(t_{0})e^{\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\textnormal{Tr}A(s)\,ds}\Big)=\textnormal{Tr}A(t)\det X(t_{0})e^{\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\textnormal{Tr}A(s)\,ds}

and we can verify that

t\mapsto\det X(t_{0})e^{\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\textnormal{Tr}A(s)\,ds}
is solution of the scalar initial value problem

(1.1.19)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}\dot{u}&=&\textnormal{Tr}A(t)u\\
u(t_{0})&=&\det X(t_{0}).\end{array}\right.

Now, let us calculate the derivative of the right part of (
1.1.18
):

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\frac{d}{dt}\left[\det X(t)\right]&=&\displaystyle\lim\limits_{h\to 0}\frac{\det X(t+h)-\det X(t)}{h}\\
\\
&=&\displaystyle\lim\limits_{h\to 0}\frac{\det[X(t+h)X^{-1}(t)X(t)]-\det X(t)}{h}\\
\\
&=&\displaystyle\lim\limits_{h\to 0}\frac{\det[X(t+h)X^{-1}(t)]\det X(t)-\det X(t)}{h}\\
\\
&=&\displaystyle\left(\lim\limits_{h\to 0}\frac{\det[X(t+h)X^{-1}(t)]-\det I}{h}\right)\det X(t).\end{array}

Now, notice that

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\displaystyle X(t+h)X^{-1}(t)&=&\displaystyle I+\int_{t}^{t+h}A(s)X(s)X^{-1}(t)\,ds\\
\\
&=&\displaystyle I+h\theta(t,h)\quad\textnormal{with}\quad\theta(t,h)=\frac{1}{h}\int_{t}^{t+h}A(s)X(s,t)\,ds.\end{array}

By the above identity, the computation of
\det X(t+h)X^{-1}(t)
can be made in a similar way as the computation of the characteristic polynomial of a matrix. In fact, note that

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\det X(t+h)X^{-1}(t)&=&\det\left(hh^{-1}I+h\theta(t,h)\right)\\
\\
&=&h^{n}\det\left(h^{-1}I+\theta(t,h)\right)\\
\\
&=&h^{n}\det\left(h^{-1}I-\sigma(t,h)\right)\,\,\,\textnormal{with $\sigma(t,h)=-\theta(t,h)$}.\end{array}

By using the Leverrier–Faddeev algorithm
[
70
]
, we have that

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\det\left(h^{-1}I-\sigma(t,h)\right)&=&h^{-n}-h^{1-n}\textnormal{Tr}(\sigma_{0})-\sum\limits_{k=2}^{n}\textnormal{Tr}(\sigma_{k-1})\frac{h^{k-n}}{k},\end{array}

where
\sigma_{k}
(with
k=0,\ldots,n-1
) is defined recursively as

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}\sigma_{0}&=&-\theta(t,h)\\
\\
\sigma_{k}&=&-\theta(t,h)\left(\sigma_{k-1}-\frac{1}{k}\textnormal{Tr}(\sigma_{k-1}I)\right).\end{array}\right.

Then, it follows that

\det X(t+h)X^{-1}(t)=1+h\,\textnormal{Tr}[\theta(t,h)]+\sum\limits_{k=2}^{n}\frac{h^{k}}{k}\textnormal{Tr}(\sigma_{k-1}).

By using the above identity combined with
\det I=1
, we can finish the computation:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\frac{d}{dt}\det X(t)&=&\lim\limits_{h\to 0}\left(\textnormal{Tr}(\theta(t,h))+\sum\limits_{k=2}^{n}\frac{h^{k-1}}{k}\textnormal{Tr}(\sigma_{k-1})\right)\det X(t)\\
\\
&=&\displaystyle\lim\limits_{h\to 0}\textnormal{Tr}\left[\frac{1}{h}\int_{t}^{t+h}A(s)X(s)X^{-1}(t)\right]\det X(t)\\
\\
&=&\textnormal{Tr}A(t)\det X(t),\end{array}

which implies that
t\mapsto\det X(t)
is also solution of the initial value problem (
1.1.19
) and the identity (
1.1.18
) follows from Lemma
1.1.2
.
∎

The Liouville’s formula is also known as Abel–Jacobi–Liouville in the literature,
we refer the reader to
[
1
]
for details.

1.2.
Bounded growth

To the best of our the property of bounded growth was introduced by W.A. Coppel in
[
41
, pp. 8–9]
. Nevertheless,
we will consider a pair of definitions proposed by K.J. Palmer in
[
102
, p.s172]
:

Definition 1.2.1
.

The system (
1.1.1
) has a
bounded growth
on
J
if and only if there exists constants
K>0
and
\alpha\geq 0
such that its transition matrix satisfies

(1.2.1)

||X(t,s)||\leq Ke^{\alpha(t-s)}\quad\textnormal{for any $t\geq s$ with $t,s\in J$.}

Definition 1.2.2
.

The system (
1.1.1
) has a
bounded decay
on
J
if and only if there exists constants
K>0
and
\alpha\geq 0
such that its transition matrix satisfies

||X(t,s)||\leq Ke^{\alpha(s-t)}\quad\textnormal{for any $s\geq t$ with $t,s\in J$.}

It is straightforward to see that the above definitions describe properties which are contained in the following definition
stated by S. Siegmund in
[
121
, p.253]
:

Definition 1.2.3
.

The system (
1.1.1
) has a
bounded growth
\&
decay
on
J
if and only if there exists constants
K>0
and
\alpha\geq 0
such that its transition matrix satisfies

||X(t,s)||\leq Ke^{\alpha|t-s|}\quad\textnormal{for any $t,s\in J$.}

An alternative characterization of the property of bounded growth on
J
is stated by W. Coppel in
[
41
]
:

Proposition 1.2.1
.

The system (
1.1.1
) has a bounded growth on
J
if and only if for each
h>0
there exists
C_{h}\geq 1
such that any solution
t\mapsto x(t)
of (
1.1.1
) verifies

(1.2.2)

|x(t)|\leq C_{h}|x(s)|\quad\textnormal{for any $t\in[s,s+h]\cap J$}.

Proof.

First, assume that (
1.2.1
) is verified. Then, given
h>0

and
0\leq t-s\leq h
it is straightforward to verify that (
1.2.2
) is satisfied with

C_{h}=\max\{1,Ke^{\alpha h}\}
.

Secondly, assume that (
1.2.2
) is verified for a given
h>0
and consider a partition of
J
in intervals of large

h
such that

t\in[s+(n-1)h,s+nh]\cap J
. It is straightforward to deduce that

|x(t)|\leq C_{h}^{n}|x(s)|
. On the other hand, it is easy to deduce
that
n-1\leq\frac{t-s}{h}\leq n
and

|x(t)|=|X(t,s)x(s)|=C_{h}^{n}|x(s)|\leq C_{h}\,e^{\frac{\ln(C_{h})}{h}(t-s)}|x(s)|,

and (
1.2.1
) is verified with
K=C_{h}
and
\alpha=\frac{\ln(C_{h})}{h}
.
∎

The next result can be proved in a similar way and its proof is left as an exercise.

Proposition 1.2.2
.

The system (
1.1.1
) has:

a)

A bounded decay on
J
if and only if for each
h>0
, there exists
C_{h}\geq 1
such that any solution
t\mapsto x(t)
of (
1.1.1
) verifies

|x(t)|\leq C_{h}|x(s)|\quad\textnormal{for any $t\in[s-h,s]\cap J$},

b)

A bounded growth
\&
decay on
J
if and only if for each
h>0
, there exists
C_{h}\geq 1
such that any solution
t\mapsto x(t)
of (
1.1.1
) verifies

|x(t)|\leq C_{h}|x(s)|\quad\textnormal{for any $t\in[s-h,s+h]\cap J$}.

Remark 1.2.1
.

As pointed out by W.A. Coppel in
[
41
, p.9]
, a careful reading of the proof of (
1.2.1
) shows that if (
1.2.1
) is satisfied then the property (
1.2.2
)
follows for any
h>0
. That is, the property of bounded growth is independent of the choice of
h
. This fact also applies to the properties of bounded decay and bounded growth
\&

decay.

From a practical point of view, it will be useful to know sufficient conditions ensuring the bounded growth properties for a linear system. The Lemma
1.1.3
provides a set of sufficient conditions, namely, when
A(t)
is bounded or integrally bounded:

Corollary 1.2.1
.

If
t\mapsto A(t)
verifies
\sup\limits_{t\in J}||A(t)||=M
, then the system (
1.1.1
) has a bounded growth
\&
decay on
J
.

Proof.

By using the inequality (
1.1.8
) from Lemma
1.1.3
it follows that

||X(t,s)||\leq||I||e^{M|t-s|}

and the result follows.

Corollary 1.2.2
.

If
t\mapsto A(t)
satisfies the property:

A_{h}:=\sup\limits_{t\in J}\int_{t}^{t+h}||A(r)||\,dr<+\infty,

for some
h>0
, then the system (
1.1.1
) has a bounded growth on
J
.

Proof.

Let
s\in J
and
t\mapsto x(t)
a solution of (
1.1.1
). Notice that

|x(t)|\leq|x(s)|+\int_{s}^{t}||A(\tau)||\,|x(\tau)|\,d\tau,

for any
t>s
. By Gronwall’s Lemma (See Appendix B), we have that

|x(t)|\leq|x(s)|e^{\int_{s}^{t}||A(\tau)||\,d\tau}<|x(s)|e^{\int_{s}^{s+h}||A(\tau)||\,d\tau}\quad\textnormal{for any
$t\in[s,s+h]$}

and we can conclude that (
1.2.2
) is satisfied with
C_{h}=e^{A_{h}}
.
∎

An interesting example of differential equation which does not has the property of bounded growth
\&
decay on
\mathbb{R}
is given by
\dot{x}=2tx
. In fact, notice that its
fundamental matrix
is given by
X(t)=e^{t^{2}}
, then we have that

X(t,s)=e^{(t-s)(t+s)}
and it is straightforward to see that the property (
1.2.1
) cannot be verified.

The following result can be found in
[
41
,
121
]
and describes an interesting consequence of the
bounded growth properties:

Proposition 1.2.3
.

If the system (
1.1.1
) has the property of bounded growth
\&
decay
on
J
, provided that
\alpha>0
, then the solutions of (
1.1.1
) have the property of uniform continuous dependence with respect to the initial conditions on any compact interval contained on
J
.

Proof.

Let
t\mapsto x_{1}(t)
and
t\mapsto x_{2}(t)

be two solutions of (
1.1.1
) respectively passing through by
\xi_{1}
and
\xi_{2}

at
t=t_{0}
, then for any
t\in[t_{0}-h,t_{0}+h]\subset J
it follows that

|x_{1}(t)-x_{2}(t)|=\left|X(t,t_{0})\xi_{1}-X(t,t_{0})\xi_{2}\right|\leq|\xi_{1}-\xi_{2}|Ke^{\alpha|t-t_{0}|}

and for any
\varepsilon>0
there exists
\delta(\varepsilon,h)=\varepsilon/Ke^{h}
such that
if
|\xi_{1}-\xi_{2}|<\delta
then
|x_{1}(t)-x_{2}(t)|<\varepsilon
for any

|t-t_{0}|\leq h
.
∎

We point out that the term uniform, in order to emphasize that
\delta
is not dependent of
t_{0}
.

∎

The following result shows that if the linear system (
1.1.1
) has
the property of bounded growth, then the property is preserved
for bounded perturbed systems

(1.2.3)

\dot{z}=[A(t)+B(t)]z.

Theorem 1.2.1
.

If (
1.1.1
) has a bounded growth on
J
and
B(\cdot)
verifies
||B(t)||\leq M
for any
t\in J
,
then this property is also verified for the system (
1.2.3
).

Proof.

Any fundamental matrix of (
1.2.3
) satisfies the matrix differential equation

\dot{Z}(t)=A(t)Z(t)+B(t)Z(t)

and by using Corollary
1.1.1
with
F(t)=B(t)Z(t)
we can deduce that

Z(t)=X(t,s)Z(s)+\int_{s}^{t}X(t,\tau)B(\tau)Z(\tau)\,d\tau,

where
X(t,s)
is the transition matrix associated to (
1.1.1
). The bounded growth of
(
1.1.1
) combined with Proposition

1.2.1
implies the existence of constants
K>0
and
\alpha\geq 0
such that

||X(t,s)||\leq Ke^{\alpha(t-s)}\quad\textnormal{for any $t\geq s$ such that $t,s\in J$}.

Let
t\mapsto z(t)=Z(t,s)\xi
be a solution of (
1.2.3
) passing through

\xi\neq 0
at
t=s
and
\sup\limits_{t\in J}||B(t)||\leq M
, then notice that

|z(t)|\leq Ke^{\alpha(t-s)}|z(s)|+\int_{s}^{t}Ke^{\alpha(t-\tau)}M|z(\tau)|\,d\tau\quad\textnormal{for any $t\geq s$ with $t,s\in J$}.

By using Gronwall’s Lemma (see Appendix B) we can deduce that

|z(t)|\leq K|z(s)|e^{(KM+\alpha)(t-s)}\quad\textnormal{for any $t\geq s$ such that $t,s\in J$}

or equivalently

\frac{|Z(t,s)z(s)|}{|z(s)|}\leq Ke^{(KM+\alpha)(t-s)}\quad\textnormal{for any $t\geq s$ such that $t,s\in J$},

which directly implies that
||Z(t,s)||\leq Ke^{(KM+\alpha)(t-s)}

for any
t\geq s
with
t,s\in J
and the bounded growth property is verified.

∎

1.3.
Kinematical Similarity

In order to state the property of kinematical similarity, let us denote the set of complex valued square matrices functions
\mathfrak{M}_{n}
defined as follows:

\mathfrak{M}_{n}=\left\{A\colon J\to M_{n}(\mathbb{C})\mid\textnormal{$A$ is continuous and $\sup\limits_{t\in J}||A(t)||<\infty$}\right\}.

Definition 1.3.1
.

A matrix function
t\mapsto Q(t)\in M_{n}(\mathbb{C})
is said to be a
Lyapunov transformation
if and only if satisfies the following properties:

(i)

t\mapsto Q(t)
is invertible for any
t\in J
,

(ii)

t\mapsto Q(t)
is differentiable for any
t\in J
,

(iii)

Q,Q^{-1},\dot{Q}\in\mathfrak{M}_{n}
, that is:

\sup\limits_{t\in J}\left[||Q(t)||+||Q^{-1}(t)||+||\dot{Q}(t)||\right]<+\infty.

The classical definition of kinematical similarity was introduced by L. Markus in
[
88
]
and considers the relation between two linear systems with bounded matrices as follows:

Definition 1.3.2
.

Let us assume that
A,B\in\mathfrak{M}_{n}
. The systems

(1.3.1a)

\displaystyle\dot{x}=A(t)x\quad\textnormal{for any $t\in J$},

(1.3.1b)

\displaystyle\dot{y}=B(t)y\quad\textnormal{for any $t\in J$}

are
kinematically similar
if there exists a Lyapunov transformation
Q(t)
such that

(1.3.2)

\dot{Q}(t)=A(t)Q(t)-Q(t)B(t)\quad\textnormal{for any $t\in J$}.

In addition, if
A
,
B
and
Q
are real valued it is said that (
1.3.1a
) and (
1.3.1b
) are kinematically similar over the real field.

Remark 1.3.1
.

In
[
88
]
, Markus considers the case
J=[0,+\infty)
. The case
J=\mathbb{R}
is considered in
[
63
]
,
which is also called
complete kinematical similarity
.

Remark 1.3.2
.

It is easy to verify that the property of kinematical similarity defines an equivalence relation.

Remark 1.3.3
.

By using the identity (
1.3.2
) it is a straightforward exercise to verify that:

i)

The change of coordinates
y=Q^{-1}(t)x
transforms the linear system (
1.3.1a
) into (
1.3.1b
).

ii)

The previous property prompts that if
X(t)
is a fundamental matrix of (
1.3.1a
)
then
Y(t)=Q^{-1}(t)X(t)
is a fundamental matrix of (
1.3.1b
).

iii)

The Corollary
1.1.1
provides a way to solve the matrix differential equation (
1.3.2
). If
0\in J
and
Q(0)=I
, we recover the identity of the previous statement.

In certain works, such as
[
43
, p.158]
and
[
41
,
99
,
100
]

the authors consider a different, and more general, definition of kinematical similarity which does not assumes boundedness properties for the derivative of
Q
1
1

1

For example: in the Encyclopaedia of Mathematics, when the Lyapunov transformation is defined, it is pointed out that in many cases the requierement
\sup\limits_{t\in J}||\dot{Q}(t)||<+\infty
can be discarded.
See link
.
:

Definition 1.3.3
.

Given two continuous matrix valued functions
J\in t\mapsto A(t),B(t)\in M_{n}(\mathbb{C})
, the linear systems

(1.3.3a)

\displaystyle\dot{x}=A(t)x\quad\textnormal{for any $t\in J$},

(1.3.3b)

\displaystyle\dot{y}=B(t)y\quad\textnormal{for any $t\in J$}

are
generally kinematically similar
if there exists a invertible and differentiable transformation
t\mapsto Q(t)
such that,
Q
,
Q^{-1}
and
\dot{Q}
are continuous in
J
,

\sup\limits_{t\in J}\left[||Q(t)||+||Q^{-1}(t)||\right]<+\infty,

and (
1.3.2
) is satisfied.
In addition, if
A
,
B
and
Q
are real valued it is said that (
1.3.3a
) and (
1.3.3b
) are generally kinematically similar over the real field.

The kinematical similarity in the sense of Definition
1.3.2

will be named as
classical
kinematical similarity in this monography. We are proposing Definition
1.3.3
because it will
be used in the next sections.

Both definitions of kinematic similarity, classical or general, are equivalent under the assumption that
A,B\in\mathfrak{M}_{n}
combined with the boundedness of
Q(t)
and
Q^{-1}(t)
in
J
since the matrix differential equation (
1.3.2
) implies that
\dot{Q}(t)
is also bounded in
J
.

On the other hand, the general Definition
1.3.3
is useful when working with unbounded matrices
A
and
B
.

The kinematical similarity, classical or general, can be seen as a generalization to the nonautonomous framework of the classical reduction of autonomous linear systems to its canonical form. In fact, when
A
and
B
are constant matrices
in
M_{n}(\mathbb{C})
, the systems (
1.3.1a
) and (
1.3.1b
) becomes

(1.3.4a)

\displaystyle\dot{x}=Ax,

(1.3.4b)

\displaystyle\dot{y}=By

and they are
similar
or
statically similar
if
there exists a non singular and constant matrix
Q\in M_{n}(\mathbb{C})
such that

Q^{-1}AQ=B
. Note that this identity is equivalent to
AQ-QB=0
and then
Q
is solution of (
1.3.2
). The static similarity is a classic topic of linear algebra which allows to obtain a system (
1.3.4b
) having the simplest possible structure, either diagonal or triangular.

Remark 1.3.4
.

The boundedness of
Q(t)
and
Q^{-1}(t)
is essential in the theory of kinematical similarity. In fact, by Corollary
1.1.1
we can see that

Q(t)=X(t,0)Q(0)Y(0,t)

satisfies the matrix differential equation (
1.3.2
) and
y=Q^{-1}(t)x
transforms
(
1.3.3a
) into (
1.3.3b
). Then, if we drop the assumption of boundedness for
Q(t)
and
Q^{-1}(t)
, we would have that any linear system would be similar to other one.

In order to point out the importance of the above remark, we state the following result:

Lemma 1.3.1
.

If a pair of fundamental matrices
X(t)
and
Y(t)
of the linear systems (
1.3.3a
) and (
1.3.3b
) and its inverses are bounded on
J
, then the systems
are generally kinematically similar.

Proof.

We can consider normalized matrices, namely,
X(0)=Y(0)=I
, then
by Corollary
1.1.1
we can see that

Q(t)=X(t)Y^{-1}(t)
satisfy the assumptions of Definition
1.3.3

and the Lemma follows.
∎

Corollary 1.3.1
.

If a fundamental matrix
X(t)
of the linear system (
1.3.3a
) and its inverse are bounded on
J
, then the system (
1.3.3a
) and

\dot{y}=0,

are generally kinematically similar.

Proof.

By Lemma
1.3.1
we have that
Q(t)=X(t)
satisfy the assumptions of Definition
1.3.3
and the general kinematical similarity follows.

In addition, if
A(t)
is bounded on
J
, it follows that
\dot{Q}(t)=A(t)Q(t)

is also bounded on
J
then
Q(t)
is a Lyapunov transformation and the classical kinematical similarity follows.
∎

Corollary 1.3.2
.

Any pair of antisymmetric linear systems (
1.3.3a
) and (
1.3.3b
) is generally kinematically similar on
J
.

Proof.

Let
X(t)
and
Y(t)
be fundamental matrices of (
1.3.3a
)
and (
1.3.3b
). By Corollary
1.1.3
it follows that
X^{T}(t)=X^{-1}(t)

and
Y^{T}(t)=Y^{-1}(t)
, which implies that

||X(t)||_{2}=\sigma_{\max}(X(t))=\sqrt{\lambda_{\max}(X^{T}(t)X(t))}=1

and similarly, we can deduce that
Y(t),X^{-1}(t)
and
Y^{-1}(t)
have
the same euclidean norm and the result follows.
∎

An interesting example of the above Corollary is given by the linear systems

\dot{x}=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}0&e^{t}\\
-e^{t}&0\end{array}\right]x\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\dot{y}=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
-1&0\end{array}\right]y,

which are kinematically similar by the transformation

y=Q^{-1}(t)x
, where
Q(t)=X(t)Y^{T}(t)
with
X(t)

and
Y(t)
stated in Remark
1.1.7
.

Theorem 1.3.1
.

The properties of bounded growth, bounded decay and bounded
\&
decay on
J
are preserved by kinematical similarity, either classical or general.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we will assume that (
1.3.3a
) and (
1.3.3b
) are generally kinematically similar by the transformation
y=Q^{-1}x
with

\max\left\{\sup\limits_{t\in J}||Q(t)||,\sup\limits_{t\in J}||Q^{-1}(t)||\right\}\leq M.

Let
X(t)
and
Y(t)
be fundamental matrices of (
1.3.3a
) and (
1.3.3b
) respectively. If (
1.3.3a
) has bounded growth
\&
decay on
J
, by Definition
1.2.3
there exist
K>0
and
\alpha\geq 0

such that

||X(t,s)||\leq Ke^{\alpha|t-s|}\quad\textnormal{for any $t,s\in J$. }

By Remark
1.3.3
we also know that general kinematical similarity implies that

Y(t)=Q^{-1}(t)X(t)
and

||Y(t,s)||=||Q^{-1}(t)X(t)X^{-1}(s)Q(s)||\leq M^{2}||X(t,s)||\leq KM^{2}e^{\alpha|t-s|},

and the bounded growth
\&
decay on
J
is verified for (
1.3.3b
). Note that the properties of bounded growth
and bounded decay are a consequence of the previous estimations.
∎

In the rest of this section, we will
study two distinguished examples of both kinematical similarities where
the fundamental matrix
X(t)
has a decomposition
X(t)=Q(t)V(t)
and
V
has special properties. Let us recall by Remark
1.1.6
that in this case it will follows that

\dot{Q}(t)=A(t)Q(t)-Q(t)\dot{V}(t)V^{-1}(t)

provided suitable conditions on
Q
and
V
.
Firstly, we will study an abridged version of Floquet’s theory which considers the
\omega
–periodic case
A(t+\omega)=A(t)
for any
t\in\mathbb{R}
. Secondly, we will study the transformation of (
1.3.1a
) in an upper triangular linear system, which has been initiated by O. Perron and S.P. Diliberto.

1.3.1.
Floquet’s Theory

Let us consider the linear nonautonomous system (
1.3.1a
) under the assumptions of continuity on
\mathbb{R}
and:

(1.3.5)

A(t+\omega)=A(t)\quad\textnormal{for any $t\in\mathbb{R}$.}

The seminal work on this topic was carried out by the french mathematician G. Floquet in
[
56
]
. Nevertheless, we will follow a modern approach
developed by several works as
[
1
]
.

Lemma 1.3.2
.

If (
1.3.5
) is verified then the transition matrix associated to the system (
1.1.1
) satisfies the following property of
\omega
–biperiodicity:

X(t+\omega,s+\omega)=X(t,s)\quad\textnormal{for any $t,s\in\mathbb{R}$}.

Proof.

Let
X(t)
be a fundamental matrix of (
1.1.1
). Firstly, it can be proved that
X(t+\omega)
is also a fundamental matrix of (
1.1.1
). In fact, note that (
1.3.5
) implies that

\dot{X}(t+\omega)=A(t+\omega)X(t+\omega)=A(t)X(t+\omega),

and we can see that the columns of
X(t+\omega)
are solutions of (
1.1.1
).

As the columns of
X(t)
are linearly independent we have that
\det X(t+\omega)\neq 0
and the columns of
X(t+\omega)
are also a basis of solutions of (
1.1.1
).

As
X(t)
and
X(t+\omega)
are fundamental matrices, there exists an invertible matrix
B
such that

(1.3.6)

X(t+\omega)=X(t)B\quad\textnormal{and}\quad X^{-1}(t+\omega)=B^{-1}X^{-1}(t).

By using the definition of transition matrix, we have that

\begin{array}[]{rcl}X(t+\omega,s+\omega)&=&X(t)BB^{-1}X^{-1}(s)\\
&=&X(t)X^{-1}(s)\\
&=&X(t,s)\end{array}

and the Lemma follows.
∎

Definition 1.3.4
.

The matrix
B
from (
1.3.6
) is known as the
monodromy matrix
of the system (
1.1.1
). The eigenvalues of
B
are known as the
Floquet’s multipliers
of the system (
1.1.1
).

Notice that, if we consider a normalized fundamental matrix, the equation (
1.3.6
) with
t=0
implies that

(1.3.7)

X(\omega)=B\quad\textnormal{and consequently}\quad X(t+\omega)=X(t)X(\omega).

Lemma 1.3.3
.

A number
\rho\in\mathbb{C}
is a Floquet’s multiplier of the system (
1.1.1
) if and only if there exists a nontrivial solution
t\mapsto x(t)
such that

(1.3.8)

x(t+\omega)=\rho\,x(t).

Proof.

Without loss of generality, it can be assumed that
X(0)=I
. In this case it follows by equation (
1.3.7
) that
X(\omega)=B
.

Firstly, we will assume that
\rho
is an eigenvalue of the monodromy matrix
X(\omega)=B
. Then, there exists a vector
v\in\mathbb{C}^{n}\setminus\{0\}
such that
X(\omega)v=\rho\,v
.
Now, let us consider the solution
t\mapsto x(t)
of (
1.1.1
) passing through
v
at
t=0
, then by Lemma
1.1.2
combined with
X(0)=I
and the right equation of (
1.3.7
), it follows that
x(t)=X(t)v
, and we can see that

x(t+\omega)=X(t+\omega)v=X(t)X(\omega)v=\rho\,x(t)

and the identity (
1.3.8
) follows.

Secondly, let us assume that (
1.3.8
) is verified. We evaluate this identity at
t=0
obtaining that

x(\omega)=\rho\,x(0).

In addition, by Lemma
1.1.2
we know that any non trivial solution
t\mapsto x(t)
is given by
x(t)=X(t)x(0)
. Then, we can see that

x(\omega)=X(\omega)\,x(0).

Now, considering the right parts of the above identities it follows that

X(\omega)x(0)=\rho\,x(0)

and we have that
\rho
is an eigenvalue of
B=X(\omega)
.
∎

Remark 1.3.5
.

A direct consequence of the definition of Floquet’s multiplier is that
the continuous
\omega
–periodic system (
1.1.1
) has a non trivial
\omega
–periodic solution if and only if
\rho=1
is a Floquet’s multiplier of (
1.1.1
) or equivalently
X(\omega,0)-I
is non singular.

We point out that Floquet’s original work
[
56
]
is difficult to read due to its anachronistic language and, in our opinion, a careful reading is still pending despite the contributions made to it
[
117
,
126
]
. The following result is a modern interpretation:

Theorem 1.3.2
.

If
X(t)
is a fundamental matrix of (
1.1.1
), then there exists a continuous and
\omega
–periodic matrix
Q(t)
and a constant matrix
D\in M_{n}(\mathbb{C})
such that:

(1.3.9)

X(t,0)=Q(t)e^{Dt}\quad\textnormal{where}\quad D=\frac{1}{\omega}\ln X(\omega,0).

Proof.

As
X(t,0)
is an invertible matrix, we know that
D
is well defined and we refer the reader to
[
14
, Appendix 3]
for details. Then we have that

X(\omega,0)=e^{D\omega}\quad\textnormal{and}\quad X(0,\omega)=e^{-D\omega}.

Now, let us define
Q(t)=X(t,0)e^{-Dt}
and note that

X(t,0)=X(t,0)e^{-Dt}e^{Dt}=Q(t)e^{Dt}.

To finish the proof, we need to verify that
Q(t)
is continuous and
\omega
–periodic. As the continuity is straightforward, we will only verify the
\omega
–periodicity by using the above identities combined with the
\omega
–biperiodicity:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}Q(t+\omega)&=&X(t+\omega,0)e^{-D\omega}e^{-Dt}\\
&=&X(t+\omega,0)X(0,\omega)e^{-Dt}\\
&=&X(t+\omega,\omega)e^{-Dt}\\
&=&X(t,0)e^{-Dt}=Q(t).\end{array}

∎

Without loss of generality, we always can consider a fundamental matrix
X(t)
such that
X(0)=I
. Then by Floquet’s theorem we have that

X(t)=Q(t)e^{Dt}\quad\textnormal{and}\quad Q(t)=X(t)e^{-Dt},

where
D
is related to the monodromy matrix.

Lemma 1.3.4
.

The matrix
Q(t)=X(t)e^{-Dt}
stated in Floquet’s Theorem is a Lyapunov transformation

Proof.

On one hand, the properties (i) and (ii) of Definition
1.3.1
are consequences of the invertibility and derivability of
X(t)
and
e^{-Dt}
. On the other hand, as
Q(t)
is continuous and
\omega
–periodic, it can be proved that
Q^{-1}(t)
and
\dot{Q}(t)
have the same properties, which implies that

\sup\limits_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\left[||Q(t)||+||Q^{-1}(t)||+||\dot{Q}(t)||\right]=\max\limits_{t\in[0,\omega]}\left[||Q(t)||+||Q^{-1}(t)||+||\dot{Q}(t)||\right]

and the property (iii) follows.
∎

Theorem 1.3.3
.

The
\omega
–periodic and continuous system
(
1.3.1a
) and the autonomous linear system

\dot{y}=Dy

are clasically kinematically similar.

Proof.

As
Q(t)=X(t)e^{-Dt}
, we note that

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\dot{Q}(t)&=&\dot{X}(t)e^{-Dt}-X(t)De^{-Dt},\\
&=&A(t)X(t)e^{-Dt}-X(t)e^{-Dt}D,\\
&=&A(t)Q(t)-Q(t)D.\end{array}

Now, if
t\mapsto x(t)
is solution of the linear system (
1.3.1a
) it is straightforward to verify that

y=Q^{-1}(t)x
transforms (
1.3.1a
) into
\dot{y}=Dy
and the Theorem follows since
Q(t)
is a Lyapunov transformation.
∎

1.3.2.
Triangularization of linear systems

The following result was proved by O. Perron in
[
106
]
and is classical in the literature. Nevertheless, simpler proofs have been carried out by S.P. Diliberto in
[
49
, Th.1]
and L.Y. Adrianova in
[
1
, Th.3.3.1]
.

Theorem 1.3.4

(Perron’s Theorem of Triangularization)
.

Any linear system (
1.3.3a
) is generally kinematically similar over the real field via the linear transformation
y=Q^{-1}(t)x
, where
Q(t)
is orthogonal, to an upper triangular system

(1.3.10)

\dot{y}=B(t)y,

whose diagonal coefficients are real. Moreover, if
||A(t)||_{2}\leq M
the systems (
1.3.1a
) and (
1.3.10
) are classically kinematically similar and
||B(t)||_{2}\leq(1+n)M
.

Proof.

Let
X(t)=[x_{1}(t)\,\ldots\,x_{n}(t)]
be a fundamental matrix of (
1.3.1a
). By using the QR factorization, we have that this fundamental matrix can be decomposed as follows

(1.3.11)

X(t)=Q(t)R(t),

where
Q(t)\in M_{n}(\mathbb{R})
is the orthogonal matrix:

Q(t)=[e_{1}(t)\,\ldots\,e_{n}(t)]\quad\textnormal{with $e_{i}(t)=\frac{\xi_{i}(t)}{||\xi_{i}(t)||_{2}}$ for $i=1,\ldots,n$}

and
\{\xi_{i}(t)\}_{i=1}^{n}
the orthogonal set obtained by applying the Gram–Schmidt process to
\{x_{1}(t),\ldots,x_{n}(t)\}
, that is

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\xi_{1}&=&x_{1}\\
\xi_{2}&=&x_{2}-\langle x_{2},e_{1}\rangle e_{1}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
\xi_{n}&=&x_{n}-\sum\limits_{j=1}^{n-1}\langle x_{n},e_{j}\rangle e_{j}.\end{array}

We can see that
\{e_{i}(t)\}_{i=1}^{n}
is an orthonormal basis of solutions of (
1.3.1a
).

On the other hand,
R(t)
is the upper triangular matrix

R(t)=\left[\begin{array}[]{cccc}||\xi_{1}(t)||_{2}&\langle x_{2}(t),e_{1}(t)\rangle&\ldots&\langle x_{n}(t),e_{1}(t)\rangle\\
0&||\xi_{2}(t)||_{2}&\ldots&\langle x_{n}(t),e_{2}(t)\rangle\\
\vdots&&&\vdots\\
0&\ldots&\ldots&||\xi_{n}(t)||_{2}\end{array}\right].

We refer the reader to Lemma 3.3.1 from
[
1
]
for a detailed computation
of
Q(t)
and
R(t)
. In addition, notice that the diagonal terms
of
R(t)
verifies

r_{ii}(t)=||\xi_{i}(t)||_{2}.

We will prove that
y=Q^{-1}(t)x=Q^{T}(t)x
transforms (
1.3.1a
) into the upper triangular system
\dot{y}=B(t)y
with

B(t)=\dot{R}(t)R^{-1}(t).

In order to prove that
B(t)
is well defined, we point out some trivial consequences of (
1.3.11
) are the identities:

(1.3.12a)

\displaystyle X^{-1}(t)

\displaystyle=R^{-1}(t)Q^{T}(t),

(1.3.12b)

\displaystyle Q(t)

\displaystyle=X(t)R^{-1}(t),

(1.3.12c)

\displaystyle Q^{T}(t)

\displaystyle=R(t)X^{-1}(t).

Note that
X(t)
is derivable since is fundamental matrix of (
1.3.1a
) while
Q(t)
is also derivable since its columns are constructed with the columns of
X(t)
. Moreover, the Remark
1.1.2
says that
X^{-1}(t)
and
Q^{-1}(t)
are also derivable. By using this fact combined with the identity

(1.3.13)

R(t)=Q^{-1}(t)X(t)=Q^{T}(t)X(t).

we have that
R(t)
is derivable
and by using Lemma
1.1.1
it follows that
R^{-1}(t)
is a derivable upper triangular matrix and
B(t)
is well defined.

It is easy to verify that
B(t)
is upper triangular with real diagonal terms described by:

b_{ii}(t)=\frac{r_{ii}^{\prime}(t)}{r_{ii}(t)}=\frac{\langle\xi_{i}^{\prime}(t),\xi_{i}(t)\rangle}{\langle\xi_{i}(t),\xi_{i}(t)\rangle}.

By using (
1.3.12c
) combined with Remark
1.1.2
and the identity (
1.3.12a
) we have that:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\dot{Q}^{T}(t)&=&\dot{R}(t)X^{-1}(t)-R(t)X^{-1}(t)A(t)\\
\\
&=&\dot{R}(t)X^{-1}(t)-Q^{T}(t)A(t)\\
\\
&=&\dot{R}(t)R^{-1}(t)Q^{T}(t)-Q^{T}(t)A(t)\\
\\
&=&B(t)Q^{T}(t)-Q^{T}(t)A(t).\end{array}

Moreover, the identity

\dot{Q}^{T}(t)=-Q^{T}(t)\dot{Q}(t)Q^{T}(t)
is equivalent to

\dot{Q}(t)=A(t)Q(t)-Q(t)B(t)

and we can see that
y=Q^{-1}(t)x=Q^{T}(t)x
transforms (
1.3.1a
) into the upper triangular system
\dot{y}=B(t)y
. In addition, as
Q(t)
is orthogonal, we have that

||Q(t)||_{2}=||Q^{T}(t)||_{2}=\sqrt{\lambda_{\max}(I_{n})}=1\quad\textnormal{for any $t\in J$}

and the general kinematical similarity is verified.

From now on, we will assume that
||A(t)||_{2}\leq M
for any
t\in J
. By using (
1.3.13
) we can note that

\begin{array}[]{rcl}B(t)&=&\frac{d}{dt}\left\{Q^{T}(t)X(t)\right\}R^{-1}(t)\\
\\
&=&\left\{\frac{d}{dt}Q^{T}(t)\right\}X(t)R^{-1}(t)+Q^{T}(t)A(t)X(t)R^{-1}(t)\\
\\
&=&\underbrace{\dot{Q}^{T}(t)Q(t)}_{=V(t)}+\underbrace{Q^{T}(t)A(t)Q(t)}_{=W(t)}.\end{array}

As
Q^{T}
is orthogonal, it follows by a straightforward computation that
V(t)
is antisymmetric.
Moreover, it easy to see that

||W(t)||_{2}\leq||Q^{T}(t)||_{2}||A(t)||_{2}||Q(t)||_{2}\leq||A(t)||_{2}\leq M.

Then, by combining the above properties, we have that:

\bullet
As
B(t)
is upper triangular we have
B_{ij}(t)=0
for
i>j
and this
implies that

V_{ij}(t)=-W_{ij}(t)\quad\textnormal{for any $i>j$}.

\bullet
As
V(t)
is antisymmetric, we have that

V_{ii}(t)=0\quad\textnormal{for any $i=1,\ldots,n$ and $V_{ij}(t)=-V_{ji}(t)=W_{ji}(t)$ for any $i<j$}.

We can see that

||V(t)||_{2}\leq\sqrt{n}||V(t)||_{1}\leq\sqrt{n}||W(t)||_{1}\leq n||W(t)||_{2}

and we conclude that
||B(t)||_{2}\leq||V||_{2}+||W||_{2}\leq M(1+n)

Finally, the classical kinematical similarity follows since
Q(t)
is a
Lyapunov transformation, in fact we already know that
||Q(t)||_{2}=||Q^{T}||_{2}=1

and the identity
\dot{Q}(t)=A(t)Q(t)-Q(t)B(t)
combined with the boundedness of
A(t)
and
B(t)
implies the boundedness of
\dot{Q}(t)
.
∎

1.4.
Comments and References

1)
The fundamental and transition matrices are solutions of
a linear matrix differential equation. In this framework, in the subsection 1.1)
we provided additional results about matrix differential equations results inspired in the variation of parameters. The reader interested in this topic is referred to

[
37
,
101
,
124
,
127
]
.

2)
There exists several definitions of bounded growth and we decide to use the Palmer’s notions
of bounded growth and bounded decay in order to make some distinctions. The definition of bounded growth has been introduced by W.A. Coppel monography
[
41
]
. To the best of our knowledge, the property of bounded growth
\&
decay has been firstly used in
[
121
]

where is also called as
bounded growth
and is used to prove the uniform continuity of the solution of (
1.1.1
) with respect
to the initial conditions.

We also provides additional details as Theorem
1.2.1
and described connections with general Bohl’s exponents.

The property of bounded growth has been generalized in several ways: a first approach considers the existence of constants

L>0
and
\beta\geq 0
such that

||X(t,s)||\leq L\left(\frac{h(t)}{h(s)}\right)^{\beta}\quad\textnormal{for any $t\geq s$ with $t,s\in J_{0}:=(a,+\infty)$},

where
h\colon J_{0}\to(0,+\infty)
is an increasing homeomorphism encompassing the exponential growth. We refer the reader to

[
53
, Sect.3]
for a deeper treatment of this approach.

A second approach is given by the property of
non uniform bounded growth
if there exist positive constants
K,\alpha
and
\varepsilon
such that

||X(t,s)||\leq Ke^{\alpha|t-s|+\varepsilon|s|}\quad\textnormal{for $t,s\in J$}

and we refer the reader to
[
35
]
for details. We point out that
the uniform continuity of the solutions of (
1.1.1
) with respect to the initial
conditions is not preserved and its dependent of the initial time.

3)
In spite that the topics of adjoint system and Liouville formula are standard in the literature, we point out the originality of our proof of the Liouville’s formula and that a nice application of Corollary
1.1.3
is made by T. Burton in
[
22
]
, where it is proved that if
A(t)
is antisymmetric and
\omega
–periodic, the solutions of (
1.1.1
) are almost
periodic.

4)
With respect to the kinematical similarity, we pointed out the nuances in the definition of kinematic similarity and make a distinction between
a
classical
and a
general

definition. This distinction has been noticed previously in the russian literature
(see for example the discussion carried out in
[
1
, pp.43–44]
) where the property of classic kinematic similarity is denoted as
reducibility
and is noticed that some reduction can be achieved without Lyapunov transformations.

The Remark
1.3.4
, Lemma
1.3.1
and Corollary
1.3.1
are based in the
monography
[
1
]
. Finally, a detailed review about the kinematical similarity
and its consequences can be founded in
[
63
, Ch.4]
.

5)
In
[
25
]
(see also
[
1
, p.37]
), B.F. Bylov coined the property of
almost reducibility
to the denote
the classical kinematical similarity to an arbitrarily perturbed system, that is, the systems

\dot{x}=A(t)x\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\dot{y}=B(t)y\quad\textnormal{for any $t\geq 0$},

are almost reducible if, for any
\delta>0
, there exists a Lyapunov transformation

L_{\delta}(t)
such that the change of variables
y=L_{\delta}^{-1}(t)x
transforms the first
system into

y^{\prime}=[B(t)+\Phi(t)]y,\quad\textnormal{where $||\Phi(t)||<\delta$ for any $t\geq 0$}.

6)
A basic summary of Floquet’s theory can be founded in
[
1
,
19
,
64
,
97
]
while a deep study of linear
\omega
–periodic systems is made in
[
129
]
. The proof of Theorem
1.3.2
assumes a basic knowledge of complex matrix functions and the reader is refered to
[
1
, Ch.1]
and
[
17
, Ch.6]
.

The Floquet theory has been successfully applied in the study of the Mathieu’s equation

x^{\prime\prime}+(a-2q\cos(2t))x=0\quad\textnormal{where $a$ and $q$ are real parameters},

which arises in astronomic and physical problems and we refer the reader to
[
87
,
129
]
for details. On the other hand, there have been many efforts to extending Floquet’s theory and we point out some directions:

\bullet
To construct a Floquet’s type theory for linear nonautonomous equations where

\mathbb{R}\ni t\mapsto A(t)
is almost periodic in the Bohr–Bochner sense. In this context, we point out the work
of J. Lillo
[
81
]
devoted to linear almost periodic systems, which introduces the property:

Definition 1.4.1
.

The almost periodic linear systems (
1.3.1a
) and (
1.3.1b
) are
approximately similar
if for any
\varepsilon>0
there exists a Lyapunov transformation matrix
Q(t,\varepsilon)
such that

\sup\limits_{t\in\mathbb{R}}||Q^{-1}(t,\varepsilon)\{A(t)Q(t,\varepsilon)-\dot{Q}(t,\varepsilon)\}-B(t)||<\varepsilon,

and note that if
\varepsilon=0
, we recover (
1.3.2
) with
J=\mathbb{R}
.

In
[
40
]
W.A. Coppel considers a linear almost periodic system (
1.3.1a
)
and tries, unsuccessfully, to prove that if (
1.3.1a
) has an exponential dichotomy
on
J=\mathbb{R}
is kinematically similar to (
1.3.1b
) by means
an almost periodic Lyapunov transformation
Q(t)
. Nevertheless, by following Lillo’s ideas it is proved that
t\mapsto X(t)PX^{-1}(t)
is almost periodic.

The work of J. Ben Slimene and J. Blot
[
20
]
considers an almost periodic system
(
1.3.1a
) and proves that if its fundamental matrix is composed by
n
almost periodic solutions then is kinematically similar to an almost periodic triangular system
(
1.3.1b
) by means of an almost periodic Lyapunov transformation.

On the other hand, the works of D. Berkey
[
18
]
and M. Pinto
[
108
]
does not follow an approach based in the kinematical similarity but are focused in to obtain a fundamental matrix emulating the structure of (
1.3.9
) for the almost periodic case.

\bullet
To generalize the Floquet’s Theorem to a nonlinear framework described by the systems

(1.4.1a)

\displaystyle\dot{x}=f(t,x)\quad\textnormal{for any $t\in\mathbb{R}$},

(1.4.1b)

\displaystyle\dot{y}=g(t,y)\quad\textnormal{for any $t\in\mathbb{R}$}

where
f,g\colon\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
are such that
the existence and uniqueness of solutions in ensured and
f(t+\omega,x)=f(t,x)

and
g(t+\omega,y)=g(t,y)
for any
t
. This task has been carried
out by researchers from the Fuzhou’s school. In
[
134
]
, C. Zou and J. Shi provide
necessary conditions ensuring the existence of a nonlinear continuous transformation
H\colon\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that:

i)

H(t,\cdot)
is an homeomorphism for any
t\in\mathbb{R}
,

ii)

H(t+\omega,x)=H(t,x)
for any
(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

iii)

If
t\mapsto x(t)
is solution of (
1.4.1a
) then
t\mapsto H(t,x(t))
is solution
of (
1.4.1b
).

The
H(t,x)
can also be seen as a particular case of a generalization of kinematical similiarity to the nonlinear framework

Definition 1.4.2
.

Given two nonlinear systems

(1.4.2a)

\displaystyle\dot{x}=f(t,x)\quad\textnormal{for any $t\in\mathbb{R}$},

(1.4.2b)

\displaystyle\dot{y}=g(t,y)\quad\textnormal{for any $t\in\mathbb{R}$}

where
f,g\colon J\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
are such that the existence and uniqueness of solutions in ensured, we say that (
1.4.2a
) and (
1.4.2b
) are kinematically similar if there exists a continuously differentiable and invertible matrix
Q(t)
such that

G(t,x)=\dot{Q}(t)Q^{-1}(t)x+Q(t)F(t,Q^{-1}(t)x).

To the best of our knowledge, the above Definition has been stated in works of F. Lin
[
83
]
.

\bullet
Another generalization of Floquet theory is made by T.A. Burton and J.S. Muldowney in
[
23
]
and extended in
[
24
,
57
]
, which state that (
1.1.1
) is a generalized Floquet system with respect to
f
(GFS–
f
) if

(1.4.3)

f^{\prime}(t)A(f(t))=A(t).

where
f\colon(\alpha,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
is absolutely continuous and

f(t)>t
.

Note that
f(t)=t+\omega
with
\omega>0
satisfies the above properties for
f

and (
1.4.3
) becomes
A(t+\omega)=A(t)
, which generalizes the
\omega
–periodic case. In addition, the identity
(
1.4.3
) implies that the matrix
Y(t)=X(f(t))
verifies
Y^{\prime}(t)=A(t)Y(t)
.

1.5.
Exercises

1.-

Prove that if the matrix valued function
t\mapsto U(t)

is continuous and invertible on
J
, then
t\mapsto U^{-1}(t)
is also continuous on
J
.

2.-

Prove the statements a) to d) from Remark
1.1.2
.

3.-

Prove that any transition matrix satisfies the matrix differential equations (
1.1.7
).

4.-

Prove that any transition matrix verifies the statements from Remark
1.1.4
.

5.-

Prove that if
X(t)\in M_{n}(\mathbb{R})
and
Y(t)\in M_{m}(\mathbb{R})
are fundamental matrices of the linear systems

\dot{x}=A(t)x\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\dot{y}=B(t)y,

then the
n+m
dimensional system

\dot{z}=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}A(t)&C(t)\\
0&B(t)\end{array}\right]z

has the following transition matrix

\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}X(t,t_{0})&W(t,t_{0})\\
0&Y(t,t_{0})\end{array}\right]\quad\textnormal{with}\quad W(t,t_{0})=\int_{t_{0}}^{t}X(t,\tau)C(\tau)Y(\tau,t_{0})\,d\tau.

6.-

The linear system
\dot{x}=A(t)x
with
t\mapsto A(t)
bounded continuous on
[t_{0},+\infty)

is
uniformly exponentially stable
if and only if there exist
K>0
and
\alpha>0
such that its transition matrix verifies

||X(t,s)||\leq Ke^{-\alpha(t-s)}\quad\textnormal{for any $t\geq t_{0}$}.

Prove that the uniform exponential stability is preserved by kinematical similarity.

7.-

If the non homogeneous system (
1.1.9
) from Lemma
1.1.4
has a function
f
such that
\lim\limits_{t\to+\infty}f(t)=0

and the corresponding linear system is uniformly exponentially stable, then prove that any solution
t\mapsto x(t)
verifies
\lim\limits_{t\to+\infty}x(t)=0
.

8.-

If
X(t,s)
is a transition matrix of a linear system, prove that

[X(t,s)^{-1}]^{T}
is a transition matrix for its adjoint.

9.-

Under the assumption that

A(t)\int_{\tau}^{t}A(s)\,ds=\left[\int_{\tau}^{t}A(s)\,ds\right]A(t),

prove that

X(t,\tau)=e^{\int_{\tau}^{t}A(s)\,ds}

is transition matrix of
\dot{x}=A(t)x
.

10.-

Prove Proposition
1.2.2
.

11.-

Under the assumption that
t\mapsto A(t)
is continuous and antisymmetric for any
t\in\mathbb{R}
prove that any fundamental matrix of
the linear system
\dot{x}=A(t)x
verifies
\det X(t)=\det X(0)
for any

t\in\mathbb{R}
.

12.-

Prove that the classical kinematical similarity is an equivalence relation.

13.-

Prove the statements of Remark
1.3.3
.

14.-

Under the assumption that the linear systems

\dot{x}=A(t)x\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\dot{y}=B(t)y

are classicaly kinematically similar on
J
by means of the transformation
Q(t)
, prove that the adjoint systems

\dot{x}=-A^{T}(t)x\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\dot{y}=-B^{T}(t)y

are also kinematically similar on
J
. Note that the corresponding transformation is strongly related to
Q(t)
.

15.-

Under the assumption that the linear systems

\dot{x}=A(t)x\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\dot{y}=B(t)y

are classicaly kinematically similar on
J
by means of the transformation
Q(t)
, prove that

\det Q(t)=\frac{\det X(t_{0})}{\det Y(t_{0})}e^{\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\textnormal{Tr}[A(s)-B(s)]\,ds}.

16.-

Let
\dot{x}=A(t)x
be a system with solutions defined in all the real line and let
\dot{y}=-A^{T}(t)y
be its adjoint. If any solution
t\mapsto x(t)
of the initial system verifies
\lim\limits_{t\to+\infty}x(t)=0
prove that any solution
t\mapsto y(t)
of the adjoint system is such that the limit
\lim\limits_{t\to+\infty}|y(t)|
diverges.

17.-

An autonomous system
\dot{y}=Dy
is
hyperbolic
if and only if the real part of all the eigenvalues of
D
is different from zero. Prove that if

\dot{x}=A(t)x
with
t\mapsto A(t)
continuous and
\omega
–periodic is classically kinematically similar to an hyperbolic system if and only if its Floquet’s multipliers are different from
1
.

18.-

Prove that if
A
is continuous,
\omega
–periodic and antisymmetric, then any Floquet multiplier of
\dot{x}=A(t)x
is
\rho=1
.

19.-

In the context of Lemma
1.3.4
, prove that
Q^{-1}(t)
and
\dot{Q}(t)
are
\omega
–periodic.

20.-

Prove that the
n+m
–dimensional system

\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\dot{x}_{1}\\
\dot{x}_{2}\end{array}\right)=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}A(t)&0\\
0&C(t)\end{array}\right]\left(\begin{array}[]{c}x_{1}\\
x_{2}\end{array}\right)

with
A(t)\in M_{n}(\mathbb{R})
and
C(t)\in M_{m}(\mathbb{R})
is generally kinematically similar to

\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\dot{y}_{1}\\
\dot{y}_{2}\end{array}\right)=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}B(t)&0\\
0&C(t)\end{array}\right]\left(\begin{array}[]{c}y_{1}\\
y_{2}\end{array}\right),

by a transformation

Q(t)=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}Q_{1}(t)&0\\
0&I_{m}\end{array}\right]

where
Q_{1}(t)\in M_{n}(\mathbb{R})
and
I_{m}
is the identity on
\in M_{m}(\mathbb{R})
, if and only if

\dot{x}_{1}=A(t)x_{1}\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\dot{y}_{1}=B(t)y_{1},

are generally kinematically similar by the transformation
Q_{1}
.

21.-

Let
X_{A}(t)
and
X_{B}(t)
be fundamental matrices of

\dot{x}=A(t)x
and
\dot{y}=B(t)y
respectively. Prove that if

B(t)X_{A}(t)=X_{A}(t)B(t),

then
X_{A}(t)X_{B}(t)
is fundamental matrix of

\dot{z}=[A(t)+B(t)]z
.

22.-

Let
X_{A}(t)
and
X_{B}(t)
be fundamental matrices of

\dot{x}=A(t)x
and
\dot{y}=B(t)y
respectively. Prove that if

A(t)X_{B}(t)=X_{B}(t)A(t)

then
X_{B}(t)X_{A}(t)
is fundamental matrix of

\dot{z}=[A(t)+B(t)]z
.

23.-

Prove that if we consider
F(t,x)=A(t)x
and
G(t,x)=B(t)x
in the Definition
1.4.2
, we can recover the definitions of general Kinematical similarity for linear systems.

24.-

A linear control system is described by

(1.5.1)

x^{\prime}=A(t)x+B(t)u\quad\textnormal{for any $t\in J$},

where
t\mapsto u(t)
is known as the
input
or
control
of the system. In addition
the uncontrolled part is given by:

x^{\prime}=A(t)x\quad\textnormal{for any $t\in J$},

which is usually know as the
plant
of (
1.5.1
) and a fundamental
matrix will be denoted by
X_{A}(t)
.

The
controllability gramians
associated to (
1.5.1
) are defined by the matrices:

W(a,b)=\displaystyle\int_{a}^{b}X_{A}(a,s)B(s)B^{T}(s)X_{A}^{T}(a,s)\;ds

and

K(a,b)=\displaystyle\int_{a}^{b}X_{A}(b,s)B(s)B^{T}(s)X_{A}^{T}(b,s)\;ds.

23.1)

Prove that above controllability gramians satisfy
the following relations:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}K(a,b)&=&X_{A}(b,a)W(a,b)X_{A}^{T}(b,a)\\
\\
W(a,b)&=&X_{A}(a,b)K(a,b)X_{A}^{T}(a,b).\end{array}\right.

Chapter 2
Exponential Dichotomy

Hyperbolicity is a fundamental property of dynamic systems that enables the splitting of phase space into subspaces that contract and expand under map
f,
respectively. When the space phase is a smooth manifold of finite dimension, and the considered function is smooth, determining the time evolution, then the evolution of an initial point
x
is obtained by solving a system of ordinary differential equations. If the system is autonomous, the hyperbolicity of an equilibrium point
x_{0}
is determined by the real parts of the eigenvalues of the Jacobian matrix of
f
at
x_{0};
however, this condition cannot be replicated in a nonautonomous system as is stated in
[
89
, p. 310]
. To study hyperbolicity effectively in that context, one might examine the property of exponential dichotomy and the related spectrum, which emulates the work done by eigenvalues in a nonautonomous framework.

2.1.
Autonomous hyperbolicity

Definition 2.1.1
.

Given a matrix
A\in M_{n}(\mathbb{R})
, the autonomous system of ordinary differential equations

(2.1.1)

\dot{x}=Ax,

is
hyperbolic
if
A
has no eigenvalues having real part zero.

The hyperbolicity condition has remarkable consequences and applications in the qualitative theory of differential equations, we will highlight the most relevant ones:

a) Splitting between stable and unstable spaces.
Let

\lambda_{j}=a_{j}+ib_{j}
be a (possible multiple) eigenvalue of
A
with
generalized eigenvector
W_{j}=\bf{u}_{j}+i\bf{v}_{j}
. Then we can see that

\mathcal{B}=\{\bf{u_{1}},\ldots,\bf{u_{k}},\bf{u_{k+1}},\bf{v_{k+1}},\ldots,\bf{u_{m}},\bf{v_{m}}\}

with
n=2m-k
is a basis of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that
\mathbb{R}^{n}=E^{s}\oplus E^{u}
, where
E^{u}
and
E^{s}
are the
stable
and
unstable

spaces defined by

E^{s}=\text{Span}\{\bf{u_{j}},\bf{v_{j}}\mid\textnormal{$a_{j}<0$}\}\hskip 11.49994pt\textnormal{and}\hskip 11.49994pt\textnormal{$E^{u}$}=\text{Span}\{\bf{u_{j}},\bf{v_{j}}\mid\textnormal{$a_{j}>0$}\}.

Let us recall that any solution
t\mapsto x(t)
of (
2.1.1
) passing
through
x_{0}
at
t=0
can be written as
x(t)=e^{At}x_{0}
, then it follows that; see for example
[
105
, p.55]
;
the subspaces
E^{s}
and
E^{u}
are invariants under the flow induced by (
2.1.1
), namely, if
x_{0}\in E^{s}
(
resp
.
x_{0}\in E^{u}
) then
x(t)\in E^{s}
(
resp
.
x(t)\in E^{u}
).

A consequence of the above mentioned invariance is that if
x_{0}=x_{s}+x_{u}
with
x_{s}\in E^{s}

and
x_{u}\in E^{u}
, then it is easy to verify that any
solution
x(t)=e^{At}x_{0}
can be splitted as

x(t)=x_{s}(t)+x_{u}(t)
where

x_{s}(t)=e^{At}x_{s}\in E^{s}\quad\textnormal{and}\quad x_{u}(t)=e^{At}x_{u}\in E^{u}\quad\textnormal{for any $t\in\mathbb{R}$}.

b) Admissibility properties.

Another consequence of the hyperbolicity condition is the following
result, see
e.g

[
43
, p.81]
for details:

Proposition 2.1.1
.

If (
2.1.1
) is hyperbolic then for any

f\in BC(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R}^{n})
there exists a unique
solution
t\mapsto y_{f}(t)\in BC(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R}^{n})
of the inhomogeneous system

\dot{y}=Ay+f(t)

such that

\sup\limits_{t\in\mathbb{R}}|y_{f}(t)|\leq\alpha(A)\sup\limits_{t\in\mathbb{R}}|f(t)|,

where
\alpha(A)
is a constant dependent of
A
.

c) Linearization results.

The classical linearization theorem currently known as the Hartman–Grobman Theorem is a result obtained by P. Hartman
[
65
]
and D.M. Grobman
[
59
]
respectively. It has a distinguished place in the
qualitative theory of ordinary differential equations and the hyperbolicity condition plays
an essential role.

In order to state the above mentioned result, let us consider the nonlinear system

(2.1.2)

\dot{y}=g(y),

where
g\in C^{1}(\Omega,\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is an open set containing the origin such that
g(0)=0
and
Dg(0)
is the derivative of
g
at the origin. The solution of (
2.1.2
) passing through
y_{0}\in\Omega
at
t=0
will be denoted by
\phi_{t}(y_{0})
and its local existence and uniqueness will be assumed.

Proposition 2.1.2

(Hartman–Grobman Theorem)
.

If the linear system

(2.1.3)

\dot{x}=Dg(0)x

is hyperbolic, then there exists an homeomorphism
H\colon U\to V

such that

a)

The sets
U
and
V
are open in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and contain the origin.

b)

For any
y_{0}\in U
, there exists an open interval
I_{y_{0}}\subset\mathbb{R}
containing zero such that

H\circ\phi_{t}(y_{0})=e^{At}H(y_{0})\quad\textnormal{for any $t\in I_{y_{0}}$}.

The Hartman–Grobman homeomorphism can be seen as a
dictionary
mapping solutions of the non linear system (
2.1.2
) into solutions of the linear one
(
2.1.3
) and viceversa for any
t\in I_{y_{0}}
as follows:

i)

If
t\mapsto\phi_{t}(y_{0})
is a solution of (
2.1.2
) passing trough
y_{0}
at
t=0
, then
t\mapsto H(\phi_{t}(y_{0}))
is solution of (
2.1.3
) passing through
H(y_{0})
at
t=0
.

ii)

If
t\mapsto e^{At}z_{0}
is solution of (
2.1.3
) passing through
z_{0}
at
t=0
, then

H^{-1}(e^{At}z_{0})
is a solution of (
2.1.2
) passing trough
H^{-1}(z_{0})
at
t=0
.

iii)

A direct consequence of i) and ii) combined with the uniqueness of solutions is that
H(0)=0
.

We point out that the three above problems cannot be directly generalized when
A
is not constant, since the eigenvalues does not provide neither qualitative nor accurate information about the system, which prevents the naive extension
of the hyperbolicity property to the nonautonomous framework.

2.2.
Nonautonomous hyperbolicity

We stress that it does not exists an ubiquitous definition of
hyperbolicity
in the nonautonomous setting. In particular, the above topics (splitting, admissibility and linearization)
cannot be addressed by using the eigenvalues of a time varying matrix.

Despite these nuances, the concept of
dichotomy
plays a relevant role, being the exponential dichotomy
[
41
,
84
,
93
]
introduced by O. Perron in the 30’s the most relevant
[
106
]
.

2.2.1.
Exponential Dichotomy

Let us consider the nonautonomous system of ordinary differential equations

(2.2.1)

x^{\prime}=A(t)x,

where
A(t)
is a locally integrable matrix.

Definition 2.2.1
.

The system (
2.2.1
) has an
exponential dichotomy
on the interval
J\subseteq\mathbb{R}
if, given a fundamental matrix
X(t)
, there exist positive numbers
K>0
,
\alpha>0
and a projection
P^{2}=P
such that

(2.2.2)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rccr}||X(t)PX^{-1}(s)||&\leq Ke^{-\alpha(t-s)}&\textnormal{if}&t\geq s,\quad t,s\in J\\
||X(t)(I-P)X^{-1}(s)||&\leq Ke^{-\alpha(s-t)}&\textnormal{if}&t\leq s,\quad t,s\in J.\end{array}\right.

It is worth to emphasize that the constants
K
and
\alpha
are known as the constants of the exponential dichotomy. Nevertheless, we point out that the above notation is not univocal and several references consider the pairs
(K_{1},\alpha_{1})
and
(K_{2},\alpha_{2})
in the first and second inequalities respectively.

In addition, we will usually consider the unbounded intervals

J=(-\infty,0]=\mathbb{R}_{0}^{-},\quad J=[0,+\infty)=\mathbb{R}_{0}^{+}\quad\textnormal{or}\quad J=\mathbb{R}.

If the exponential dichotomy is verified on
\mathbb{R}
, it is common to say that the linear system
has a dichotomy on the full line. Similarly, if the exponential dichotomy is verified
on
\mathbb{R}_{0}^{+}
or
\mathbb{R}_{0}^{-}
, it is respectively said that
the system has a dichotomy on the right or left half line.

If the above matrix norm is unitary, that is

||I||=1
, the limit case
P=I
and
t=s
allows to consider
K\geq 1
in Definition
2.2.1
.

In order to illustrate this property, we will revisit the example from L. Markus and H. Yamabe
[
89
]
stated
in the previous chapter:

Example 1
.

The system

\dot{x}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}-1+\frac{3}{2}\cos^{2}(t)&1-\frac{3}{2}\cos(t)\sin(t)\\
-1-\frac{3}{2}\cos(t)\sin(t)&-1+\frac{3}{2}\sin^{2}(t)\end{array}\right)x

has a normalized fundamental matrix

X(t)=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}e^{\frac{t}{2}}\cos(t)&e^{-t}\sin(t)\\
-e^{\frac{t}{2}}\sin(t)&e^{-t}\cos(t)\end{array}\right].

It can be verified that the system has exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}
with projection

P=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&0\\
0&1\end{array}\right)

and constants
K=1
and
\alpha=1/2
.

Remark 2.2.1
.

Note that the identity matrix
P=I
and the null matrix
P=0

are projections, which are called as
trivial projections
. If

P
is a non trivial projection in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, it will be useful to recall that
[
69
, p. 210]
:

(1)

0<\text{Rank}\,P<n
,

(2)

Any
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
has the decomposition
\xi=P\xi+(I-P)\xi
,

(3)

v\in\text{Im}\,P
if and only if
Pv=v
,

(4)

\mathbb{R}^{n}=\ker\,P\oplus\text{Im}\,P
.

Remark 2.2.2
.

In linear algebra, a projector
P
is associated to a decomposition
of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
as a direct sum
\mathbb{R}^{n}=U\oplus W
such that
U

is invariant under
P
. This arise the following question: If the property of exponential dichotomy is verified in
J
, which is the subspace of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
invariant under the projector
P
? We will back on this question in the next section and the next chapter.

A careful reading of Definition
2.2.1
shows that the projection
P
is strongly associated
to a fundamental matrix
X(t)
such that (
2.2.2
) is verified. Note that if
Y(t)
is
another fundamental matrix, there exists a change of basis matrix
V
such
that
Y(t)=X(t)V
and we refer to the statement a) of Remark
1.1.1
in Chapter 1 for details. Now,
we rewrite the equation (
2.2.2
) as follows

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rccr}||X(t)VV^{-1}PVV^{-1}X^{-1}(s)||&\leq Ke^{-\alpha(t-s)}&\textnormal{if}&t\geq s,\quad t,s\in J\\
||X(t)VV^{-1}[I-P]VV^{-1}X^{-1}(s)||&\leq Ke^{-\alpha(s-t)}&\textnormal{if}&t\leq s,\quad t,s\in J.\end{array}\right.

Moreover, as
V
is a nonsingular matrix, it follows that
\tilde{P}=VPV^{-1}
is also a projection and
I-\tilde{P}=V[I-P]V^{-1}
. Then, the above inequality is equivalent to

(2.2.3)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rccr}||Y(t)\tilde{P}Y^{-1}(s)||&\leq Ke^{-\alpha(t-s)}&\textnormal{if}&t\geq s,\quad t,s\in J\\
||Y(t)[I-\tilde{P}]Y^{-1}(s)||&\leq Ke^{-\alpha(s-t)}&\textnormal{if}&t\leq s,\quad t,s\in J.\end{array}\right.

In summary, the above estimates allow us to state the following remark:

Remark 2.2.3
.

Assume that the linear system (
2.2.1
) has an exponential dichotomy on
J
with constants
K>0
and
\alpha>0
. If
X(t)
and
Y(t)
are fundamental matrices of (
2.2.1
) the inequalities
(
2.2.2
) and (
2.2.3
) are equivalent. Namely, there exists a nonsingular matrix
V
such that

Y(t)=X(t)V\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\tilde{P}=VPV^{-1}

and, consequently, the projections are not unique but are similar.

Remark 2.2.4
.

As any projection
P
is idempotent, it can be proved that the eigenvalues of any non trivial projection
P
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
are either
0
or
1
, which implies its diagonalizability. Then, for any projection
P
there exists a nonsingular matrix
V
such that

V^{-1}PV=P_{r}=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}I_{r}&0\\
0&0\end{array}\right]\quad\textnormal{and}\quad V^{-1}[I-P]V=I-P_{r}=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}0&0\\
0&I_{r-1}\end{array}\right]

where
r
is the rank of
P
. Moreover, it follows (see
e.g.

[
17
, p.67]
) that
P
is semi definite positive. From now on,
P_{r}
which will be called as the
canonical projection
.

A useful consequence of Remarks
2.2.3
and
2.2.4
is the following result, which will play a key role in future chapters:

Lemma 2.2.1
.

If the system (
2.2.1
) has an exponential dichotomy on the interval
J\subseteq\mathbb{R}
constants
K>0
,
\alpha>0
, then there exist a fundamental matrix
Y(t)
and a canonic projection
P_{r}
such that

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rccr}||Y(t)P_{r}Y^{-1}(s)||&\leq Ke^{-\alpha(t-s)}&\textnormal{if}&t\geq s,\quad t,s\in J\\
||Y(t)[I-P_{r}]Y^{-1}(s)||&\leq Ke^{-\alpha(s-t)}&\textnormal{if}&t\leq s,\quad t,s\in J.\end{array}\right.

The next result is only an alternative characterization of the exponential
dichotomy property. Nevertheless, its intermediate computations and estimations will be useful in the future.

Proposition 2.2.1
.

Let
X(t)
be a fundamental matrix of the linear system (
2.2.1
). This system has an exponential dichotomy on
J
with positive constants
K_{1},K_{2},\alpha
and a projection
P^{2}=P
if and only if for any
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
it follows that:

(2.2.4)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcll}|X(t)P\xi|&\leq&K_{1}e^{-\alpha(t-s)}|X(s)P\xi|&\textnormal{{if} $t\geq s$,}\\
|X(t)(I-P)\xi|&\leq&K_{2}e^{-\alpha(s-t)}|X(s)(I-P)\xi|&\textnormal{{if} $s\geq t$,}\\
||X(t)PX^{-1}(t)||&\leq&M&\textnormal{{for any} $t$,}\end{array}\right.

where
t,s\in J
.

Proof.

Firstly, we will assume that (
2.2.1
) has an exponential dichotomy and (
2.2.2
) is satisfied. Notice that if
t\geq s
, the first inequality of (
2.2.2
) allow us to deduce:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}|X(t)P\xi|&=&|X(t)PX^{-1}(s)X(s)P\xi|\\
&\leq&||X(t)PX^{-1}(s)||\,|X(s)P\xi|\\
&\leq&K_{1}e^{-\alpha(t-s)}|X(s)P\xi|,\end{array}

and the first inequality of (
2.2.4
) follows with
K_{1}=K
. The second inequality can be proved
in a similar way with
K_{2}=K
while the third inequality follows directly (with
M=K
) by considering
t=s
in
(
2.2.2
).

Secondly, assume that the inequalities (
2.2.4
) are verified. In the case
t\geq s
we can consider
\xi=X^{-1}(s)\eta

with
\eta\neq 0
. The first inequality of (
2.2.4
) implies that

\begin{array}[]{rcl}|X(t)P\xi|=|X(t)PX^{-1}(s)\eta|&\leq&K_{1}e^{-\alpha(t-s)}|X(s)PX^{-1}(s)\eta|\\
\\
&\leq&MK_{1}e^{-\alpha(t-s)}|\eta|,\end{array}

then for any
t\geq s
, we can deduce that:

\sup\limits_{\eta\neq 0}\frac{|X(t)PX^{-1}(s)\eta|}{|\eta|}=||X(t)PX^{-1}(s)||\leq MK_{1}e^{-\alpha(t-s)}.

Similarly, if
t\leq s
and considering
\xi=X^{-1}(s)\eta
,
the second inequality of (
2.2.4
) implies that

\begin{array}[]{rcl}|X(t)[I-P]X^{-1}(s)\eta|&\leq&K_{2}e^{-\alpha(s-t)}|X(s)[I-P]X^{-1}(s)\eta|\\
\\
&\leq&MK_{2}e^{-\alpha(t-s)}|\eta|,\end{array}

and for any
t\leq s
, we can deduce that:

\sup\limits_{\eta\neq 0}\frac{|X(t)[I-P]X^{-1}(s)\eta|}{|\eta|}=||X(t)[I-P]X^{-1}(s)||\leq MK_{2}e^{-\alpha(s-t)},

then the exponential dichotomy is verified with projector
P
and the constants
(K,\alpha)

with
K=\max\{MK_{1},MK_{2}\}
.
∎

The above result is stated without proof in
[
41
, p.11]
, to fill this gap we follow the lines of
[
21
, Prop. 3.4]
.
As pointed out in
[
41
]
, the third inequality of (
2.2.4
) is not implied
by the previous ones. Indeed it provides an example of linear system
uniquely satisfying the first two inequalities of (
2.2.4
) but not having an exponential dichotomy. This example is described in the section of exercises.

Nevertheless, if the property of bounded growth is verified,
then the third inequality of (
2.2.4
) is implied by the previous ones as stated by
the following result:

Lemma 2.2.2
.

If the linear system (
2.2.1
) has a uniform bounded growth on
J
and there exists a non null projector

P
and positive constants
K_{1},K_{2}
and
\alpha
such that the first two inequalities of (
2.2.4
) are verified, then
there exists
M>0
such that the third one is also verified.

Proof.

Firstly, let us define the positive maps for any
t\in J
:

\sigma(t)=||X(t)PX^{-1}(t)||\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\rho(t)=||X(t)[I-P]X^{-1}(t)||.

By using triangular inequality, it can be proved that

\rho(t)-\sigma(t)\leq||I||\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\sigma(t)-\rho(t)\leq||I||,

which implies that

(2.2.5)

|\sigma(t)-\rho(t)|\leq||I||\quad\textnormal{for any $t\in J$}.

Now, let us consider
T>0
and
\eta\neq 0
. By using the first and second inequalities of (
2.2.4
) with
\xi=X^{-1}(t)\eta
, it follows that

\begin{array}[]{rcl}|X(t+T)PX^{-1}(t)\eta|&\leq&\displaystyle K_{1}e^{-\alpha T}|X(t)PX^{-1}(t)\eta|,\end{array}

and

\begin{array}[]{rcl}|X(t)[I-P]X^{-1}(t)\eta|&\leq&\displaystyle K_{2}e^{-\alpha T}|X(t+T)[I-P]X^{-1}(t)\eta|,\end{array}

which leads to

||X(t+T)PX^{-1}(t)||\leq K_{1}e^{-\alpha T}\sigma(t),

and

K_{2}^{-1}e^{\alpha T}\rho(t)\leq||X(t+T)[I-P]X^{-1}(t)||.

Let us construct the strictly increasing map

T\mapsto\gamma(T)=K_{2}^{-1}e^{\alpha T}-K_{1}e^{-\alpha T}

and choose
T
big enough such that
\gamma(T)>0
