. Now, notice that the above estimations imply:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\gamma(T)&\leq&||\rho^{-1}(t)X(t+T)[I-P]X^{-1}(t)||-||\sigma^{-1}(t)X(t+T)PX^{-1}(t)||\\
\\
&\leq&||\rho^{-1}(t)X(t+T)[I-P]X^{-1}(t)+\sigma^{-1}(t)X(t+T)PX^{-1}(t)||\\
\\
&\leq&||\rho^{-1}(t)X(t+T,t)\Phi(t)[I-P]X^{-1}(t)+\sigma^{-1}(t)X(t+T,t)X(t)PX^{-1}(t)||\\
\\
&\leq&||X(t+T,t)||\,||\rho^{-1}(t)X(t)[I-P]X^{-1}(t)+\sigma^{-1}(t)X(t)PX^{-1}(t)||.\end{array}

Moreover, we know by hypothesis that (
2.2.1
) has
a bounded growth on
J
. That is, according to Definition
1.2.3
from Chapter 1, there exist
K_{0}\geq 1

and
\beta\geq 0
such that:

||X(t+T,t)||\leq K_{0}e^{\beta T}.

Now, by the above inequalities combined with (
2.2.5
). we have that:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\gamma(T)K_{0}^{-1}e^{-\beta T}&\leq&||\rho^{-1}(t)X(t)[I-P]X^{-1}(t)+\sigma^{-1}(t)X(t)PX^{-1}(t)||\\
\\
&=&||\rho^{-1}(t)I+(\sigma^{-1}(t)-\rho^{-1}(t))X(t)PX^{-1}(t)||\\
\\
&\leq&\rho^{-1}(t)||I||+|\sigma^{-1}(t)-\rho^{-1}(t)|\sigma(t)\\
\\
&=&\rho^{-1}(t)\left(||I||+|\rho(t)-\sigma(t)|\right)\\
\\
&\leq&2||I||\rho^{-1}(t),\end{array}

and we have that

\rho(t)\leq\frac{2||I||K_{0}e^{\beta T}}{\gamma(T)}.

Finally by (
2.2.5
) we have that

||X(t)PX^{-1}(t)||\leq||I||\left(1+\frac{2K_{0}e^{\beta T}}{\gamma(T)}\right),

and the Lemma follows since the above estimation is valid for any
t\in J
.
∎

2.2.2.
Distinguished examples of exponential dichotomy

Lemma 2.2.3
.

If
A(t)=A\in M_{n}(\mathbb{C})
is a constant matrix and any eigenvalue
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
of
A
has non zero real part, then the autonomous system
\dot{x}=Ax
has an exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

Firstly, we will assume that
A
is a diagonalizable matrix with eigenvalues
\lambda_{j}=\alpha_{j}+i\beta_{j}\in\mathbb{C}
with
j=1\ldots,n
whose real parts satisfy

(2.2.6)

\alpha_{1}\leq\ldots\leq\alpha_{k}<0<\alpha_{k+1}\ldots\leq\alpha_{n}.

Then, there exists an invertible matrix
Q
such that

Q^{-1}AQ=D=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}\lambda_{1}&&\\
&\ddots&\\
&&\lambda_{n}\end{array}\right),

and
y=Q^{-1}x
transforms
\dot{x}=Ax
into the diagonal system

(2.2.7)

\dot{y}=Dy,

which has the following fundamental matrix

Y(t)=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}Y_{1}(t)&0\\
0&Y_{2}(t)\end{array}\right],

where the matrices
Y_{1}(t)\in M_{k}(\mathbb{C})
and
Y_{2}(t)\in M_{n-k}(\mathbb{C})
are respectively defined by

Y_{1}(t)=\left[\begin{array}[]{ccc}e^{\lambda_{1}t}&&\\
&\ddots&\\
&&e^{\lambda_{k}t}\end{array}\right]\quad\textnormal{and}\quad Y_{2}(t)=\left[\begin{array}[]{ccc}e^{\lambda_{k+1}t}&&\\
&\ddots&\\
&&e^{\lambda_{n}t}\end{array}\right].

Let
I_{k}
be the identity matrix of
M_{k}(\mathbb{C})
and consider the canonical projection

P_{k}=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}I_{k}&0\\
0&0\end{array}\right].

By using the matrix norm
||\cdot||_{1}
combined with (
2.2.6
) we can see that if
t\geq s
then

\begin{array}[]{rcl}||Y(t)P_{k}Y^{-1}(s)||_{1}&=&\max\{e^{-|\alpha_{1}|(t-s)},\ldots,e^{-|\alpha_{k}|(t-s)}\}\\
&=&e^{-|\alpha_{k}|(t-s)}\quad\textnormal{if $t\geq s\quad t,s\in\mathbb{R}$}.\end{array}

Similarly, if
t\leq s
we can see that

\begin{array}[]{rcl}||Y(t)[I-P_{k}]Y^{-1}(s)||_{1}&=&\max\{e^{\alpha_{k+1}(t-s)},\ldots,e^{\alpha_{n}(t-s)}\}\\
&=&\max\{e^{-\alpha_{k+1}(s-t)},\ldots,e^{-\alpha_{n}(s-t)}\}\\
&=&e^{-|\alpha_{k+1}|(s-t)}\quad\textnormal{if $s\geq t\quad t,s\in\mathbb{R}$}.\end{array}

Let
\alpha=\min\{|\alpha_{k}|,\alpha_{k+1}\}
and note that

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rccr}||Y(t)P_{k}Y^{-1}(s)||_{1}&\leq e^{-\alpha(t-s)}&\textnormal{if}&t\geq s,\quad t,s\in\mathbb{R}\\
||Y(t)(I-P_{k})Y^{-1}(s)||_{1}&\leq e^{-\alpha(s-t)}&\textnormal{if}&t\leq s,\quad t,s\in\mathbb{R},\end{array}\right.

then we can conclude that the diagonal system (
2.2.7
) has an exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}
.

It is an easy exercise to show that
X(t)=QY(t)
is a fundamental matrix of
\dot{x}=Ax
. Then we can deduce

\begin{array}[]{rcl}||X(t)P_{k}X^{-1}(s)||_{1}&=&||QY(t)P_{k}Y^{-1}(s)Q^{-1}||_{1}\\
\\
&\leq&||Q||_{1}\,||Q^{-1}||_{1}\,||X(t)P_{k}X^{-1}(s)||_{1}\\
\\
&\leq&||Q||_{1}||Q^{-1}||_{1}e^{-\alpha(t-s)}\quad t\geq s,\end{array}

and similarly

\begin{array}[]{rcl}||X(t)[I-P_{k}]X^{-1}(s)||_{1}&\leq&||Q||_{1}||Q^{-1}||_{1}e^{-\alpha(s-t)}\quad s\geq t,\end{array}

which can be summarized as

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rccr}||X(t)P_{k}X^{-1}(s)||_{1}&\leq||Q||_{1}||Q^{-1}||_{1}e^{-\alpha(t-s)}&\textnormal{if}&t\geq s,\quad t,s\in\mathbb{R}\\
||X(t)(I-P_{k})X^{-1}(s)||_{1}&\leq||Q||_{1}||Q^{-1}||_{1}e^{-\alpha(s-t)}&\textnormal{if}&t\leq s,\quad t,s\in\mathbb{R}\end{array}\right.

and the exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}
follows.

When the matrix is not diagonalizable we refer the reader to
[
42
, pp.214–215]
where the proof follows a less constructive approach which is based in the Riesz’s projector.

∎

The method used to proof the above Lemma (in the diagonalizable case) illustrates a particular case of the following result:

Theorem 2.2.1
.

If the linear system (
2.2.1
) has an exponential dichotomy on
J
and is generally kinematically similar to

(2.2.8)

y^{\prime}=B(t)y\quad\textnormal{with $t\in J$,}

then (
2.2.8
) has an exponential dichotomy on
J
with the same projection
P
and rate
\alpha
.

Proof.

If (
2.2.1
) and (
2.2.8
) are generally kinematically similar by the transformation
Q(t)
. It is an easy exercise to prove that if
X(t)
is the fundamental matrix of (
2.2.1
), then
Y(t)=Q^{-1}(t)X(t)
is the fundamental matrix of (
2.2.8
).

Now, let us assume that

t\geq s
,
t,s\in J
and
P
is the projection of Definition
2.2.1
. By using the boundedness of
Q
and
Q^{-1}
we can see that

\begin{array}[]{rcl}||Y(t)PY^{-1}(s)||&=&||Q^{-1}(t)X(t)PX^{-1}(s)Q(s)||\\
\\
&\leq&||Q(s)||\,||Q^{-1}(t)||\,||X(t)PX^{-1}(s)||\\
\\
&\leq&||Q||_{\infty}||Q^{-1}||_{\infty}Ke^{-\alpha(t-s)}\quad t\geq s.\end{array}

The inequality for the case
t\leq s
can be proved similarly.
∎

Corollary 2.2.1
.

If
t\mapsto A(t)
is continuous and
A(t+\omega)=A(t)
for any
t\in\mathbb{R}
, then the system
\dot{x}=A(t)x
has an exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}
if the Floquet multipliers are not in the unit circle.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, let us assume that the fundamental matrix is normalized,
i.e.
,
X(0)=I
. Then by Floquet’s theory we know that

X(t)=Q(t)e^{Dt}
where
t\mapsto Q(t)
is
\omega
–periodic and
D\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}
satisfies

D=\frac{1}{\omega}\ln X(\omega).

As the Floquet’s multipliers are the eigenvalues of
X(\omega)
, it is a technical exercise of matrix functions to prove that the eigenvalues of
D
cannot be purely imaginary and by Lemma
2.2.3
we have that the autonomous system

\dot{y}=Dy

has an exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}
.

Finally, by Theorem
1.3.3
from Chapter 1, we know that the above system is classically kinematically similar to
\dot{x}=A(t)x
, the Theorem
2.2.1
implies that the
\omega
–periodic system
has an exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}
and the result follows.
∎

2.2.3.
An alternative characterization for the exponential dichotomy

Theorem 2.2.2
.

Let
X(t)
be a fundamental matrix of the linear system (
2.2.1
). This system has an exponential dichotomy on
J
with projection

P(\cdot)
and positive constants
K
,
\alpha
if and only if

(2.2.9)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rccr}||X(t,s)P(s)||&\leq Ke^{-\alpha(t-s)}&\textnormal{if}&t\geq s,\quad t,s\in J\\
||X(t,s)[I-P(s)]||&\leq Ke^{-\alpha(s-t)}&\textnormal{if}&t\leq s,\quad t,s\in J,\end{array}\right.

where
t\mapsto P(t)
is also a projection for any
t\in J
such that

(2.2.10)

P(t)X(t,s)=X(t,s)P(s)

and the rank of
P(t)
and
P
are identical for any
t\in J
.

Proof.

(\Rightarrow
Let us assume that the linear system (
2.2.1
) has an exponential dichotomy on

J
described by (
2.2.2
). Now, we construct the matrix

P(t)=X(t)PX^{-1}(t)\quad\textnormal{for any $t\in J$}.

Firstly, it is easy to verify that
P(t)
is a projection for any
t\in J
, moreover

\begin{array}[]{rcl}P(t)X(t,s)&=&X(t)PX^{-1}(s)\\
\\
&=&X(t,s)X(s)PX^{-1}(s)\\
\\
&=&X(t,s)P(s),\end{array}

and (
2.2.10
) is satisfied.

A direct byproduct of the above identities are

X(t)PX^{-1}(s)=X(t,s)P(s)\quad\textnormal{and}\quad X(t)[I-P]X^{-1}(s)=X(t,s)[I-P(s)],

then, the inequalities (
2.2.2
) imply (
2.2.9
).

Finally, a direct consequence of (
2.2.10
) is that if

\{\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\ldots,\xi_{d}\}
is a basis of
\ker P(s)
then
\{X(t,s)\xi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{d}
is a basis of
\ker P(t)
, this is implies

\text{Rank}P(t)
is invariant for any
t\in J
and consequently is equal to

\text{Rank}\,P(0)=\text{Rank}\,X(0)PX^{-1}(0)=\text{Rank}P
since
X(0)
is invertible.

\Leftarrow)
On the other hand, let us assume that the transition matrix of the linear system
(
2.2.1
) is such that the inequalities (
2.2.9
) are verified and the projections
P(\cdot)
verify (
2.2.10
).

Without loss of generality, we will consider a normalized fundamental matrix
X(t)
, then we can see that

\begin{array}[]{rcl}X(t,s)P(s)&=&X(t,0)X(0,s)P(s)\\
\\
&=&X(t,0)P(0)X(0,s)\\
\\
&=&X(t)P(0)X^{-1}(s),\end{array}

while the identity
X(t,s)[I-P(s)]=X(t)[I-P(0)]X^{-1}(s)
can be deduced in a similar
way and the inequalities (
2.2.9
) imply (
2.2.2
) with projection
P(0)
.
∎

Remark 2.2.5
.

As
t\mapsto\text{Rank}P(t)
is constant for any
t\in J
, it is said in the literature
that
P(\cdot)
satisfying (
2.2.10
) is an
invariant projection
.

Remark 2.2.6
.

The above theorem allows to introduce the
uniquess of projector problem
:
Given a fundamental matrix
X(t)
of a linear system (
2.2.1
)
having an exponential dichotomy on
J
, the Theorem
2.2.2
only ensures
the existence of a projector
P(\cdot)
. Is this
projector unique? The answer is dependent of the interval
J
.

In spite that the above characterization of exponential dichotomy by variable projections is known,
there exists few detailed explanations in the literature. Among them we highlight
[
28
, Prop.7.4]
and
[
35
]
. We stress that, in
[
28
]
, the exponential dichotomy with constant projections is called as
Coppel’s dichotomy
.

This alternative characterization of the exponential dichotomy allows to carry out a simple proof
of the following result:

Lemma 2.2.4
.

If the linear system (
2.2.1
) has the property of bounded growth
\&
decay on
J
:

||X(t,s)||\leq K_{0}e^{M|t-s|}\quad\textnormal{where $J=[t_{0},+\infty)$}

and moreover
has an exponential dichotomy on the interval
J
with constants
K
and
\alpha
then it follows that
\alpha\leq M
.

Proof.

By using properties of matrix norms verifying
||I||=1
combined with the invariance property (
2.2.10
), we can deduce that

\begin{array}[]{rcl}1&=&||X(t,s)X(s,t)||\\
\\
&=&||X(t,s)[P(s)+I-P(s)]X(s,t)||\\
\\
&\leq&||X(t,s)P(s)||\,||X(s,t)||+||X(t,s)||\,||X(s,t)[I-P(t)]||.\end{array}

Now, without loss of generality, we will assume that
t\geq s
. By using the property of exponential dichotomy combined with the bounded growth growth,
we can deduce that for any
t\geq s
with
t,s\in J
it follows that:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}1&\leq&K_{0}e^{M(t-s)}||X(t,s)P(s)||+K_{0}e^{M(t-s)}\,||X(s,t)[I-P(t)]||\\
\\
&\leq&KK_{0}e^{M(t-s)}e^{-\alpha(t-s)}+KK_{0}e^{M(t-s)}e^{-\alpha(t-s)}\end{array}

and we conclude that

1\leq 2KK_{0}e^{(M-\alpha)(t-s)}\quad\textnormal{for any $t\geq s$ with $t,s\in J$}.

We have either
M\geq\alpha
or
M<\alpha
, if the last inequality is verified, by letting
t\to+\infty
we will have that

1\leq 0
obtaining a contradiction and the result follows.
∎

In spite that the above result is rather intuitive, it is difficult to find a formal proof in the literature. In some cases, as for example
[
119
, p.823]
, this result is stated without proof.

Finally, we point out that this alternative characterization allow us to revisit Theorem
2.2.1
which states that the kinematical
similarity preserves the exponential dichotomy with the same projection and rate.

Theorem 2.2.3
.

If the linear system (
2.2.1
) is generally kinematically similar to

(2.2.11)

y^{\prime}=B(t)y\quad\textnormal{with $t\in J$},

via the transformation
y=Q^{-1}(t)x
and has an exponential dichotomy on
J
with constants
(K,\alpha)
and
invariant projector
P(t)
, then (
2.2.11
) has an exponential dichotomy on
J
with
projections
\tilde{P}(t)=Q^{-1}(t)P(t)Q(t)
and constants
(K||Q||_{\infty}||Q^{-1}||_{\infty},\alpha)
.

Proof.

Notice that
\tilde{P}^{2}(t)=\tilde{P}(t)
can be deduced straightforwardly. Moreover, if
X(t)
is a fundamental matrix of (
2.2.1
) then we know that
Y(t)=Q^{-1}(t)X(t)
is a fundamental matrix
of (
2.2.11
) and the identity
X(t,s)=Q(t)Y(t,s)Q^{-1}(s)
is fulfilled.

By using the above identity combined with the invariance of the projector
P(t)

described by (
2.2.10
), we can deduce that

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\tilde{P}(t)Y(t,s)&=&Q^{-1}(t)P(t)Q(t)Y(t,s)Q^{-1}(s)Q(s)\\
&=&Q^{-1}(t)P(t)X(t,s)Q(s)\\
&=&Q^{-1}(t)X(t,s)P(s)Q(s)\\
&=&Y(t,s)Q^{-1}(s)P(s)Q(s)\\
&=&Y(t,s)\tilde{P}(s),\end{array}

and the invariance of
\tilde{P}(s)
follows.

If
t\geq s
, the above estimations together with the first inequality of (
2.2.9
)
allow us to deduce that

\begin{array}[]{rcl}||Y(t,s)\tilde{P}(s)||&=&||Y(t,s)Q(s)P(s)Q^{-1}(s)||\\
&=&||Q(s)X(t,s)P(s)Q^{-1}(s)||\\
&\leq&K||Q||_{\infty}||Q^{-1}||_{\infty}||X(t,s)P(s)||\\
&\leq&K||Q||_{\infty}||Q^{-1}||_{\infty}e^{-\alpha(t-s)}\end{array}

whereas

||Y(t,s)[I-\tilde{P}(s)]||\leq K||Q||_{\infty}||Q^{-1}||_{\infty}e^{-\alpha(s-t)}

can be deduced in a similar way when
t\leq s
.
∎

2.3.
Exponential dichotomy on the full line

In this section we will study the Definition
2.2.1
of exponential dichotomy
considering
J=\mathbb{R}
, this case will allow to take a look at two consequences of the hyperbolicity, namely, the properties of splitting of solutions and admissibility from a nonautonomous point of view.

2.3.1.
Splitting of solutions

Theorem 2.3.1
.

If
\dot{x}=A(t)x
has an exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}
with projector
P(\cdot)
and positive constants
K
and
\alpha
, then any solution

t\mapsto x(t,t_{0},\xi):=X(t,t_{0})\xi
passing through
\xi
at
t=t_{0}

can be splitted as follows:

(2.3.1)

x(t,t_{0},\xi)=\underbrace{X(t,t_{0})P(t_{0})\xi}_{:=x^{+}(t,t_{0},\xi)}+\underbrace{X(t,t_{0})[I-P(t_{0})]\xi}_{:=x^{-}(t,t_{0},\xi)}

such that for any
t\geq t_{0}
it follows that

(2.3.2a)

\displaystyle|x^{+}(t,t_{0},\xi)|\leq Ke^{-\alpha(t-t_{0})}|P(t_{0})\xi|,

(2.3.2b)

\displaystyle\frac{|Q(t_{0})\xi|}{K}e^{\alpha(t-t_{0})}\leq|x^{-}(t,t_{0},\xi)|,

where
Q(t_{0})=I-P(t_{0})
. Morevoer, for any
t\leq t_{0}
it follows that

(2.3.3a)

\displaystyle\frac{|P(t_{0})\xi|}{K}e^{\alpha(t_{0}-t)}\leq|x^{+}(t,t_{0},\xi)|,

(2.3.3b)

\displaystyle|x^{-}(t,t_{0},\xi)|\leq Ke^{\alpha(t-t_{0})}|Q(t_{0})\xi|.

Proof.

Firstly note that by using
\xi=P(t_{0})\xi+[I-P(t_{0})]\xi
, the decomposition

x(t,t_{0},\xi)=x^{+}(t,t_{0},\xi)+x^{-}(t,t_{0},\xi)
is straightforwardly deduced.

Let us assume that
t\geq t_{0}
. Then, by using the matrix norm definition combined with
P(t_{0})\xi=P^{2}(t_{0})\xi
and the first inequality of (
2.2.9
) with
s=t_{0}
and
J=\mathbb{R}
, it follows that

|x^{+}(t,t_{0},\xi)|\leq||X(t,t_{0})P(t_{0})||\,|P(t_{0})\xi|\leq Ke^{-\alpha(t-t_{0})}|P(t_{0})\xi|,

and (
2.3.2a
) is deduced. On the other hand, as
Q(\cdot)
is an invariant projection, notice that

\begin{array}[]{rcl}Q(t_{0})\xi&=&X(t_{0},t)X(t,t_{0})Q(t_{0})\xi\\
\\
&=&X(t_{0},t)X(t,t_{0})Q(t_{0})Q(t_{0})\xi\\
\\
&=&X(t_{0},t)Q(t)X(t,t_{0})Q(t_{0})\xi\\
\\
&=&X(t_{0},t)Q(t)x^{-}(t,t_{0},\xi)\\
\\
\end{array}

Now, by using the matrix norm definition and the second inequality if (
2.2.9
)
with
(t_{0},t)
instead of
(t,s)
, we have that

|Q(t_{0})\xi|\leq||X(t_{0},t)Q(t)||\,|x^{-}(t,t_{0},\xi)|\leq Ke^{-\alpha(t-t_{0})}|x^{-}(t,t_{0},\xi)|

and (
2.3.2b
) can be easily deduced.

The proof of the inequalities (
2.3.3a
)–(
2.3.3b
)
can be made by following the lines of the previous one and is left for the reader.
∎

Remark 2.3.1
.

Despite the above theorem considered an exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}
as assumption. A careful reading of the proof will show that a similar splitting of solutions can be obtained if we consider an exponential dichotomy on

\mathbb{R}_{0}^{+}
or
\mathbb{R}_{0}^{-}
.

As Theorem
2.3.1
ensures the decomposition of any nontrivial solution
t\mapsto x(t,t_{0},\xi):=X(t,t_{0})\xi
of a system (
2.2.1
) having the property of exponential
dichotomy on the real line, we will see some consequences:

\bullet
The solutions

x^{+}(t,t_{0},\xi)=X(t,t_{0})P(t_{0})\xi\quad\textnormal{and}\quad x^{-}(t,t_{0},\xi)=X(t,t_{0})Q(t_{0})\xi

have an asymptotic behavior dependent of the splitting of any initial condition: observe that
t\mapsto x^{+}(t,t_{0},\xi)
has an exponential decay to the origin (with rate
\alpha
) when
t\to+\infty
, while
t\mapsto x^{-}(t,t_{0},\xi)
has an exponential growth (with rate
\alpha
)
when
t\mapsto+\infty
. This
dichotomic
asymptotic behavior with exponential rate motivates the use of the name exponential dichotomy.

\bullet
The above behavior is reversed when
t\to-\infty
.

\bullet
The exponential growth or decay of any solution –at rate
\alpha
– above mentioned is dependent of the difference
t-t_{0}
but it is independent of the initial time
t_{0}
. Due to this fact, several authors use the concept of
uniform exponential dichotomy
in order to emphasize the uniformity with respect to the initial time.

\bullet
Last but not least, an important consequence of
Theorem
2.3.1
is the following result:

Corollary 2.3.1
.

If the system
\dot{x}=A(t)x
has an exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}
, then the unique solution bounded on
\mathbb{R}
is the trivial one.

Proof.

Let
t\mapsto x(t,t_{0},\xi)
be a nontrivial solution of the linear system. Then, by the above comments, we have that
t\mapsto x^{+}(t,t_{0},\xi)
and
t\mapsto x^{-}(t,t_{0},\xi)

must be unbounded at
-\infty
and
+\infty
respectively, which implies that any nontrivial solution cannot be bounded on
\mathbb{R}
.
∎

The above mentioned splitting of the solutions of a linear system
\dot{x}=A(t)x
having an exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}
also provides a decomposition of the initial conditions as direct sum of
two subspaces which is reminiscent to the description of the hyperbolicity condition in the
autonomous case. In fact, for any initial time
t_{0}
, we can define the
\mathbb{R}
–vector subspaces
\mathcal{V}(t_{0})

and
\mathcal{W}(t_{0})
as follows:

\mathcal{V}(t_{0})=\left\{\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\colon t\mapsto|X(t,t_{0})\xi|\quad\textnormal{is bounded in $[t_{0},+\infty)$}\right\}

and

\mathcal{W}(t_{0})=\left\{\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\colon t\mapsto|X(t,t_{0})\xi|\quad\textnormal{is bounded in $(-\infty,t_{0}]$}\right\}.

The following result describes the relation between these subspaces with the projector
P(t_{0})
:

Lemma 2.3.1
.

Let
X(t)
be a fundamental matrix of (
2.2.1
). If this system has an exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}
with projection
P(\cdot)
and positive constants
K
and
\alpha
, then the above defined subspaces
\mathcal{V}(t_{0})
and
\mathcal{W}(t_{0})
verify

\textnormal{\text{Im}}P(t_{0})=\mathcal{V}(t_{0}),\quad\ker P(t_{0})=\mathcal{W}(t_{0})\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\mathbb{R}^{n}=\mathcal{V}(t_{0})\oplus\mathcal{W}(t_{0}).

Moreover, the corresponding subspaces
\mathcal{V}(t)

and
\mathcal{W}(t)
are well defined for any
t\in\mathbb{R}
and verify

\dim\mathcal{V}(t)=\dim\mathcal{V}(t_{0})\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\dim\mathcal{W}(t)=\dim\mathcal{W}(t_{0})\quad\textnormal{for any $t\in\mathbb{R}$}.

Proof.

Let
\xi\in\text{Im}P(t_{0})
, then
P(t_{0})\xi=\xi
and assume that
t\geq t_{0}
. By using inequality (
2.3.2a
), we have that

|X(t,t_{0})\xi|=|X(t,t_{0})P(t_{0})\xi|\leq Ke^{-\alpha(t-t_{0})}|\xi|\leq K|\xi|,

and the boundedness of
t\mapsto X(t,t_{0})\xi
on
[t_{0},+\infty)
follows, then
\xi\in\mathcal{V}(t_{0})
and
\text{Im}P(t_{0})\subseteq\mathcal{V}(t_{0})
is verified.

Now let
\xi\in\mathcal{V}(t_{0})
and assume that
t\geq t_{0}
.
The proof of
\xi\in\text{Im}P(t_{0})
will be made by contradiction. Indeed, if
\xi\notin\text{Im}P(t_{0})
, we can deduce the property
[I-P(t_{0})]\xi\neq 0
and, by using triangle’s inequality, we have that:

|X(t,t_{0})[I-P(t_{0})]\xi|\leq|X(t,t_{0})\xi|+|X(t,t_{0})P(t_{0})\xi|.

Note that the left term is unbounded on
[t_{0},+\infty)
since (
2.3.2b
), while the right terms are bounded on
[t_{0},+\infty)
, the first term is bounded by hypothesis whereas the second one is bounded due to (
2.3.2a
), obtaining a contradiction. Then
\mathcal{V}(t_{0})\subseteq\text{Im}P(t_{0})
and the identity of both sets follows.

In order to prove that
\ker P(t_{0})=\mathcal{W}(t_{0})
, let
\xi\in\ker P(t_{0})
, then
\xi=[I-P(t_{0})]\xi
. As we are assuming that
t\leq t_{0}
the inequality (
2.3.3a
) leads to

|X(t,t_{0})\xi|=|X(t,t_{0})[I-P(t_{0})]\xi|\leq Ke^{-\alpha(t_{0}-t)}|\xi|\leq K|\xi|,

then the boundedness of
t\mapsto X(t,t_{0})\xi
on
(-\infty,t_{0}]
follows and
\xi\in\mathcal{W}(t_{0})
which implies that
\ker P(t_{0})\subseteq\mathcal{W}(t_{0})
.

Now let
\xi\in\mathcal{W}(t_{0})
and assume that
t\leq t_{0}
. As before, the proof of
\xi\in\ker P(t_{0})
will be made by contradiction. Indeed, if
\xi\notin\ker P(t_{0})
, it follows that the property
P(t_{0})\xi\neq 0
and, by using triangle’s inequality, we have that:

|X(t,t_{0})P(t_{0})\xi|\leq|X(t,t_{0})\xi|+|X(t,t_{0})[I-P(t_{0})]\xi|

Note that the left term is unbounded on
(-\infty,t_{0}]
since (
2.3.3a
), while the right terms are bounded on
(-\infty,t_{0}]
, the first since
\xi\in\mathcal{W}(t_{0})
and the second for (
2.3.3b
), obtaining a contradiction. Then
\mathcal{W}(t_{0})\subseteq\text{Im}P(t_{0})
and the identity of both sets follows.

Note that
\mathcal{V}(t_{0})\cap\mathcal{W}(t_{0})=\{0\}
. In fact, if
\xi\in\mathcal{V}(t_{0})\cap\mathcal{W}(t_{0})
, we will have that
t\mapsto X(t,t_{0})\xi
is solution of the linear system and is bounded on
\mathbb{R}
, then Corollary
2.3.1
imply that
\xi=0
. This fact, combined with the Rank–Nullity Theorem imply that
\mathbb{R}^{n}=\mathcal{V}(t_{0})\oplus\mathcal{W}(t_{0})
.

Finally, we know by Theorem
2.2.2
that
\dim\textnormal{Im}P(t)
is constant for
any
t\in\mathbb{R}
, then it follows that that the dimension of the subspaces
\mathcal{V}(t_{0})
and
\mathcal{W}(t_{0})
is not dependent of the initial time
t_{0}
.
∎

By using Theorem
2.2.2
we can see that as similar result can be obtained when considering constant
projectors

Lemma 2.3.2
.

Let
X(t)
be a fundamental matrix of (
2.2.1
). If this system has an exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}
with projector
P
and positive constants
K
and
\alpha
, then if follows that:

\textnormal{\text{Im}}P=\mathcal{V},\quad\ker P=\mathcal{W}\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\mathbb{R}^{n}=\mathcal{V}\oplus\mathcal{W},

where

\mathcal{V}=\left\{\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\colon t\mapsto|X(t,0)\xi|\quad\textnormal{is bounded in $[0,+\infty)$}\right\}

and

\mathcal{W}=\left\{\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\colon t\mapsto|X(t,0)\xi|\quad\textnormal{is bounded in $(-\infty,0]$}\right\}.

The above result provides the answer to a question stated in Remark
2.2.2
.

2.3.2.
Admissibility

The property of exponential dichotomy on the full line will allow us to
generalize the Proposition
2.1.1
to the nonautonomous framework. In order to do that,
we need to introduce the Green function associated to the dichotomy:

Definition 2.3.1
.

Let
X(t)
be fundamental matrix of (
2.2.1
). If this system has an exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}
with projector
P(\cdot)
, its
Green function
is defined by

\mathcal{G}(t,s)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}X(t)PX^{-1}(s)&\quad\textnormal{if}&t\geq s,\\
-X(t)(I-P)X^{-1}(s)&\quad\textnormal{if}&t\leq s.\\
\end{array}\right.

Obviously, if we work with the alternative characterization of exponential
dichotomy stated in subsection 2.2, the Green function is

\mathcal{G}(t,s)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}X(t,s)P(s)&\quad\textnormal{if}&t\geq s,\\
-X(t,s)[I-P(s)]&\quad\textnormal{if}&t\leq s.\\
\end{array}\right.

A remarkable consequence of Corollary
2.3.1
, and consequently, of exponential
dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}
is the following result:

Proposition 2.3.1
.

If the linear system
(
2.2.1
)
has an exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}

with constants
K
,
\alpha
and projector
P(\cdot)
, then for each bounded continous function
t\mapsto g(t)
, the inhomogeneous system

(2.3.4)

z^{\prime}=A(t)z+g(t)

has a unique solution
x_{g}^{*}\in BC(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R}^{n})
defined by

x_{g}^{*}(t)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathcal{G}(t,s)g(s)\,ds.

Moreover, the map
g\mapsto x_{g}^{*}
is Lipschitz and verifies:

|x_{g}^{*}|_{\infty}\leq\frac{2K}{\alpha}|g|_{\infty}.

Proof.

Step 1:
It is easy to prove that

x_{g}^{*}(t)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathcal{G}(t,s)g(s)\,ds

is a bounded solution of (
2.3.4
). Moreover, given a pair of functions

g_{1},g_{2}\in B(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R}^{n})
, a careful reading of Definition
2.3.1
combined with the property of exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}
shows that

\begin{array}[]{rcl}|x_{g_{1}}^{*}(t)-x_{g_{2}}^{*}(t)|&\leq&\displaystyle\frac{2K}{\alpha}|g_{1}(t)-g_{2}(t)|_{\infty}.\end{array}

Step 2:
We will prove that
x_{g}^{*}(t)
is the unique bounded solution of (
2.3.4
). Indeed, let
t\mapsto x(t)

be a bounded solution. By variation of parameters, we have that

\begin{array}[]{rcl}x(t)&=&\displaystyle X(t,0)x(0)+\int_{0}^{t}X(t,s)g(s)\,ds\end{array}

which can be written as

\begin{array}[]{rcl}x(t)&=&\displaystyle X(t,0)x(0)+\int_{0}^{t}\{X(t)PX^{-1}(s)g(s)+X(t)(I-P)X^{-1}(s)g(s)\}\,ds\\
\\
&=&\displaystyle X(t,0)x(0)-\underbrace{\int_{-\infty}^{0}X(t)PX^{-1}(s)g(s)\,ds}_{:=x_{1}}+\int_{-\infty}^{t}X(t)PX^{-1}(s)g(s)\,ds\\
\\
&&\displaystyle+\underbrace{\int_{0}^{\infty}X(t)(I-P)X^{-1}(s)g(s)\,ds}_{:=x_{2}}-\int_{t}^{\infty}X(t)(I-P)X^{-1}(s)g(s)\,ds\end{array}

and we can see that the bounded solution
t\mapsto x(t)
can be written as follows

x(t)=X(t,0)\{x(0)-x_{1}+x_{2}\}+x_{g}^{*}(t).

As
t\mapsto x_{g}^{*}(t)
is a bounded solution of the inhomogeneous system (
2.3.4
), we have that

t\mapsto x(t)-x_{g}^{*}(t)
is a bounded solution of the linear system (
2.2.1
). Finally, Corollary
2.3.1
implies that
x(t)=x_{g}^{*}(t)
and the uniqueness follows.
∎

In spite of the ubiquitousness and well knowness of this result in the literature,
it is surprising to find few proofs with the exception with the skectched proof in the Coppel’s monography
[
41
]
. The above proof follow the ideas from the paper of X. Chen and Y. Xia
[
34
]
, which is remarkable by its simplicity.

The Proposition
2.3.1
has a plethora of consequences
and applications. In particular, the previous admissibility result can be
adapted for closed subspaces of the Banach space
(BC(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R}^{n}),|\cdot|_{\infty})

as the almost periodic and
\omega
–periodic functions. We will consider here the case when
f
is
\omega
–periodic
and will also consider the case of bounded nonlinear Lipschitz perturbations.

Corollary 2.3.2
.

If the linear and continuous
\omega
–periodic system
(
2.2.1
)
has an exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}
, then for each continuous
\omega
–periodic function
t\mapsto g(t)
, the
system

(2.3.5)

z^{\prime}=A(t)z+g(t)

has a unique continuous
\omega
–periodic solution
t\mapsto x^{*}(t)
satisfying

(2.3.6)

x^{*}(0)=[I-X(\omega,0)]^{-1}\int_{0}^{\omega}X(\omega,s)g(s)\,ds=\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\mathcal{G}(0,s)g(s)\,ds.

Proof.

The proof will use the property of exponential dichotomy from two perspectives. A first one
recalls that the exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}
is equivalent to the fact that
the Floquet’s multipliers of the linear and continuous
\omega
–periodic system (
2.2.1
) cannot be in the unit circle. Then, by using Remark
1.3.5
from Chapter 1, we have that the fundamental matrix
X(t)
is such that
X(\omega,0)-I
is non singular and the vector

(2.3.7)

x^{*}(0)=[I-X(\omega,0)]^{-1}\int_{0}^{\omega}X(\omega,s)g(s)\,ds

is well defined. Now, a direct consequence of the above identity is that
t\mapsto x(t)=X(t,0)x(0)
is a continuous
\omega
–periodic solution of (
2.3.5
) if and only if
x(0)=x^{*}(0)
. In fact, notice that

x(\omega)=X(\omega,0)x(0)+\int_{0}^{\omega}X(\omega,s)g(s)\,ds=x(0)

is equivalent to

x(0)=[I-X(\omega,0)]^{-1}\int_{0}^{\omega}X(\omega,s)g(s)\,ds,

and the existence of a continuous
\omega
–periodic solution
t\mapsto x^{*}(t)
is ensured.

As the continuous
\omega
–periodic functions are bounded on
\mathbb{R}
, we will follow a second perspective of the exponential dichotomy property: the Proposition
2.3.1
implies that the unique bounded solution is defined by the Green function, which must be identical to the
\omega
–periodic solution obtained previously, that is

t\mapsto x^{*}(t)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathcal{G}(t,s)g(s)\,ds=X(t,0)x^{*}(0)+\int_{0}^{t}X(t,s)g(s)\,ds,

and the identity (
2.3.6
) is obtained easily by evaluating at
t=0
and using (
2.3.7
).
∎

The proof of the above result was carried out considering the identity

x(0)=x(\omega)
but the proof could also be deduced by considering
x(0)=x(-\omega)
, which leads to the identity

(2.3.8)

[I-X(\omega,0)]^{-1}\int_{0}^{\omega}X(\omega,s)g(s)\,ds=[I-X(-\omega,0)]^{-1}\int_{0}^{-\omega}\hskip-8.5359ptX(-\omega,s)\,ds.

In addition, the identity (
2.3.6
) has been obtained as consequence
of uniqueness of the bounded solution. Nevertheless, it can be deduced directly
by Proposition
2.3.1
applied to the
\omega
–periodic case. In fact, notice that:

(2.3.9)

\begin{array}[]{rcl}x^{*}(0)&=&\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\mathcal{G}(0,s)g(s)\,ds\\
\\
&=&\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{0}\hskip-5.69046ptX(0,s)P(s)g(s)\,ds-\int_{0}^{\infty}\hskip-5.69046ptX(0,s)[I-P(s)]g(s)\,ds\\
\\
&=&\displaystyle P(0)\int_{-\infty}^{0}\hskip-5.69046ptX(0,s)g(s)\,ds-[I-P(0)]\int_{0}^{\infty}\hskip-5.69046ptX(0,s)g(s)\,ds.\end{array}

In addition, by using a natural change of variables combined with by the identity (
1.3.2
) from Chapter 1 we can deduce that

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}X(0,s)g(s)\,ds&=&\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\omega}X(0,s)g(s)\,ds+\int_{\omega}^{\infty}X(0,s)g(s)\,ds\\
\\
&=&\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\omega}X(0,s)g(s)\,ds+\int_{0}^{\infty}X(0,s+\omega)g(s)\,ds\\
\\
&=&\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\omega}X(0,s)g(s)\,ds+X(0,\omega)\int_{0}^{\infty}X(\omega,s+\omega)g(s)\,ds\\
\\
&=&\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\omega}X(0,s)g(s)\,ds+X(0,\omega)\int_{0}^{\infty}X(0,s)g(s)\,ds.\end{array}

We multiply the above identity by
X(\omega,0)
and easily verify that

\int_{0}^{\infty}X(0,s)g(s)=[X(\omega,0)-I]^{-1}\int_{0}^{\omega}X(\omega,s)g(s)\,ds.

and we deduce that

-[I-P(0)]\int_{0}^{\infty}X(0,s)g(s)=[I-P(0)][I-X(\omega,0)]^{-1}\int_{0}^{\omega}X(\omega,s)g(s)\,ds.

Similarly as in the previous case, by using a change of variables combined with the identity (
1.3.2
) from Chapter 1 we can deduce that

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{0}X(0,s)g(s)\,ds&=&\displaystyle\int_{-\omega}^{0}X(0,s)g(s)\,ds+X(0,-\omega)\int_{0}^{\infty}X(0,s)g(s)\,ds.\end{array}

By multipliying by
X(-\omega,0)
and using (
2.3.8
) we can deduce that

P(0)\int_{-\infty}^{0}X(0,s)g(s)\,ds=P(0)[I-X(\omega,0)]^{-1}\int_{0}^{\omega}X(\omega,s)g(s)\,ds

and (
2.3.9
) combined with the identities for the integral terms
\int_{-\infty}^{0}X(0,s)g(s)\,ds
and
-[I-P(0)]\int_{0}^{\infty}X(0,s)g(s)
obtained above leads to

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\mathcal{G}(0,s)g(s)\,ds&=&\displaystyle[I-X(\omega,0)]^{-1}\int_{0}^{\omega}X(\omega,s)g(s)\,ds.\end{array}

Proposition 2.3.2
.

If the system
(
2.2.1
)
has an exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}
with constants
K
,
\alpha
and projection
P
. Moreover, if the nonlinear system:

(2.3.10)

w^{\prime}=A(t)w+h(t,w)

has a continuous perturbation

t\mapsto h(t,w)
satisfying:

(2.3.11)

|h(t,w_{1})-h(t,w_{2})|\leq\gamma|w_{1}-w_{2}|\quad\textnormal{and}\quad|h(t,w)|\leq\mu

for any
t\in\mathbb{R}
and
w,w_{1},w_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
where
\gamma
is such that

2K\gamma<\alpha,

then the system (
2.3.10
)
has a unique bounded function
t\mapsto w^{*}(t)
satisfying the fixed point property:

(2.3.12)

w^{*}(t)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathcal{G}(t,s)h(s,w^{*}(s))\,ds.

Proof.

Let
\varphi_{1}
in the Banach space
(BC(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R}^{n}),|\cdot|_{\infty})
and construct a sequence
\{\varphi_{k}\}_{k}\subset BC(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R}^{n})
, where
t\mapsto\varphi_{k}(t)
is the unique
bounded solution of the inhomogeneous system

w^{\prime}=A(t)w+h(t,\varphi_{k-1}(t))\quad\textnormal{for any}\quad k\geq 2.

In fact, notice that
t\mapsto h(t,\varphi_{k-1}(t))\in BC(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R}^{n})
; in consequence, Proposition
2.3.1
implies the existence of the sequence
\{\varphi_{k}\}_{k}
, which verifies the recursivity:

\varphi_{k}(t)=\int_{\infty}^{\infty}\mathcal{G}(t,s)h(s,\varphi_{k-1}(s))\,ds\quad\textnormal{for any $t\in\mathbb{R}$}.

Finally, notice that as
h(t,z)
satisfies (
2.3.11
), it follows that

\begin{array}[]{rcl}|\varphi_{k}(t)-\varphi_{k-1}(t)|&\leq&\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|\mathcal{G}(t,s)|\,|h(s,\varphi_{k-1}(s))-h(s,\varphi_{k-2}(s))|\,ds\\
\\
&\leq&\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}Ke^{-\alpha|t-s|}\gamma|\varphi_{k-1}-\varphi_{k-2}|_{\infty}\,ds\\
\\
&\leq&\displaystyle\frac{2K\gamma}{\alpha}|\varphi_{k-1}-\varphi_{k-2}|_{\infty}\end{array}

and we have that
\{\varphi_{k}\}_{k}
is a Cauchy sequence. As
(BC(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R}^{n}),|\cdot|_{\infty})
is a Banach space, we have that
\varphi_{k}

converges uniformly to (
2.3.12
).
∎

We will finish this section with two comments about the property of exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}
.

\bullet
Firstly, a direct consequence of Definition
2.2.1

is that if the linear system (
2.2.1
) has an exponential dichotomy on the interval
J
then this property is also verified on any interval
\tilde{J}\subset J
. In consequence, the property of exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}
implies this property on
\mathbb{R}_{0}^{-}
and
\mathbb{R}_{0}^{+}
.

\bullet
Secondly, it is important to emphasize that
the linear system (
2.2.1
) can have simultaneously an exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}_{0}^{-}
and
\mathbb{R}_{0}^{+}
but this not implies necessarily the existence of an exponential dichotomy
on
\mathbb{R}
. An interesting question is to find complementary conditions ensuring the
exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}
when the exponential dichotomy on
the half lines is simultaneously verified. In order
to address this problem it will be useful to carry out a study of the exponential
dichotomy on the half lines, which is the topic of the next chapter.

2.4.
Comments and References

1)
A nice description of the exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}
as
a property emulating the hyperbolicity and its consequences is given in the article
of Császár and Kovács
[
42
]
. We also
highlight the interesting survey from Elaydi and Hajek
[
52
]
.

2)
The property of exponential dichotomy on
J
can be generalized as the existence of an invariant projection
P(\cdot)
, a pair of positive constants
(\alpha,K)
and an increasing homeomorphism
h\colon(a_{0},+\infty)\to(0,+\infty)

such that the transition matrix
X(t,s)
of (
2.2.1
) satisfies:

(2.4.1)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}||X(t,s)P(s)||\leq K\left(h(t)h^{-1}(s)\right)^{-\alpha}&\textnormal{if}&t\geq s\geq a_{0},\\
\\
||X(t,s)[I-P(s)]||\leq K\left(h(s)h^{-1}(t)\right)^{-\alpha}&\textnormal{if}&s\geq t\geq a_{0}.\end{array}\right.

The above property is called
h
–dichotomy has been introduced by Naulin and Pinto in
[
94
,
95
]
, it encompasses
the exponential dichotomy and the
generalized exponential dichotomy
introduced by Martin
[
90
]

and studied in depth by Jiang
[
75
]
, Xia
[
34
,
125
]
and its collaborators. The
h
–dichotomy has been
recently revisited in
[
53
]
where the expansions and contractions are studied from a group theory
approach.

A generalization of the
h
–dichotomy has been done by replacing the map
h
in the second inequality of (
2.4.1
)
by another increasing homeomorphism
k\colon(a_{0},+\infty)\to(0,+\infty)
which has been introduced by Naulin
and Pinto in
[
95
,
107
]
.

The case considering a map
s\mapsto K(s)
for any
s\in J
instead of a constant corresponds to
the
nonuniform
h
–dichotomy
, which has been deeply
studied for the case
h(t)=e^{t}
by Barreira, Valls and Dragičević in
[
11
,
12
]
and references therein.

We point out the existence of a wide family of dichotomies which can be seen as
generalizations of the previous ones and we refer the reader to
[
131
, Table 1]

for a detailed summary.

3)
The above description of generalized dichotomies allows
a classification of asymptotic stabilities in the nonautonomous case
if we consider
J=[t_{0},+\infty)
and the projection
P(s)=I
, the
above construction becomes the nonuniform contraction

\begin{array}[]{rcl}||X(t,s)||\leq K(s)\left(h(t)\right)^{-\alpha}&\textnormal{for}&t\geq s>t_{0},\end{array}

where
K\colon[0,+\infty[\to(0,+\infty)
,
\alpha>0
.

The most distinguished and studied case is
the
uniform asymptotic stability

or
uniform exponential stability
, which corresponds
to
K(s)=K>0
for any
s\geq t_{0}
and
h(t)=e^{t}
.

The
nonuniform asymptotic stability
corresponds to

K(s)=Ke^{\varepsilon|s|}
for any
s\geq t_{0}

with
K,\varepsilon>0
and
h(t)=e^{t}
.

The
uniform
h
–stability
corresponds to
K(s)=K>0
and
h
satisfying the above properties, which has been introduced by Naulin
and Pinto in
[
95
,
107
]
. This stability has been revisited recently from a group theory perspective by Peña and Rivera–Villagrán in
[
104
]
.

4)
To the best of our knowledge, the notion of admissibility has been introduced by Massera and Schäffer
[
91
]
.

Definition 2.4.1
.

Given two spaces of functions
\mathcal{B}

and
\mathcal{D}
, consisting in mappings from
J\subseteq\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
, the pair
(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{D})
is admissible for the linear system
\dot{y}=A(t)y+g(t)
if for each
t\mapsto g(t)\in\mathcal{B}
there exists a solution

t\mapsto y(t)\in\mathcal{D}
.

The property of admissibility is strongly related to the exponential dichotomy
on
\mathbb{R}
. In fact, Proposition
2.3.1
and Corollary
2.3.2
can be seen as results of
(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{B})
–admissibility for
\mathcal{B}=BC(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
\mathcal{B}=C_{\omega}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R}^{n})
provided that

a_{ij}
and
g
are elements of
\mathcal{B}
and
\dot{x}=A(t)x

has an exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}
. There exists generalizations to the Banach spaces of almost periodic functions
[
55
, Ch.7]
, pseudo almost periodic functions
[
2
,
130
]
and remotely almost periodic functions
[
92
]
.

5)
There exists numerical methods oriented to detect the exponential dichotomy on the real line and we refer to the works carried out by L. Dieci, C. Elia and E. Van Vleck in
[
47
,
48
]
.

2.5.
Exercises

1.-

Prove the Remark
2.2.4
in detail.

2.-

Prove that if
P
is a projection and
V
is a nonsingular matrix, then
\tilde{P}=V^{-1}PV
is also a projection.

3.-

Prove that if
P
is a nontrivial projection, then its eigenvalues are
0
and
1
.

4.-

Prove that if
\{P_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is a sequence of projections convergent to
Q

then
Q
is also a projection.

5.-

If the linear system (
2.2.1
) has an exponential dichotomy on
J
with projection

P
then prove that its adjoint has an exponential dichotomy with projection
I-P^{T}
.

6.-

In
[
41
, p.12]
, the author consider the linear system

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\dot{x}_{1}&=&-x_{1}+e^{2t}x_{2}\\
\dot{x}_{2}&=&x_{2},\end{array}

whose fundamental matrix is

X(t)=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}e^{-t}&(e^{3t}-e^{-t})/4\\
0&e^{t}\end{array}\right].

6.1)

Prove that the above system has not the bounded growth property.

6.2)

Prove that the first two inequalities of (
2.2.4
) are verified
with the projector

P=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
0&0\end{array}\right].

6.3)

Prove that
||X(t)PX^{-1}(t)||
is not bounded.

7.-

Prove the inequalities (
2.3.3a
)–(
2.3.3b
) from Theorem
2.3.1
.

8.-

If the linear system (
2.2.1
) is antisymmetric for any
t\in\mathbb{R}
then prove that cannot have an exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}
.

9.-

Let
[0,+\infty)\ni t\mapsto a(t)
a bounded and locally integrable function. Prove that if

\limsup\limits_{t-s\to+\infty}\frac{1}{t-s}\int_{s}^{t}a(\tau)\,d\tau<-\alpha<0,

then the scalar differential equation
\dot{x}=a(t)x
has an exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
with projection
p=1
.

10.-

Similarly, if
[0,+\infty)\ni t\mapsto a(t)
is a bounded and locally integrable function. Prove that if

\liminf\limits_{t-s\to+\infty}\frac{1}{t-s}\int_{s}^{t}a(\tau)\,d\tau>\alpha>0,

then the scalar equation
\dot{x}=a(t)x
has an exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
with projection
p=0
.

11.-

Prove that the following linear scalar differential equations have not an exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}
:

\dot{x}=\frac{1}{1+t^{2}}x,\quad\dot{x}=e^{-t^{2}}x\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\dot{x}=a(t)x

with
a(t)>0
for any
t\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}a(t)\,dt<\infty
.

12.-

The average of a
\omega
–periodic and continuous function
f

is defined by

\mathcal{M}(f):=\frac{1}{\omega}\int_{0}^{\omega}f(t)\,dt.

Prove that the scalar linear equation
\dot{x}=f(t)x
has an exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}

if and only if
\mathcal{M}(f)\neq 0
. Determine the relation between the corresponding projection and the sign of
\mathcal{M}(f)
.

13.-

Let us consider the linear system
\dot{x}=A(t)x
where

t\mapsto A(t)
is continuous and
\omega
–periodic. If the system has an exponential dichotomy on
[0,+\infty)
, can we say that the system has an exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}
with the same projection and constant
\alpha
?

14.-

Provide examples showing that Corollary
2.3.1
cannot be extended for systems
having an exponential dichotomy on
[0,+\infty)
.

15.-

Prove Lemma
2.3.2
.

16.-

Let us consider the Proposition
2.3.1
under the more specific assumption that the linear system (
2.2.1
) has an exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}
with the identity as projection. Then for each bounded function
t\mapsto g(t)
, prove that the
system (
2.3.4
) has a unique solution
t\mapsto x_{g}^{*}(t)
bounded on
\mathbb{R}
such that any other solution
t\mapsto x(t)
satisfies

\lim\limits_{t\to+\infty}[x(t)-x^{*}(t)]=0.

17.-

Similarly, let us consider the Proposition
2.3.1
under the more specific assumption that the linear system (
2.2.1
) has an exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}
with the null projection
P=0
. Then for each bounded function
t\mapsto g(t)
, prove that the
system (
2.3.4
) has a unique solution
t\mapsto x_{g}^{*}(t)
bounded on
\mathbb{R}
such that any other solution
t\mapsto x(t)
satisfies

\lim\limits_{t\to-\infty}[x(t)-x^{*}(t)]=0.

18.-

Let
t\mapsto x(t)
a solution of
\dot{x}=A(t)x
and define

x_{1}(t)=X(t)PX^{-1}(t)x(t)\quad\textnormal{and}\quad x_{2}(t)=X(t)(I-P)X^{-1}(t)x(t),

where
P
is a projection. Prove that
x_{1}(t)
and
x_{2}(t)
satisfy the following properties:

a)
X(t)PX^{-1}(t)x_{1}(t)=x_{1}(t)
,

b)
X(t)(I-P)X^{-1}(t)x_{2}(t)=x_{2}(t)
,

c)
x(t)=X(t)PX^{-1}(s)x_{1}(s)+X(t)(I-P)X^{-1}(s)x_{2}(s)
,

d)
x(t)=x_{1}(t)+x_{2}(t)
.

19.-

In the context of Proposition
2.3.2
, can we obtain additional information of the fixed point in the cases where the linear part has exponential dichotomy with trivial projections?.

20.-

Is it possible to extend Proposition
2.3.2
and to prove the existence
of
\omega
–periodic solutions of (
2.3.10
) when
A(t)
is
\omega
–periodic and continuous
and
t\mapsto h(t,w)
is
\omega
–periodic for any
w\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
?

21.-

Let us consider a linear system
\dot{x}=A(t)x
having exponential dichotomy on
[0,+\infty)
with nontrivial projection
P
. Prove that if
t\mapsto A(t)

is bounded on
[0,+\infty)
then it follows that

X(t)PX^{-1}(t)=I+\int_{0}^{+\infty}\mathcal{G}(t,s)A(s)\,ds,

where
X(t)
is a fundamental matrix and
\mathcal{G}(t,s)
is the Green’s function introduced in Definition
2.3.1
restriced to
t,s\geq 0
.

Chapter 3
Exponential dichotomy on the half lines

3.1.
Preliminaries

In this chapter we will consider the linear system

(3.1.1)

\dot{x}=A(t)x

by focusing our study on the properties of exponential dichotomy on the half lines
\mathbb{R}_{0}^{-}
or
\mathbb{R}_{0}^{+}
, which are more subtle compared with the case
J=\mathbb{R}
. For example the Proposition
2.3.1
from Chapter 2 and its consequences are no longer verified since any solution of the linear system (
3.1.1
) with exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}_{0}^{+}
and projection
P=I
will be bounded on the half line. Nevertheless, as we will see, the restriction to a half line will allow alternative and sharper characterizations of the dichotomy property.

3.2.
Basic properties and alternative characterizations

As in the previous chapter, we can directly deduce by the definition of exponential dichotomy on
J
that, if the linear system (
3.1.1
) has an exponential dichotomy on the interval
J
, then it also has this property on any interval
\tilde{J}\subset J
. In consequence, the property of exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}_{0}^{+}
implies the
dichotomy property on
[t_{0},+\infty)
for any
t_{0}\geq 0
. The following result from W. Coppel
[
41
, p.13]
provides a converse statement

Lemma 3.2.1
.

If the linear system (
3.1.1
) has an exponential dichotomy on
[t_{0},+\infty)
with
t_{0}>0
then it also has an exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}_{0}^{+}
and preserve the same projection
P

and constant
\alpha>0
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we will consider a unitary norm. Then,
note that, given a unitary norm the number

N=\exp\left(\int_{0}^{t_{0}}||A(u)||\,du\right)>1

is well defined. Now, let
t
and
s
such that
0\leq s,t\leq t_{0}
and
notice that

(3.2.1)

||X(t)X^{-1}(s)||\leq N\quad\textnormal{for $0\leq s,t\leq t_{0}$}.

We will verify (
3.2.1
) when
s<t
but the other case can be proved in a similar way. In fact, by using the inequality (
1.1.8
) from Chapter 1 we have that
for any
s\leq t
with
0,\leq s,t\leq t_{0}
it follows that

||X(t)X^{-1}(s)||\leq\exp\left(\int_{s}^{t}|A(u)|\,du\right)\leq\exp\left(\int_{0}^{t_{0}}|A(u)|\,du\right)=N

and (
3.2.1
) is verified.

By using (
3.2.1
) combined with the property of exponential dichotomy on
[t_{0},+\infty)
we can deduce the following estimations:

\bullet
When
0\leq s\leq t_{0}\leq t
, it follows that:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}||X(t)PX^{-1}(s)||&=&||X(t)PX^{-1}(t_{0})X(t_{0})X^{-1}(s)||\\
\\
&\leq&||X(t)PX^{-1}(t_{0})||\,||X(t_{0})X^{-1}(s)||\\
\\
&=&N||X(t)PX^{-1}(t_{0})||\\
\\
&\leq&NKe^{-\alpha(t-t_{0})}\\
\\
&\leq&NKe^{\alpha t_{0}}e^{-\alpha(t-s)}.\end{array}

\bullet
When
0\leq s\leq t\leq t_{0}
, it follows that

\begin{array}[]{rcl}||X(t)PX^{-1}(s)||&=&||X(t)PX^{-1}(t_{0})X(t_{0})X^{-1}(s)||\\
\\
&\leq&||X(t)PX^{-1}(t_{0})||\,||X(t_{0})X^{-1}(s)||\\
\\
&=&N||X(t)PX^{-1}(t_{0})||\\
\\
&=&N||X(t)X^{-1}(t_{0})X(t_{0})PX^{-1}(t_{0})||\\
\\
&\leq&N^{2}||X(t_{0})PX^{-1}(t_{0})||\\
\\
&\leq&N^{2}K=N^{2}Ke^{\alpha t_{0}}e^{-\alpha t_{0}}\\
\\
&\leq&N^{2}e^{\alpha t_{0}}Ke^{-\alpha(t-s)}.\end{array}

Let
K_{1}=\max\{N^{2}e^{\alpha t_{0}}K,NK\}
, then we have that

||X(t)PX^{-1}(s)||\leq K_{1}e^{-\alpha(t-s)}\quad\textnormal{for any $0\leq s\leq t$},

while the other inequality can be proved similarly and the Lemma follows.
∎

The next result is symmetric to the previous one and its proof is given to the reader:

Lemma 3.2.2
.

If the linear system (
3.1.1
) has an exponential dichotomy on
(-\infty,-t_{0}]
with
t_{0}>0
then it also has an exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}_{0}^{-}
and preserve the same projection
P

and constant
\alpha>0
.

3.2.1.
An equation having exponential dichotomy on the semiaxes but not in
\mathbb{R}

The following example proposed by K.J. Palmer in
[
102
]
provides a simple case of a linear system having an exponential dichotomy on the
two semiaxes
(-\infty,0]
and
[0,+\infty)
but not in
\mathbb{R}
: let us consider the scalar equation

(3.2.2)

x^{\prime}=a(t)x,

where
a\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
is defined by

a(t)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccl}1&\textnormal{if}&t\leq-T\\
\phi(t)&\textnormal{if}&t\in[-T,T]\\
-1&\textnormal{if}&t\geq T,\end{array}\right.

where
T>0
and
\phi\colon[-T,T]\to\mathbb{R}
is a continuous function verifying
\phi(-T)=1
and
\phi(T)=-1
.

It is easy to see that (
3.2.2
) has an exponential dichotomy on
[T,+\infty)

with
P=1
,
K=1
and
\alpha=1
and also has exponential dichotomy on
(-\infty,-T]
with

P=0
,
K=1
and
\alpha=1
. Now, by Lemmas
3.2.1
–
3.2.2

we can see that
x^{\prime}=a(t)x
has an exponential dichotomy on
(-\infty,0]
and
[0,+\infty)
.
Nevertheless, by using Corollary
2.3.1
from Chapter 2 we have this equation has not an exponential dichotomy on
(-\infty,+\infty)
because any solution is bounded on
\mathbb{R}
.

3.2.2.
Noncritical uniformity

The property of noncritical uniformity has been introduced by N.N. Krasovskii
in
[
79
]
as a necessary and sufficient condition for the existence
of Lyapunov functions of nonautonomous systems having an equilibrium at the origin.
The following definition, tailored for linear systems, has been used without being explicitly mentioned
by W.A. Coppel in
[
39
, p.14]
while K.J. Palmer proposes it explicitly in
[
98
, Def.4]
:

Definition 3.2.1
.

The linear system (
3.1.1
) is
uniformly
noncritical
on
J\subseteq\mathbb{R}
if there exists

T>0
and
\theta\in(0,1)
such that any solution
t\mapsto x(t)
of (
3.1.1
) satisfies

(3.2.3)

|x(t)|\leq\theta\sup\limits_{|u-t|\leq T}|x(u)|\quad\textnormal{for any $t$ such that $[t-T,t+T]\subset J$}.

It will be useful to revisit the above definition for specific intervals
J
. If
J=[0,+\infty)
, the
property (
3.2.3
) becomes

|x(t)|\leq\theta\sup\limits_{|u-t|\leq T}|x(u)|\quad\textnormal{for any $t\geq T$},

whereas if
J=(-\infty,0]
, the property (
3.2.3
) becomes

|x(t)|\leq\theta\sup\limits_{|u-t|\leq T}|x(u)|\quad\textnormal{for any $t\leq-T$}.

The following result from Coppel
[
39
, pp.13–14]
states that
the noncritical uniformity is implied by the exponential dichotomy on
[0,+\infty)
:

Theorem 3.2.1
.

If the system (
2.2.1
) has an exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}_{0}^{+}
with nontrivial projector
P

and constants
K\geq 1
and
\alpha>0
, then
it has the property of noncritically uniformity on
\mathbb{R}_{0}^{+}
.

Proof.

Suppose that (
2.2.1
) has an exponential dichotomy on
[0,+\infty)
with constants
K
and
\alpha
. For any solution
t\mapsto x(t)
of (
2.2.1
) let us define the auxiliary functions:

(3.2.4)

x_{1}(t):=\Phi(t,s)P(s)x(s)\quad\textnormal{ and }\quad x_{2}(t):=\Phi(t,s)Q(s)x(s).

Now, it can be easily verified that
x_{1}(t)
and
x_{2}(t)
satisfy the following properties:

(3.2.5a)

\displaystyle x(t)=x_{1}(t)+x_{2}(t),

(3.2.5b)

\displaystyle x_{1}(s)=P(s)x(s)\quad\textnormal{and}\quad x_{2}(s)=Q(s)x(s),

(3.2.5c)

\displaystyle P(t)x_{1}(t)=x_{1}(t)\quad\textnormal{and}\quad Q(t)x_{2}(t)=x_{2}(t).

By the exponential dichotomy property, (
3.2.4
),(
3.2.5b
) and (
3.2.5c
) we can deduce the following estimations
when
t\geq s
:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}|x_{1}(t)|&=&|P(t)\Phi(t,s)x_{1}(s)|=|\Phi(t,s)P(s)x_{1}(s)|\leq Ke^{-\alpha(t-s)}|x_{1}(s)|,\\
\\
|x_{2}(s)|&=&|\Phi(s,t)Q(t)\Phi(t,s)Q(s)x_{2}(s)|\leq Ke^{-\alpha(t-s)}|x_{2}(t)|.\end{array}\right.

Similarly, if
t\leq s
we can deduce the estimations:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}|x_{2}(t)|&=&|\Phi(t,s)Q(s)x(s)|=|\Phi(t,s)Q(s)x_{2}(s)|\leq Ke^{-\alpha(s-t)}|x_{2}(s)|,\\
\\
|x_{1}(s)|&=&|\Phi(s,t)P(t)\Phi(t,s)P(s)x_{1}(s)|\leq Ke^{-\alpha(s-t)}|x_{1}(t)|.\end{array}\right.

The above inequalities can be summarized as follows:

|x_{1}(t)|\leq Ke^{-\alpha(t-s)}|x_{1}(s)|\quad\textnormal{and}\quad|x_{2}(t)|\geq K^{-1}e^{\alpha(t-s)}|x_{2}(s)|\,\,\,\textnormal{if}\,\,t\geq s

and

|x_{2}(t)|\leq Ke^{-\alpha(s-t)}|x_{2}(s)|\quad\textnormal{and}\quad|x_{1}(t)|\geq K^{-1}e^{\alpha(s-t)}|x_{1}(s)|\,\,\,\textnormal{if}\,\,t\leq s.

Now, we will assume that
|x_{2}(s)|\geq|x_{1}(s)|
, then if
t\geq s
, the above inequalities and (
3.2.5a
) imply that

\displaystyle|x(t)|

\displaystyle\geq|x_{2}(t)|-|x_{1}(t)|

\displaystyle\geq K^{-1}e^{\alpha(t-s)}|x_{2}(s)|-Ke^{-\alpha(t-s)}|x_{1}(s)|

\displaystyle\geq\left\{K^{-1}e^{\alpha(t-s)}-Ke^{-\alpha(t-s)}\right\}|x_{2}(s)|.

Similarly, if
|x_{2}(s)|<|x_{1}(s)|
, then for
s\geq t
,

\displaystyle|x(t)|

\displaystyle\geq|x_{1}(t)|-|x_{2}(t)|

\displaystyle\geq K^{-1}e^{\alpha(s-t)}|x_{1}(s)|-Ke^{-\alpha(s-t)}|x_{2}(s)|

\displaystyle\geq\left\{K^{-1}e^{\alpha(s-t)}-Ke^{-\alpha(s-t)}\right\}|x_{1}(s)|.

Defining the auxiliary function

\Psi(t):=K^{-1}e^{\alpha t}-Ke^{-\alpha t},

it follows that

(3.2.6)

|x(t)|\geq\Psi(t-s)|x_{2}(s)|\quad\textnormal{ if }t\geq s,

and

(3.2.7)

|x(t)|\geq\Psi(s-t)|x_{1}(s)|\quad\textnormal{ if }t\leq s.

For the function
\Psi
, we can deduce that

•

\Psi(0)=\dfrac{1}{K}-K\leq 0

•

Notice that:

\Psi^{\prime}(t)=K^{-1}\alpha e^{\alpha t}+K\alpha e^{-\alpha t}>0

•

\Psi(t_{0})=0
, where
t_{0}=\frac{1}{\alpha}\ln(K)\geq 0
.

For any
\theta\in(0,1)
we can choose
T>\frac{1}{\alpha}\ln(K)
such that
\Psi(T)\geq\dfrac{2}{\theta}
. Then if
t-s\geq T>0
, from (
3.2.6
), we have

\displaystyle|x(t)|>\Psi(T)|x_{2}(s)|\geq\dfrac{2}{\theta}|x_{2}(s)|

and since that
|x_{1}(s)|\leq|x_{2}(s)|
, we get

|x(s)|\leq|x_{1}(s)|+|x_{2}(s)|\leq 2|x_{2}(s)|\leq\theta|x(t)|.

In a similar way, if
s-t\geq T>0
, from (
3.2.7
) and the inequality
|x_{2}(s)|<|x_{1}(s)|
, it is shown that

|x(t)|>\dfrac{2}{\theta}|x_{1}(s)|>\dfrac{1}{\theta}|x(s)|.

Thus, we can conclude that

|x(s)|<\theta|x(t)|,\quad\textnormal{ if }\quad|s-t|\geq T.

Next, for a fixed
s\geq T
, we choose
t=T+s\geq s
, then

\displaystyle|x(s)|

\displaystyle\leq\theta|x(T+s)|

(3.2.8)

\displaystyle\leq\theta\sup\limits_{s\leq u\leq s+T}|x(u)|=\theta\sup\limits_{0\leq u-s\leq T}|x(u)|.

For a fixed
s\geq T
, we now choose
t=s-T<s
, then

\displaystyle|x(s)|

\displaystyle\leq\theta|x(s-T)|

(3.2.9)

\displaystyle\leq\theta\sup\limits_{s-T\leq u\leq s}|x(u)|=\theta\sup\{|x(u)|:-T\leq u-s\leq 0\}.

Finally, (
3.2.8
) and (
3.2.9
) imply that

|x(s)|\leq\theta\sup\limits_{|u-s|\leq T}|x(u)|\quad\textnormal{ for }s\geq T,

which concludes the proof.
∎

A converse result can be obtained provided the additional property of bounded
growth stated in Chapter 1.

Theorem 3.2.2
.

If the linear system (
3.1.1
) has the properties of bounded growth and noncritical uniformity
on
[0,+\infty)
then it also has an exponential dichotomy on
[0,+\infty)
.

As the linear system (
3.1.1
) is noncritical uniform on
[0,+\infty)
, there exists constants
T>0
and
0<\theta<1
such that every solution
t\mapsto x(t)
satisfy the property

(3.2.10)

|x(t)|\leq\theta\sup\limits_{|u-t|\leq T}|x(u)|\quad\textnormal{for every $t\geq T$}.

In addition, by considering the above constant
T>0
, the bounded growth property implies the existence of
C>1
such that every solution
t\mapsto x(t)
of the linear system (
3.1.1
) satisfy the property

(3.2.11)

|x(t)|\leq C|x(s)|\quad\textnormal{for $0\leq s\leq t\leq s+T$}.

The proof will be consequence of several Lemmas. Firstly, notice that any nontrivial solution
t\mapsto x(t)
of (
3.1.1
) is either
bounded or unbounded on
[0,+\infty)
. The idea is to prove that bounded and unbounded solutions
has the same qualitative properties that the functions described in the splitting of solution given in (
2.3.1
) from the previous chapter.

Lemma 3.2.3
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
3.2.2
, there exists
K\geq 1
and
\alpha>0
such that any nontrivial solution
t\mapsto x(t)
which is bounded on
[0,+\infty)
verifies:

(3.2.12)

|x(t)|\leq\displaystyle Ke^{-\alpha(t-s)}|x(s)|\quad\textnormal{for any $t\geq s\geq 0$}.

Proof.

Step 1: A first estimation.
As (
3.2.11
) provides information about the solution
x(\cdot)
on
[s,s+T]
, we will study its properties when
t\geq s+T
. In order to do that let us define the map
\mu\colon[0,+\infty)\to[0,+\infty)
as follows:

\mu(s):=\sup\limits_{u\geq s}|x(u)|.

Notice that
t\geq s+T
is equivalent to

t-T\geq s
and also implies that
t\geq T
since
s\geq 0
. These facts combined with (
3.2.10
) imply that:

(3.2.13)

\begin{array}[]{rl}|x(t)|&\leq\theta\sup\limits_{|u-t|\leq T}|x(u)|\\
&=\theta\sup\{|x(u)|:t-T\leq u\leq t+T\}\\
&\leq\theta\sup\limits_{s\leq u\leq t+T}|x(u)|\\
&\leq\theta\sup\limits_{u\geq s}|x(u)|=\theta\mu(s).\end{array}

The above estimation implies that

(3.2.14)

\mu(s)=\sup\limits_{s\leq u\leq s+T}|x(u)|.

Indeed, by using
\sup(A\cup B)=\max\{\sup(A),\sup(B)\}
and the estimation (
3.2.13
),
we can deduce that

\mu(s)=\max\left\{\sup\limits_{s\leq u\leq s+T}|x(u)|,\sup\limits_{s+T\leq u}|x(u)|\right\}\leq\max\left\{\sup\limits_{s\leq u\leq s+T}|x(u)|,\theta\mu(s)\right\},

which leads to

\mu(s)\leq\sup\limits_{s\leq u\leq s+T}|x(u)|,

whereas the inverse inequality is trivial and (
3.2.14
) follows.

Therefore, by using (
3.2.11
),(
3.2.13
),(
3.2.14
) and recalling that
t\geq s+T
, we deduce that

\displaystyle|x(t)|

\displaystyle\leq\theta\mu(s)

\displaystyle=\theta\sup\limits_{s\leq u\leq s+T}|x(u)|

\displaystyle\leq\theta C|x(s)|,

which combined with (
3.2.11
) implies

|x(t)|\leq C|x(s)|\quad\mbox{ for }\quad 0\leq s\leq t.

Step 2: A useful inequality.
At this step, we will assume that

t\geq s+nT
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
. In addition, by recalling that
nT
is strictly increasing, we have that
t\geq T
and
t-T\geq s+(n-1)T
. These inequalities and (
3.2.10
) imply:

(3.2.15)

\begin{array}[]{rl}|x(t)|&\leq\theta\sup\limits_{|u-t|\leq T}|x(u)|\\
&\leq\theta\sup\limits_{u\geq s+(n-1)T}|x(u)|=\theta\mu(s+(n-1)T).\end{array}

As
t\geq s+nT
and noticing that
n\in\mathbb{N}
is arbitrary, the above estimation implies that

(3.2.16)

\mu(s+nT)\leq\theta\mu(s+(n-1)T).

Step 3:
t\mapsto|x(t)|
is an exponential contraction.

At this step we will assume that
t\geq s
. Notice there always exists some
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that
s+nT\leq t\leq s+(n+1)T
, then the estimations (
3.2.15
)–(
3.2.16
) allow us to deduce that:

|x(t)|\leq\theta\mu(s+nT)\leq\theta^{2}\mu(s+(n-2)T)\leq\cdots\leq\theta^{n}\mu(s),

and, by using (
3.2.11
) and (
3.2.14
), we have that

(3.2.17)

|x(t)|\leq\theta^{n}\sup\limits_{s\leq u\leq s+T}|x(u)|\leq\theta^{n}C|x(s)|.

On the other hand, as
nT\leq t-s\leq(n+1)T
, we can see that

\displaystyle\theta^{n+1}\leq\theta^{\frac{t-s}{T}}\leq\theta^{n}.

The above estimation together with (
3.2.17
) implies:

\displaystyle|x(t)|

\displaystyle\leq\theta^{-1}C\theta^{n+1}|x(s)|

\displaystyle\leq\theta^{-1}Ce^{\frac{\ln(\theta)}{T}(t-s)}|x(s)|.

Taking
K=\theta^{-1}C\geq 1
and
\alpha=-\ln(\theta)/T>0
since
\theta\in(0,1)
and
T>0
, we obtain (
3.2.12
) and the Lemma follows.
∎

Lemma 3.2.4
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
3.2.2
, there exists
K\geq 1
,
\alpha>0
and
T_{1}>0
such that any solution
t\mapsto x(t)

unbounded on
[0,+\infty)
verifies:

(3.2.18)

|x(t)|\leq Ke^{-\alpha(s-t)}|x(s)|\quad\mbox{ for }\quad s\geq t\geq T_{1}>0.

Proof.

Step 1: Preliminaries.

Let
t\mapsto x(t)
be an unbounded solution with
|x(0)|=1
. As
x(\cdot)
is also continuous, we can take a sequence
\{t_{n}\}
with
t_{n}>0
such that

(3.2.19)

|x(t_{n})|=\theta^{-n}C\quad\textnormal{and}\quad|x(t)|<\theta^{-n}C\quad\mbox{ for }\quad 0\leq t<t_{n},

which implies that the sequence is strictly increasing and upper unbounded. On the other hand, by the uniform bounded growth (
3.2.11
) with
s=0
we have that:

|x(t)|\leq C|x(0)|=C<\theta^{-1}C\quad\mbox{ for }\quad 0\leq t\leq T,

which allow us to see that
T<t_{1}
.

Step 2:
t_{n+1}\leq t_{n}+T
for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Otherwise, there exists
n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
t_{n_{0}+1}>t_{n_{0}}+T
, this fact combined with recalling
t_{n_{0}}>T
and (
3.2.10
) leads to:

|x(t_{n_{0}})|\leq\theta\sup\limits_{|u-t_{n_{0}}|\leq T}|x(u)|\leq\theta\sup\limits_{0\leq u\leq t_{n_{0}}+T}|x(u)|\leq\theta\sup\limits_{0\leq u\leq t_{n_{0}+1}}|x(u)|.

In addition, a direct consequence from (
3.2.19
) is that

\sup\limits_{0\leq u\leq t_{n_{0}+1}}|x(u)|=C\theta^{-(n_{0}+1)}=\theta^{-1}|x(t_{n_{0}})|.

By gathering the above estimations, we obtain that

|x(t_{n_{0}})|\leq\theta\sup\limits_{0\leq u\leq t_{n_{0}+1}}|x(u)|\leq|x(t_{n_{0}})|,

which is a contradiction.

Step 3: End of proof.

Suppose that
0\leq t\leq s
and
t_{m}\leq t<t_{m+1}
,
t_{n}\leq s<t_{n+1}
with
1\leq m<n
. By (
3.2.19
)
and the qualitative properties of
\{t_{n}\}_{n}
we have that:

(3.2.20)

\begin{array}[]{rl}|x(t)|&\leq\theta^{-m-1}C\\
&=\theta^{n-m}|x(t_{n+1})|\\
&\leq\theta^{-1}C\theta^{n-m+1}|x(s)|,\end{array}

where the last estimation follows from
t_{n+1}\leq t_{n}+T
combined with (
3.2.11
).

In order to obtain a better estimation of (
3.2.20
), we have that

s-t\leq t_{n+1}-t_{m}\leq(t_{n}+T)-t_{m}=(t_{n}-t_{m})+T.

Moreover,

t_{n}\leq t_{n-1}+T\leq t_{n-2}+2T\leq\cdots\leq t_{m}+(n-m)T,

which implies that

e^{s-t}\leq e^{(n-m+1)T}=(e^{T})^{n-m+1}

and so
(s-t)/T\leq n-m+1
, which leads to:

\theta^{n-m+1}\leq\theta^{\frac{s-t}{T}}.

Next, upon inserting the above estimation in (
3.2.20
), we get that

\displaystyle|x(t)|

\displaystyle\leq\theta^{-1}C_{T}\theta^{\frac{s-t}{T}}|x(s)|

\displaystyle=\theta^{-1}C_{T}e^{(s-t)\frac{\ln(\theta)}{T}}|x(s)|,

then (
3.2.18
) is obtained with
K=\theta^{-1}C\geq 1
,
\alpha=-\ln(\theta)/T>0

and
T_{1}=t_{1}
.
∎

3.2.3.
Uniform exponential dichotomy and uniform noncriticality: Proof of Theorem
3.2.2

Let us consider the subspace:

V_{1}:=\left\{\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:\sup\limits_{t\geq 0}|X(t,0)\xi|<+\infty\right\}

and let
V_{2}
be a subspace of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that
\mathbb{R}^{n}=V_{1}\oplus V_{2}
. For any
\xi\in V_{2}
, with
|\xi|=1
, let
t\mapsto x(t)=x(t,\xi)
be the solution of (
3.1.1
) with initial condition
x(0)=\xi
. Then
t\mapsto x(t,\xi)
is unbounded and so there exists a value
t_{1}:=t_{1}(\xi)
such that

|x(t,\xi)|<\theta^{-1}C\quad\textnormal{for any $0\leq t<t_{1}(\xi)$}\quad\textnormal{and}\quad|x(t_{1},\xi)|=\theta^{-1}C.

We will show that the set
B:=\{t_{1}(\xi):\xi\in V_{2}\quad\textnormal{ and }\quad|\xi|=1\}
is bounded. In fact, if
B
is unbounded, there is a sequence
\{\xi_{\nu}\}_{\nu\in\mathbb{N}}\subset V_{2}
with
|\xi_{\nu}|=1
and
t_{1}^{(\nu)}=t_{1}(\xi_{\nu})\to+\infty
as
\nu\to+\infty
. By the compactness of the unit sphere in
V_{2}
, we may assume that
\xi_{\nu}\to\xi_{0}
as
\nu\to+\infty
, for some unit vector
\xi_{0}\in V_{2}
. Thus, by Proposition
1.2.3
from Chapter 1, we have that the uniform bounded growth property implies that

x(t,\xi_{\nu})\to x(t,\xi)\quad\textnormal{ as }\quad\nu\to+\infty,

for all
t\geq 0
. Since
|x(t,\xi_{\nu})|<\theta^{-1}C
for
0\leq t<t_{1}^{(\nu)}
, it follows that

|x(t,\xi_{0})|\leq\theta^{-1}C\quad\textnormal{ for }\quad 0\leq t<+\infty,

which is a contradiction with the fact that
\xi_{0}\in V_{2}
. Then there is
T_{1}>0
such that
t_{1}(\xi)\leq T_{1}
for all unit vector
\xi\in V_{2}
. Next, for all
\xi\in V_{2}
, with
\xi\neq 0
, Lemma
3.2.4
implies that

|x(t,\xi)|\leq|\xi|Ke^{-\alpha(s-t)}|x(s,\frac{\xi}{|\xi|})|=Ke^{-\alpha(s-t)}|x(s,\xi)|,

for
s\geq t\geq T_{1}>0
.

Now, let
P
be the projection from the split
\mathbb{R}^{n}=V_{1}\oplus V_{2}
on the subspace
V_{1}
. Then for each
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, by Lemmas
3.2.3
and
3.2.4
, we have that

|X(t)P\xi|\leq Ke^{-\alpha(t-s)}|X(s)P\xi|\quad\textnormal{ for }\quad t\geq s\geq T_{1}

and

|X(t)(I-P)\xi|\leq Ke^{-\alpha(s-t)}|X(s)(I-P)\xi|\quad\textnormal{ for }\quad s\geq t\geq T_{1}.

The uniform bounded growth property together with Lemma
2.2.2
imply that there exists a positive constant
M
such that

||X(t)PX^{-1}(t)||\leq M\quad\textnormal{for any $t\geq T_{1}$}.

Hence, by Proposition
2.2.1
from Chapter 2 we have that (
3.1.1
) has a uniform exponential dichotomy on the interval
[T_{1},+\infty)
and by Lemma
3.2.1
, it has a uniform exponential dichotomy on
[0,+\infty)
.

3.3.
Bounded solutions on the half lines and exponential dichotomy

As we shown in an example at the beginning of this chapter, if the linear system (
3.1.1
) has simultaneously an exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}_{0}^{-}
and
\mathbb{R}_{0}^{+}
this not always implies the existence of an exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}
. Now, in this section we will provide a necessary and sufficient condition ensuring the exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}
. In this context, we recall the following definition.

Definition 3.3.1
.

The index of a
n
–dimensional linear system
\dot{x}=A(t)x
with exponential dichotomies both on
\mathbb{R}_{0}^{+}
and
\mathbb{R}_{0}^{-}
with constant projections
P_{+}
and
P_{-}
respectively, is given by

i(A)=\textnormal{\text{dim}\,\text{Im}}P_{+}+\textnormal{\text{dim}}\,\ker P_{-}-n.

Moreover, we will consider the linear system (
3.1.1
) with fundamental matrix
X(t)
and let us redefine the subspaces

(3.3.1a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}:=\left\{\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\colon\sup\limits_{t\geq 0}|X(t,0)\xi|<+\infty\right\},

(3.3.1b)

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}:=\left\{\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\colon\sup\limits_{t\leq 0}|X(t,0)\xi|<+\infty\right\}.

The next two results consider different projections but its proof is
similar to the Lemma
2.3.1
studied in the previous chapter.

Lemma 3.3.1
.

If the linear system (
3.1.1
) has an exponential dichotomy on
[0,+\infty)

with projector
P_{+}
and constants
(K,\alpha)
, then
it follows that
\mathcal{V}=\textnormal{\text{Im}}P_{+}
.

Lemma 3.3.2
.

If the linear system (
3.1.1
) has an exponential dichotomy on
(-\infty,0]

with projector
P_{-}
and constants
(K,\alpha)
, then
it follows that
\mathcal{W}=\ker P_{-}
.

The following result has been stated in
[
102
, Lemma 1]
.

Lemma 3.3.3
.

Assume that the linear system (
3.1.1
) has exponential dichotomies both on
\mathbb{R}_{0}^{+}
and
\mathbb{R}_{0}^{-}
with projections
P_{+}
and
P_{-}
respectively, then the following properties are equivalent:

i)

The unique bounded solution of the lineal system (
3.1.1
) is the trivial one,

ii)

The projectors
P_{+}
and
P_{-}
satisfy the identity

P_{+}P_{-}=P_{-}P_{+}=P_{+}.

Proof.

By using lemmas (
3.3.1
)–(
3.3.2
) it follows that:

\text{Im}P_{+}=\mathcal{V}\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\ker P_{-}=\mathcal{W}.

If we assume the statement i), it can be straightforwardly proved that
\ker P_{-}\cap\text{Im}P_{+}=\{0\}
. Now, for any nonzero vector
\xi\in\ker P_{-}
it follows that
\xi
must be
in a complementary subspace of
\text{Im}P_{+}
, which leads to

(3.3.2)

\ker P_{-}\subseteq\ker P_{+},

this allow us to deduce that
\ker P_{-}\oplus\text{Im}P_{+}\subset\ker P_{+}\oplus\text{Im}P_{+}=\mathbb{R}^{n}
. We can deduce the existence of a complementary subspace
V
such that:

\ker P_{-}\oplus V\oplus\text{Im}P_{+}=\mathbb{R}^{n},

then
V\oplus\text{Im}P_{+}=\text{Im}P_{-}
which leads to

(3.3.3)

\text{Im}P_{+}\subseteq\text{Im}P_{-}.

For any
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
it follows that
P_{+}\xi\in\text{Im}P_{+}\subset\text{Im}P_{-}
, which implies that
P_{-}P_{+}\xi=P_{+}\xi
and the identity
P_{-}P_{+}=P_{+}
is verified.

Now, for any
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
we have that
\xi=\eta+\lambda
with

\eta\in\ker P_{-}
and
\lambda\in\text{Im}P_{-}
. We have that
P_{-}\xi=\lambda

and, as
\ker P_{-}\subset\ker P_{+}
, we also have that
P_{+}\eta=0
which allow us to conclude that
P_{+}P_{-}\xi=P_{+}\lambda=P_{+}(\eta+\lambda)=P_{+}\xi
, then
P_{+}P_{-}=P_{+}

and the statement (ii) follows.

The converse implication is left as an exercise for the reader, we also refer to
[
102
, p.S176]
.

∎

Proposition 3.3.1
.

The linear system
\dot{x}=A(t)x
has an exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}

if and only if:

a)

The system has exponential dichotomies on
\mathbb{R}_{0}^{+}
and
\mathbb{R}_{0}^{-}

with projections
P_{+}
and
P_{-}
respectively,

b)

The system has no nontrivial bounded solutions,

c)

The index of the linear system is
i(A)=0
.

Proof.

Firstly, if the linear system has an exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}
with projector
P
,
the properties a) and c) are verified straightforwardly with
P=P_{-}=P_{+}
. In addition,
the property b) follows from the Corollary
2.3.1
from Chapter 2.

Secondly, let us assume that the properties a),b) and c) are satisfied. Now, by Lemma
3.3.3
we can prove that the contentions (
3.3.2
)–(
3.3.3
) cannot be strict, namely:

\ker P_{-}=\ker P_{+}\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\text{Im}P_{+}=\text{Im}P_{-}.

Indeed, otherwise, if
\ker P_{-}\subset\ker P_{+}
we will have that

\text{dim}\ker P_{-}<\text{dim}\ker P_{+}
, which combined with
i(A)=0
leads to

\begin{array}[]{rcl}n&=&\text{dim}\ker P_{+}+\text{dim}\text{Im}P_{+}\\
&>&\text{dim}\ker P_{-}+\text{dim}\text{Im}P_{+}=n,\end{array}

obtaining a contradiction. The identity
\text{Im}P_{+}=\text{Im}P_{-}
can be proved in
a similar way.

Let
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. As
P_{-}\xi\in\text{Im}P_{-}=\text{Im}P_{+}
, it follows that

P_{+}P_{-}\xi=P_{-}\xi
. On the other hand, by using the statement ii) of Lemma
3.3.3

we also have that
P_{+}P_{-}\xi=P_{+}\xi
, which implies that

P_{-}\xi=P_{+}\xi\quad\textnormal{for any $\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{n}$},

and the identity
P_{+}=P_{-}
follows, this implies that the linear system has
an exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}
with projector
P=P_{+}=P_{-}
.

∎

3.4.
Backing to the projector problem

In Remark
2.2.6
we called as the
projector problem
the following question: if a linear system (
2.2.1
) with a fundamental matrix
X(t)
has an exponential dichotomy on
J
with projector
P
, is this
projector unique?

The results of the previous section will allow to provide an answer considering
J=\mathbb{R}_{0}^{-}
,
J=\mathbb{R}_{0}^{+}
,
and
J=\mathbb{R}
. In the case of the half axes we will provide sufficient conditions ensuring the existence of other projectors whereas the uniqueness is proved for the case
J=\mathbb{R}
.

If a linear system (
2.2.1
) has an exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}_{0}^{-}
with projector
P
we
know, as we have seen in Remark
2.3.1
from Chapter 2, that any solution
x(t)=\Phi(t,0)\xi
of (
2.2.1
) can be splitted
between an exponential
expansion
t\mapsto\Phi(t,0)P\xi
and an exponential contraction
t\mapsto\Phi(t,0)[I-P]\xi
when
t\to-\infty
. Now,
if there exists another projector
\overline{P}
such that
\ker P=\ker\overline{P}
, the Lemma
3.3.2

states that

\ker\overline{P}=\mathcal{W}=\left\{\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\colon\sup\limits_{t\leq 0}|X(t,0)\xi|<+\infty\right\}.

As the solutions of (
2.2.1
) also can be splitted as
t\mapsto x(t)=\Phi(t,0)\xi=\Phi(t,0)\overline{P}\xi+\Phi(t,0)[I-\overline{P}]\xi
where
t\mapsto\Phi(t,0)[I-\overline{P}]\xi
is an exponential contraction when
t\to-\infty
. A natural question is to determine what are additional assumptions on
\overline{P}
in order to ensure the existence
an exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}_{0}^{-}
with projector
\overline{P}
. The following result provides an answer.

Lemma 3.4.1
.

If the linear system (
3.1.1
) has an exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}_{0}^{-}
with projection
P
and there exist another projection
\overline{P}
satisfying the properties

i)

There exists
\tilde{K}>0
such that

(3.4.1)

\tilde{K}=\sup_{t\in(-\infty,0]}\|X(t)(I-\overline{P})X^{-1}(t)\|,

ii)

\ker P=\ker\overline{P}
,

then the system (
3.1.1
) has also an exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}_{0}^{-}
with projection
\overline{P}
.

Proof.

The property
\ker P=\ker\overline{P}
implies the following identities whose proof is left an exercise:

(I-\overline{P})=(I-P)(I-\overline{P})\text{ and }\overline{P}=(I-P+\overline{P})P.

By using the above characterization of
(I-\overline{P})
and considering
t\leq s\leq 0
, we have that

\displaystyle\|X(t)(I-\overline{P})X^{-1}(s)\|

\displaystyle=\|X(t)(I-P)(I-\overline{P})X^{-1}(s)\|

\displaystyle=\|X(t)(I-P)X^{-1}(s)X(s)(I-\overline{P})X^{-1}(s)\|

\displaystyle\leq\|X(t)(I-P)X^{-1}(s)\|\|X(s)(I-\overline{P})X^{-1}(s)\|

\displaystyle\leq Ke^{-\alpha(s-t)}\|X(s)(I-\overline{P})X^{-1}(s)\|

\displaystyle\leq K\tilde{K}e^{-\alpha(s-t)}.

By using the above characterization of
\overline{P}
and considering
s\leq t\leq 0
, we have that

\displaystyle\|X(t)\overline{P}X^{-1}(s)\|

\displaystyle=\|X(t)(I-P+\overline{P})PX^{-1}(s)\|

\displaystyle=\|X(t)(I-P+\overline{P})X^{-1}(t)X(t)PX^{-1}(s)\|

\displaystyle\leq\|X(t)(I-P+\overline{P})X^{-1}(t)\|\|X(t)PX^{-1}(s)\|

\displaystyle\leq Ke^{-\alpha(t-s)}\|X(t)(I-P+\overline{P})X^{-1}(t)\|

\displaystyle\leq Ke^{-\alpha(t-s)}\{\|X(t)(I-P)X^{-1}(t)\|+\|X(t)\overline{P}X^{-1}(t)||\}

\displaystyle\leq K(K+K\tilde{K}+||I||)e^{-\alpha(t-s)}

where the last estimation is obtained by using

||X(t)\overline{P}X^{-1}(t)||-||I||\leq||X(t)(I-\overline{P})X^{-1}(t)||\leq K\tilde{K}

and the result follows.
∎

The above result has a symmetric version when considering the exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}_{0}^{+}
.

Lemma 3.4.2
.

If the linear system (
3.1.1
) has an exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}_{0}^{+}
with projection
P
and there exist another projection
P^{\prime}
satisfying the properties

i)

There exists
K^{\prime}>0
such that

(3.4.2)

K^{\prime}=\sup_{t\in[0,+\infty)}\|X(t)P^{\prime}X^{-1}(t)\|,

ii)

\textnormal{Im}\,P=\textnormal{Im}\,P^{\prime}
,

then the system (
3.1.1
) has also an exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}_{0}^{+}
with projection
P^{\prime}
.

Proof.

We can see that the property
\textnormal{Im}\,P=\textnormal{Im}\,P^{\prime}
implies the identities

(3.4.3)

P^{\prime}P=P\quad\textnormal{and}\quad PP^{\prime}=P^{\prime},

which allow us to deduce:

P-P^{\prime}=P(P-P^{\prime})=(P-P^{\prime})(I-P)\quad\textnormal{and}\quad I-P^{\prime}=[I+P-P^{\prime}](I-P).

By using
P^{\prime}=PP^{\prime}
and considering
0\leq s\leq t
,
we have that

\displaystyle\|X(t)P^{\prime}X^{-1}(s)\|

\displaystyle=\|X(t)PP^{\prime}X^{-1}(s)\|

\displaystyle=\|X(t)PX^{-1}(s)X(s)P^{\prime}X^{-1}(s)\|

\displaystyle\leq\|X(t)PX^{-1}(s)||||X(s)P^{\prime}X^{-1}(s)\|

\displaystyle\leq KK^{\prime}e^{-\alpha(t-s)}.

By using the above characterization of
(I-P^{\prime})
and considering
0\leq t\leq s
, we have that

\displaystyle\|X(t)(I-P^{\prime})X^{-1}(s)\|

\displaystyle=\|X(t)(I+P-P^{\prime})(I-P)X^{-1}(s)\|

\displaystyle=\|X(t)(I+P-P^{\prime})X^{-1}(t)X(t)(I-P)X^{-1}(s)\|

\displaystyle\leq\|X(t)(I+P-P^{\prime})X^{-1}(t)|||X(t)(I-P)X^{-1}(s)\|

\displaystyle\leq Ke^{-\alpha(s-t)}\|X(t)(I-P^{\prime}+P)X^{-1}(t)||

\displaystyle\leq Ke^{-\alpha(s-t)}\left(\|X(t)[I-P^{\prime}]X^{-1}(t)||+||X(t)PX^{-1}(t)||\right)

\displaystyle\leq K(||I||+K+KK^{\prime})e^{-\alpha(s-t)},

where the last estimation is a consequence of

||X(t)P^{\prime}X^{-1}(t)||-||I||\leq||X(t)[I-P^{\prime}]X^{-1}(t)||\leq KK^{\prime}

and the result follows.
∎

Finally, as we stated at the beginning of this section, we conclude it
with an affirmative answer to the projector problem when
J=\mathbb{R}
.

Lemma 3.4.3
.

If the linear system (
3.1.1
) has an exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}
with projection
P

this projection is unique.

Proof.

Let us assume that there exists another projector
P^{\prime}
.
In addition, it follows that the system (
3.1.1
) also has an exponential dichotomy
on
\mathbb{R}_{0}^{-}
and
\mathbb{R}_{0}^{+}
, then by using lemmas
3.3.1
and
3.3.2

we have that

\ker P=\ker P^{\prime}\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\text{Im}\,P=\text{Im}\,P^{\prime}.

By Lemma
3.3.3
we have that

PP^{\prime}=P^{\prime}P
which combined with the identity (
3.4.3
) from Lemma
3.4.2

leads to
P=P^{\prime}
.
∎

3.5.
Comments and references

1)
The majority of the results stated in this section
are from the Coppel’s book, namely, Lemma
3.2.1
, Theorem
3.2.1

and Theorem
3.2.2
. We only added
intermediate computations and explanations in order to improve its clarity.

2)
The Theorem
3.2.2
is revisited and improved
by K.J. Palmer in
[
102
, Th.1]
where it is proved that if the system
(
3.1.1
) is continuous on
\mathbb{R}_{0}^{+}
and has the property of
uniform bounded growth then, the properties of exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}_{0}^{+}
,
uniform noncriticality and exponential expansiveness are equivalent.

We recall that
(
3.1.1
) is exponentially expansive on
\mathbb{R}_{0}^{+}
if there exist positive
constants
L
and
\beta
such that for any solution
t\mapsto x(t)
of (
3.1.1
)
and compact interval
[a,b]\subset\mathbb{R}_{0}^{+}
it follows that:

|x(t)|\leq L\left\{e^{-\beta(t-a)}|x(a)|+e^{-\beta(b-t)}|x(b)|\right\}\quad\textnormal{for any $t\in[a,b]$}.

3)
The Lemma
3.3.3

is from K.J. Palmer
[
102
]
and allowed to carry out an easy proof of Proposition
3.3.1
.

4)
The Lemmas
3.4.1
and
3.4.2
about the projector problem
where adapted from Kloeden
\&
Rasmussen’s book
[
78
, p.83]
. Notice that, by considering
the section 2.3 from Chapter 2, we can construct the projectors
\overline{P}(t)=X(t)\overline{P}X^{-1}(t)

and
P^{\prime}(t)=X(t)P^{\prime}X^{-1}(t)
such that the properties (
3.4.1
) and (
3.4.2
) becomes

\tilde{K}=\sup_{t\in(-\infty,0]}\|[I-\overline{P}(t)]\|,\quad\textnormal{and}\quad K^{\prime}=\sup_{t\in[0,+\infty)}\|P^{\prime}(t)\|.

Last but not least, Lemmas
3.4.1
and
3.4.2
allowed us to provide an easy proof
for Lemma
3.4.3
.

3.6.
Exercises

1.-

Prove the Lemma
3.2.2
.

2.-

Prove the Lemma
3.3.2
.

3.-

Prove that ii) implies i) in Lemma
3.3.3
.

4.-

Under the assumptions of Lemma
3.4.1
prove the identities

(I-\overline{P})=(I-P)(I-\overline{P})\text{ and }\overline{P}=(I-P+\overline{P})P.

5.-

Under the assumptions of Lemma
3.4.2
prove the identities

P-\overline{P}=P(P-\overline{P})=(P-\overline{P})(I-P)

and

I-\overline{P}=[I+P-\overline{P}](I-P).

6.-

Prove Lemmas
3.4.1
and
3.4.2
by considering variable projectors.

7.-

Prove a symmetric version of Theorem
3.2.2
: if the linear system (
3.1.1
) has the properties of bounded decay and noncritical uniformity
on
(-\infty,0]
then it also has an exponential dichotomy on
(-\infty,0]
.

Chapter 4
Reducibility

The main result of this section states that any linear system having an exponential dichotomy on
J
with nontrivial projection is generally kinematically similar to a block diagonal system.

4.1.
Preliminaries

4.1.1.
Definition

The reducibility is a particular case of general kinematical similarity introduced by W. Coppel in

[
40
]
and revisited in
[
41
, p.38]
.

Definition 4.1.1
.

The linear
n
–dimensional system

(4.1.1)

\dot{x}=A(t)x\quad\textnormal{with $t\in J\subseteq\mathbb{R}$}

with
A(t)\in M_{n}(\mathbb{R})
continuous on
J
is
reducible
to

(4.1.2)

\dot{y}=B(t)y\quad\textnormal{with $t\in J\subseteq\mathbb{R}$}

if the systems (
4.1.1
) and (
4.1.2
) are generally kinematically
similar on
J
. Additionally the matrix
B(t)
has the block form:

B(t)=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}B_{1}(t)&0\\
\\
0&B_{2}(t)\end{array}\right],

where
B_{1}(t)\in M_{n_{1}}(\mathbb{C})
and
B_{2}(t)\in M_{n_{2}}(\mathbb{C})

with
n_{1}+n_{2}=n
and
n_{1},n_{2}<n
.

In order to avoid confusion, as pointed by W. Coppel in
[
40
]
, it is important to say that in a vast number of references, the property of reducibility is a different thing, namely, the kinematical similarity to an autonomous system, which was introduced by A. Lyapunov and strongly influenced the mathematical literature. In addition, W. Coppel states that Definition
4.1.1
agrees with the definition of reducibility in linear algebra.

4.1.2.
Technical results

Lemma 4.1.1
.

Let
P
be an orthogonal projection (i.e.,
P^{*}=P
) and
X
be an invertible matrix, then there exists an invertible matrix
S
such that

SPS^{-1}=XPX^{-1}.

Moreover, the matrix
S
satisfies the following properties:

||S||_{2}\leq\sqrt{2}\quad\textnormal{and}\quad||S^{-1}||_{2}\leq\sqrt{||XPX^{-1}||_{2}^{2}+||X(I-P)X^{-1}||_{2}^{2}}.

Proof.

In this proof, we will write
||\cdot||
instead of
||\cdot||_{2}
. Moreover, we will divide the proof in several steps.

Step 1:
The matrix

(4.1.3)

U=PX^{*}XP+(I-P)X^{*}X(I-P),

is Hermitian and positive.

The identity
U=U^{*}
is a direct consequence of
P=P^{*}
. In order to prove that
U
is positive, let
\xi\in\mathbb{C}^{n}\setminus\{0\}
and notice that

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\xi^{*}U\xi&=&\xi^{*}PX^{*}XP\xi+\xi^{*}(I-P)X^{*}X(I-P)\xi\\
\\
&=&||XP\xi||^{2}+||X(I-P)\xi||^{2}\geq 0\\
\\
\end{array}

and we will see that the inequality is strict. Indeed, otherwise if
\xi^{*}U\xi=0
, it will be equivalent to

XP\xi=0\quad\textnormal{and}\quad X(I-P)\xi=0,

which implies that
XP\xi+X(I-P)\xi=X\xi=0
and
\xi=0
since
X
is invertible, obtaining a contradiction.

Step 2:
The positiveness of
U
implies the
existence and uniqueness of a matrix
R=R^{*}>0
