such that
R^{2}=U
, we
refer the reader to the Appendix A and references for details.

Step 3
: The matrices
R
and
P
commute.

As
P^{2}=P
and
R^{2}=U
, by using (
4.1.3
), it is easy to see that

R^{2}P=PR^{2}=PX^{*}XP.

As
R^{2}
and
P
are Hermitian matrices they are diagonalizable. In addition, as they commute it is well known that they have simultaneous diagonalization (see
e.g.

[
71
, pp.69–72]
,
[
113
]
), that is, there exists an invertible matrix
Q
such that

R^{2}=Q^{-1}AQ\quad\textnormal{and}\quad P=Q^{-1}BQ,

where
A
and
B
are diagonal matrices containing the eigenvalues of
R^{2}
and
P
respectively.

As the diagonal matriz
A
has only positive terms, we can consider
A=DD
, where
D
is a diagonal matrix

D=\textnormal{Diag}\{d_{11},\ldots,d_{nn}\}
such that
a_{ii}=\sqrt{d_{ii}}
. Then, it follows that

R^{2}=Q^{-1}DDQ=(Q^{-1}DQ)(Q^{-1}DQ)

and we have that
R=Q^{-1}DQ
.

As
BD=DB
since are diagonal matrices, we have that

Q^{-1}BDQ=Q^{-1}DBQ,

which is equivalent to

(Q^{-1}BQ)(Q^{-1}DQ)=(Q^{-1}DQ)(Q^{-1}BQ),

which is also equivalent to

PR=RP
and also implies that

R(I-P)=R-RP=R-PR=(I-P)R.

Step 4
: Let us consider the transformation
S=XR^{-1}
. Now, note that
PR=RP
implies:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}SPS^{-1}&=&XR^{-1}PRX^{-1}\\
\\
&=&XR^{-1}RPX^{-1}\\
\\
&=&XPX^{-1},\end{array}

and we deduce the first identity stated in the Lemma.

Step 5
: By using
SR=X
,
R^{*}=R
and recalling that
PR=RP
where
R^{2}=U
, we have that

\begin{array}[]{rcl}R^{2}&=&PX^{*}XP+(I-P)X^{*}X(I-P)\\
\\
&=&PR^{*}S^{*}SRP+(I-P)R^{*}S^{*}SR(I-P)\\
\\
&=&PRS^{*}SRP+(I-P)RS^{*}SR(I-P)\\
\\
&=&RPS^{*}SPR+R(I-P)S^{*}S(I-P)R.\end{array}

We multiply the above identity by
R^{-1}
by the right and the left, obtaining

I=PS^{*}SP+(I-P)S^{*}S(I-P).

Now, let
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{0\}
and note that

\begin{array}[]{rcl}||S\xi||^{2}&=&||SP\xi+S(I-P)\xi||^{2}\\
\\
&\leq&\{||SP\xi||+||S(I-P)\xi||\}^{2}\\
\\
&\leq&2\{||SP\xi||^{2}+||S(I-P)\xi||^{2}\}\\
\\
&\leq&2\{\xi^{*}PS^{*}SP\xi+\xi^{*}(I-P)S^{*}S(I-P)\xi\}\\
\\
&\leq&2\xi^{*}\{PS^{*}SP+(I-P)S^{*}S(I-P)\}\xi\\
\\
&\leq&2\xi^{*}\xi=2||\xi||^{2}\end{array}

then we can conclude that
||S||\leq\sqrt{2}
.

Step 6:
Finally, as
S=XR^{-1}
, we have that
S^{-1}=RX^{-1}
and

\begin{array}[]{rcl}(S^{-1})^{*}S^{-1}&=&(X^{-1})^{*}R^{*}RX^{-1}\\
\\
&=&(X^{-1})^{*}R^{2}X^{-1}\\
\\
&=&(X^{-1})^{*}[PX^{*}XP+(I-P)X^{*}X(I-P)]X^{-1}\\
\\
&=&(X^{-1})^{*}PX^{*}XPX^{-1}+(X^{-1})^{*}(I-P)X^{*}X(I-P)X^{-1}\\
\\
\end{array}

then we can deduce, similarly as in the previous step, that

||S^{-1}||^{2}\leq||XPX^{-1}||^{2}+||X(I-P)X^{-1}||^{2}

and the result follows.
∎

A careful reading of the statement of Lemma
4.1.1
shows that given a fixed orthogonal projection
P
and any function
t\mapsto X(t)
of invertible matrices, there will be a collection of matrices
t\mapsto S(t)
satisfying the above identities and estimations. This prompt the following byproducts:

Corollary 4.1.1
.

Let
J\ni t\mapsto X(t)
be a fundamental matrix of the linear system
\dot{x}=A(t)x
and
P
be an orthogonal projection (i.e.,
P^{*}=P
), then there exists an invertible and differentiable (with derivable inverse) matrix
J\ni t\mapsto S(t)
such that

S(t)PS^{-1}(t)=X(t)PX^{-1}(t).

Moreover,
S(t)
satisfies the following properties:

||S(t)||_{2}\leq\sqrt{2}\,\,\textnormal{and}\,\,||S^{-1}(t)||_{2}\leq\sqrt{||X(t)PX^{-1}(t)||_{2}^{2}+||X(t)(I-P)X^{-1}(t)||_{2}^{2}}

and

(4.1.4)

\dot{S}(t)=A(t)S(t)-S(t)\dot{R}(t)R^{-1}(t).

Proof.

The existence of a matrix valued function
t\mapsto S(t)
satisfying the above identities and inequalities for any
t\in J
is a consequence of Lemma
4.1.1
. Moreover,
by following the lines of the previous proof, we know that
S(t)=X(t)R^{-1}(t)
,
where
R^{2}(t)P=PR^{2}(t)
and
R^{2}(t)
defined by:

(4.1.5)

R^{2}(t)=PX^{*}(t)X(t)P+[I-P]X^{*}(t)X(t)[I-P]>0.

The derivability of
X(t)
combined with (
4.1.5
) implies that
R^{2}(t)
is derivable on
J
. Now, we
will prove that
R(t)
is derivable on
J
. Firstly, let us recall that as
P
is hermitian it follows that
is diagonalizable and there exists a nonsingular and constant matrix
Z
such that
Z^{-1}PZ=D_{P}
.

On the other hand, as
R^{2}(t)P=PR^{2}(t)
, we know that
R^{2}(t)
and
P
have simultaneous diagonalization, we can assume that

Z^{-1}R^{2}(t)Z=\textnormal{Diag}\{a_{1}(t),\ldots,a_{n}(t)\},

that is,
R^{2}(t)
is diagonalizable
by a constant basis. As we know that
R^{2}(t)
is derivable on
J
it follows that
t\mapsto a_{i}(t)
are derivable for any
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
.

The property
R(t)>0
implies that

R(t)=Z\,\textnormal{Diag}\{\sqrt{a_{1}(t)},\ldots,\sqrt{a_{n}(t)}\}Z^{-1}

is well defined and derivable on
J
. Now, by Lemma
1.1.1
from Chapter 1, we have that
\dot{R}^{-1}(t)=-R^{-1}(t)\dot{R}(t)R^{-1}(t)
. Then, the derivability of
S(t)=X(t)R^{-1}(t)
follows and the identity (
4.1.4
)
can be deduced easily.
∎

Note that the equation (
4.1.4
) is related with the definitions of kinematical similarity stated
in the Chapter 1. In fact, the linear systems
\dot{x}=A(t)x
and
\dot{y}=\dot{R}(t)R^{-1}(t)y
will be generally kinematically similar provided that the matrix valued functions
t\mapsto S(t)
and
t\mapsto S^{-1}(t)
are bounded on
J
. This is the topic of the next section.

4.2.
Exponential Dichotomy and Reducibility

The main result of this section states that any linear system
having the property of exponential dichotomy is reducible. This allow us to revisit the
fact that any solution of a linear system having an exponential dichotomy on
J
can be decomposed in an asymptotically stable
component and an asymptotically unstable one since
the reducibility implies a decoupling in two systems having the same asymptotic behavior.

Theorem 4.2.1
.

Let
X(t)
be a fundamental matrix of
\dot{x}=A(t)x
, where
t\mapsto A(t)
is continuous on
J
. Moreover, assume the existence of an orthogonal and nontrivial projection
P
with rank
r
such that
t\mapsto||X(t)PX^{-1}(t)||_{2}
is
bounded on
J
. Then, the linear system is reducible to the diagonal block system

(4.2.1)

\dot{y}=\dot{R}(t)R^{-1}(t)y=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}B_{1}(t)&0\\
\\
0&B_{2}(t)\end{array}\right]y,

where
B_{1}(t)
has rank
r
while
B_{2}(t)
has rank
n-r
for any
t\in J
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, by using Lemma
2.2.1
from Chapter 2, we can consider
a fundamental matrix
X(t)
and a canonical projection
P_{r}
with rank
0<r<n
as follows:

X(t)=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}X_{11}(t)&X_{12}(t)\\
X_{21}(t)&X_{22}(t)\end{array}\right]\quad\textnormal{and}\quad P_{r}=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}I_{r}&0\\
0&0\end{array}\right]

with
X_{11}\in M_{r}(\mathbb{C})
,
X_{12}\in M_{r,n-r}(\mathbb{C})
,
X_{21}\in M_{n-r,r}(\mathbb{C})
and
X_{22}\in M_{n-r}(\mathbb{C})
.

The boundedness of
t\mapsto||X(t)P_{r}X^{-1}(t)||_{2}
on
J

implies the boundedness of
t\mapsto||X(t)[I-P_{r}]X^{-1}(t)||_{2}
, then Corollary
4.1.1
implies the existence of
S(t)=X(t)R^{-1}(t)
satisfying (
4.1.4
),
such that
S(t)
and
S^{-1}(t)
are bounded and derivable on
J
.

Secondly, it can be easily seen that the general kinematical similarity on
J
between (
4.1.1
) and (
4.2.1
) is a consequence of (
4.1.4
) combined with the transformation
y=S^{-1}(t)x
. Then, the Theorem will follows if we prove that
\dot{R}(t)R^{-1}(t)
is block diagonal.

In order to verify that
\dot{R}(t)R^{-1}(t)
is block diagonal,
let us recall that the identity (
4.1.5
) is considered with any orthogonal
projection
P
, in particular we can use
P_{r}
. In addition, note that

R^{2}(t)
can be represented as follows:

R^{2}(t)=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}X_{11}^{*}(t)X_{11}(t)+X_{21}^{*}(t)X_{21}(t)&0\\
0&X_{12}^{*}(t)X_{12}(t)+X_{22}^{*}(t)X_{22}(t)\end{array}\right],

which has blocks of dimensions
r
and
n-r
respectively.

By using the properties
R^{2}(t)>0
and
R(t)>0

combined with the fact that the submatrices of
R^{2}(t)
are also
positive (see
[
72
, p.13]
) it is easy to see that
R(t)
is also block diagonal with the same dimensions that
R^{2}(t)
. Then, this block diagonal property is also preserved by
R^{-1}(t)
and
\dot{R}(t)
and the reducibility follows.
∎

The next result is devoted to the subsystems of (
4.2.1
), namely

(4.2.2)

\dot{y}_{1}=B_{1}(t)y_{1}\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\dot{y}_{2}=B_{2}(t)y_{2}

and describes its relation with (
4.1.1
).

Corollary 4.2.1
.

If the linear system (
4.1.1
) has an exponential dichotomy on
J
with constants
K,\alpha>0
and projection

P
having rank equal to
r
with
0<r<n
, then

a)
If
Y_{i}(t)
is a fundamental matrix of
\dot{y}_{i}=B_{i}(t)y_{i}
with
i=1,2
, then
there exists
\tilde{K}>0
such that:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}||Y_{1}(t,s)||_{2}\leq\tilde{K}e^{-\alpha(t-s)}&\textnormal{for $t\geq s$ with $t,s\in J$}\\
||Y_{2}(t,s)||_{2}\leq\tilde{K}e^{-\alpha(s-t)}&\textnormal{for $t\leq s$ with $t,s\in J$}.\end{array}\right.

b)
The subsystems (
4.2.2
) have an exponential dichotomy on
J
with the trivial projections
P_{1}=I_{r}\in M_{r}(\mathbb{R})

and
P_{2}=0\in M_{n-r}(\mathbb{R})
respectively.

Proof.

As (
4.1.1
) has an exponential dichotomy on
J
, we have that

t\mapsto||X(t)PX^{-1}(t)||
is bounded on
J
. Then, by Theorem
4.2.1
we know that (
4.1.1
) is reducible to (
4.1.2
), which can be written as

(4.2.3)

\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\dot{y}_{1}\\
\dot{y}_{2}\end{array}\right)=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}B_{1}(t)&0\\
\\
0&B_{2}(t)\end{array}\right]\left(\begin{array}[]{c}y_{1}\\
y_{2}\end{array}\right).

On the other hand, as the reducibility is a particular case of kinematical similarity
and, as in the proof of the previous result, we are assuming that the exponential dichotomy
of (
4.1.1
) have positive constants
K,\alpha
and a canonical projection
P_{r}
, by
using Theorem
2.2.1
from Chapter 2 we have the existence of a fundamental matrix
Y(t)
for (
4.2.3
) and
\tilde{K}>0
such that

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}||Y(t)P_{r}Y^{-1}(s)||_{2}\leq\tilde{K}e^{-\alpha(t-s)}&\textnormal{for $t\geq s$ with $t,s\in J$}\\
||Y(t)[I-P_{r}]Y^{-1}(s)||_{2}\leq\tilde{K}e^{-\alpha(s-t)}&\textnormal{for $t\leq s$ with $t,s\in J$},\end{array}\right.

where
Y(t)
is a fundamental matrix of (
4.2.3
) such that
Y(t)=S^{-1}(t)X(t)
.

Let us recall that
S(t)=X(t)R^{-1}(t)
, then it follows that
Y(t)=R(t)
which is a diagonal block matrix, as we see in the proof of Theorem
4.2.1
. In consequence,
Y(t)
must have the form

Y(t)=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}Y_{1}(t)&0\\
\\
0&Y_{2}(t)\end{array}\right],

where
Y_{1}(t)
and
Y_{2}(t)
are a pair of fundamental matrices of the subsystems (
4.2.2
) respectively.

Then, by considering the stucture of the canonical projection
P_{r}
, it is easy to deduce that

Y(t)P_{r}Y^{-1}(s)=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}Y_{1}(t,s)&0\\
\\
0&0\end{array}\right]\,\,\textnormal{and}\,\,\,Y(t)[I-P_{r}]Y^{-1}(s)=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}0&0\\
\\
0&Y_{2}(t,s)\end{array}\right]

and we have that

||Y(t)P_{r}Y^{-1}(s)||_{2}=||Y_{1}(t,s)||_{2}\quad\textnormal{and}\quad||Y(t)[I-P_{r}]Y^{-1}(s)||_{2}=||Y_{2}(t,s)||_{2}

where the left terms
are norms for matrices of order
n
while the right terms are norms for matrices of orders

r
and
n-r
respectively and the statement a) is proved.

Finally, a direct consequence is that
the subsystems (
4.2.2
) have an exponential dichotomy on
J
with projections
P_{1}=I_{r}\in M_{r}(\mathbb{R})
and
P_{2}=0\in M_{n-r}(\mathbb{R})
respectively.

∎

A careful reading of the above results show us that any linear system
having exponential dichotomy with no trivial projection is reducible to
a block system, which can be decomposed in a stable system and a
unstable one.

4.3.
Comments and References

1)
As pointed out in the subsection 1.1, there exist a different property
of reducibility introduced by Lyapunov. In fact, in
[
80
, pp. 241–242]
, Lyapunov describes
the currently called Lyapunov transformations and says "
\ldots
The considered systems of differential equations are, by an appropriate choice of transformations, transformed in a system with constant coefficients. In this case, we will call such systems as
reducible
\ldots
"
1
1

1

Free translation of the authors.
. On other hand, we refer
to
[
26
]
where the kinematical similarity to a block system is treated.

2)
Lemma
4.1.1
has been stated in
[
41
, Lemma 1,p.39]
, we only provide
intermediate computations and additional estimations in order to facilitate its reading.
In particular, we use the fact that
R^{2}
and
P
have simultaneous diagonalization
as a tool to prove that if
R^{2}
and
P
commute then
R
and
P
also commute.

3)

Corollary
4.1.1
has been partially stated in
[
40
, Lemma 1, p.39]
, where
the derivability of
t\mapsto R(t)
is proved by using a result from
[
114
, Lemma 3.1]

while our proof uses the fact that
P
and
R^{2}(t)
have simultaneous diagonalization.

4)
There exist several works in the literature, such as
[
35
,
122
,
123
,
132
]
, that mentioned
[
40
, Lemma 1, p.39]
as a useful tool for diverse results into their respective works. We emphasize that in
[
122
]
makes a work different from W. Coppel due to the S. Siegmund deals with the more general class of systems with locally integrable
t-
dependence rather than systems with continuous
t-
dependence.

Exercises
