Let us consider the linear system (
4.1.1
) with
t\mapsto A(t)
continuous on
\mathbb{R}
and
\omega
–periodic (
4.1.1
). Prove that if (
4.1.1
) has Floquet
multipliers inside an outside the unit circle then it is is reducible to an autonomous system.

2.-

If (
4.1.1
) is reducible to (
4.1.2
), prove that
x^{\prime}=-A^{T}(t)x
is reducible to

\dot{y}=-\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}B_{1}^{T}(t)&0\\
\\
0&B_{2}^{T}(t)\end{array}\right]y

3.-

Prove that the system considered in the Example
1
from Chapter 2 is reducible and obtain an explicit representation.

Chapter 5
Exponential Dichotomy Spectrum

5.1.
Introduction

Given a matrix
A\in M_{n}(\mathbb{R})
, let us consider the following systems:

(5.1.1a)

\displaystyle\dot{x}=Ax,

(5.1.1b)

\displaystyle\dot{x}=Ax+f(t),

(5.1.1c)

\displaystyle\dot{x}=Ax+Bu(t),

where
f\in BC(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
B\in M_{n,p}(\mathbb{R})

and
u\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
is a measurable function.

The eigenvalues of
A
provides essential information about several properties of the above systems.

We know that (
5.1.1a
) is
hyperbolic
if and only if the eigenvalues
of
A
have non zero real part. In addition, (
5.1.1a
) is
uniformly asymptotically stable

if and only if the eigenvalues of
A
have negative real part.

We also know by Proposition
2.1.1
from Chapter 2 that if (
5.1.1a
) is hyperbolic,
then the system (
5.1.1b
) has the
admissibility
property, that is, for any
f\in BC(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R}^{n})
the system (
5.1.1b
) has bounded
continuous solutions.

The system (
5.1.1c
) is
controllable
at
t_{0}
if for any nontrivial initial condition
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, there exists a finite time
t_{1}>t_{0}
and a function
u_{0}\colon[t_{0},t_{1}]\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
, such that the solution
t\mapsto x(t,t_{0},x_{0},u_{0})
of (
5.1.1c
) with
u=u_{0}(\cdot)
verifies
x(t_{1},t_{0},x_{0},u_{0})=0
. A nice result
[
133
, Th.3.1]
states that the system (
5.1.1c
)
is controllable if and only if for any set of
\mathcal{S}\subset\mathbb{C}
composed by
n

arbitrary points, module conjugation, there exists
F\in M_{p,n}(\mathbb{R})
such that the eigenvalues of
A-BF
are exactly
\mathcal{S}
.

It is well known that there no exists an eigenvalues–based approach able to replicate the above results for the nonautonomous systems

(5.1.2a)

\displaystyle\dot{x}=A(t)x,

(5.1.2b)

\displaystyle\dot{x}=A(t)x+f(t),

(5.1.2c)

\displaystyle\dot{x}=A(t)x+B(t)u(t),

where
A\colon J\subseteq\mathbb{R}\to M_{n}(\mathbb{R})
and the interval
J
will be assumed either
\mathbb{R}
,
\mathbb{R}_{0}^{-}
or
\mathbb{R}_{0}^{+}
.

In order to fill this essential gap, several spectral theories have been developed
for the linear system (
5.1.2a
) and the definition proposed by Dieci
\&
Van Vleck in
[
46
, p.267]
encompasses the essential properties of these ones when considering
J=[0,+\infty)
:

Definition 5.1.1
.

A subset
\Sigma(A)\subset\mathbb{R}
it is a
spectrum
associated to (
5.1.2a
) if one or more of the following properties are verified:

(P1)

For
\Sigma(A)\cap(-\infty,0)\neq\varnothing
and
0\notin\Sigma(A)
, there exists
a non–zero bounded solution of (
5.1.2a
).

(P2)

For
0\notin\Sigma(A)
, there exists a bounded solution of
(
5.1.2b
) for any

f\in BC(J,\mathbb{R})\setminus\{0\}
.

(P3)

For
\Sigma(A)\cap(-\infty,0)\neq\varnothing
and
0\notin\Sigma(A)
, there exists
a non–zero bounded solution of (
5.1.2b
) for any
f\in BC(J,\mathbb{R})
.

We have to point out that if
A(t)\equiv A
, the set

\Sigma_{EIG}(A):=\left\{\lambda\in\mathbb{R}\colon\textnormal{$\lambda$ is the real part of an eigenvalue of $A$}\right\}

satisfies
(P1)–(P3)
. The
property
0\notin\Sigma_{EIG}(A)
is the
hyperbolicity
condition and
A
is a Hurwitz matrix if
\Sigma_{EIG}(A)\subset(-\infty,0)
, which is equivalent to the
uniform asymptotic stability
.

As we have seen in the Chapter 2, the property of exponential dichotomy
can be seen as a nonautonomous hyperbolicity, in this section we will construct a spectrum
\Sigma(A)
based in the property of exponential dichotomy.

5.2.
Definitions and basic results

5.2.1.
A formal definition

Let us consider the family of linear shifted systems:

(5.2.1)

\dot{x}=[A(t)-\lambda I]x

with
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
and notice that it has the following fundamental matrix:

X_{\lambda}(t)=X(t)e^{-\lambda t},

where
X(t)
is a fundamental matrix of (
5.1.2a
), then it is straightforward to introduce the next property:

Definition 5.2.1
.

The system (
5.2.1
) has an
exponential dichotomy
on
J
if there exist positive numbers
K_{\lambda}
,
\alpha_{\lambda}
and a projection
P_{\lambda}^{2}=P_{\lambda}
such that the fundamental matrix of (
5.2.1
) satisfies

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rl}\|X(t)e^{-\lambda t}P_{\lambda}X^{-1}(s)e^{\lambda s}\|\leq K_{\lambda}e^{-\alpha_{\lambda}(t-s)}&\textnormal{if}\,\,t\geq s,\,\,\,\textnormal{$t,s\in J$}\\
\|X(t)e^{-\lambda t}(I-P_{\lambda})X^{-1}(s)e^{\lambda s}\|\leq K_{\lambda}e^{-\alpha_{\lambda}(s-t)}&\textnormal{if}\,\,t\leq s,\,\,\,\textnormal{$t,s\in J$}.\end{array}\right.

The reader can deduce easily that, for any
t,s\in J
, the above property is equivalent to

(5.2.2)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rl}\|X(t)P_{\lambda}X^{-1}(s)\|\leq K_{\lambda}e^{\lambda(t-s)}e^{-\alpha_{\lambda}(t-s)}&\textnormal{if}\,\,t\geq s,\\
\|X(t)(I-P_{\lambda})X^{-1}(s)\|\leq K_{\lambda}e^{\lambda(t-s)}e^{-\alpha_{\lambda}(s-t)}&\textnormal{if}\,\,t\leq s.\end{array}\right.

Definition 5.2.2
.

The
exponential dichotomy spectra
of (
5.1.2a
) are defined by the sets:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\Sigma(A)&:=&\{\lambda\in\mathbb{R}\colon\textnormal{(\ref{ec:2.4}) has not an exponential dichotomy on $\mathbb{R}$}\},\\
\Sigma^{-}(A)&:=&\{\lambda\in\mathbb{R}\colon\textnormal{(\ref{ec:2.4}) has not an exponential dichotomy on $\mathbb{R}_{0}^{-}$}\},\\
\Sigma^{+}(A)&:=&\{\lambda\in\mathbb{R}\colon\textnormal{(\ref{ec:2.4}) has not an exponential dichotomy on $\mathbb{R}_{0}^{+}$}\}.\end{array}

In some works, the above spectrum
\Sigma(A)
is also known as the Sacker
\&
Sell spectrum since
it has been developed in 1978 by R.J. Sacker & G. Sell in
[
118
]
in a more general
framework of nonautonomous dynamics. As stated in
[
78
]
, the spectra

\Sigma^{-}(A)
and
\Sigma^{+}(A)
are also respectively known as
past dichotomy spectrum
and
future dichotomy spectrum
. On the other hand, it is important to emphasize that, in general, the above spectra are not the same.

The study of the properties of the above spectra is strongly related
with the properties of its complements, which will be defined as follows:

Definition 5.2.3
.

The
resolvents
associated to (
5.1.2a
) are the sets

\rho(A)=\mathbb{R}\setminus\Sigma(A),\quad\rho^{-}(A)=\mathbb{R}\setminus\Sigma^{-}(A)\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\rho^{+}(A)=\mathbb{R}\setminus\Sigma^{+}(A).

Note that if
\lambda_{0}\in\rho(A)
, then the exponential dichotomy property (
5.2.2
) is satisfied with
\lambda=\lambda_{0}
and
J=\mathbb{R}
. This is also verified when considering

\lambda_{0}\in\rho^{\pm}(A)
and
J=\mathbb{R}_{0}^{\pm}
.

Remark 5.2.1
.

By using the resolvent definition, it is straightforward to verify that

(5.2.3)

\Sigma^{+}(A)\subseteq\Sigma(A)\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\Sigma^{-}(A)\subseteq\Sigma(A).

In fact, by revisiting the equation
3.2.2
from Chapter 3, we can see that
0\notin\Sigma^{+}(a)
whereas
0\in\Sigma(a)
.

5.2.2.
Backwardly and forwardly bounded solutions of (
5.2.1
)

The study of the family of shifted linear systems (
5.2.1
) is also
essential to study of the above mentioned spectra and resolvents. Then, similarly as we have done in the previous chapters,
for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
we will introduce the sets:

\mathcal{S}^{\lambda}:=\left\{\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}_{0}^{+}}|X(t,0)\xi|e^{-\lambda t}<+\infty\right\}

and

\mathcal{U}^{\lambda}:=\left\{\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}_{0}^{-}}|X(t,0)\xi|e^{-\lambda t}<+\infty\right\}.

Remark 5.2.2
.

As the solution of (
5.2.1
) with initial condition
x(0)=\xi
is given by
t\mapsto X(t,0)e^{-\lambda t}\xi
. It is direct to see that
\mathcal{S}^{\lambda}
(resp.
\mathcal{U}^{\lambda}
) is the set of initial conditions
\xi
such that, the solutions of (
5.2.1
) passing trough
\xi
at
t=0
are bounded in
\mathbb{R}_{0}^{+}
(resp.
\mathbb{R}_{0}^{-}
).

Remark 5.2.3
.

Notice that:

a)

\mathcal{S}^{\lambda}
and
\mathcal{U}^{\lambda}
are
\mathbb{R}
–vector subspaces of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

b)

If
\lambda\leq\mu
, then
\mathcal{S}^{\lambda}\subseteq\mathcal{S}^{\mu}
,

c)

If
\lambda\leq\mu
, then
\mathcal{U}^{\mu}\subseteq\mathcal{U}^{\lambda}
,

d)

If
\lambda=0
, we recover the sets
\mathcal{V}=\mathcal{S}^{0}
and
\mathcal{W}=\mathcal{U}^{0}
defined by (
3.3.1a
)–(
3.3.1b
) in Chapter 3.

In case of
\lambda
is such that the shifted linear system (
5.2.1
) has an exponential dichotomy, the following lemmas – whose proof is left as an exercise – describe the strong relationship between the vector spaces
\mathcal{S}^{\lambda}
and
\mathcal{U}^{\lambda}
with the projection
P_{\lambda}
.

Lemma 5.2.1
.

If the shifted linear system (
5.2.1
) has an exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}_{0}^{+}

with projection
P_{\lambda}
, then
\mathcal{S}^{\lambda}=\textnormal{\text{Im}}P_{\lambda}
.

Lemma 5.2.2
.

If the linear shifted system (
5.2.1
) has an exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}_{0}^{-}
with projection
P_{\lambda}
, then
\mathcal{U}^{\lambda}=\ker P_{\lambda}
.

Corollary 5.2.1
.

If the linear system (
5.2.1
) has an exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}
with projection
P_{\lambda}
, then

\mathcal{U}^{\lambda}=\ker P_{\lambda}\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\mathcal{S}^{\lambda}=\textnormal{\text{Im}}P_{\lambda}.

If the shifted linear system (
5.2.1
) has an exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}_{0}^{+}
with projection
P_{\lambda}^{+}
and also has an exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}_{0}^{-}
with projection
P_{\lambda}^{-}

then

\mathcal{U}^{\lambda}=\ker P_{\lambda}^{+}\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\mathcal{S}^{\lambda}=\textnormal{\text{Im}}P_{\lambda}^{-}.

As we have seen when addressing the projector problem in the section 4 from Chapter 3, the projectors

P_{\lambda}^{+}
and
P_{\lambda}^{-}
will not be necessarily the same.

Lemma 5.2.3
.

If the shifted system (
5.2.1
) has an exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}_{0}^{-}
with projection
P_{\lambda}
and there exist another projection
\overline{P}
satisfying the properties

i)

There exists
K_{1}>0
such that

K_{1}=\sup_{t\in(-\infty,0]}\|X_{\lambda}(t)(I-\overline{P})X_{\lambda}^{-1}(t)\|,

ii)

\ker P_{\lambda}=\ker\overline{P}
,

then the system (
5.2.1
) has also an exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}_{0}^{-}
with projection
\overline{P}
.

Proof.

The proof is similar to the proof of Lemma
3.4.1
from Chapter 3.
∎

Lemma 5.2.4
.

If the shifted system (
5.2.1
) has an exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}_{0}^{+}
with projection
P_{\lambda}
and there exist another projection
P^{\prime}
satisfying the properties

i)

There exists
K_{2}>0
such that

K_{2}=\sup_{t\in[0,+\infty)}\|X_{\lambda}(t)P^{\prime}X_{\lambda}^{-1}(t)\|,

ii)

\textnormal{Im}P_{\lambda}=\textnormal{Im}P^{\prime}
,

then the system (
5.2.1
) has also an exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}_{0}^{+}
with projection
\overline{P}
.

Proof.

The proof is similar to the proof of Lemma
3.4.2
from Chapter 3.
∎

5.3.
Properties of the exponential dichotomy spectrum and the resolvent

5.3.1.
Topological Properties

Proposition 5.3.1
.

The resolvents
\rho(A)
,
\rho^{-}(A)
and
\rho^{+}(A)
of the linear
system (
5.1.2a
) are open sets in
\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

The proof will be made only for
\rho(A)
since the other resolvents
can be addressed similarly. The cases
\rho(A)=\varnothing
and
\rho(A)=\mathbb{R}
are direct. Now, let us assume that
\rho(A)
is a proper subset of
\mathbb{R}
and
\lambda\in\rho(A)
. Then, by Definition
5.2.1
, we have that the shifted system
\dot{x}=[A(t)-\lambda I]x
has an exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}
, namely, there exist a projection
P_{\lambda}
and two positive constants
K_{\lambda}
and
\alpha_{\lambda}
such that:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rlcr}\|X(t)e^{-\lambda t}P_{\lambda}X^{-1}(s)e^{\lambda s}\|&\leq K_{\lambda}e^{-\alpha_{\lambda}(t-s)}&\text{if}&t\geq s\\
\|X(t)e^{-\lambda t}(I-P_{\lambda})X^{-1}(s)e^{\lambda s}\|&\leq K_{\lambda}e^{-\alpha_{\lambda}(s-t)}&\text{if}&t\leq s.\end{array}\right.

Now, when considering
\mu\in(\lambda-\alpha_{\lambda},\lambda+\alpha_{\lambda})
, we will prove that the linear system

(5.3.1)

\dot{x}=[A(t)-\mu I]x

has an exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}
. Indeed, the inequality above is equivalent to

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rlcr}\|X(t)P_{\lambda}X^{-1}(s)\|&\leq K_{\lambda}e^{-(\alpha_{\lambda}-\lambda)(t-s)}&\text{if}&t\geq s\\
\|X(t)(I-P_{\lambda})X^{-1}(s)\|&\leq K_{\lambda}e^{-(\alpha_{\lambda}+\lambda)(s-t)}&\text{if}&t\leq s,\end{array}\right.

or also equivalently to

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rlcr}\|X(t)e^{-\mu t}P_{\lambda}X^{-1}(s)e^{\mu s}\|&\leq K_{\lambda}e^{-(\alpha_{\lambda}-\lambda+\mu)(t-s)}&\text{if}&t\geq s\\
\|X(t)e^{-\mu t}(I-P_{\lambda})X^{-1}(s)e^{\mu s}\|&\leq K_{\lambda}e^{-(\alpha_{\lambda}+\lambda-\mu)(s-t)}&\text{if}&t\leq s.\end{array}\right.

Now, let
\beta=\min\{\alpha_{\lambda}-\lambda+\mu,\alpha_{\lambda}+\lambda-\mu\}>0
and we can deduce that

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rlcr}\|X(t)e^{-\mu t}P_{\lambda}X^{-1}(s)e^{\mu s}\|&\leq K_{\lambda}e^{-\beta(t-s)}&\text{if}&t\geq s\\
\|X(t)e^{-\mu t}(I-P_{\lambda})X^{-1}(s)e^{\mu s}\|&\leq K_{\lambda}e^{-\beta(s-t)}&\text{if}&t\leq s,\end{array}\right.

and we can see that the linear system (
5.3.1
) has an exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}
with the same projection
P_{\lambda}
.

Finally, we can deduce that
(\lambda-\alpha_{\lambda},\lambda+\alpha_{\lambda})\subset\rho(A)
and the Proposition follows.
∎

A careful reading of the above proof show us that we worked with the same projection

P_{\lambda}
, this is a deeper property of the resolvents:

Corollary 5.3.1
.

For any
\lambda\in\rho(A),\rho^{-}(A),\rho^{+}(A)
, there exists
\delta_{\lambda}>0
such that for any
\mu\in(\lambda-\delta_{\lambda},\lambda+\delta_{\lambda})
, the system

\dot{x}=[A(t)-\mu I]x

has an exponential dichotomy with the same projection
P_{\lambda}
.

A direct consequence of the definition of resolvent sets is that the spectra
\Sigma(A)
,
\Sigma^{-}(A)
and
\Sigma^{+}(A)
are closed sets in
\mathbb{R}
. We can obtain
a sharper result provided that the linear system has the property of bounded growth
\&
decay
stated in the Chapter 1:

Proposition 5.3.2
.

Under the assumption that the linear system (
5.1.2a
) has the property of bounded growth
\&
decay on
\mathbb{R}
(resp.
\mathbb{R}_{0}^{-}
,
\mathbb{R}_{0}^{+}
) with
constants
K>0
and
\alpha\geq 0
, if the spectrum
\Sigma(A)
(resp.
\Sigma^{-}(A)
,
\Sigma^{+}(A)
) is non empty, then
\Sigma(A)
is a compact subset of
[-\alpha,\alpha]
when
\alpha>0
and
\Sigma(A)=\{0\}
when
\alpha=0
.

Proof.

As before, the proof will be made only for the spectrum
\Sigma(A)
and considering
\alpha>0
. The other ones can be obtained in a similar way.

It will be useful to note that

\Sigma(A)\subset[-\alpha,\alpha]
if and only

(-\infty,-\alpha)\cup(\alpha,+\infty)\subset\rho(A).

By Definition
1.2.3
of bounded growth
\&
decay from Chapter 1, we know that the transition
matrix of (
5.1.2a
) verifies

||X(t,s)||\leq Ke^{\alpha|t-s|}\quad\textnormal{for any $t,s\in\mathbb{R}$}.

Case a):
If
t\geq s
, we have that

||X(t,s)e^{-\lambda(t-s)}||\leq Ke^{(\alpha-\lambda)(t-s)}

and, we can see that if
\lambda>\alpha
, there exists
\delta>0
such that
\lambda=\alpha+\delta
, then we have that

\|X(t)e^{-\lambda t}X^{-1}(s)e^{\lambda s}\|\leq Ke^{-\delta(t-s)},

which implies that for any
\lambda>\alpha
, the system
\dot{x}=[A(t)-\lambda I]x
has an exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}
with projection
P=I
. This is equivalent to say that
(\alpha,+\infty)\subset\rho(A)
.

Case b):
If
t\leq s
, we have that

\|X(t)e^{-\lambda t}X^{-1}(s)e^{\lambda s}\|\leq Ke^{(\alpha+\lambda)(s-t)}

and, we can see that if
\lambda<-\alpha
, there exists
\delta>0
such that
\lambda=-(\alpha+\delta)
, then we can deduce that

\|X(t)e^{-\lambda t}X^{-1}(s)e^{\lambda s}\|\leq Ke^{-\delta(s-t)},

which implies that for any
\lambda<-\alpha
, the system
\dot{x}=[A(t)-\lambda I]x
has an exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}
with projection
P=0
. This is equivalent to say that
(-\infty,-\alpha)\subset\rho(A)
.

Summarizing, we have proved that

(-\infty,-\alpha)\cup(\alpha,+\infty)\subset\rho(A)
, and the Proposition follows.
∎

Remark 5.3.1
.

By following the lines of the proof of the above result, we can deduce that:

a)

If (
5.1.2a
) has the property of bounded growth on
\mathbb{R}
(resp.
\mathbb{R}_{0}^{-}
,
\mathbb{R}_{0}^{+}
) with constants
K>0
and
\alpha\geq 0
, if the spectrum
\Sigma(A)
(resp.
\Sigma^{-}(A)
,
\Sigma^{+}(A)
) is non empty, then

\Sigma(A)\subset(-\infty,\alpha].

b)

If (
5.1.2a
) has the property of bounded decay on
\mathbb{R}
(resp.
\mathbb{R}_{0}^{-}
,
\mathbb{R}_{0}^{+}
) with
constants
K>0
and
\alpha\geq 0
, if the spectrum
\Sigma(A)
(resp.
\Sigma^{-}(A)
,
\Sigma^{+}(A)
) is non empty, then

\Sigma(A)\subset[-\alpha,+\infty).

Notice that we explicitly assumed that the spectrum
\Sigma(A)
is a not empty set. We will see, as a consequence of next results, that the spectra cannot be
empty when the property of bounded growth
\&
decay is satisfied.

In case that the exponential dichotomy spectrum is bounded, we will introduce
the notation

(5.3.2)

\Sigma_{\min}=\min\Sigma(A)\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\Sigma_{\max}=\max\Sigma(A).

A direct consequence of the boundedness of
\Sigma(A)
is
that the resolvent
\rho(A)
is an unbounded set satisfying

(-\infty,\Sigma_{\min})\cup(\Sigma_{\max},+\infty)\subset\rho(A).

In particular, if the spectrum
\Sigma(A)
is bounded, the resolvent
\rho(A)
is not connected and has two unbounded connected components, namely,
(-\infty,\Sigma_{\min})
and
(\Sigma_{\max},+\infty)
. The characterization of the bounded connected components
will be carried out in the next subsection.

5.3.2.
Basic properties of the resolvents

In the last subsection we have seen that:

\bullet
The Corollary
5.3.1
states that for any
\lambda\in\rho(A),\rho^{-}(A),\rho^{+}(A)
, there exists a neighborhood
V_{\lambda}
of
\lambda

such that for any
\mu\in V_{\lambda}
, the linear system
\dot{x}=[A(t)-\mu\,I]x
has an exponential dichotomy with the same projection.

\bullet
If the linear system
\dot{x}=A(t)x
has a bounded exponential dichotomy spectrum, we can deduce that the resolvents
\rho(A)
and
\rho^{\pm}(A)
are neither connected nor bounded.

In this subsection we will explore the consequences of above properties in order to characterize
the spectra in terms of the connected components of its resolvents.

Proposition 5.3.3
.

If
\Sigma(A)\neq\varnothing
and
[\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}]\subset\rho(A)
, then the shifted systems

\dot{x}=[A(t)-\gamma_{1}I]x\text{ and }\dot{x}=[A(t)-\gamma_{2}I]x

have an exponential dichotomy on

\mathbb{R}
with projections
P_{\gamma_{1}}
and
P_{\gamma_{2}}
, which have the same rank.

Proof.

The proof will be made in several steps:

Step 1:
As
[\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}]\subset\rho(A)
, it follows by definition that shifted
systems
\dot{x}=[A(t)-\gamma_{i}I]x
have an exponential dichotomy on

\mathbb{R}
with projections
P_{\gamma_{i}}
with
i=1,2
.

Step 2: A covering of
[\gamma_{1},\gamma_{1}].

By Corollary
5.3.1
we know that for any
\lambda\in[\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}]\subset\rho(A)
, there exists
\delta_{\lambda}>0
such that
(\lambda-\delta_{\lambda},\lambda+\delta_{\lambda})\subset\rho(A)
. This implies that the family of the open sets
\{(\lambda-\delta_{\lambda},\lambda+\delta_{\lambda}):\lambda\in[\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}]\}
is an open covering of
[\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}]
.

By using the compactness of
[\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}]
, we can choose a finite covering

[\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}]\subseteq\bigcup_{i=1}^{m}(\lambda_{i}-\delta_{\lambda_{i}},\lambda_{i}+\delta_{\lambda_{i}}),

such that
\lambda_{1}<\lambda_{2}<\ldots<\lambda_{m}
and

(\lambda_{i}-\delta_{\lambda_{i}},\lambda_{i}+\delta_{\lambda_{i}})\cap(\lambda_{i+1}-\delta_{\lambda_{i+1}},\lambda_{i+1}+\delta_{\lambda_{i+1}})\neq\varnothing

for any
i\in\{1,\ldots,m-1\}
, where

\gamma_{1}\in(\lambda_{1}-\delta_{\lambda_{1}},\lambda_{1}+\delta_{\lambda_{1}})\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\gamma_{2}\in(\lambda_{m}-\delta_{\lambda_{m}},\lambda_{m}+\delta_{\lambda_{m}}).

Step 3: Study of the covering’s first interval.
Without loss of generality, we will assume that

\lambda_{1}-\delta_{\lambda_{1}}<\gamma_{1}<\lambda_{1}
. By statement b) of Remark
5.2.3
we have that

\mathcal{S}^{\mu}\subseteq\mathcal{S}^{\gamma_{1}}\subseteq\mathcal{S}^{\lambda_{1}}\quad\textnormal{for any $\lambda_{1}-\delta_{\lambda_{1}}<\mu<\lambda_{1}$}.

As the exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}
implies the exponential
dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}_{0}^{+}
, we can use the Lemma
5.2.1
, which leads to:

\dim\textnormal{Im}P_{\mu}\leq\dim\textnormal{Im}P_{\gamma_{1}}\leq\dim\textnormal{Im}P_{\lambda_{1}}\quad\textnormal{for any $\lambda_{1}-\delta_{\lambda_{1}}<\mu<\lambda_{1}$}.

By Corollary
5.3.1
we know that
P_{\mu}=P_{\lambda_{1}}
, which implies
that
\dim\textnormal{Im}P_{\gamma_{1}}=\dim\textnormal{Im}P_{\lambda_{1}}
, or equivalently,

P_{\lambda_{1}}
and
P_{\gamma_{1}}
have the same rank.

Step 4: Study of the covering’s last interval.
By following
the lines of the previous step, it can be proved easily that
P_{\lambda_{m}}
and
P_{\gamma_{2}}
have the same rank.

Step 5: End of proof.
Finally, when choosing

\mu\in(\lambda_{i}-\delta_{\lambda_{i}},\lambda_{i}+\delta_{\lambda_{i}})\cap(\lambda_{i+1}-\delta_{\lambda_{i+1}},\lambda_{i+1}+\delta_{\lambda_{i+1}}),

we have that
\dot{x}=[A(t)-\mu I]x
has an exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}
with
projections
P_{\lambda_{i}}
and
P_{\lambda_{i+1}}
. Then, similarly as before, we conclude
that
\text{Rank}\,P_{\lambda_{i}}=\text{Rank}\,P_{\lambda_{i+1}}
for any

i\in\{1,\ldots,m-1\}
and it follows that

\text{Rank}\,P_{\gamma_{1}}=\text{Rank}\,P_{\lambda_{1}}=\ldots=\text{Rank}\,P_{\lambda_{m}}=\text{Rank}P_{\gamma_{2}},

and the result follows.
∎

Remark 5.3.2
.

If
\Sigma^{+}(A)
and
\Sigma^{-}(A)
are non–empty and the property
[\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}]\subset\rho(A)
is verified, then the shifted systems

\dot{x}=[A(t)-\gamma_{1}I]x\text{ and }\dot{x}=[A(t)-\gamma_{2}I]x

also have an exponential dichotomy on

\mathbb{R}
with projections
P_{\gamma_{1}}
and
P_{\gamma_{2}}
having the same rank. Indeed:

a)

The proof for
\Sigma^{+}(A)
is identical since Lemma
5.2.1
still can be used.

b)

The proof for
\Sigma^{-}(A)
changes at the step 3, where it can be deduced that

\mathcal{U}^{\lambda_{1}}\subseteq\mathcal{U}^{\gamma_{1}}\subseteq\mathcal{U}^{\mu}\quad\textnormal{for any $\lambda_{1}-\delta_{\lambda_{1}}<\mu<\lambda_{1}$}.

Now, by using Lemma
5.2.2
, it follows in a similar ay that
\dim\ker P_{\lambda_{1}}=\dim\ker P_{\gamma_{1}}
, and by the Rank–Nullity Theorem we can see that

P_{\lambda_{1}}
and
P_{\gamma_{1}}
have the same rank. The rest of the proof follows as before.

The next result can be seen as a converse of the previous one

Proposition 5.3.4
.

If
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\in\rho(A)
are such that

\gamma_{1}<\gamma_{2}\quad\textnormal{with}\quad\textnormal{\text{Rank}}(P_{\gamma_{1}})=\textnormal{\text{Rank}}(P_{\gamma_{2}}),

then
[\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}]\subset\rho(A)
.

Proof.

The proof will be made in several steps:

Step 1:
Note that
\mathcal{U}^{\gamma_{2}}\subseteq\mathcal{U}^{\gamma_{1}}
is a consequence of
\gamma_{1}<\gamma_{2}
combined with Remark
5.2.3
. Now, the identity
\textnormal{Rank}(P_{\gamma_{1}})=\textnormal{Rank}(P_{\gamma_{2}})
implies that
\dim\ker(P_{\gamma_{1}})=\dim\ker(P_{\gamma_{2}})
and Lemma
5.2.2
says that
\dim(\mathcal{U}^{\gamma_{1}})=\dim(\mathcal{U}^{\gamma_{2}})
.

As
\mathcal{U}^{\gamma_{2}}
is a subspace of
\mathcal{U}^{\gamma_{1}}
having the same dimension, it follows that
\mathcal{U}^{\gamma_{2}}=\mathcal{U}^{\gamma_{1}}
and by using again Lemma
5.2.2

we have that
\ker P_{\gamma_{1}}=\ker P_{\gamma_{2}}
.

Step 2:
As
\gamma_{2}\in\rho(A)
, let us recall that the system

\dot{x}=[A(t)-\gamma_{2}I]x

has an exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}_{0}^{-}
, namely, there exist
a projection
P_{\gamma_{2}}
and a pair
of positive constants
K_{2}
and
\alpha_{2}
such that:

(5.3.3)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rlcr}\|X(t)P_{\gamma_{2}}X^{-1}(s)\|&\leq K_{2}e^{\gamma_{2}(t-s)}e^{-\alpha_{2}(t-s)}&\text{if}&t\geq s\\
\|X(t)(I-P_{\gamma_{2}})X^{-1}(s)\|&\leq K_{2}e^{\gamma_{2}(t-s)}e^{-\alpha_{2}(s-t)}&\text{if}&t\leq s.\end{array}\right.

Step 3:
At step 1 we verified that
\ker(P_{\gamma_{1}})=\ker(P_{\gamma_{2}})
. Now, by using Lemma
5.2.3

with
P_{\gamma_{2}}=\overline{P}
and
P_{\lambda}=P_{\gamma_{1}}
, we can see that

\dot{x}=[A(t)-\gamma_{1}I]x

has also an exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}_{0}^{-}
with projection
P_{\gamma_{2}}
:

(5.3.4)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rlcr}\|X(t)P_{\gamma_{2}}X^{-1}(s)\|&\leq K_{1}e^{\gamma_{1}(t-s)}e^{-\alpha_{1}(t-s)}&\text{if}&t\geq s\\
\|X(t)(I-P_{\gamma_{2}})X^{-1}(s)\|&\leq K_{1}e^{\gamma_{1}(t-s)}e^{-\alpha_{1}(s-t)}&\text{if}&t\leq s.\end{array}\right.

Step 4:
If
\gamma\in[\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}]
it is easy to verify that

e^{\gamma_{1}(t-s)}\leq e^{\gamma(t-s)}\text{ if }t\geq s

and

e^{\gamma_{2}(t-s)}\leq e^{\gamma(t-s)}\text{ if }s\geq t.

We will pair the first inequality of (
5.3.4
) and the second inequality of (
5.3.3
) by considering
K=\max\{K_{1},K_{2}\}
and
\alpha=\min\{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\}
:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rlcr}\|X(t)P_{\gamma_{2}}X^{-1}(s)\|&\leq Ke^{\gamma(t-s)}e^{-\alpha(t-s)}&\text{if}&t\geq s\\
\|X(t)(I-P_{\gamma_{2}})X^{-1}(s)\|&\leq Ke^{\gamma(t-s)}e^{-\alpha(s-t)}&\text{if}&t\leq s,\end{array}\right.

and we have that
\dot{x}=[A(t)-\gamma I]x
has an exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}_{0}^{-}
with projection
P_{\gamma_{2}}
which implies that
\gamma\in\rho^{-}(A)
and
[\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}]\subset\rho^{-}(A)
.

Step 5:
Similarly as done in step 1, it can be proved that
\mathcal{S}^{\gamma_{1}}=\mathcal{S}^{\gamma_{2}}
and the Lemma
5.2.1
implies the identity
\textnormal{Im}P_{\gamma_{1}}=\textnormal{Im}P_{\gamma_{2}}
. Then, by using
Lemma
5.2.4
we can replicate the steps 2, 3, and 4 and deduce that, for any
\gamma\in[\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}]
, the shifted system has an exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}_{0}^{+}

with the same projector
P_{\gamma_{2}}
.

Step 6:
We have proved that, for any
\gamma\in[\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}]
, the shifted system
\dot{x}=[A(t)-\gamma I]x
has an exponential dichotomy on the both half lines
with the same projection
P_{\gamma_{2}}
. Then by using Proposition
3.3.1
from Chapter 3,
the exponential dichotomy is verified on
\mathbb{R}
and the result follows.
∎

Remark 5.3.3
.

An attentive reading of the above result shows that the result is also true when considering

\rho^{-}(A)
and
\rho^{+}(A)
.

Corollary 5.3.2
.

If the linear system (
5.1.2a
) has the property of bounded growth
\&
decay on

\mathbb{R}
(resp.
\mathbb{R}_{0}^{-}
,
\mathbb{R}_{0}^{+}
) with constants
K>0

and
\alpha\geq 0
then its corresponding
spectrum
\Sigma(A)
(resp.
\Sigma^{-}(A)
,
\Sigma^{+}(A)
) is non empty.

Proof.

We will only consider the case
\Sigma(A)
since the other cases can be addressed
in a similar way. The proof will be made by contradiction. In fact, if we assume
\Sigma(A)=\varnothing
, it follows
that
\rho(A)=\mathbb{R}
. Then, by Proposition
5.3.3
we have that for any
\gamma_{1}<\gamma_{2}
it follows that
\textnormal{Rank}P_{\gamma_{1}}=\textnormal{Rank}P_{\gamma_{2}}
since

[\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}]\subset\rho(A)
.

On the other hand, by observing the proof of Proposition
5.3.2
and considering constants
\gamma_{1}<-\alpha

and
\gamma_{2}>\alpha
, we will have that
P_{\gamma_{1}}=0
and
P_{\gamma_{2}}=I
, which leads to

\textnormal{Rank}P_{\gamma_{1}}=0<n=\textnormal{Rank}P_{\gamma_{2}}
, obtaining a contradiction.
∎

In case that the exponential dichotomy spectrum
\Sigma(A)
has the bounded growth
\&
decay property, the following result provides
information about the unbounded connected subsets on
\rho(A)
, namely,

(-\infty,\Sigma_{\min})
and
(\Sigma_{\max},+\infty)
, where
\Sigma_{\min}

and
\Sigma_{\max}
are the upper and lower bounds of the spectrum described in (
5.3.2
).

Theorem 5.3.1
.

If the linear system (
5.1.2a
) has the property of bounded growth
\&
decay on
\mathbb{R}
(resp.
\mathbb{R}_{0}^{+}
,
\mathbb{R}_{0}^{-}
), it follows that:

i)

For any
\lambda<\Sigma_{\min}
, the shifted system
\dot{x}=[A(t)-\lambda I]x
has an exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}
(resp.
\mathbb{R}_{0}^{+}
,
\mathbb{R}_{0}^{-}
) with projection
P_{\lambda}=0
. That is, there exists
\delta>0
such that

(5.3.5)

\|X(t)e^{-\lambda t}X^{-1}(s)e^{\lambda s}\|\leq Ke^{-\delta(s-t)},\text{ for any }t\leq s.

ii)

For any
\lambda>\Sigma_{\max}
, the shifted system
\dot{x}=[A(t)-\lambda I]x
has an exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}
with projection
P_{\lambda}=I
. That is, there exists
\delta>0
such that

\|X(t)e^{-\lambda t}X^{-1}(s)e^{\lambda s}\|\leq Ke^{-\delta(t-s)},\text{ for any }t\geq s.

Proof.

We will only consider the case of
\mathbb{R}
since the other ones can be addressed similarly.
Now, as the linear system (
5.1.2a
) has a bounded growth
\&
decay on
\mathbb{R}
, by Proposition
5.3.2

we have the existence of
\alpha\geq 0
such that the spectrum verifies
\Sigma(A)\subset[-\alpha,\alpha]
or

\Sigma(A)=\{0\}
, as appropriate.

The proof will be made only for the case
\lambda>\Sigma_{\max}
while the other case is left to the reader. Firstly notice that if
\lambda\geq\alpha+\varepsilon
, with
\varepsilon>0
arbitrarily small,
the case a) from the proof of Proposition
5.3.2
says that (
5.3.5
) is verified, that is, the shifted system
\dot{x}=[A(t)-\lambda I]x

has an exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}
with projection
P_{\lambda}=I
.

Secondly, let
\lambda\in(\Sigma_{\max},\alpha+\varepsilon)
and consider
\gamma>\alpha+\varepsilon
.
Notice that as
[\lambda,\gamma]\subset(\Sigma_{\max},+\infty)\subset\rho(A)
, the Proposition
5.3.3
implies that
P_{\lambda}
and
P_{\gamma}=I
have the same rank and the result follows since
P_{\lambda}=I
.
∎

The above result has a converse one:

Theorem 5.3.2
.

Assume that the spectrum
\Sigma(A)
of the linear system (
5.1.2a
) is non empty and bounded. If:

a)

For any
\lambda\in(\Sigma_{\max},+\infty)
, the shifted system
\dot{x}=[A(t)-\lambda I]x

has an exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}
with projector
P_{\lambda}=I
,

b)

For any
\gamma\in(-\infty,\Sigma_{\min})
, the shifted system
\dot{x}=[A(t)-\gamma I]x

has an exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}
with projector
P_{\gamma}=0
,

then the linear system a bounded growth
\&
decay on
\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

Let us consider a fixed
\lambda>\Sigma_{\max}
, then there exist positive constants
K_{\lambda}

and
\alpha_{\lambda}
such that

||X_{\lambda}(t,s)||\leq K_{\lambda}e^{-\alpha_{\lambda}(t-s)}\quad\textnormal{for any $t\geq s$}.

By using
\alpha_{\lambda}>0
and considering
\Delta>0

the above estimation implies

\begin{array}[]{rcll}||X(t,s)||&\leq&K_{\lambda}e^{\lambda(t-s)}&\textnormal{for any $t\geq s$},\\
&\leq&K_{\lambda}e^{\max\{\Delta,\lambda\}|t-s|}&\textnormal{for any $t\geq s$}.\end{array}

Similarly, we will consider a fixed
\gamma<\Sigma_{\min}
, then there exist positive constants
K_{\gamma}

and
\alpha_{\gamma}
such that

||X_{\gamma}(t,s)||\leq K_{\gamma}e^{-\alpha_{\gamma}(s-t)}\quad\textnormal{for any $t\leq s$}.

As
\alpha_{\gamma}>0
, by choosing again
\Delta>0

the above estimation implies

\begin{array}[]{rcll}||X(t,s)||&\leq&K_{\gamma}e^{\gamma(s-t)}&\textnormal{for any $t\leq s$},\\
&\leq&K_{\gamma}e^{\max\{\Delta,\gamma\}|t-s|}&\textnormal{for any $t\leq s$}.\end{array}

By gathering the above estimations we conclude that

||X(t,s)||\leq Ke^{\beta|t-s|}\quad\textnormal{for any $t,s\in\mathbb{R}$}

with
K=\max\{K_{\lambda},K_{\gamma}\}
and
\beta=\max\{\Delta,\gamma\}+\max\{\Delta,\lambda\}
,
and the result follows.
∎

Remark 5.3.4
.

Let us observe that if, in the above result, only the hypotesis a) is verified,
we can deduce the property of bounded growth on
\mathbb{R}
. Analogously,
if only b) is verified we can deduce the property of bounded decay on
\mathbb{R}
.

5.3.3.
Structure of the resolvent and the spectrum

We know that the resolvents
\rho(A)
,
\rho^{+}(A)
and
\rho^{-}(A)
are open sets.
By using the results of the previous subsection, we will prove that this resolvents
are a finite union of open intervals.

Definition 5.3.1
.

Under the assumption that
\rho(A)
(resp.
\rho^{\pm}(A)
) is a non empty set. For any
\lambda\in\rho(A)

(resp.
\lambda\in\rho^{\pm}(A)
), the set
C_{\lambda}\subset\rho(A)
(resp.
C_{\lambda}\subset\rho^{\pm}(A)
) denotes the
maximal open interval
containing
\lambda
.

We can deduce several consequences of the above definition:

(i)

As Proposition
5.3.1
ensures that
\rho(A)
is an open set in
\mathbb{R}
, we know
that
C_{\lambda}\neq\varnothing
;

(ii)

C_{\lambda_{1}}\cap C_{\lambda_{2}}\neq\varnothing
if and only if
C_{\lambda_{1}}=C_{\lambda_{2}}
;

(iii)

Let us denote
\lambda_{1}\sim\lambda_{2}
if
C_{\lambda_{1}}=C_{\lambda_{2}}
and note that
this defines an equivalence relation on
\mathbb{R}
;

(iv)

If
\Sigma(A)
(resp
\Sigma^{\pm}(A)
) is lowerly bounded and
\lambda<\Sigma_{\min}
, it follows that
C_{\lambda}=(-\infty,\Sigma_{\min})
;

(v)

If
\Sigma(A)
(resp
\Sigma^{\pm}(A)
) is upperly bounded and
\lambda>\Sigma_{\max}
, it follows that
C_{\lambda}=(\Sigma_{\max},+\infty)
;

(vi)

If
\Sigma(A)=\varnothing
it follows that
C_{\lambda}=(-\infty,+\infty)
for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
.

The statement (iii) implies that
\rho(A)
can be partitioned by the equivalence classes
associated to equivalence relation
\sim
. Given
\lambda\in\rho(A)
is equivalence class
will be denoted by

[\lambda]:=\left\{\mu\in\rho(A)\colon\mu\sim\lambda\right\}

and the set of equivalence classes will be denoted by
\rho(A)/\sim
.

Lemma 5.3.1
.

Let
\lambda_{1}\in\rho(A)
and
\lambda_{2}\in\rho(A)
such that
\lambda_{1}<\lambda_{2}
, then
Rank
(P_{\lambda_{1}})\leq

Rank
(P_{\lambda_{2}})
. Moreover, the inequality is strict if
the respective sets
C_{\lambda_{1}}
and
C_{\lambda_{2}}
are disjoint.

Proof.

By using statements b) and c) of Remark
5.2.3
, we have that

\mathcal{S}^{\lambda_{1}}\subseteq\mathcal{S}^{\lambda_{2}}
and
\mathcal{U}^{\lambda_{2}}\subseteq\mathcal{U}^{\lambda_{1}}
. Now, by using Lemma
5.2.1

or Lemma
5.2.2
, we
obtain that
\dim\textnormal{Im}P_{\lambda_{1}}\leq\dim\textnormal{Im}P_{\lambda_{2}}
or
\dim\ker\,P_{\lambda_{2}}\leq\dim\ker\,P_{\lambda_{1}}
, which implies that
\text{Rank}(P_{\lambda_{1}})\leq\text{Rank}(P_{\lambda_{2}})
.

Firstly, under the assumption that
C_{\lambda_{1}}\cap C_{\lambda_{2}}\neq\varnothing

the above stated properties of the
C_{\lambda}
sets imply that
C_{\lambda_{1}}=C_{\lambda_{2}}
, then
we have that
[\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}]\subset C_{\lambda_{1}}\subseteq\rho(A)
.
Then, by using Proposition
5.3.3
we deduce that Rank
(P_{\lambda_{2}})=
Rank
(P_{\lambda_{1}})
.

Secondly, under the assumption that
C_{\lambda_{1}}\cap C_{\lambda_{2}}=\varnothing
, we will prove that Rank
(P_{\lambda_{1}})<
Rank
(P_{\lambda_{2}})
. Indeed, otherwise, if Rank
(P_{\lambda_{1}})=
Rank
(P_{\lambda_{2}})
. Hence, the Proposition
5.3.4
says that
[\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}]\subset\rho(A)
, obtaining a contradiction with
C_{\lambda_{1}}\cap C_{\lambda_{2}}=\varnothing
.
∎

Notice that the result is still valid when considering
\rho^{+}(A)
and
\rho^{-}(A)
. In fact, the
resolvents
\rho^{+}(A)
and
\rho^{-}(A)
can be addressed by using
\mathcal{S}^{\lambda_{1}}\subseteq\mathcal{S}^{\lambda_{2}}
and
\mathcal{U}^{\lambda_{2}}\subseteq\mathcal{U}^{\lambda_{1}}
respectively.

Theorem 5.3.3
.

If the linear system
\dot{x}=A(t)x

has the property of bounded growth
\&
decay on
\mathbb{R}
(resp.
\mathbb{R}_{0}^{-}
,
\mathbb{R}_{0}^{+}
), then the resolvent
\rho(A)
is the union of –at most–
n+1
open intervals, where
n
is the dimension of the system.

Proof.

By using Definition
5.3.1
, we note that

\rho(A)=\bigcup\limits_{\lambda\in\rho(A)}C_{\lambda}=\bigcup\limits_{[\lambda]\in\rho(A)/\sim}C_{\lambda}.

Let us denote
C_{[\gamma]}=(-\infty,\Sigma_{\min})
and
C_{[\mu]}=(\Sigma_{\max},+\infty)
for any
\gamma<\Sigma_{\min}
and
\mu>\Sigma_{\max}
.

This allows to deduce several properties of
\rho(A)
. Firstly, let us consider the function

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\phi&\colon&\rho(A)\to\{0,1,\ldots,n\}\\
&&\lambda\mapsto\phi(\lambda)=\textnormal{\text{Rank}}\,P_{\lambda}\end{array}

and, by Lemma
5.3.1
we have that
\phi
is nondecreasing and constant at each set
C_{[\lambda]}\subset\rho(A)
.

By Theorem
5.3.1
combined with the local properties of
\phi
, we can see that

a)

The statement i) from Theorem
5.3.1
implies that
\phi(\gamma)=0
for any
\gamma<\Sigma_{\min}
,

b)

the statement ii) from Theorem
5.3.1
implies that
\phi(\mu)=n
for any
\mu>\Sigma_{\max}
,

c)

by a) and b), we know that
\phi^{-1}(n)
and
\phi^{-1}(0)
are nonempty sets. On the other hand, for any
k\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}
either
\phi^{-1}(k)=\varnothing
or there exists an equivalence class
[\lambda]\in\rho(A)/\sim
such that

\phi^{-1}(k)=C_{\lambda},

which is unique due to Lemma
5.3.1
,

d)

by Lemma
5.3.1
we have that if
\lambda_{1}<\lambda_{2}
and
C_{\lambda_{1}}\cap C_{\lambda_{2}}=\varnothing
then
\phi(\lambda_{1})<\phi(\lambda_{2})
.

By summarizing this properties, we can conclude that

\rho(A)=\bigcup\limits_{i=0}^{n}\phi^{-1}(i),

that is,
\rho(A)
is the union of at most
n+1
open intervals.

When considering the cases of bounded growth
\&
decay on
\mathbb{R}_{0}^{-}

and
\mathbb{R}_{0}^{+}
, the proof can be addressed in a similar way and
it is left to the reader.
∎

The sets
\phi^{-1}(i)
constructed in the above proof, are open intervals
which usually are named
spectral gaps
in the literature.

Theorem 5.3.4

(Spectral Theorem – Special case)
.

If the linear system (
5.1.2a
) has the property of bounded growth
\&
decay
on
\mathbb{R}
, then

\Sigma(A)=[a_{1},b_{1}]\cup[a_{2},b_{2}]\cup\ldots\cup[a_{\ell-1},b_{\ell-1}]\cup[a_{\ell},b_{\ell}],

where
a_{1}\leq b_{1}<a_{2}\leq b_{2}<\ldots<a_{\ell}\leq b_{\ell}
with
1\leq\ell\leq n
.

Proof.

By Theorem
5.3.3
, we know that

\rho(A)
is a union of at most
n+1
open intervals defined by:

\rho(A)=\bigcup\limits_{i=0}^{n}\phi^{-1}(i),

where
\phi^{-1}(i)
is either
\varnothing
or an open interval. Then, as
\Sigma(A)=\rho(A)^{c}
it follows easily that

\Sigma(A)
is the union of at most
n
closed intervals.

Firstly, we will assume that
\phi^{-1}(i)\neq\varnothing
for all
i\in\{0,\ldots,n\}
. Then, we have that
\Sigma(A)
is the union of
n
closed sets:

\Sigma(A)=\bigcup\limits_{i=1}^{n}[a_{i},b_{i}]\quad\textnormal{where $[a_{i},b_{i}]=[\sup\phi^{-1}(i-1),\inf\phi^{-1}(i)]$}.

Secondly, we will assume that
\phi^{-1}(i)=\varnothing
for at least one index
i\in\{0,1,\ldots,n\}
. Then, the number of indexes
i\in\{0,1,\ldots,n\}
such that
\phi^{-1}(i)\neq\varnothing

will be
\ell<n
. In consequence, the number of indexes
i\in\{0,1,\ldots,n\}
such that
\phi^{-1}(i)=\varnothing
will be
k\geq 1
with
k+\ell=n+1
. Then we have that
\Sigma(A)
is the union of
\ell<n
closed sets:

\Sigma(A)=\bigcup\limits_{i=1}^{\ell}[a_{i},b_{i}].

∎

The above result deserves some remarks:

a) For
i=1,\ldots,\ell
, the intervals
[a_{i},b_{i}]
are called
spectral intervals
.

b) We will see in the next examples that some spectral intervals could be degenerate, that is,
a_{i}=b_{i}

since the identity
\sup\phi^{-1}(i-1)=\inf\phi^{-1}(i)
is possible.

c) When
\ell=n
, it is said that the system has the
full spectrum
property.

5.3.4.
Illustrative examples

In order to describe the above results, we will see
some examples.

First Example:
Let us consider the scalar linear differential equation

(5.3.6)

\dot{x}=a(t)x\quad\textnormal{with}\quad a(t)=\frac{1}{1+t^{2}},

and note that
X(t)=e^{\arctan(t)}
is a fundamental matrix. As
X(t)
is bounded on
\mathbb{R}
, the Corollary
2.3.1
from Chapter 2 implies that the above equation has not an exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}
and consequently
0\in\Sigma(a)
. Moreover, as
|a(t)|\leq 1
for any
t\in\mathbb{R}
, we know by Corollary

1.2.1
from Chapter 1 that the equation (
5.3.6
) has the property of
bounded growth
\&
decay with constants
K=\alpha=1
and the
Proposition
5.3.2
implies that
\Sigma(a)\subset[-1,1]
.

In order to study

(5.3.7)

\dot{x}=[a(t)-\lambda]x\quad\textnormal{with}\quad a(t)=\frac{1}{1+t^{2}},

let us recall that
X_{\lambda}(t)=e^{\arctan(t)-\lambda\,t}
. Then, for any
\lambda>0
and
t\geq s
it follows that

\begin{array}[]{rcl}|X_{\lambda}(t)X_{\lambda}^{-1}(s)|&=&e^{\arctan(t)}e^{-\arctan(s)}e^{-\lambda(t-s)}\leq e^{\pi}e^{-\lambda(t-s)},\end{array}

then (
5.3.7
) has an exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}
with projection
P_{\lambda}=1
for any
\lambda>0
, which implies that
(0,\infty)\subset\rho(a)
.

Similarly, it can be proved that for any
\lambda<0
and
t\leq s
, then

|X_{\lambda}(t)X_{\lambda}^{-1}(s)|\leq e^{\pi}e^{-|\lambda|(s-t)}

and (
5.3.7
) has an exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}
with projection
P_{\lambda}=0
. This implies that
(-\infty,0)\subset\rho(A)
and we can conclude that

\rho(a)=(-\infty,0)\cup(0,+\infty)\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\Sigma(a)=\{0\}.

In addition, by Remark
5.2.1
, we know that
\Sigma^{+}(a)\subseteq\Sigma(a)=\{0\}
and
it can be proved that
\Sigma^{+}(a)=\{0\}
. Indeed, otherwise, we will have that
0\in\rho^{+}(a)
and (
5.3.6
) would have and exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}_{0}^{+}
. Then,
by Theorem
2.3.1
and Remark
2.3.1
from Chapter 2 it follows that any nontrivial solution
x(t)=x(0)e^{\arctan(t)}
of (
5.3.6
) would have either exponential growth or exponential decay, obtaining a contradiction.

Second Example:
Let us consider the autonomous system

\dot{x}=Ax\quad\textnormal{where}\quad A=\left[\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&0&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&-1\end{array}\right].

On the one hand, we know that the exponential dichotomy spectrum
\Sigma(A)
is composed by the numbers
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\dot{x}=[A-\lambda I]x
has not an exponential dichotomy. On the other hand, by Lemma
2.2.3
from Chapter 2 it follows that
\dot{x}=[A-\lambda I]x
has an exponential dichotomy if and only if the eigenvalues of
A-\lambda I
have nonzero real part. Finally,
as
\Sigma(A-\lambda I)=\{1-\lambda,-1-\lambda\}
we can deduce that

\Sigma(A)=\{-1,1\}\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\rho(A)=(-\infty,-1)\cup(-1,1)\cup(1,+\infty).

In order to study the function
\phi\colon\rho(A)\to\{0,1,2,3\}

a)

If
\lambda>1
. We can prove that
\dot{x}=[A-\lambda I]x

has an exponential dichotomy with projection
P=I
. Then,
\phi(\lambda)=3

for any
\lambda>1
.

b)

If
\lambda<-1
. We can prove that
\dot{x}=[A-\lambda I]x

has an exponential dichotomy with projection
P=0
. Then,
\phi(\lambda)=0

for any
\lambda<-1
.

c)

If
\lambda\in(-1,1)
, it is easy to verify that the system

\dot{x}=[A-\lambda I]x
has an exponential dichotomy with the projection

P=\left[\begin{array}[]{ccc}0&0&0\\
0&0&0\\
0&0&1\end{array}\right].

and
\phi(\lambda)=1
for any
\lambda\in(-1,1)
.

Then we can conclude that

\phi^{-1}(0)=(-\infty,-1),\quad\phi^{-1}(1)=(-1,1),\quad\phi^{-1}(2)=\varnothing\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\phi^{-1}(3)=(1,\infty).

Third Example:
We will verify that the spectrum of the scalar equation

\dot{x}=a(t)x\quad\textnormal{with $a(t)=t$}

verifies
\Sigma(a)=\mathbb{R}
. In fact, note that if there exists some
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
such
that
\lambda\in\rho(a)
then

\dot{x}=(t-\lambda)x

has an exponential dichotomy with projection either
P_{\lambda}=1
or
P_{\lambda}=0
.

As any solution of the perturbed equation is of type

x_{\lambda}(t)=x_{\lambda}(0)e^{\frac{(t-\lambda)^{2}}{2}}
,
which is not exponentially decreasing to zero when
t\to+\infty
,
we can see that the perturbed equation cannot have an exponential
dichotomy with projection
P_{\lambda}=1
.

On the other hand, if the system has an exponential dichotomy
with the null projection
P_{\lambda}=0
, we will have the existence
of
K>0
and
\alpha>0
such that

\begin{array}[]{rcl}x_{\lambda}(t,s)&=&\displaystyle e^{\frac{(t-\lambda)^{2}-(s-\lambda)^{2}}{2}}=e^{\frac{(t-s)(t+s-2\lambda)}{2}}\leq Ke^{-\alpha(s-t)}\quad\textnormal{for $t\leq s$},\end{array}

which is equivalent to

(t-s)(t+s-\lambda)\leq 2\ln(K)-\alpha(s-t)\quad\textnormal{for $t<s$}

or

-(t+s-\lambda)\leq\frac{2\ln(K)}{s-t}-\alpha\quad\textnormal{for $t<s$}.

Now, letting
t\to-\infty
we will have that
+\infty<-\alpha
, obtaining
a contradiction and
\lambda\notin\rho(a)
for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
.

The last example will be written as a Lemma due to its importance.

Lemma 5.3.2
.

Let
a\colon\mathbb{R}_{0}^{+}\to\mathbb{R}
be a bounded continuous function such that

\beta^{-}(a):=\liminf\limits_{L,s\to+\infty}\frac{1}{L}\int_{s}^{s+L}a(r)\,dr\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\beta^{+}(a):=\limsup\limits_{L,s\to+\infty}\frac{1}{L}\int_{s}^{s+L}a(r)\,dr,

then spectrum of the scalar equation

\dot{x}=a(t)x

is described by
\Sigma^{+}(a)=[\beta^{-}(a),\beta^{+}(a)]
.

Proof.

See Proposition
A.0.3
from Appendix A.
∎

5.3.5.
The Spectral Theorem

An essential assumption of Theorem
5.3.4
was that the system (
5.1.2a
) has a property of bounded growth
\&
decay
which implies the boundedness of
\Sigma(A)
. If we drop this assumption by using the Remark
5.3.1

and following the lines of the proof of Theorem
5.3.4
we can prove the next result:

Theorem 5.3.5
.

Assume that the exponential dichotomy spectrum of linear system (
5.1.2a
) is non empty, then

a)

If (
5.1.2a
) has the property of bounded growth on
\mathbb{R}
then

\Sigma(A)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lc}&(-\infty,b_{1})\cup[a_{2},b_{2}]\cup\cdots\cup[a_{\ell},b_{\ell}]\\
&\textnormal{or}\\
&\hskip 14.22636pt[a_{1},b_{1}]\cup[a_{2},b_{2}]\cup\cdots\cup[a_{\ell},b_{\ell}].\end{array}\right.

b)

If (
5.1.2a
) has the property of bounded decay on
\mathbb{R}
then

\Sigma(A)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lc}&\hskip 11.38092pt[a_{1},b_{1}]\cup[a_{2},b_{2}]\cup\cdots\cup[a_{\ell},+\infty)\\
&\textnormal{or}\\
&[a_{1},b_{1}]\cup[a_{2},b_{2}]\cup\cdots\cup[a_{\ell},b_{\ell}].\end{array}\right.

c)

If (
5.1.2a
) has neither the property of bounded growth nor bounded decay on
\mathbb{R}
then

\Sigma(A)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lc}&\hskip-14.22636pt(-\infty,b_{1})\cup[a_{2},b_{2}]\cup\cdots\cup[a_{\ell},b_{\ell}],\\
\\
&\hskip 9.95863pt[a_{1},b_{1}]\cup[a_{2},b_{2}]\cup\cdots\cup[a_{\ell},+\infty),\\
\\
&(-\infty,b_{1}]\cup[a_{2},b_{2}]\cup\cdots\cup[a_{\ell},+\infty),\\
&\textnormal{or}\\
&[a_{1},b_{1}]\cup[a_{2},b_{2}]\cup\cdots\cup[a_{\ell},b_{\ell}].\end{array}\right.

Proof.

See the list of exercises.
∎

5.4.
Spectral Manifolds and Block Diagonalization

The main goal of this subsection is to prove that any linear system

\dot{x}=A(t)x
having a bounded growth
\&
decay on
\mathbb{R}
(resp.
\mathbb{R}_{0}^{+}
,
\mathbb{R}_{0}^{-}
) is generally kinematically similar to a block diagonal system

\dot{y}=\left[\begin{array}[]{cccc}B_{1}(t)&&&\\
&B_{2}(t)&&\\
&&\ddots&\\
&&&B_{\ell}(t)\end{array}\right]y.

In addition, notice that the above system can be seen as
\ell
uncoupled subsystems

\dot{z}=B_{i}(t)z
for any
i\in\{1,\ldots,\ell\}
. In this context it will be also proved that

\Sigma(B_{i})
is the
i
–th spectral interval of
\Sigma(A)
.

5.4.1.
Spectral manifolds

Before starting to work on the spectrum, let us recall that if
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
are vector subspaces of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we denote as uual

V_{1}+V_{2}:=\left\{z\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\colon z=v_{1}+v_{2}\quad\textnormal{where $v_{1}\in V_{1}$ and $v_{2}\in V_{2}$}\right\}.

In addition, the following Lemma will be useful to deduce interesting properties of the spectrum:

Lemma 5.4.1
.

Let
E,F
and
G
be subspaces of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
with
G\subset E
, then

E\cap(F+G)=(E\cap F)+G.

Proof.

Firstly, we will prove that
(E\cap F)+G\subseteq E\cap(F+G):

If
x\in(E\cap F)+G
, then

x=z+g,\text{ where }z\in E\cap F\text{ and }g\in G.

We have to prove that
x\in F+G
and
x\in E
. Notice that
x\in F+G
follows directly from the above identity
since
z\in E\cap F\subset F
and
g\in G
. Now, as
g\in G\subset E
,
z\in E\cap F\subset E
, and
E
is a subspace, we have that
x=z+g\in E
and we conclude that
x\in E\cap(F+G)
.

Secondly, we will prove that
E\cap(F+G)\subseteq(E\cap F)+G
:

If
x\in E\cap(F+G)
, it follows that
x\in E
and
x=f+g
where
f\in F
and
g\in G
, which implies that

x-g=f\in F
. As
g\in G\subset E
and
x\in E
, we also have
x-g\in E\cap F
. This fact combined with
g\in G
implies that
x\in(E\cap F)+G
and the Lemma follows.
∎

Theorem 5.4.1
.

Let us assume that the linear system
\dot{x}=A(t)x
has an exponential dichotomy spectrum
described by

\Sigma(A)=[a_{1},b_{1}]\cup[a_{2},b_{2}]\cup\ldots\cup[a_{\ell},b_{\ell}],

where
a_{1}\leq b_{1}<a_{2}\leq b_{2}<\ldots<b_{\ell-1}<a_{\ell}\leq b_{\ell}
and
\ell\leq n
.

Let us consider the trivial projections
P_{\gamma_{0}}=0
and
P_{\gamma_{\ell}}=I
, where

\gamma_{0}\in(-\infty,a_{1})
and
\gamma_{\ell}=(b_{\ell},+\infty)
. Moreover, for

i\in\{1,\ldots,\ell-1\}
, choose
\gamma_{i}\in(b_{i},a_{i+1})
and projections
P_{\gamma_{i}}
. Then, the sets

\mathcal{W}_{i}=\textnormal{Im}(P_{\gamma_{i}})\cap\ker(P_{\gamma_{i-1}})

are subspaces of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that

\mathcal{W}_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathcal{W}_{\ell}=\mathbb{R}^{n}.

Proof.

Firstly, we will prove that

\mathcal{W}_{i}\cap\mathcal{W}_{j}=0\text{ for any }1\leq i\neq j\leq\ell\leq n.

As
\gamma_{0}<\gamma_{1}<\gamma_{2}<\cdots<\gamma_{\ell}
the statement c) from Remark
5.2.3
says that

\mathcal{U}^{\gamma_{\ell}}\subset\cdots\subset\mathcal{U}^{\gamma_{2}}\subset\mathcal{U}^{\gamma_{1}}\subset\mathcal{U}^{\gamma_{0}},

and Lemma
5.2.2
implies

\ker(P_{\gamma_{\ell}})\subset\cdots\subset\ker(P_{\gamma_{2}})\subset\ker(P_{\gamma_{1}}).

As
i\neq j
, we have thar either
i\geq j+1
or
i\leq j-1
. Without loss of generality, let us assume that
i\leq j-1
. Notice that

\mathcal{W}_{i}=\text{Im}(P_{\gamma_{i}})\cap\ker(P_{\gamma_{i-1}})\subseteq\text{Im}(P_{\gamma_{i}})

and
\ker(P_{\gamma_{j-1}})\subset\ker(P_{\gamma_{i}})
, then we can deduce

\mathcal{W}_{j}=\text{Im}(P_{\gamma_{j}})\cap\ker(P_{\gamma_{j-1}})\subseteq\ker(P_{\gamma_{j-1}})\subseteq\ker(P_{\gamma_{i}}).

The above properties imply that

\mathcal{W}_{i}\cap\mathcal{W}_{j}\subseteq\ker(P_{\gamma_{i}})\cap\textnormal{Im}(P_{\gamma_{i}})=0.

Secondly, we will prove that

\mathcal{W}_{1}+\cdots+\mathcal{W}_{\ell}=\mathbb{R}^{n}.

By using the fact that
\ker(P_{\gamma_{0}})=\mathbb{R}^{n}
we note that

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{n}

\displaystyle=\text{Im}(P_{\gamma_{1}})+\ker(P_{\gamma_{1}})

\displaystyle=[\text{Im}(P_{\gamma_{1}})\cap\ker(P_{\gamma_{0}})]+\ker(P_{\gamma_{1}})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{W}_{1}+\ker(P_{\gamma_{1}})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{W}_{1}+\{\ker P_{\gamma_{1}}\cap[\text{Im}(P_{\gamma_{2}})+\ker(P_{\gamma_{2}})]\}.

By using Lemma 4.1 with
E=\ker(P_{\gamma_{1}}),F=\text{Im}(P_{\gamma_{2}})
and
G=\ker(P_{\gamma_{2}})
combined with
G=\ker(P_{\gamma_{2}})\subset\ker(P_{\gamma_{1}})=E
, it follows that

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{n}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{W}_{1}+\{[\ker(P_{\gamma_{1}})\cap\text{Im}(P_{\gamma_{2}})]+\ker(P_{\gamma_{2}})\}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{W}_{1}+\mathcal{W}_{2}+\ker(P_{\gamma_{2}})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{W}_{1}+\mathcal{W}_{2}+\ker(P_{\gamma_{2}})\cap[\text{Im}(P_{\gamma_{3}})+\ker(P_{\gamma_{3}})]

and the identity
\mathcal{W}_{1}+\ldots+\mathcal{W}_{\ell}=\mathbb{R}^{n}
can be proved recursively.
∎

Remark 5.4.1
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
5.4.1
and supposing that
\dot{x}=A(t)x
has an exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}
, it follows by definition that
0\notin\Sigma(A)
and consequently
0
must be an element of some spectral gap. By the Theorem
5.3.1
, we will consider three cases:

a) If
0\in(b_{\ell},+\infty)
, or equivalently
\Sigma(A)\subset(-\infty,0)
, then the linear system has an exponential dichotomy with the trivial projection
P=I
and

||X(t,s)||\leq Ke^{-\alpha(t-s)}\quad\textnormal{with $t\geq s$}.

b) If
0\in(-\infty,a_{1})
, or equivalently
\Sigma(A)\subset(0,+\infty)
, then the linear system has an exponential dichotomy with the trivial projection
P=0
and

||X(t,s)||\leq Ke^{-\alpha(s-t)}\quad\textnormal{with $s\geq t$}.

c) If
0\in(b_{i-1},a_{i})
for some
i=2,\ldots,\ell
, or equivalently

\bigcup\limits_{j=1}^{i-1}[a_{j},b_{j}]\subset(-\infty,0)\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\bigcup\limits_{j=i}^{\ell}[a_{j},b_{j}]\subset(0,\infty),

then the linear system has an exponential dichotomy with a nontrivial projection
P
having
Rank
(P)=\text{Rank}(P_{\gamma})
for any

\gamma\in(b_{i-1},a_{i})
. Moreover, there exists

i-1
spectral intervals to the left of
0
and
\ell-i+1
spectral intervals to the right.

5.4.2.
A Block Diagonalization Result

The following Lemmas will provide useful tools for the block diagonalization
result.

Lemma 5.4.2
.

If the system
\dot{x}=A(t)x
has bounded spectrum

\Sigma(A)=[a_{1},b_{1}]\cup[a_{2},b_{2}]\cup\cdots\cup[a_{\ell-1},b_{\ell-1}]\cup[a_{\ell},b_{\ell}],

and
\lambda\notin\Sigma(A)
, then it follows that

\Sigma(A-\lambda I)=[a_{1}-\lambda,b_{1}-\lambda]\cup[a_{2}-\lambda,b_{2}-\lambda]\cup\ldots\cup[a_{\ell-1}-\lambda,a_{\ell-1}-\lambda]\cup[a_{\ell}-\lambda,b_{\ell}-\lambda].

Proof.

By definition of spectrum, we know that

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\Sigma(A-\lambda I)&=&\left\{\mu\in\mathbb{R}:\dot{x}=[A(t)-(\lambda+\mu)I]x\text{ has not an exponential dichotomy on $\mathbb{R}$}\right\}\\
\\
&=&\left\{\mu\in\mathbb{R}:\lambda+\mu\in\Sigma(A)\right\}.\end{array}

Notice that

\displaystyle\mu\in\Sigma(A-\lambda I)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow(\mu+\lambda)\in\Sigma(A)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow a_{j}\leq\mu+\lambda\leq b_{j}\text{ for some}\,j=1,\ldots,\ell

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow a_{j}-\lambda\leq\mu\leq b_{j}-\lambda,

and the results follows.
∎

Lemma 5.4.3
.

If the systems

(5.4.1)

\dot{x}=A(t)x\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\dot{y}=B(t)y

are generally kinematically similar, then
the systems

(5.4.2)

\dot{x}=[A(t)-\lambda I]x\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\dot{y}=[B(t)-\lambda I]y

are also generally kinematically similar for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

As the systems (
5.4.1
) are generally kinematically similar, by Definition

1.3.3
from Chapter 1 there exists a matrix
Q(t)
which verifies the matrix differential equation
\dot{Q}(t)=A(t)Q(t)-Q(t)B(t)
and the change of variables
y=Q^{-1}(t)x
transforms the first system of (
5.4.1
) into the second one.

Now, notice that

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\dot{Q}(t)&=&A(t)Q(t)-\lambda Q(t)I-Q(t)B(t)+\lambda Q(t)I\\
&=&\{A(t)-\lambda I\}Q(t)-Q(t)\{B(t)-\lambda I\}\end{array}

and we can easily verify that the change of variable
y=Q^{-1}(t)x
transforms the first system of (
5.4.2
) into the second one.
∎

Lemma 5.4.4
.

If the systems (
5.4.1
) are generally kinematically similar, then its spectra are the same, namely,
\Sigma(A)=\Sigma(B)
.

Proof.

Firstly, by hypothesis combined with Lemma
5.4.3
we know that the systems (
5.4.2
) are generally kinematically similar. Secondly, as the kinematical similarity is a symmetric relation,
the Theorem
2.2.1
from Chapter 2 implies that
\lambda\in\rho(A)
if and only if
\lambda\in\rho(B)
, which is equivalent to
\Sigma(A)=\Sigma(B)
and the Theorem follows.
∎

If
\dot{x}=A(t)x
has an exponential dichotomy
with non trivial projection, from Corollary
4.2.1
from Chapter 4,
we know that this linear system is generally kinematically similar to

(5.4.3)

\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\dot{y}_{1}\\
\dot{y}_{2}\end{array}\right)=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}B_{1}(t)&0\\
\\
0&B_{2}(t)\end{array}\right]\left(\begin{array}[]{c}y_{1}\\
y_{2}\end{array}\right),

where
B_{1}(t)\in M_{n_{1}}(\mathbb{R})
,
B_{2}(t)\in M_{n_{2}}(\mathbb{R})

and
n_{1}+n_{2}=n
. Moreover, Lemma
5.4.4
implies that both systems have the same exponential
dichotomy spectrum, namely

\Sigma(A)=\Sigma\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}B_{1}(t)&0\\
0&B_{2}(t)\end{array}\right).

The next result provides a sharper characterization of

\Sigma(A)
in terms of the spectra
\Sigma(B_{1})
and
\Sigma(B_{2})

of the subsystems

(5.4.4)

\dot{y}_{1}=B_{1}(t)y_{1}\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\dot{y}_{2}=B_{2}(t)y_{2}.

Lemma 5.4.5
.

If
\dot{x}=A(t)x
has bounded spectrum

\Sigma(A)=[a_{1},b_{1}]\cup[a_{2},b_{2}]\cup\ldots\cup[a_{\ell},b_{\ell}]

and also has an exponential dichotomy with non trivial projection, then it follows that:

a)

The exponential dichotomy spectrum
\Sigma(A)
and the exponential dichotomy spectrum of the system (
5.4.3
) verify

(5.4.5)

\Sigma(A)=\Sigma\left(\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}B_{1}&0\\
0&B_{2}\end{array}\right]\right)=\Sigma(B_{1})\cup\Sigma(B_{2});

b)

There exists a spectral gap
(b_{j-1},a_{j})\subset\rho(A)

containing
0
such that

\Sigma(B_{1})=[a_{1},b_{1}]\cup\cdots\cup[a_{j-1},b_{j-1}]\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\Sigma(B_{2})=[a_{j},b_{j}]\cup\cdots\cup[a_{\ell},b_{\ell}].

Proof.

As we stated above, the first identity of (
5.4.5
) follows from Lemma
5.4.4
combined with the reducibility property. Then, we only
need to prove that

\Sigma\left(\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}B_{1}&0\\
0&B_{2}\end{array}\right]\right)=\Sigma(B_{1})\cup\Sigma(B_{2}).

Firstly, the property

\Sigma\left(\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}B_{1}&0\\
0&B_{2}\end{array}\right]\right)\subset\Sigma(B_{1})\cup\Sigma(B_{2}).

will be proved by contradiction. In fact, if we assume that

\lambda\in\Sigma\left(\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}B_{1}&0\\
0&B_{2}\end{array}\right]\right)\quad\textnormal{but}\quad\lambda\notin\Sigma(B_{1})\cup\Sigma(B_{2}).

we will have that

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\lambda\notin\Sigma(B_{1})\cup\Sigma(B_{2})&\Rightarrow&\lambda\in[\Sigma(B_{1})\cup\Sigma(B_{2})]^{c}\\
\\
&\Rightarrow&\lambda\in[\Sigma(B_{1})]^{c}\cap[\Sigma(B_{2})]^{c}\\
\\
&\Rightarrow&\lambda\in\rho(B_{1})\cap\rho(B_{2})\end{array}

which implies that
\lambda
is such that the linear systems

\dot{y}_{1}=[B_{1}(t)-\lambda I_{n_{1}}]y_{1}\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\dot{y}_{2}=[B_{2}(t)-\lambda I_{n_{2}}]y_{2}

have an exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}
with positive constants
K_{i}
,
\alpha_{i}

(
i=1,2
) and
projections
P_{1}\in M_{n_{1}}(\mathbb{R})

and
P_{2}\in M_{n_{2}}(\mathbb{R})
(
n_{1}+n_{2}=n
) respectively. Namely

(5.4.6)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}||Y_{1}(t)e^{-\lambda t}P_{1}Y_{1}^{-1}(s)e^{\lambda s}||\leq K_{1}e^{-\alpha_{1}(t-s)}&\textnormal{for $t\geq s$}\\
||Y_{1}(t)e^{-\lambda t}[I_{n_{1}}-P_{1}]Y_{1}^{-1}(s)e^{\lambda s}||\leq K_{1}e^{-\alpha_{1}(s-t)}&\textnormal{for $s\geq t$},\end{array}\right.

and

(5.4.7)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}||Y_{2}(t)e^{-\lambda t}P_{2}Y_{2}^{-1}(s)e^{\lambda s}||\leq K_{2}e^{-\alpha_{2}(t-s)}&\textnormal{for $t\geq s$}\\
||Y_{2}(t)e^{-\lambda t}[I_{n_{2}}-P_{2}]Y_{2}^{-1}(s)e^{\lambda s}||\leq K_{2}e^{-\alpha_{2}(s-t)}&\textnormal{for $s\geq t$},\end{array}\right.

where
Y_{1}
and
Y_{2}
are the respective fundamental matrices of
(
5.4.4
). Now, let us define

Y_{\lambda}(t)=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}Y_{1}(t)e^{-\lambda t}&0\\
0&Y_{2}(t)e^{-\lambda t}\end{array}\right]\quad\textnormal{and}\quad P_{0}=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}P_{1}&0\\
0&P_{2}\end{array}\right].

By following an approach similar to the carried out in
the proof of Corollary
4.2.1
from Chapter 4, it can be verified that
Y_{\lambda}(t)
is a fundamental matrix
of

(5.4.8)

\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\dot{y}_{1}\\
\dot{y}_{2}\end{array}\right)=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}B_{1}(t)-\lambda I_{n_{1}}&0\\
\\
0&B_{2}(t)-\lambda I_{n_{2}}\end{array}\right]\left(\begin{array}[]{c}y_{1}\\
y_{2}\end{array}\right).

Morever, by using (
5.4.6
)–(
5.4.7
) it can be deduced
that the above system has an exponential dichotomy with projection

P_{0}
and positive constants
K=\max\{K_{1},K_{2}\}
and
\alpha=\min\{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\}
, which implies that

\lambda\notin\Sigma\left(\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}B_{1}&0\\
0&B_{2}\end{array}\right]\right),

obtaining a contradiction and the first contention follows.

Secondly, to prove that

\Sigma(B_{1})\cup\Sigma(B_{2})\subset\Sigma\left(\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}B_{1}&0\\
0&B_{2}\end{array}\right]\right),

we will prove the equivalent property

\rho(A)=\rho\left(\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}B_{1}&0\\
0&B_{2}\end{array}\right]\right)\subset\rho(B_{1})\cap\rho(B_{2}).

Now, if
\lambda\in\rho(A)
, it must be an element of some spectral gap.
Without loss of generality, we will assume that

\lambda\in(b_{i-1},a_{i})\subset\rho(A)=\rho\left(\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}B_{1}&0\\
0&B_{2}\end{array}\right]\right),

and we have that (
5.4.8
) has an exponential dichotomy with
non trivial projection since
(b_{i-1}-\lambda,a_{i}-\lambda)
is a spectral
gap of (
5.4.8
) containing
0
. Then, we can we can apply Corollary

4.2.1
from Chapter 4 to system (
5.4.8
) with
\lambda\in]b_{i-1},a_{i}[

and we will obtain that

\dot{y}_{1}=[B_{1}(t)-\lambda I_{n_{1}}]y_{1}\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\dot{y}_{2}=[B_{2}(t)-\lambda I_{n_{2}}]y_{2}

have an exponential dichotomy, then
\lambda\in\rho(B_{1})\cap\rho(B_{2})

and the second contention follows, as well as the statement a).

To prove the statement b), as we know that
\dot{x}=A(t)x
has
an exponential dichotomy with non trivial projection, then by Remark
5.4.1
-c) it follows that
there exists
j<\ell
such that
0\in(b_{j-1},a_{j})
and then
\Sigma(A)
verifies

[a_{1},b_{1}]\cup\cdots\cup[a_{j-1},b_{j-1}]\subset(-\infty,0).

Moreover, we can use the statement b) from Corollary
4.2.1
of Chapter 4, which implies that
\dot{y}_{1}=B_{1}(t)y_{1}
and
\dot{y}_{2}=B_{2}(t)y_{2}

has an exponential dichotomy with trivial projections
I_{n_{1}}
and
0_{n_{2}}
respectively, then by Remark
5.4.1
-a) and
5.4.1
-b) we can see that
\Sigma(B_{1})\subset(-\infty,0)
and
\Sigma(B_{2})\subset(0,+\infty)
.

Finally, as
\Sigma(A)=\Sigma(B_{1})\cup\Sigma(B_{2})
, it follows that

[a_{1},b_{1}]\cup\cdots\cup[a_{j-1},b_{j-1}]=\Sigma(B_{1})\subset(-\infty,0)

and the result follows.
∎

Theorem 5.4.2
.

For any linear system (
1.1.1
) with bounded
spectrum of
\ell
intervals
\{[a_{i},b_{i}]\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
, then there exist
\ell
matrices
B_{i}(t)
where
\Sigma(B_{i})=[a_{i},b_{i}]
such that (
1.1.1
) is generally kinematically similar to the system

\dot{x}=\left[\begin{array}[]{cccc}B_{1}(t)&&&\\
&B_{2}(t)&&\\
&&\ddots&\\
&&&B_{\ell}(t)\end{array}\right]x.

Proof.

The proof will be divided in several steps:

Step 1:
Let us choose
\lambda_{\ell}\in(b_{\ell-1},a_{\ell})
, then, the linear system

(5.4.9)

\dot{x}=[A(t)-\lambda_{\ell}I]x

has an exponential dichotomy with projection
P_{\lambda_{\ell}}
with Rank
(P_{\lambda_{\ell}})=m_{\ell}<n
.

Moreover, as
0\in(b_{\ell-1}-\lambda_{\ell},a_{\ell}-\lambda_{\ell})
, Lemma

5.4.5
applied to the system (
5.4.9
) implies that it is reducible to

(5.4.10)

\dot{y}(t)=\left[\begin{matrix}B_{1}^{*}(t)&0\\
0&B_{\ell}^{*}(t)\end{matrix}\right]y(t),

where
B_{1}^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{m_{\ell}\times m_{\ell}}
and
B_{\ell}^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{(n-m_{\ell})\times(n-m_{\ell})}
are such that

\Sigma(A-\lambda_{\ell}I)=\bigcup_{i=1}^{\ell}\left[a_{i}-\lambda_{\ell},b_{i}-\lambda_{\ell}\right]=\Sigma(B_{1}^{*})\cup\Sigma(B_{\ell}^{*}),

and verify

\Sigma(B_{1}^{*})=\bigcup\limits_{i=1}^{\ell-1}\Big[a_{i}-\lambda_{\ell},b_{i}-\lambda_{\ell}\Big]\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\Sigma(B_{\ell}^{*})=\Big[a_{\ell}-\lambda_{\ell},b_{\ell}-\lambda_{\ell}\Big].

As we know that (
5.4.9
) is reducible to (
5.4.10
), by
Lemma
5.4.3
we also can deduce that the linear system
\dot{x}=A(t)x
is reducible to

\dot{y}=\left[\begin{matrix}B_{1}^{*}(t)+\lambda_{\ell}I_{m_{\ell}}&0\\
0&B_{\ell}^{*}(t)+\lambda_{\ell}I_{n-m_{\ell}}\end{matrix}\right]y.

Now we define
B_{\ell}(t):=B_{\ell}^{*}(t)+\lambda_{\ell}I_{n-m_{\ell}}
and use again Lemma
5.4.5
to obtain that

\Sigma(A)=\Sigma(B_{1}^{*}(t)+\lambda_{\ell}I_{m_{\ell}})\cup\Sigma(B_{\ell}(t)),

where

\Sigma(B_{\ell})=[a_{\ell},b_{\ell}]\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\Sigma(B_{1}^{*}+\gamma_{\ell}I_{m_{\ell}})=\bigcup\limits_{i=1}^{\ell-1}[a_{i},b_{i}]=\Sigma(\hat{B}_{1}).

Summarizing, we have proved that the linear system
\dot{x}=A(t)x
is reducible to

(5.4.11)

\dot{y}(t)=\left[\begin{matrix}\hat{B}_{1}(t)&0\\
0&B_{\ell}(t)\end{matrix}\right]y(t),

by a transformation
Q_{\ell}(t)\in M_{n}(\mathbb{R})
.

Step 2:
Note that (
5.4.11
) can be decoupled as

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\dot{y}&=&\hat{B}_{1}(t)y\\
\dot{z}&=&B_{\ell}(t)z\end{array}

where

\Sigma(\hat{B}_{1})=[a_{1},b_{1}]\cup\ldots\cup[a_{\ell-2},b_{\ell-2}]\cup[a_{\ell-1},b_{\ell-1}].

Now, the subsystem
\dot{y}=\hat{B}_{1}(t)y
can be treated as in the previous step
by choosing
\lambda_{\ell-1}\in(b_{\ell-2},a_{\ell-1})
or, equivalently,
0\in(b_{\ell-2}-\lambda_{\ell-1},a_{\ell-1}-\lambda_{\ell-1})
.

By following the lines of Step 1,
we can prove that
\dot{y}=\hat{B}_{1}y
is reducible to

\dot{y}=\left[\begin{matrix}\hat{B}_{2}(t)&0\\
0&B_{\ell-1}(t)\end{matrix}\right]y,

by a transformation
\tilde{Q}_{\ell-1}(t)\in M_{m_{\ell}}(\mathbb{R})

where

\Sigma(\hat{B}_{2})=\bigcup\limits_{i=1}^{\ell-2}[a_{i},b_{i}]\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\Sigma(B_{\ell-1})=[a_{\ell-1},b_{\ell-2}]

and we can see that
\dot{x}=A(t)x
is kinematically similar to

\dot{y}=\left[\begin{array}[]{ccc}\hat{B}_{2}(t)&&\\
&B_{\ell-1}(t)&\\
&&B_{\ell}(t)\end{array}\right]

by a composition

Q_{\ell-1}(t)=\left[\begin{matrix}\widetilde{Q}_{\ell-1}(t)&0\\
0&I_{n-m_{\ell}}\end{matrix}\right]\circ Q_{\ell}(t).

Step 3:
The rest of the proof can be achieved recursively.

∎

5.5.
Comments and References

1)
The exponential dichotomy spectrum is also called as the
Sacker
\&
Sell’s spectrum

due to the seminal 1978 article of Sacker and Sell
[
118
]
which constructs a spectrum for linear skew–products dynamical systems encompassing those arising from linear nonautonomous differential and difference equations. A step ahead is made at the beginning of the century with the works of Aulbach and Siegmund which the construct a spectral theory focused
on linear nonautonomous differential and difference equations
[
7
,
8
,
121
]
, providing a solid basis for a vast research effort. An abridged but well written version of this topic can be founded on
[
78
, Ch.5]
.

2)
The sections 2 and 3 of this chapter have been writing emulating the structure and proofs presented in
[
78
]
. Moreover, the step 2 in the proof of Proposition
5.3.3
follows an idea from
[
82
]
.

3)
At the beginning of this chapter we made references to several

spectral theories
. We refer the reader to for
Lyapunov spectrum
and
Bohl spectrum

which are studied in
[
50
, Ch.1]
.

4)
The assignability problem for the control system (
5.1.2c
)
has been studied in relation with the exponential dichotomy spectra and we refer
the reader to
[
5
,
6
]
.

5)
The exponential dichotomy spectrum for the block system

\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\dot{y}_{1}\\
\dot{y}_{2}\end{array}\right)=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}B_{1}(t)&C(t)\\
\\
0&B_{2}(t)\end{array}\right]\left(\begin{array}[]{c}y_{1}\\
y_{2}\end{array}\right),

has been studied in depth by F. Battelli and K.J. Palmer in
[
13
]
, where
it was proved that, in general,

\Sigma\left(\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}B_{1}(t)&C(t)\\
\\
0&B_{2}(t)\end{array}\right]\right)\neq\Sigma(B_{1})\cup\Sigma(B_{2}).

On the other hand, it was also proved that

\Sigma^{+}\left(\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}B_{1}(t)&C(t)\\
\\
0&B_{2}(t)\end{array}\right]\right)=\Sigma^{+}(B_{1})\cup\Sigma^{+}(B_{2}),

provided that
C(t)
is bounded on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
.

6)
The property of
diagonal significance
, which has been coined by C. Pötzsche
in
[
111
]
, refers to
the identity between the spectra of a block system and the union of diagonal spectra. Some new results about
diagonal significance have been extended for the nonuniform exponential dichotomy spectrum in
[
33
]
.

7)
To the best of our knowledge, the first proof of Theorem
5.4.2

has been carried out by S. Siegmund in
[
122
, Th.3.2]
. Nevertheless, we emphasize that our proof
has several differences which are based on our results from Chapter 4.
