Chapter 6
Global linearization

As we stated at the beginning of the Chapter 2, the problem of local linearization, in the framework of autonomous equations, was initially considered by
D. Grobman
[
59
]
and P. Hartman
[
65
, Theorem I]
; later a specific type of those equations regarded in the previous mentioned works, namely a linear and a quasi-linear system, were studied by C. Pugh
[
112
]
in order to achieve a global homeomorphism between the solutions of the systems. In this chapter, we will study
the generalization of the Pugh’s result to the nonautonomous case carried out by K.J. Palmer
[
98
]
under the assumption that the linear part has an exponential dichotomy.

6.1.
Introduction

Let us consider the linear system

(6.1.1)

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\dot{x}&=&A(t)x,\end{array}

and the family of quasilinear systems

(6.1.2)

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\dot{y}&=&A(t)y+f(t,y),\end{array}

where
t\mapsto A(t)\in M_{n}(\mathbb{R})
and

f\colon\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
has suitable properties. In this chapter, we will revise several results devoted to the global linealization of (
6.1.2
).

The concept of topological equivalence for nonautonomous systems has been introduced by K.J. Palmer in
[
98
, pp.754–755]
:

Definition 6.1.1
.

The systems
(
6.1.1
)
and
(
6.1.2
)
are
topologically equivalent

if there exists a function
H\colon\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
with the properties

(i)

For each fixed
t\in\mathbb{R}
,
u\mapsto H(t,u)
is an homeomorphism of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, with inverse

u\mapsto G(t,u)
,

(ii)

G(t,u)-u
is bounded in
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

(iii)

If
t\mapsto x(t)
is a solution of
(
6.1.1
)
, then
t\mapsto H[t,x(t)]
is a solution
of
(
6.1.2
)
,

(iv)

If
t\mapsto y(t)
is a solution of
(
6.1.2
)
, then
t\mapsto G[t,y(t)]
is a solution
of
(
6.1.1
)
.

The concept of strongly topologically equivalence was introduced by Shi and Xiong
[
119
]
, who realized that, in several
examples of topological equivalence, the maps
u\mapsto H(t,u)
and
u\mapsto G(t,u)
could have properties sharper than continuity.

Definition 6.1.2
.

The systems
(
6.1.1
)
and
(
6.1.2
)
are strongly topologically equivalent
if they are topologically equivalent and
H
and
G
are uniformly continuous for all
t\in\mathbb{R}.

6.2.
Palmer’s Homeomorphism

Theorem 6.2.1
.

[
98
]

Assume that (
6.1.1
) has an exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}
with constants
K\geq 1
,
\alpha>0
and projection
P
. Moreover, we will assume that

(A1)

The matrix
A(\cdot)
of the linear system is bounded, namely

\sup\limits_{t\in\mathbb{R}}||A(t)||=M,

(A2)

there exists a positive constant
\mu
such that

|f(t,y)|\leq\mu\quad\textnormal{for any}\quad(t,y)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n},

(A3)

there exists a positive constant
\gamma
such that

|f(t,y)-f(t,y^{\prime})|\leq\gamma|y-y^{\prime}|\quad\textnormal{for any $t\in\mathbb{R}$ and $(y,y^{\prime})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}$}.

If the following inequality is satisfied

(6.2.1)

2K\gamma<\alpha,

then
(
6.1.1
)
and
(
6.1.2
)
are strongly topologically equivalent.

The proof of this Theorem will be a consequence of several intermediate results.

6.2.1.
Continuity with respect to initial conditions

The unique solution of (
6.1.1
) passing through
\xi
at
t=\tau
will be denoted by
t\mapsto x(t,\tau,\xi)
. Similarly,
the unique solution of (
6.1.2
) passing through
\eta
at
t=\tau
will be denoted by
t\mapsto y(t,\tau,\eta)
.

Proposition 6.2.1
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
6.2.1
, given two solutions
t\mapsto y(t,\tau,\eta)
and
t\mapsto y(t,\tau,\eta^{\prime})
of
(
6.1.2
)
it follows that

(6.2.2)

|y(t,\tau,\eta)-y(t,\tau,\eta^{\prime})|\leq|\eta-\eta^{\prime}|e^{(M+\gamma)|t-\tau|}.

Proof.

Firstly, let us assume that
t>\tau
. By integrating the system, we have that

\begin{array}[]{rcl}|y(t,\tau,\eta)-y(t,\tau,\eta^{\prime})|&=&\displaystyle|\eta-\eta^{\prime}|+\int_{\tau}^{t}|A(s)y(s,\tau,\eta))-A(s)y(s,\tau,\eta^{\prime}))|\,ds\\
\\
&&\displaystyle+\int_{\tau}^{t}|f(s,y(s,\tau,\eta))-f(s,y(s,\tau,\eta^{\prime}))|\,ds.\end{array}

Now, by
(A1)
and
(A3)
we can deduce that

|y(t,\tau,\eta)-y(t,\tau,\eta^{\prime})|\leq|\eta-\eta^{\prime}|+(M+\gamma)\int_{\tau}^{t}|y(s,\tau,\eta)-y(s,\tau,\eta^{\prime})|\,ds

and (
6.2.2
) is a consequence from Gronwall’s Lemma.

Now, if
t<\tau
, we can use the integral representation

\begin{array}[]{rcl}y(t,\tau,\eta)-y(t,\tau,\eta^{\prime})&=&\displaystyle\eta-\eta^{\prime}-\int_{\tau}^{t}\{A(s)y(s,\tau,\eta)-A(s)y(s,\tau,\eta^{\prime})\}\,ds\\
\\
&&\displaystyle-\int_{\tau}^{t}\{f(s,y(s,\tau,\eta))-f(s,y(s,\tau,\eta^{\prime}))\}\,ds,\end{array}

and it follows that

\begin{array}[]{rcl}|y(t,\tau,\eta)-y(t,\tau,\eta^{\prime})|&\leq&\displaystyle|\eta-\eta^{\prime}|\\
\\
&&\displaystyle+(M+\gamma)\int_{\tau}^{t}\,|y(s,\tau,\eta))-y(s,\tau,\eta^{\prime})|\,ds\end{array}

and the rest of the proof can be made in a similar way.
∎

The following result is a direct consequence of the bounded growth growth property

Proposition 6.2.2
.

Given to solutions
t\mapsto x(t,\tau,\xi)
and
t\mapsto x(t,\tau,\xi^{\prime})
of
(
6.1.1
)
it follows that

|x(t,\tau,\xi)-x(t,\tau,\xi^{\prime})|\leq|\xi-\xi^{\prime}|e^{M|t-\tau|}.

Proof.

See Exercises.
∎

6.2.2.
Some intermediate Lemmas

The following result are classical tools developed
by K.J. Palmer in his global linearization result.

Lemma 6.2.1
.

For any solution
t\mapsto y(t,\tau,\eta)
of
(
6.1.2
)
passing
through
\eta
at
t=\tau
, there exists a unique bounded solution
t\mapsto\chi(t;(\tau,\eta))
of

(6.2.3)

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\dot{z}(t)&=&A(t)z(t)-f(t,y(t,\tau,\eta)).\end{array}

Proof.

By using Proposition
2.3.1
from Chapter 2 with
g(t)=-f(t,y(t,\tau,\eta))
, we have that

(6.2.4)

\chi(t;(\tau,\eta))=-\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathcal{G}(t,s)f(s,y(s,\tau,\eta))\,ds

is the unique bounded solution of (
6.2.3
). In addition,
(A2)
implies
that
|\chi(t;(\tau,\eta))|\leq 2K\mu\alpha^{-1}
.
∎

Remark 6.2.1
.

By uniqueness of solutions of (
6.1.2
), we know that

y\big(t,r,y(r,\tau,\eta)\big)=y\big(t,\tau,\eta\big)\quad\textnormal{for any $r\in\mathbb{R}$},

this fact implies that system (
6.2.3
) can be written as

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\dot{z}(t)&=&A(t)z(t)-f(t,y(t,r,y(r,\tau,\eta)))\end{array}

and Lemma
6.2.1
implies the identity

(6.2.5)

\chi(t;(\tau,\xi))=\chi(t;(r,x(r,\tau,\xi)))\quad\textnormal{for any $r\in\mathbb{R}$}.

Lemma 6.2.2
.

For any solution
t\mapsto x(t,\tau,\xi)
of
(
6.1.1
)
passing through
\xi
at
t=\tau
, there
exists a unique bounded solution
t\mapsto\vartheta(t;(\tau,\xi))
of

(6.2.6)

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\dot{w}&=&A(t)w+f(t,x(t,\tau,\xi)+w).\end{array}

Proof.

By using Proposition
2.3.2
from Chapter 2 with
h(t,w)=f(t,x(t,\tau,\xi)+w)
, we know that there exists
a unique bounded solution

(6.2.7)

\begin{array}[]{l}\vartheta(t;(\tau,\xi))=\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\mathcal{G}(t,s)f(s,x(s,\tau,\xi)+\vartheta(s;(\tau,\xi)))\,ds,\end{array}

where
\mathcal{G}
is the Green function stated in Definition
2.3.1
from Chapter 2.
∎

Remark 6.2.2
.

Similarly as in Remark
6.2.1
, the reader can verify the identity

(6.2.8)

\vartheta(t;(\tau,\xi))=\vartheta(t;(r,x(r,\tau,\xi)))\quad\textnormal{for any $r\in\mathbb{R}$}.

Lemma 6.2.3
.

There exists a function

H\colon\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
, satisfying:

(i)

H(t,x)-x
is bounded in
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

(ii)

For any solution
t\mapsto x(t,\tau,\xi)
of
(
6.1.1
)
, we have that

t\mapsto H[t,x(t,\tau,\xi)]
is a solution of the nonlinear system
(
6.1.2
)
verifying

(6.2.9a)

\displaystyle|H[t,x(t,\tau,\xi)]-x(t,\tau,\xi)|\leq 2\mu K\alpha^{-1},

(6.2.9b)

\displaystyle H[t,x(t,\tau,\xi)]=y(t,\tau,H(\tau,\xi)).

Proof.

Let us define
H\colon\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
as follows:

(6.2.10)

\begin{array}[]{rcl}H(t,\xi)&=&\xi+\vartheta(t;(t,\xi))\\
\\
&=&\displaystyle\xi+\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathcal{G}(t,s)f(s,x(s,t,\xi)+\vartheta(s;(t,\xi)))\,ds.\end{array}

By using (
6.2.7
) and (
6.2.8
), we can deduce the alternative characterizations

(6.2.11)

H[t,x(t,\tau,\xi)]=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}x(t,\tau,\xi)+\vartheta(t;(t,x(t,\tau,\xi)))\\
\\
x(t,\tau,\xi)+\vartheta(t;(\tau,\xi))\\
\\
x(t,\tau,\xi)+\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\mathcal{G}(t,s)f(s,\underbrace{x(s,\tau,\xi)+\vartheta(s;(\tau,\xi))}_{=H[s,x(s,\tau,\xi)]})\,ds.\end{array}\right.

It will be useful to describe
H[t,x(t,\tau,\xi)]
as follows

(6.2.12)

\begin{array}[]{rcl}H[t,x(t,\tau,\xi)]&=&\displaystyle x(t,\tau,\xi)+\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathcal{G}(t,s)f(s,H[s,x(s,\tau,\xi)])\,ds,\end{array}

and the property (
6.2.9a
) can be deduced by property
(A2)
followed by a standard estimation of the Green’s function combined with the exponential dichotomy constants.

On the other hand, as
t\mapsto x(t,\tau,\xi)
is solution of (
6.1.1
) and
t\mapsto\vartheta(t;(\tau,\xi))
is solution
of (
6.2.6
), the alternative characterizations for
H[t,x(t,\tau,\xi)]
allow us to deduce that

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\frac{d}{dt}H[t,x(t,\tau,\xi)]&=&A(t)x(t,\tau,\xi)+\frac{d}{dt}\vartheta(t;(\tau,\xi))\\
\\
&=&A(t)x(t,\tau,\xi)+A(t)\vartheta(t;(\tau,\xi))+f(t,x(t,\tau,\xi)+\vartheta(t;(\tau,\xi)))\\
\\
&=&A(t)\{x(t,\tau,\xi)+\vartheta(t;(\tau,\xi))\}+f(t,x(t,\tau,\xi)+\vartheta(t;(\tau,\xi)))\\
\\
&=&A(t)H[t,x(t,\tau,\xi)]+f(t,H[t,x(t,\tau,\xi)]).\end{array}

Then,
t\mapsto H[t,x(t,\tau,\xi)]
is a solution of (
6.1.2
) passing through
H(\tau,\xi)
at
t=\tau
, and the identity
(
6.2.9b
) follows by the uniqueness of solutions.
∎

Lemma 6.2.4
.

There exists a function

G\colon\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
, satisfying:

(i)

G(t,y)-y
is bounded in
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

(ii)

For any solution
t\mapsto y(t,\tau,\eta)
of
(
6.1.2
)
, then
t\mapsto G[t,y(t,\tau,\eta)]

is a solution of
(
6.1.1
)
satisfying

(6.2.13a)

\displaystyle|G[t,y(t,\tau,\eta)]-y(t,\tau,\eta)|\leq 2\mu K\alpha^{-1},

(6.2.13b)

\displaystyle G[t,y(t,\tau,\eta)]=x(t,\tau,G(\tau,\eta)).

Proof.

Let us define the
function
G\colon\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}

as follows

(6.2.14)

\begin{array}[]{rcl}G(t,\eta)&=&\eta+\chi(t;(t,\eta))\\
\\
&=&\displaystyle\eta-\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathcal{G}(t,s)f(s,y(s,t,\eta))\,ds,\end{array}

and
(A2)
combined with (
6.2.4
) implies
|G(t,\eta)-\eta|\leq 2\mu K\alpha^{-1}
.

By replacing
(t,\eta)
by
(t,y(t,\tau,\eta))
in (
6.2.14
) and using (
6.2.5
), we can deduce the following alternative characterizations

(6.2.15)

G[t,y(t,\tau,\eta)]=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}y(t,\tau,\eta)+\chi(t;(t,y(t,\tau,\eta)))\\
\\
y(t,\tau,\eta)+\chi(t;(\tau,\eta))\\
\\
\displaystyle y(t,\tau,\eta)-\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathcal{G}(t,s)f(s,y(s,\tau,\eta))\,ds.\end{array}\right.

As in the previous result, the property (
6.2.13a
) can be deduced by property
(A2)
followed by a standard estimation of the Green’s function combined with the exponential dichotomy constants.

Finally, as
t\mapsto y(t,\tau,\eta)
is solution of (
6.1.2
) and
t\mapsto\chi(t;(\tau,\eta))
is solution
of (
6.2.3
), the above characterizations of

G[t,y(t,\tau,\eta)]

allow us to deduce that

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\frac{d}{dt}G[t,y(t,\tau,\eta)]&=&\displaystyle A(t)y(t,\tau,\eta)+f(t,y(t,\tau,\eta))+\frac{d}{dt}\chi(t;(\tau,\eta))\\
\\
&=&\displaystyle A(t)\{y(t,\tau,\eta)+\chi(t;(\tau,\eta))\}\\
\\
&=&A(t)G[t,y(t,\tau,\eta)],\end{array}

and we verify that
t\mapsto G[t,y(t,\tau,\eta)]
is solution of (
6.1.1
) passing through by
G(\tau,\eta)
at
t=\tau
and (
6.2.13b
) is verified.
∎

Lemma 6.2.5
.

For any
solution
t\mapsto y(t,\tau,\eta)
of
(
6.1.2
)
with fixed
t
, it follows that

H[t,G[t,y(t,\tau,\eta)]]=y(t,\tau,\eta).

Proof.

By using (
6.2.12
) combined with (
6.2.13b
)
we have that

\begin{array}[]{rcl}H[t,G[t,y(t,\tau,\eta)]]&=&H[t,x(t,\tau,G(\tau,\eta))]\\
\\
&=&\displaystyle x(t,\tau,G(\tau,\eta))+\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{G}(t,s)f(s,H[s,x(s,\tau,G(s,\eta)]])\,ds\\
\\
&=&\displaystyle G[t,y(t,\tau,\eta)]+\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{G}(t,s)f(s,H[s,G[s,y(s,\tau,\eta)]])\,ds.\end{array}

The third characterization of (
6.2.15
) and the identity above implies that

H[t,G[t,y(t,\tau,\eta)]]-y(t,\tau,\eta)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{G}(t,s)\{f(s,H[s,G[s,y(s,\tau,\eta)]])-f(s,y(s,\tau,\eta))\}\,ds.

By
(A3)
combined with standard estimations of the Green’s function allow to deduce that

|H[t,G[t,y(t,\tau,\eta)]]-y(t,\tau,\eta)|\leq\frac{2K\gamma}{\alpha}\sup\limits_{t\in\mathbb{R}}|H[t,G[t,\tau,\eta)]-y(t,\tau,\eta)|.

The Lemma is a direct consequence of the identity

\sup\limits_{t\in\mathbb{R}}|H[\cdot,G[\cdot,\tau,\eta)]-y(\cdot,\tau,\eta)|=0.

In fact, the above term cannot be positive since it will implies a contradiction with (
6.2.1
).
∎

Lemma 6.2.6
.

For any solution
t\mapsto x(t,\tau,\xi)
of
(
6.1.1
)
, it follows that

G[t,H[t,x(t,\tau,\xi)]]=x(t,\tau,\xi).

Proof.

Let us observe that (
6.2.9b
), (
6.2.12
) and (
6.2.14
) imply that

\begin{array}[]{rcl}G[t,H[t,x(t,\tau,\xi)]]&=&\displaystyle H[t,x(t,\tau,\xi)]-\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{G}(t,s)f(s,y(s,t,H[t,x(t,\tau,\xi)])\,ds\\
\\
&=&\displaystyle H[t,x(t,\tau,\xi)]-\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{G}(t,s)f(s,y(s,t,y(t,\tau,H(\tau,\xi))))\,ds\\
\\
&=&\displaystyle H[t,x(t,\tau,\xi)]-\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{G}(t,s)f(s,y(s,\tau,H(\tau,\xi)))\,ds\\
\\
&=&x(t,\tau,\xi)\\
\\
&&\displaystyle+\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{G}(t,s)\{f(s,H[s,x(s,\tau,\xi)])-f(s,y(s,\tau,H(\tau,\xi)))\}\,ds\\
\\
&=&x(t,\tau,\xi),\end{array}

and the result follows.
∎

The reader can notice (see also Definition
6.1.1
) that the notation
H[\cdot,\cdot]
and
G[\cdot,\cdot]

is reserved to the case when
H
and
G
are respectively defined on solutions of (
6.1.2
) and (
6.1.1
).

Lemma 6.2.7
.

For any fixed
t
and any pair
(\xi,\eta)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
, it follows that

(6.2.16)

G(t,H(t,\xi))=\xi

and

(6.2.17)

H(t,G(t,\eta))=\eta.

Proof.

By using Lemma
6.2.5
, we have that

G[t,H[t,x(t,\tau,\xi)]]=x(t,\tau,\xi)\quad\textnormal{for any}\quad t\in\mathbb{R}.

Now, if we consider the particular case
\tau=t
, we obtain (
6.2.16
). The identity (
6.2.17
) can be deduced
similarly.
∎

Remark 6.2.3
.

Notice that the maps
\xi\mapsto H(t,\xi)
and
\eta\mapsto G(t,\eta)
satisfy properties
(ii) and (iii) of Definition
6.1.1
, which is a consequence of Lemmas
6.2.4
–
6.2.5
. In addition,
Lemma
6.2.7
says that
u\mapsto G(t,u)=H^{-1}(t,u)
for any
t\in\mathbb{R}
. In consequence, the last step is to prove
the uniform continuity of the maps, which will be made in the next two sections.

6.2.3.
End of the Proof: Continuity of the maps
H
and
G

We follow the approach developed in the nice paper of Shi and Xiong
[
119
]
.

Lemma 6.2.8
.

The map
\eta\mapsto G(t,\eta)=\eta+\chi(t;(t,\eta))
is uniformly continuous for any
t\in\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

As the identity is uniformly continuous, we only need to prove that the map
\eta\to\chi(t;(t,\eta))
is uniformly continuous.

Let
\eta
and
\eta^{\prime}
be two initial conditions of (
6.1.2
). Notice that (
6.2.14
) allows to say that

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\chi(t;(t,\eta))-\chi(t;(t,\eta^{\prime}))&=&\displaystyle-\int_{-\infty}^{t}\mathcal{G}(t,s)\big\{f(s,y(s,t,\eta))-f(s,y(s,t,\eta^{\prime}))\big\}\,ds\\
\\
&&\displaystyle+\int_{t}^{\infty}\mathcal{G}(t,s)\big\{f(s,y(s,t,\eta))-f(s,y(s,t,\eta^{\prime}))\big\}\,ds\\
\\
&=&-I_{1}+I_{2}.\end{array}

Now, we divide
I_{1}
and
I_{2}
as follows:

I_{1}=\int_{-\infty}^{t-L}+\int_{t-L}^{t}=I_{11}+I_{12}\quad\textnormal{and}\quad I_{2}=\int_{t}^{t+L}+\int_{t+L}^{\infty}=I_{21}+I_{22},

where
L
is a positive constant.

By using
(A2)
combined with exponential dichotomy, we can see that the integrals

I_{11}
and
I_{22}
are always finite since

|I_{11}|\leq 2K\mu\int_{-\infty}^{t-L}e^{-\alpha(t-s)}\,ds=\frac{2K\mu}{\alpha}e^{-\alpha L}

and

|I_{22}|\leq 2K\mu\int_{t+L}^{\infty}e^{-\alpha(s-t)}\,ds=\frac{2K\mu}{\alpha}e^{-\alpha L}.

Now, by
(A3)
, we have that

\begin{array}[]{rcl}|I_{12}|&\leq&\displaystyle\int_{t-L}^{t}Ke^{-\alpha(t-s)}\gamma|y(s,t,\eta)-y(s,t,\eta^{\prime})|\,ds\\
\\
&\leq&\displaystyle\int_{0}^{L}Ke^{-\alpha u}\gamma|y(t-u,t,\eta)-y(t-u,t,\eta^{\prime})|\,du.\\
\\
\end{array}

On the other hand, by Proposition
6.2.1
, we have that

0\leq|y(t-u,t,\eta)-y(t-u,t,\eta^{\prime})|\leq|\eta-\eta^{\prime}|e^{(M+\gamma)u}\quad\textnormal{for any}\quad u\in[0,L].

Upon inserting this inequality in the previous estimation of
|I_{12}|
, we obtain that

\begin{array}[]{rcl}|I_{12}|&\leq&\displaystyle\int_{0}^{L}Ke^{-\alpha u}\gamma|y(t-u,t,\eta)-y(t-u,t,\eta^{\prime})|\,du\\
\\
&\leq&\displaystyle K\gamma|\eta-\eta^{\prime}|\int_{0}^{L}e^{-\alpha u}e^{(M+\gamma)u}\,du\\
&\leq&\displaystyle K\gamma|\eta-\eta^{\prime}|e^{(M+\gamma)L}\int_{0}^{L}e^{-\alpha u}\,du\\
&=&\displaystyle\frac{K\gamma e^{(M+\gamma)L}}{\alpha}(1-e^{-\alpha L})|\eta-\eta^{\prime}|\end{array}

The reader can deduce that the inequalities above implies

(6.2.18)

|I_{12}|\leq D|\eta-\eta^{\prime}|\quad\textnormal{with}\quad D=\frac{K\gamma e^{(M+\gamma)L}}{\alpha}(1-e^{-\alpha L}).

Analogously, we can deduce that

(6.2.19)

|I_{21}|\leq D|\eta-\eta^{\prime}|.

For any
\varepsilon>0
, we can choose

L\geq\displaystyle\frac{1}{\alpha}\ln\left(\frac{8K\mu}{\alpha\varepsilon}\right),

which implies that

|I_{11}|+|I_{22}|<\varepsilon/2
. By using this fact combined with (
6.2.18
)–(
6.2.19
), we obtain
that

\forall\varepsilon>0\hskip 2.84544pt\exists\delta=\frac{\varepsilon}{4D}>0\,\,\textnormal{such that}\,\,|\eta-\eta^{\prime}|<\delta\Rightarrow|\chi(t;(t,\eta))-\chi(t;(t,\eta^{\prime}))|<\varepsilon

and the uniform continuity follows.
∎

Lemma 6.2.9
.

The map
\xi\mapsto H(t,\xi)=\xi+\vartheta(t;(t,\xi))
is uniformly continuous for any
t\in\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

We only need to prove that the map
\xi\mapsto\vartheta(t;(t,\xi))
is uniformly continuous. In order to prove that,
let
\xi
and
\xi^{\prime}
be two initial conditions of (
6.1.1
) and define the differences

\Delta\vartheta:=\vartheta(t;(t,\xi))-\vartheta(t;(t,\xi^{\prime})),

and

\Delta f:=f(s,x(s,t,\xi)+\vartheta(s;(t,\xi)))-f(s,x(s,t,\xi^{\prime})+\vartheta(s;(t,\xi^{\prime}))).

By using (
6.2.7
), we can see that
\Delta\vartheta
can be written as follows:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\Delta\vartheta&=&\displaystyle\underbrace{\int_{-\infty}^{t-\tilde{L}}\mathcal{G}(t,s)\Delta f\,ds}_{:=J_{11}}+\underbrace{\int_{t-\tilde{L}}^{t}\mathcal{G}(t,s)\Delta f\,ds}_{:=J_{12}}\\
\\
&&+\displaystyle\underbrace{\int_{t}^{t+\tilde{L}}\mathcal{G}(t,s)\Delta f\,ds}_{:=J_{21}}+\underbrace{\int_{t+\tilde{L}}^{+\infty}\mathcal{G}(t,s)\Delta f\,ds}_{:=J_{22}}.\end{array}

By
(A2)
and the exponential dichotomy property, it is straightforward to verify that

|J_{11}|\leq\frac{2K\mu}{\alpha}e^{-\alpha\tilde{L}}\quad\textnormal{and}\quad|J_{22}|\leq\frac{2K\mu}{\alpha}e^{-\alpha\tilde{L}}.

Let us define

||\vartheta(\cdot;(t,\xi))-\vartheta(\cdot;(t,\xi^{\prime}))||_{\infty}=\sup\limits_{s\in\mathbb{R}}|\vartheta(s;(t,\xi))-\vartheta(s;(t,\xi^{\prime}))|,

and notice that
(A3)
implies:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}|J_{12}|&\leq&\displaystyle\frac{K\gamma}{\alpha}||\vartheta(\cdot;(t,\xi))-\vartheta(\cdot;(t,\xi^{\prime}))||_{\infty}\\
\\
&&\displaystyle+K\gamma\int_{t-\tilde{L}}^{t}e^{-\alpha(t-s)}|x(s,t,\xi)-x(s,t,\xi^{\prime})|\,ds\\
\\
&\leq&\displaystyle\frac{K\gamma}{\alpha}||\vartheta(\cdot;(t,\xi))-\vartheta(\cdot;(t,\xi^{\prime}))||_{\infty}\\
\\
&&\displaystyle+K\gamma\int_{0}^{\tilde{L}}e^{-\alpha u}|x(t-u,t,\xi)-x(t-u,t,\xi^{\prime})|\,du.\\
\\
\end{array}

By using Proposition
6.2.2
, we know that

|x(t-u,t,\xi)-x(t-u,t,\xi^{\prime})|\leq|\xi-\xi^{\prime}|e^{Mu}\quad\textnormal{for any}\quad u\in[0,\tilde{L}].

Upon inserting this inequality in
|J_{12}|
, we have that

\begin{array}[]{rcl}|J_{12}|&\leq&\displaystyle\frac{K\gamma}{\alpha}||\vartheta(\cdot;(t,\xi))-\vartheta(\cdot;(t,\xi^{\prime}))||_{\infty}+K\gamma|\xi-\xi^{\prime}|\int_{0}^{\tilde{L}}e^{-\alpha u}e^{M\tilde{L}}\,du\\
\\
&\leq&\displaystyle\frac{K\gamma}{\alpha}||\vartheta(\cdot;(t,\xi))-\vartheta(\cdot;(t,\xi^{\prime}))||_{\infty}+\frac{K\gamma}{\alpha}e^{M\tilde{L}}|\xi-\xi^{\prime}|\end{array}

and the reader can deduce that

|J_{12}|\leq\frac{K\gamma}{\alpha}||\vartheta(\cdot;(t,\xi))-\vartheta(\cdot;(t,\xi^{\prime}))||_{\infty}+\tilde{D}|\xi-\xi^{\prime}|\quad\textnormal{with}\quad\tilde{D}=\frac{K\gamma}{\alpha}e^{M\tilde{L}}.

The next inequality can be proved in a similar way

|J_{21}|\leq\frac{K\gamma}{\alpha}||\vartheta(\cdot;(t,\xi))-\vartheta(\cdot;(t,\xi^{\prime}))||_{\infty}+\tilde{D}|\xi-\xi^{\prime}|.

By using the inequalities stated above combined with (
6.2.1
), he have

\begin{array}[]{rcl}|\vartheta(t;(t,\xi))-\vartheta(t;(t,\xi^{\prime}))|&\leq&|J_{11}|+|J_{22}|+|J_{12}|+|J_{21}|\\
\\
&\leq&\displaystyle\frac{4K\mu}{\alpha}e^{-\alpha\tilde{L}}+2\tilde{D}|\xi-\xi^{\prime}|\\
\\
&&\displaystyle+\frac{2K\gamma}{\alpha}||\vartheta(\cdot;(t,\xi))-\vartheta(\cdot;(t,\xi^{\prime}))||_{\infty}.\end{array}

Note that

\begin{array}[]{rcl}||\vartheta(\cdot;(t,\xi))-\vartheta(\cdot;(t,\xi^{\prime}))||_{\infty}&\leq&\displaystyle\frac{4K\mu}{\alpha}e^{-\alpha\tilde{L}}+2\tilde{D}|\xi-\xi^{\prime}|\\
\\
&&\displaystyle+\frac{2K\gamma}{\alpha}||\vartheta(\cdot;(t,\xi))-\vartheta(\cdot;(t,\xi^{\prime}))||_{\infty}.\end{array}

By defining
\Gamma^{*}:=\frac{2K\gamma}{\alpha}<1
,
and using the inequality

|\vartheta(t;(t,\xi))-\vartheta(t;(t,\xi^{\prime}))|\leq||\vartheta(\cdot;(t,\xi))-\vartheta(\cdot;(t,\xi^{\prime}))||_{\infty},

we easily deduce that

\begin{array}[]{rcl}|\vartheta(t;(t,\xi))-\vartheta(t;(t,\xi^{\prime}))|&\leq&\displaystyle\frac{4K\mu e^{-\alpha\tilde{L}}}{\alpha(1-\Gamma^{*})}+\frac{2\tilde{D}}{1-\Gamma^{*}}|\xi-\xi^{\prime}|.\end{array}

Finally, for any
\varepsilon>0
, we can choose

\tilde{L}\geq\displaystyle\frac{1}{\alpha}\ln\Big(\frac{8K\mu}{\alpha\varepsilon(1-\Gamma^{*})}\Big),

which implies that

\frac{4K\rho^{*}\mu}{\alpha(1-\Gamma^{*})}e^{-\alpha\tilde{L}}<\varepsilon/2
. By using this fact, we obtain
that

\forall\varepsilon>0\,\,\exists\delta=\frac{\varepsilon}{4\tilde{D}(1-\Gamma^{*})}>0\,\,\textnormal{such that}\,\,|\xi-\xi^{\prime}|<\delta\Rightarrow|\vartheta(t;(t,\xi))-\vartheta(t;(t,\xi^{\prime}))|<\varepsilon

and the uniform continuity follows.
∎

6.3.
Comments and References

1)
The proof of Theorem
6.2.1
is inspired in the global linearization result from K.J. Palmer
[
98
]
,
where construction of the maps
H
and
G
is carried out in a nicely way but its continuity with respect to
t
is only sketched. Furthermore, the uniform continuity with respect to this time variable has been proved by
using the boundedness of
A(t)
and adapting the proof of a global linearization from J. Shi and K. Xiong
[
119
]
carried out for a more general
family.

On the other hand, we emphasize that our demonstration has made an effort to be clear in several intricate details of Lemmas
6.2.3
and

6.2.4
. We also highlight
the triple characterization of the maps
t\mapsto H[t,x(t,\tau,\xi)]
and
t\mapsto G[t,y(t,\tau,\eta)]

described in (
6.2.11
) and (
6.2.15
), respectively.

2)
The methods and ideas developed by K.J. Palmer in
[
98
]
have been
extended for several families of equations: difference equations
[
115
]
, piecewise constant delay equations
[
103
]
, impulsive equations
[
85
]
and time–scales equations
[
67
,
110
]
. We also refer to
[
116
]
for dichotomies beyond the exponential.

3)
The global linearization result of Palmer was applied by R. Naulin
[
96
]

in order to study asymptotic properties of the quasilinear system

\dot{x}=Ax+f(t,x)

where
A
has purely imaginary eigenvalues and the perturbation verifies

\lim\limits_{x\to 0}f(t,x)=0\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\lim\limits_{x\to 0}Df(t,x)=0

uniformly with respect to
t
.

4)
The boundedness of the nonlinearity played a key role in the global linearization
carried out in this chapter and we point out the existence of different approaches for global linearization
which drop this assumption. In this context, a first result in this line is obtained by F. Lin in
[
84
]
who showed that if the linear system has an exponential dichotomy with
P=I
on
\mathbb{R},
then the linearization is of class
C^{0}.
In order to obtain his result Lin uses the concept of
almost reducibility
,
i.e
, the linear system can be written as a linear diagonal system perturbed by a bounded linear term where the diagonal part is contained in the spectrum of the exponential dichotomy (see Ch.5), and it uses the concept of
crossing times
with respect to the unit sphere. A second approach is carried out by I. Huerta in
[
73
]
which generalizes the previous work of Lin to a nonuniform framework, that is, it is constructed a linearization of class
C^{0}
in two steps: the first one considers to write the linear system on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
as in Lin’s case where the diagonal part lies on the spectrum of the nonuniform exponential dichotomy (see
[
35
]
and
[
132
]
for details abouth this spectrum), and the second step is devoted to construct a suitable Lyapunov function that plays the role of crossing time with respect to the unit sphere. Recently, M. Wu and Y. Xia in
[
128
]
generalize the previous works considering projections different from trivial ones.

6.4.
Exercises

1.-

If
t\mapsto y(t,\tau,\eta)
is the solution of (
6.1.2
) passing trough
\eta

at
t=\tau
, prove that

G[t,y(t,\tau,\eta)]=X(t,\tau)G(\tau,\eta).

2.-

If
t\mapsto x(t,\tau,\xi)
and
t\mapsto y(t,\tau,\eta)

are respectively solutions of (
6.1.1
) and (
6.1.2
)
passing respectively trough
\xi
and
\eta
at

, use Lemmas
6.2.3
and
6.2.6

to prove that

x(t,\tau,\xi)=y(t,\tau,H(\tau,\xi))+\vartheta(t;(\tau,H(\tau,\xi))).

3.-

If
t\mapsto x(t,\tau,\xi)
and
t\mapsto y(t,\tau,\eta)

are respectively solutions of (
6.1.1
) and (
6.1.2
)
passing respectively trough
\xi
and
\eta
at
t=\tau
, use Lemmas
6.2.4
and
6.2.5

to prove that

y(t,\tau,\eta)=x(t,\tau,G(\tau,\eta))+\chi(t;(\tau,G(\tau,\eta))).

4.-

Use exercises 2 and 3 to prove that

\vartheta(t;(\tau,G(\tau,\eta)))+\chi(t;(\tau,\eta))=0\quad\textnormal{for any $\eta\in\mathbb{R}^{n}$}.

5.-

Use exercises 2 and 3 to prove that

\vartheta(t;(\tau,\xi)))+\chi(t;(\tau,H(\tau,\xi)))=0\quad\textnormal{for any $\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{n}$}.

6.-

Use the identities (
6.2.16
)–(
6.2.17
) together with
the Definitions of
H
and
G
given respectively by (

) and (

) to prove that:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}G(t,\eta)&=&\eta-\chi(t;(t,G(t,\eta)))\quad\textnormal{for any $\eta\in\mathbb{R}^{n}$},\\
H(t,\xi)&=&\xi-\vartheta(t;(H(t,\xi)))\quad\textnormal{for any $\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{n}$}.\end{array}\right.

7.-

Let us consider the quasilinear systems

(6.4.1a)

\displaystyle\dot{x}=A(t)x+f_{1}(t,x),

(6.4.1b)

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636pt\dot{y}=A(t)y+f_{2}(t,y),

where the linear part has exponential dichotomy on
\mathbb{R}
with constants
K
and
\alpha

and also satisfies
(A1)
whereas both nonlinearities verifies
(A2)
–
(A3)

with
\mu
and
\alpha
. Prove that if
2K\gamma<\alpha
then there exists a map
H\colon\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that for any solution
t\mapsto x(t)
of (
6.4.1a
) it follows that
t\mapsto H[t,x(t)]
is solution
of (
6.4.1b
) and viceversa.

8.-

If we assume that
t\mapsto A(t)
and
t\mapsto f(t,x)
are continuous and
\omega
–periodic,
prove that the exists a unique
\omega
–periodic map
t\mapsto\chi(t;(\tau,\eta))
in Lemma
6.2.1

by considering a suitable function
y(t,\tau,\eta)
.
Hint
: See Corollary
2.3.2
from Chapter 2.

9.-

Let us consider the nonlinear control system

\dot{x}=A(t)x+B(t)u(t)+f(t,u(t)),

where
A\in M_{nn}(\mathbb{R})
,
B\in M_{np}(\mathbb{R})
,
u\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
are continuous
and
f\colon\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{p}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
verifies

|f(t,u)|\leq\mu\quad\textnormal{and}\quad|f(t,u)-f(t,u^{\prime})|\leq\gamma|u-u^{\prime}|

for any
(t,u,u^{\prime})\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{p}\times\mathbb{R}^{p}
.

If there exists a bounded continuous map
t\mapsto F(t)\in M_{pn}(t)
such that the linear system
\dot{x}=[A(t)+B(t)F(t)]x
has an exponential dichotomy
on
\mathbb{R}
, find conditions on
||F(t)||_{\infty}
and the exponential dichotomy constants ensuring
the topological equivalence with the system

\dot{x}=[A(t)+B(t)F(t)]x+f(t,F(t)x),

which arises from the feedback control
u(t)=F(t)x
.

.

.

).

).

).
