6.2.1
.

Chapter 7
Differentiability of Global Linearization

7.1.
Motivation

The previous chapter was devoted to construct a global homeomorphism
relating the solutions of the linear system

(7.1.1)

\dot{x}=A(t)x

with the solutions of its quasilinear perturbation

(7.1.2)

\dot{y}=A(t)y+f(t,y)

under the following assumptions:

(A1)

The matrix
A(\cdot)
of the linear system is bounded, namely

\sup\limits_{t\in\mathbb{R}}||A(t)||=M,

(A2)

there exists a positive constant
\mu
such that

|f(t,y)|\leq\mu\quad\textnormal{for any}\quad(t,y)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n},

(A3)

there exists a positive constant
\gamma
such that

|f(t,y)-f(t,y^{\prime})|\leq\gamma|y-y^{\prime}|\quad\textnormal{for any $t\in\mathbb{R}$ and $(y,y^{\prime})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}$}.

The smoothness of the homeomorphism
\eta\mapsto G(t,\eta)
described by (
6.2.14
) from Chapter 6
has started to be studied a few years ago in a series of articles. Nevertheless, an additional and more restrictive assumption
was considered:

(A4)

The linear system (
7.1.1
) has an exponential dichotomy with projection
P=I

and constants
K
and
\alpha
such that

\frac{2K}{\alpha}<\gamma.

A first approach in order to establish the differentiability of this homeomorphism was done in
[
29
]
where it was proved that; under suitable differentiability conditions for
y\mapsto f(t,y)
; the map
G
is
C^{2}.
After, in
[
30
]
it was obtained that
G\in C^{r}
with
r\geq 1
. This last work only considered homeomorphisms
\eta\mapsto G(t,\eta)
for
t\geq 0
.

7.2.
First Approach on the whole line

The main goal of this section is to study the differentiability properties
of the homeomorphism
\eta\mapsto G(t,\eta)
for any
t\in\mathbb{R}
. To achieve this objective,
it will be interesting to consider the map
\xi\mapsto y(t,t_{0},\xi)

and its properties. Indeed, if
y\mapsto f(t,y)
is
C^{1}
for any
t\in\mathbb{R}
, it is well known (see
e.g.

[
36
, Chap. 2]
) that

\partial y(t,t_{0},\xi)/\partial\xi=y_{\xi}(t,t_{0},\xi)
satisfies the matrix differential equation

(7.2.1)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\frac{\partial y}{\partial\xi}(t,t_{0},\xi)&=&\displaystyle\{A(t)+Df(t,y(t,t_{0},\xi))\}\frac{\partial y}{\partial\xi}(t,t_{0},\xi),\\
\\
\displaystyle\frac{\partial y}{\partial\xi}(t_{0},t_{0},\xi)&=&I.\end{array}\right.

As
\xi=(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{n})
, the above equation can be written as a set of
n
systems of
ordinary differential equations

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\frac{\partial y}{\partial\xi_{i}}(t,t_{0},\xi)&=&\displaystyle\{A(t)+Df(t,y(t,t_{0},\xi))\}\frac{\partial y}{\partial\xi_{i}}(t,t_{0},\xi),\\
\\
\displaystyle\frac{\partial y}{\partial\xi_{i}}(t_{0},t_{0},\xi)&=&e_{i},\end{array}\right.

for all
i=1,\ldots,n
.

Moreover, it is proved that (see
e.g.
, Theorem 4.1 from
[
64
, Ch.V]
) if
y\mapsto f(t,y)
is
C^{r}

with
r>1
for any
t\in\mathbb{R}
, then the map

\xi\mapsto y(t,t_{0},\xi)
is also
C^{r}
. In particular, if
f
is
C^{2}
, we can verify that
the second derivatives
\partial^{2}y(t,t_{0},\xi)/\partial\xi_{j}\partial\xi_{i}
are solutions
of the system of differential equations

(7.2.2)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\frac{\partial^{2}y}{\partial\xi_{j}\partial\xi_{i}}&=&\displaystyle\{A(t)+Df(t,y)\}\frac{\partial^{2}y}{\partial\xi_{j}\partial\xi_{i}}+D^{2}f(t,x)\frac{\partial y}{\partial\xi_{j}}\frac{\partial y}{\partial\xi_{i}}\\
\\
\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}y}{\partial\xi_{j}\partial\xi_{i}}&=&0,\end{array}\right.

with
y:=y(t,t_{0},\xi)
, for any
i,j=1,\ldots,n
.

Now, let us introduce the following conditions on the nonlinearities:

(D1)

y\mapsto f(t,y)
is

C^{2}
and, for any fixed
t
, its first derivative is such that

\int_{-\infty}^{t}\,\sup\limits_{\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}||X(t,r)Df(r,y(r,0,\xi))X(r,t)||\,dr<1.

(D2)

For any fixed
\in\mathbb{R}
and any initial condition
\xi
,
it follows that

\int_{-\infty}^{t}\textnormal{Tr}\,Df(r,y(r,t,\xi)\,dr<+\infty.

(D3)

For any fixed
t\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
i,j=1,\ldots,n
, the following limit exists

\lim\limits_{s\to-\infty}\frac{\partial Z_{\eta}(s)}{\partial\eta_{j}}e_{i},

where
\eta=(\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{n})
,
e_{i}
is the
i
–th component of the canonical
basis of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
Z_{\eta}(s)
is a fundamental matrix of the
\eta
–parameter dependent system

(7.2.3)

\dot{z}=F(s,\eta)z

satisfying
Z_{\eta}(t)=I
, where
F(r,\eta)
is defined as follows

(7.2.4)

F(r,\eta)=X(t,r)Df(r,y(r,t,\eta))X(r,t).

Remark 7.2.1
.

We will see that the construction of the homeomorphism
G
considers the behavior of

y(t,0,\xi)
for any
t\in(-\infty,\infty)
. In particular, to prove that
G
is a
C^{2}
preserving orientation diffeomorphism
will require to know the behavior on
(-\infty,t]
. Indeed, notice that:

(D1)
is a technical assumption, introduced to ensure that the homeomorphism
y\mapsto G(t,y)
is
C^{1}
. In fact,
by using (
6.2.13b
) from Chapter 6 we have that
G[s,t_{0},y(s,t_{0},\xi)]=x(s,t_{0},G(t_{0},\xi))
is a solution
of (
7.1.1
) passing through
G(t_{0},\xi)
at
s=t_{0}
. In addition, by using (
2.3.3a
) from Chapter 2 and recalling that
P(\cdot)=I
, it follows that
|G[s,y(s,t_{0},\xi)]|\to+\infty
when
s\to-\infty
since

G[s,t_{0},y(s,t_{0},\xi)]=|x(s,t_{0},G(t_{0},\xi))|\geq\frac{|G(t_{0},\xi)|}{K}e^{\alpha(t_{0}-s)},

this fact combined with (
6.2.13a
) from Chapter 6 and triangle’s inequality implies that
|y(s,t_{0},\xi)|\to+\infty
. In consequence,

(D1)
suggest that the asymptotic behavior of
Df(s,y)
(when
|y|\to+\infty
and
s\to-\infty
) must ensure integrability.

(D2)
is introduced in order to assure that
G
is a preserving orientation diffeomorphism.
We emphasize that this asumption is related to Liouville’s formula.

(D3)
is introduced to ensure that
G
is a
C^{2}
diffeomorphism.

Theorem 7.2.1
.

If (
7.1.1
) and (
7.1.2
) satisfy
(A1)–(A4)
and
(D1)–(D2)
, then, for
any fixed
t
, the function
y\mapsto G(t,y)
is a preserving orientation diffeomorphism.

Proof.

The proof is decomposed in several steps.

Step 1:
Differentiability of the map
\eta\mapsto G(t,\eta)
. By using the fact that
y\mapsto f(t,y)
is
C^{1}
for any
t\in\mathbb{R}
combined with (
7.2.1
) and (
1.1.7
) from Chapter 1
we can deduce that:

(7.2.5)

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\displaystyle\frac{\partial G(t,\eta)}{\partial\eta}&=&\displaystyle I-\int_{-\infty}^{t}X(t,s)Df(s,y(s,t,\eta))\frac{\partial y(s,t,\eta)}{\partial\eta}\,ds\\
\\
&=&\displaystyle I-\int_{-\infty}^{t}\frac{d}{ds}\Big\{X(t,s)\frac{\partial y(s,t,\eta)}{\partial\eta}\Big\}\,ds\\
\\
&=&\displaystyle\lim\limits_{s\to-\infty}X(t,s)\frac{\partial y(s,t,\eta)}{\partial\eta}.\end{array}

In consequence, the differentiability of
\eta\mapsto G(t,\eta)
follows if and only if the limit above exists.

Step 2:
The limit (
7.2.5
) is well defined. By (
7.2.1
), we have verified that

s\mapsto\frac{\partial y}{\partial\eta}(s,t,\eta)
is solution of the equation:

(7.2.6)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}\dot{Y}(s)&=&F(s,\eta)Y(s)\\
Y(t)&=&I.\end{array}\right.

By (
1.1.7
) from Chapter 1, the identity (
7.2.4
) and (
7.2.6
), the reader can
verify that
s\mapsto Z_{\eta}(s)=X(t,s)\frac{\displaystyle\partial y(s,t,\eta)}{\displaystyle\partial\eta}

is solution of the matrix differential equation

(7.2.7)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}\displaystyle\dot{U}(s)&=&\big\{X(t,s)Df(s,y(s,t,\eta))X(s,t)\big\}U(s)\\
\\
U(t)&=&I.\end{array}\right.

By using (
1.1.3
) from Chapter 1, we have the following estimation for the transition matrix
associated to
Z_{\eta}

||Z_{\eta}(s,t)||\leq\exp\Big(\Big|\int_{t}^{s}X(t,r)Df(r,y(r,t,\eta))X(r,t)\,dr\Big|\Big)

and
(D1)
implies that (
7.2.5
) is well defined.

Step 3:

\eta\mapsto G(t,\eta)
is a preserving orientation diffeomorphism.
We already proved that
\eta\mapsto G(t,\eta)
is
C^{1}
for any
t\in\mathbb{R}
and that

\frac{\partial G(t,\eta)}{\partial\eta}=\lim\limits_{s\to-\infty}X(t,s)\frac{\partial y(s,t,\eta)}{\partial\eta}=\lim\limits_{s\to-\infty}Z_{\eta}(s),

where
Z_{\eta}(\cdot)
is a fundamental matrix of (
7.2.7
), namely

\dot{Z}(s)=F(s,\eta)Z(s),\qquad Z(t)=I.

By Liouville’s formula (see Theorem
1.1.2
from Chapter 1) applied to (
7.2.7
) we have, for every
s\leq t
,

\det Z_{\eta}(s)=\exp\left(\int_{t}^{s}\textnormal{Tr}\,F(r,\eta)\,dr\right)=\exp\left(-\int_{s}^{t}\textnormal{Tr}\,F(r,\eta)\,dr\right)>0.

Since
F(r,\eta)=X(t,r)Df(r,y(r,t,\eta)X(r,t)
and
X(r,t)=X(t,r)^{-1}
,
by using the cyclicity of the trace, we obtain the invariance of the trace under conjugation,

\textnormal{Tr}\,F(r,\eta)=\textnormal{Tr}\,Df(r,y(r,t,\eta)).

Therefore, for any fixed
t\in\mathbb{R}
we have that

\frac{\partial G(t,\eta)}{\partial\eta}=\lim_{s\to-\infty}\det Z_{\eta}(s)=\exp\left(-\int_{-\infty}^{t}\textnormal{Tr}\,Df(r,y(r,t,\eta)\,dr\right).

Hence, under assumption
(D2)
, we that
\det\frac{\partial G(t,\eta)}{\partial\eta}>0
and
\frac{\partial G(t,\eta)}{\partial\eta}
is invertible for any fixed
t\in\mathbb{R}
.
Since
\eta\mapsto G(t,\eta)
is a homeomorphism, the inverse function theorem implies that
\eta\mapsto G(t,\eta)
is a global

C^{1}
diffeomorphism preserving orientation.
∎

Remark 7.2.2
.

The matrix differential equation (
7.2.6
) can be seen as a perturbation of the matrix equation

(7.2.8)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}\dot{W}(s)&=&A(s)W(s)\\
W(t)&=&I\end{array}\right.

related to (
6.1.1
). In addition, (
7.2.8
) has a solution
s\mapsto W(s)=X(s,t)
for any
fixed
t\in\mathbb{R}
. Moreover, notice that
X(t,s)W(s)=I
while Theorem
7.2.1
says
that
s\mapsto X(t,s)\frac{\partial y(s,t,\eta)}{\partial\eta}
exists when
s\to-\infty
. This fact prompt us that the asymptotic behavior of
(
7.2.6
) and (
7.2.8
) when
s\to-\infty
has some relation weaker than asymptotic equivalence. Indeed,
in
[
3
]
it is proved that
(D1)
is a necessary condition for asymptotic equivalence between a linear system
and a linear perturbation.

Theorem 7.2.2
.

If (
7.1.1
) and (
7.1.2
) satisfy
(A1)–(A4)
and
(D1)–(D3)
then, for
any fixed
t
, the function
\eta\mapsto G(t,\eta)
is a
C^{2}
preserving orientation diffeomorphism.

Proof.

Let us denote
\eta=(\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{n})
. As in the previous result, the proof will
be decomposed in several steps:

Step 1:
About
\partial^{2}G(t,\eta)/\partial\eta_{j}\partial\eta_{i}
.

For any
i,j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
, we can verify that

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}G}{\partial\eta_{j}\partial\eta_{i}}(t,\eta)&=&\displaystyle-\int_{-\infty}^{t}\hskip-8.5359ptX(t,s)D^{2}f(s,y(s,t,\eta))\frac{\partial y(s,t,\eta)}{\partial\eta_{j}}\frac{\partial y(s,t,\eta)}{\partial\eta_{i}}\,ds\\
\\
&&\displaystyle-\int_{-\infty}^{t}X(t,s)Df(s,y(s,t,\eta))\frac{\partial^{2}y(s,t,\eta)}{\partial\eta_{j}\partial\eta_{i}}\,ds.\end{array}

Now, by using (
7.2.2
) and (
1.1.7
) from Chapter 1, the reader can see that

(7.2.9)

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}G}{\partial\eta_{j}\partial\eta_{i}}(t,\eta)&=&\displaystyle-\int_{-\infty}^{t}\frac{d}{ds}\Big\{X(t,s)\frac{\partial^{2}y(s,t,\eta)}{\partial\eta_{j}\partial\eta_{i}}\Big\}\,ds\\
\\
&=&\displaystyle\lim\limits_{s\to-\infty}X(t,s)\frac{\partial^{2}y(s,t,\eta)}{\partial y_{j}\partial y_{i}}\end{array}

and the existence of
\partial^{2}G(t,\eta)/\partial\eta_{j}\partial\eta_{i}
follows if and only if the limit above exists.

Step 2:

\partial^{2}G(t,\eta)/\partial\eta_{j}\partial\eta_{i}
is well defined.

By using (
7.2.1
) and (
1.1.7
) from Chapter 1, we can see that
s\mapsto X(t,s)\partial y(s,t,\eta)/\partial\eta_{i}
is a
solution of (
7.2.3
) passing through
e_{i}
at
s=t
. In consequence, we can deduce that

X(t,s)\frac{\partial y(s,t,\eta)}{\partial\eta_{i}}=Z_{\eta}(s)e_{i}

and

X(t,s)\frac{\partial^{2}y(s,t,\eta)}{\partial\eta_{j}\partial\eta_{i}}=\frac{\partial Z_{\eta}(s)}{\partial\eta_{j}}e_{i}.

By
(D3)
, the last identity has limit when
s\to-\infty
and

\partial^{2}G(t,\eta)/\partial\eta_{j}\partial\eta_{i}
is well defined and continuous with respect to
\eta

for any fixed
t\in\mathbb{R}
.
∎

7.3.
Second approach on the half–line

A careful reading of the previous section shows that the first and second derivatives of

\eta\mapsto G(t,\eta)
for any
t\in\mathbb{R}
are related to the existence of limits when
t\to-\infty
, which are ensured
by rather restrictive assumptions as
(D1)
and
(D3)
. In order to address this issue, notice that
theses assumptions can be dropped if we restrict the parameter
t\in[0,+\infty)
. Nevertheless, this prompt us to
tailor the topological equivalence Definition
6.1.1
from Chapter 6 for any
t\in J\subset\mathbb{R}
as follows:

Definition 7.3.1
.

The systems
(
6.1.1
)
and
(
6.1.2
)
are
J
–continuously topologically equivalent if
there exists a function
H\colon J\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
with the properties

(i)

If
t\mapsto x(t)
is a solution of
(
7.1.1
)
, then
t\mapsto H[t,x(t)]
is a solution
of
(
7.1.2
)
,

(ii)

H(t,u)-u
is bounded in
J\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

(iii)

For each fixed
t\in J
,
u\mapsto H(t,u)
is an homeomorphism of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

(iv)

H
is continuous in any
(t,u)\in J\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

In addition, the function
u\mapsto G(t,u)=H^{-1}(t,u)
has properties
(ii)–(iv)
and maps solutions of
(
7.1.2
)
into solutions of
(
7.1.1
)
.

The above definition encompasses Definition
6.1.1
since
J=\mathbb{R}
is only a particular case.
Furthermore, observe that the continuity of
H
and
G
in any
(t,u)\in J\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
has not been considered in Definition
6.1.1
. However, a careful
review of the topological equivalence literature shows that this property has a disparate treatment. Indeed, it has been considered by Palmer
[
98
, p.754]
and omitted by
Shi
et al.

[
119
, p.814]
. Furthermore, in order to simplify the text, we will refer to
H
and
G
as the
Palmer’s homeomorphisms
.

To the best of our knowledge, the differentiability of the Palmer’s homeomorphisms
u\mapsto H(t,u)
and
u\mapsto G(t,u)
in for any
t\in J
has not been studied before
[
30
]
, which prompt us to introduce the following definition:

Definition 7.3.2
.

The systems
(
7.1.1
)
and
(
7.1.2
)
are
C^{r}
continuously topologically equivalent on
J
if:

(i)

The systems are
J-
continuously topologically equivalent,

(ii)

For any fixed
t\in J
; the map
u\mapsto H(t,u)
is a
C^{r}
–diffeomorphism of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
with
r\geq 1
,

(iii)

The partial derivatives of
H
and
G
up to order
r
with respect to
u
are continuous functions of
(t,u)\in J\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

In this subsection, we work on
J=\mathbb{R}^{+}
and we are interested to show that the systems (
7.1.1
) and (
7.1.2
) are
C^{r}
continuously topologically equivalent on
\mathbb{R}^{+}.
For this purpose, we require the properties
(A1)–(A4)
to be satisfied for
t\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
rather than
t\in\mathbb{R}.

The following Theorem has a proof similar to the linearization result studied in the Chapter 6. Nevertheless,
the restriction of
t
to the interval
[0,+\infty)
induces some subtleties that deserve to be treated in detail. In particular, a noteworthy consequence is that all the solutions of (
7.1.1
) and (
7.1.2
) are bounded on
[0,+\infty)
.

Theorem 7.3.1
.

If the systems (
7.1.1
) and (
7.1.2
) satisfy
(A1)–(A4)
for any
t\in[0,+\infty)
, then they are
\mathbb{R}^{+}-
continuously topologically equivalent.

Proof.

In order to make a more readable proof, we will decompose it in several steps. Namely, the step 1 defines two auxiliary systems whose solutions are used in the step 2 to construct the maps
H
and
G
. To prove that these maps establish a topological equivalence, the properties (i)–(ii) from Definition
7.3.1
are verified in the step 3, while the uniform continuity is proved in the steps 4 and 5. Finally the continuity of
H
in any
(t_{0},\xi_{0})\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
is proved in the step 6.

Step 1: Preliminaries.
We will consider the initial value problems

(7.3.1)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}\dot{w}&=&A(t)w-f(t,y(t,\tau,\eta))\\
w(0)&=&0,\end{array}\right.

and

(7.3.2)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}\dot{z}&=&A(t)z+f(t,x(t,\tau,\xi)+z)\\
z(0)&=&0.\end{array}\right.

By using the variation of parameters formula we have that

(7.3.3)

w^{*}(t;(\tau,\eta))=-\int_{0}^{t}X(t,s)f(s,y(s,\tau,\eta))\,ds

is the unique solution of (
7.3.1
). Now, in order to study (
7.3.2
) let
BC(\mathbb{R}^{+},\mathbb{R}^{n})
be the Banach space of bounded continuous functions with the supremum norm. Now, for any pair
(\tau,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{n},
by using
(A2)
we define the operator
\Gamma_{(\tau,\xi)}\colon BC(\mathbb{R}^{+},\mathbb{R}^{n})\to BC(\mathbb{R}^{+},\mathbb{R}^{n})
as follows

\phi(t)\mapsto\Gamma_{(\tau,\xi)}\phi(t):=\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}X(t,s)f(s,x(s,\tau,\xi)+\phi)\,ds.

Since
2\gamma K/\alpha<1
it is easy to see by
(A3)
that the operator
\Gamma_{(\tau,\xi)}
is a contraction and by the Banach fixed point theorem it follows that

z^{*}(t;(\tau,\xi))=\int_{0}^{t}X(t,s)f(s,x(s,\tau,\xi)+z^{*}(s;(\tau,\xi)))\,ds

is the unique solution of (
7.3.2
).

On the other hand, by uniqueness of solutions it can be proved that

(7.3.4)

z^{*}(t;(\tau,\xi))=z^{*}(t;(r,x(r,\tau,\xi)))\quad\textnormal{for any $r\geq 0$},

and

w^{*}(t;(\tau,\eta))=w^{*}(t;(r,y(r,\tau,\eta)))\quad\textnormal{for any $r\geq 0$}.

Step 2: Construction of the maps
H
and
G
.
For any
t\geq 0
we define the maps
H(t,\cdot)\colon\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
and

G(t,\cdot)\colon\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
as follows:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}H(t,\xi)&:=&\displaystyle\xi+\int_{0}^{t}X(t,s)f(s,x(s,t,\xi)+z^{*}(s;(t,\xi)))\,ds\\
\\
&=&\xi+z^{*}(t;(t,\xi)),\end{array}

and

\begin{array}[]{rcl}G(t,\eta)&:=&\displaystyle\eta-\int_{0}^{t}X(t,s)f(s,y(s,t,\eta))\,ds\\
\\
&=&\eta+w^{*}(t;(t,\eta)).\end{array}

Let
t\mapsto x(t,\tau,\xi)
be a solution of (
7.1.1
) passing trough
\xi
at
t=\tau
. By using (
7.3.4
), we can verify that

\begin{array}[]{rcl}H[t,x(t,\tau,\xi)]&=&\displaystyle x(t,\tau,\xi)+\int_{0}^{t}X(t,s)f(s,x(s,t,x(t,\tau,\xi)+z^{*}(s;(t,x(t,\tau,\xi))))\,ds\\
\\
&=&\displaystyle x(t,\tau,\xi)+\int_{0}^{t}X(t,s)f(s,x(s,\tau,\xi)+z^{*}(s;(\tau,\xi)))\,ds\\
\\
&=&x(t,\tau,\xi)+z^{*}(t;(\tau,\xi)).\end{array}

Step 3:
H
and
G
satisfy properties
(i)–(ii)
of Definition
7.3.1
.

By (
7.1.1
) and (
7.3.2
) combined with the above equality, we have that

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}H[t,x(t,\tau,\xi)]&=&\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}x(t,\tau,\xi)+\frac{\partial}{\partial t}z^{*}(t;(\tau,\xi))\\
\\
&=&A(t)x(t,\tau,\xi)+A(t)z^{*}(t;(\tau,\xi))+f(t,H[t,x(t,\tau,\xi)])\\
\\
&=&A(t)H[t,x(t,\tau,\xi)]+f(t,H[t,x(t,\tau,\xi)]),\end{array}

then
t\mapsto H[t,x(t,\tau,\xi)]
is solution of (
6.1.2
) passing trough

H(\tau,\xi)
at
t=\tau
. As consequence of uniqueness of solution we obtain

(7.3.5)

H[t,x(t,\tau,\xi)]=y(t,\tau,H(\tau,\xi)),

similarly, it can be proved that
t\mapsto G[t,y(t,\tau,\eta)]
is solution of (
6.1.1
) passing through
G(\tau,\eta)

at
t=\tau
and

G[t,y(t,\tau,\eta)]=x(t,\tau,G(\tau,\eta))=X(t,\tau)G(\tau,\eta),

and the property (i) follows.
Secondly, by using (
A2
) and (
A4
) it follows that

|H(t,\xi)-\xi|\leq K\mu\int_{0}^{t}e^{-\alpha(t-s)}\,ds\leq\displaystyle\frac{K\mu}{\alpha}

for any
t\geq 0
. A similar inequality can be obtained for

|G(t,\eta)-\eta|
and the property (ii) is verified.

Step 4:
H
is bijective for any
t\geq 0
.
We will
first show the identity
H(t,G(t,\eta))=\eta
for any
t\geq 0
. Indeed,

\begin{array}[]{rcl}H[t,G[t,y(t,\tau,\eta)]]&=&G[t,y(t,\tau,\eta)]\\
\\
&&+\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}X(t,s)f(s,x(s,t,G[t,y(t,\tau,\eta)])+z^{*}(s;(t,G[t,y(t,\tau,\eta)])))\,ds\\
\\
&=&y(t,\tau,\eta)-\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}X(t,s)f(s,y(s,\tau,\eta))\,ds\\
\\
&&+\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}X(t,s)f(s,x(s,t,G[t,y(t,\tau,\eta)])+z^{*}(s;(t,G[t,y(t,\tau,\eta)])))\,ds.\end{array}

Let
\omega(t)=|H[t,G[t,y(t,\tau,\eta)]]-y(t,\tau,\eta)|.
Hence by using
(A2)
and
(A3)
we have that

\begin{array}[]{ll}\omega(t)&=\left|\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}X(t,s)\{f(s,x(s,t,G[t,y(t,\tau,\eta)])+z^{*}(s;(t,G[t,y(t,\tau,\eta)])))-f(s,y(s,\tau,\eta))\}\,ds\right|\\
\\
&\leq K\gamma\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}e^{-\alpha(t-s)}|\{x(s,t,G[t,y(t,\tau,\eta)])+z^{*}(s;(t,G[t,y(t,\tau,\eta)]))-y(s,\tau,\eta)\}|\,ds.\end{array}

The boundedness of all the solutions of systems (
7.1.1
) and (
7.1.2
) on
\mathbb{R}^{+}

combined with
s\mapsto z^{*}(s;(t,G[t,y(t,\tau,\eta)]))\in BC(\mathbb{R}^{+},\mathbb{R}^{n})
implies that
\sup\limits_{t\geq 0}\{\omega(t)\}<+\infty
.

In addition, by using again the definition of
\xi\mapsto H(s,\xi)
we can deduce that

x(s,t,G[t,y(t,\tau,\eta)])+z^{*}(s;(t,G[t,y(t,\tau,\eta)]))=H[s,x(s,t,G[t,y(t,\tau,\eta)])],

which leads to

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\omega(t)&\leq&K\gamma\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}e^{-\alpha(t-s)}|\{H[s,x(s,t,G[t,y(t,\tau,\eta)])]-y(s,\tau,\eta)\}|\,ds\\
\\
&=&K\gamma\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}e^{-\alpha(t-s)}|\{H[s,G[s,y(s,\tau,\eta)]]-y(s,\tau,\eta)\}|\,ds,\end{array}

where the last step follows from the identities

x(s,t,G[t,y(t,\tau,\eta)])=x(s,\tau,G(\tau,\eta))=G[s,y(s,\tau,\eta)].

Therefore, we obtain

\omega(t)\leq K\gamma\int_{0}^{t}e^{-\alpha(t-s)}\omega(s)\,ds\leq\frac{K\gamma}{\alpha}\displaystyle\sup_{s\in\mathbb{R}^{+}}\{\omega(s)\}\quad\rm{for\,\,all}\quad t\geq 0.

Now, we take the supremum on the left side above and due to
K\gamma/\alpha<1
it follows that
\omega(t)=0
for any
t\geq 0.
In particular, when we take
t=\tau
we obtain
H(\tau,G(\tau,\eta))=\eta.

Next, we will prove that
G(t,H(t,\xi))=\xi.
In fact, due to (
7.3.5
) we have that

\begin{array}[]{rcl}G[t,H[t,x(t,\tau,\xi)]]&=&H[t,x(t,\tau,\xi)]\\
\\
&&-\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}X(t,s)f(s,y(s,t,H[t,y(x,\tau,\xi)]))\,ds\\
\\
&=&x(t,\tau,\xi)\\
\\
&&\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}X(t,s)\{f(s,H[s,x(s,\tau,\xi)])-f(s,y(s,\tau,H(\tau,\xi)))\}\,ds\\
\\
&=&x(t,\tau,\xi),\end{array}

and taking
t=\tau
leads to
G(\tau,H(\tau,\xi))=\xi
. In consequence, for any
t\geq 0
,
\xi\mapsto H(t,\xi)
is a bijection and
\eta\mapsto G(t,\eta)
is its inverse.

Step 5:
H
and
G
are uniformly continuous for any fixed
t
.
Firstly, we prove that
\eta\mapsto G(t,\eta)
is uniformly continuous.

By using the property
(A1)
combined with Corollary
1.2.1
from Chapter 1, we can see that

||X(t,s)||\leq||I||e^{M|t-s|}
for any
t,s\geq 0
. Then Lemma
2.2.4
from Chapter 2 says that
\alpha\leq M
.
Now, we construct the auxiliary functions
\theta,\theta_{0}\colon[0,+\infty)\to[0,+\infty)
defined by

\theta(t)=1+K\gamma\left(\frac{e^{(M+\gamma-\alpha)t}-1}{M+\gamma-\alpha}\right)\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\theta_{0}(t)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}K\gamma t&\textnormal{if}&\alpha=M,\\
\\
K\gamma\left(\frac{e^{(M-\alpha)t}-1}{M-\alpha}\right)&\textnormal{if}&\alpha<M.\end{array}\right.

Now, given
\varepsilon>0
, let us define the constants

(7.3.6)

L(\varepsilon)=\frac{1}{\alpha}\ln\left(\frac{2\mu K}{\alpha\varepsilon}\right),\,\,\theta_{0}^{*}=\max\limits_{t\in[0,L(\varepsilon)]}\theta_{0}(t)\,\,\textnormal{and}\,\,\theta^{*}=\max\limits_{t\in[0,L(\varepsilon)]}\theta(t).

We will prove the uniform continuity of
G
by considering two cases:

Case i)
t\in[0,L(\varepsilon)]
. By
(A2)
and
(A4)
we can deduce that

(7.3.7)

|G(t,\eta)-G(t,\bar{\eta})|\leq|\eta-\bar{\eta}|+K\gamma\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}e^{-\alpha(t-s)}|y(s,t,\eta)-y(s,t,\bar{\eta})|\,ds.

Now, by
(A1)
,
(A3)
and Proposition
6.2.1
from Chapter 6 we know that
for any
0\leq s\leq t
:

(7.3.8)

|y(s,t,\eta)-y(s,t,\bar{\eta})|\leq|\eta-\bar{\eta}|e^{(M+\gamma)(t-s)}.

Upon inserting (
7.3.8
) in (
7.3.7
), we obtain that

\begin{array}[]{rcl}|G(t,\eta)-G(t,\bar{\eta})|&\leq&\left(1+K\gamma e^{(M+\gamma-\alpha)t}\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}e^{-(M+\gamma-\alpha)s}\right)|\eta-\bar{\eta}|\\
\\
&=&\displaystyle\left(1+K\gamma\left\{\frac{e^{(M+\gamma-\alpha)t}-1}{M+\gamma-\alpha}\right\}\right)|\eta-\bar{\eta}|\\
\\
&\leq&\theta(t)|\eta-\bar{\eta}|\leq\theta^{*}|\eta-\bar{\eta}|.\end{array}

Case ii)
t>L(\varepsilon)
. By
(A2)
–
(A4)
, we have that

(7.3.9)

\begin{array}[]{rcl}|G(t,\eta)-G(t,\bar{\eta})|&\leq&\displaystyle|\eta-\bar{\eta}|+\mu K\int_{0}^{t-L(\varepsilon)}e^{-\alpha(t-s)}\,ds\\
\\
&&\displaystyle+K\gamma\int_{t-L(\varepsilon)}^{t}e^{-\alpha(t-s)}|y(s,t,\eta)-y(s,t,\bar{\eta})|\,ds\\
\\
&\leq&\displaystyle|\eta-\bar{\eta}|+\frac{\mu K}{\alpha}e^{-\alpha L(\varepsilon)}\\
\\
&&\displaystyle+K\gamma\int_{0}^{L(\varepsilon)}e^{-\alpha u}|y(t-u,t,\eta)-y(t-u,t,\bar{\eta})|\,du.\end{array}

As in case i), the inequality (
7.3.8
)
implies that

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\displaystyle K\gamma\int_{0}^{L(\varepsilon)}e^{-\alpha u}|y(t-u,t,\eta)-y(t-u,t,\bar{\eta})|\,du&\leq&\displaystyle K\gamma\int_{0}^{L(\varepsilon)}e^{(M+\gamma-\alpha)u}|\eta-\bar{\eta}|\,du\\
\\
&=&\displaystyle K\gamma\left\{\frac{e^{(M+\gamma-\alpha)L(\varepsilon)}-1}{M+\gamma-\alpha}\right\}|\eta-\bar{\eta}|.\end{array}

Upon inserting the above inequality in (
7.3.9
) and using (
7.3.6
), we have that

\begin{array}[]{rcl}|G(t,\eta)-G(t,\bar{\eta})|&\leq&\displaystyle\left(1+K\gamma\left\{\frac{e^{(M+\gamma-\alpha)L(\varepsilon)}-1}{M+\gamma-\alpha}\right\}\right)|\eta-\bar{\eta}|+\frac{\mu K}{\alpha}e^{-\alpha L(\varepsilon)}\\
\\
&\leq&\displaystyle\theta^{*}|\eta-\bar{\eta}|+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}.\end{array}

Summarizing, given
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
L(\varepsilon)>0
and
\theta^{*}>0
such that:

|G(t,\eta)-G(t,\bar{\eta})|\leq\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}\displaystyle\theta^{*}|\eta-\bar{\eta}|&\textnormal{if}&t\in[0,L(\varepsilon)]\\
\\
\displaystyle\theta^{*}|\eta-\bar{\eta}|+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}&\textnormal{if}&t>L(\varepsilon),\end{array}\right.

then it follows that

\forall\varepsilon>0\,\exists\delta(\varepsilon):=\frac{\varepsilon}{2\theta^{*}}\quad\textnormal{such that}\quad|\eta-\bar{\eta}|<\delta\Rightarrow|G(t,\eta)-G(t,\bar{\eta})|<\varepsilon

and the uniform continuity of
G
follows.

Finally, we will prove that
\xi\mapsto H(t,\xi)
is uniformly continuous for any
t\geq 0
. As the identity is uniformly continuous, we will only prove that the map
\xi\mapsto z^{*}(t;(t,\xi))
is uniformly continuous.

Note that the fixed point
z^{*}(t;(t,\xi))
can be seen as the uniform limit on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
of a sequence
z_{j}^{*}(t;(t,\xi))
defined recursively as follows:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}z_{j+1}^{*}(t;(t,\xi))&=&\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}X(t,s)f(s,x(s,t,\xi)+z_{j}^{*}(s;(t,\xi)))\,ds\quad\textnormal{for any $j\geq 1$},\\
\\
z_{0}^{*}(t;(t,\xi))&=&0.\end{array}\right.

The uniform continuity of each map
\xi\mapsto z_{j}^{*}(t;(t,\xi))
will be proved inductively by following the lines of
[
75
,
119
]
. First, it is clear that

\xi\mapsto z_{0}^{*}(t;(t,\xi))
verify this property. Secondly, we will assume the inductive hypothesis

\forall\varepsilon>0\,\exists\delta_{j}(\varepsilon)>0\,\,\textnormal{s.t.}\,|\xi-\bar{\xi}|<\delta_{j}\Rightarrow|z_{j}^{*}(t;(t,\xi))-z_{j}^{*}(t;(t,\bar{\xi}))|<\varepsilon\,\,\textnormal{for any $t\geq 0$}.

For the step
j+1
and given
\varepsilon>0
, we will only consider
\alpha<M
since the case
\alpha=M
can be carried out easily. We will use the constants
L(\varepsilon)
and
\theta_{0}^{*}
defined in (
7.3.6
) and introduce the notation

\Delta_{j}(t,\xi,\bar{\xi})=z_{j}^{*}(t;(t,\xi))-z_{j}^{*}(t;(t,\bar{\xi})).

As before, we will distinguish the cases
t\in[0,L(\varepsilon)]
and
t>L(\varepsilon)
. First, for
t\in[0,L(\varepsilon)]
we use
(A1)
combined with the estimation

(7.3.10)

|x(s,t,\xi)-x(s,t,\bar{\xi})|\leq|\xi-\bar{\xi}|e^{M|t-s|},

and we can
verify that

\begin{array}[]{rcl}|\Delta_{j+1}(t,\xi,\bar{\xi})|&\leq&\displaystyle K\gamma e^{-\alpha t}\int_{0}^{t}e^{\alpha s}\{|x(s,t,\xi)-x(s,t,\bar{\xi})|+|\Delta_{j}(s,\xi,\bar{\xi})|\}\,ds\\
\\
&\leq&\displaystyle K\gamma e^{-\alpha t}\int_{0}^{t}e^{\alpha s}\{|\xi-\bar{\xi}|e^{M(t-s)}+||\Delta_{j}(\cdot,\xi,\bar{\xi})||_{\infty}\}\,ds\\
\\
&\leq&\displaystyle K\gamma\left\{\frac{e^{(M-\alpha)t}-1}{M-\alpha}\right\}|\xi-\bar{\xi}|+\frac{K\gamma}{\alpha}||\Delta_{j}(\cdot,\xi,\bar{\xi})||_{\infty}\\
\\
&\leq&\displaystyle\theta_{0}^{*}|\xi-\bar{\xi}|+\frac{K\gamma}{\alpha}||\Delta_{j}(\cdot,\xi,\bar{\xi})||_{\infty},\end{array}

where
||\Delta_{j}(\cdot,\xi,\bar{\xi})||_{\infty}=\sup\limits_{t\geq 0}|\Delta_{j}(t,\xi,\bar{\xi})|
.

On the other hand, when
t>L(\varepsilon)
, we use
(A2)
,
(A4)
combined with the boundedness of
f
in
[0,t-L(\varepsilon)]
and Lipschitzness in
[t-L(\varepsilon),t)
to deduce that

\begin{array}[]{rcl}|\Delta_{j+1}(t,\xi,\bar{\xi})|&\leq&\displaystyle K\mu\int_{0}^{t-L(\varepsilon)}e^{-\alpha(t-s)}\,ds\\
\\
&&\displaystyle+K\gamma\int_{t-L(\varepsilon)}^{t}e^{-\alpha(t-s)}\{|x(s,t,\xi)-x(s,t,\bar{\xi})|+|\Delta_{j}(s,\xi,\bar{\xi})|\}\,ds.\end{array}

By using
(A4)
combined with
u=t-s
, (
7.3.6
) and (
7.3.10
), we have that

\begin{array}[]{rcl}|\Delta_{j+1}(t,\xi,\bar{\xi})|&\leq&\displaystyle\frac{K\mu}{\alpha}e^{-\alpha L(\varepsilon)}+\frac{K\gamma}{\alpha}||\Delta_{j}(\cdot,\xi,\bar{\xi})||_{\infty}\\
\\
&&\displaystyle+K\gamma\int_{0}^{L(\varepsilon)}e^{-\alpha u}|x(t-u,t,\xi)-x(t-u,t,\bar{\xi})|\,du\\
\\
&\leq&\displaystyle\frac{K\mu}{\alpha}e^{-\alpha L(\varepsilon)}+\frac{K\gamma}{\alpha}||\Delta_{j}(\cdot,\xi,\bar{\xi})||_{\infty}+K\gamma|\xi-\bar{\xi}|\int_{0}^{L(\varepsilon)}e^{(M-\alpha)u}\,du\\
\\
&\leq&\displaystyle\frac{\varepsilon}{2}+\frac{K\gamma}{\alpha}||\Delta_{j}(\cdot,\xi,\bar{\xi})||_{\infty}+K\gamma\left\{\frac{e^{(M-\alpha)L(\varepsilon)}-1}{M-\alpha}\right\}|\xi-\bar{\xi}|\\
\\
&\leq&\displaystyle\frac{\varepsilon}{2}+\frac{K\gamma}{\alpha}||\Delta_{j}(\cdot,\xi,\bar{\xi})||_{\infty}+\theta_{0}^{*}|\xi-\bar{\xi}|.\end{array}

Summarizing, for any
t\geq 0
it follows that

|\Delta_{j+1}(t,\xi,\bar{\xi})|\leq\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}\displaystyle\theta_{0}^{*}|\xi-\bar{\xi}|+\frac{K\gamma}{\alpha}||\Delta_{j}(\cdot,\xi,\bar{\xi})||_{\infty}&\textnormal{if}&t\in[0,L(\varepsilon)]\\
\\
\displaystyle\frac{\varepsilon}{2}+\frac{K\gamma}{\alpha}||\Delta_{j}(\cdot,\xi,\bar{\xi})||_{\infty}+\theta_{0}^{*}|\xi-\bar{\xi}|&\textnormal{if}&t>L(\varepsilon).\end{array}\right.

Now, for any
\varepsilon>0
there exists
L(\varepsilon)>0
,
\theta_{0}^{*}>0
and

\delta_{j+1}(\varepsilon)=\min\left\{\delta_{j}(\varepsilon/2),\frac{\varepsilon}{\theta_{0}^{*}}\left(1-\frac{K\gamma}{\alpha}\right)\right\}

such that for any
t\geq 0
, we have

\forall\varepsilon>0\,\exists\delta_{j+1}(\varepsilon)>0\,\,\textnormal{s.t.}\,|\xi-\bar{\xi}|<\delta_{j+1}\Rightarrow|z_{j+1}^{*}(t;(t,\xi))-z_{j+1}^{*}(t;(t,\bar{\xi}))|<\varepsilon.

and the uniform continuity of
\xi\mapsto z_{j}^{*}(t;(t,\xi))
follows for any
j\in\mathbb{N}
.

In order to finish our proof, we choose
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that for any
j>N
it follows that

||z^{*}(\cdot;(\cdot,\xi))-z_{j}^{*}(\cdot;(\cdot,\xi))||_{\infty}<\varepsilon\quad\textnormal{for any}\quad\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{n},

and therefore, if
|\xi-\bar{\xi}|<\delta_{j}
with
j>N
, it is true that

\begin{array}[]{rcl}|z^{*}(t;(t,\xi))-z^{*}(t;(t,\bar{\xi}))|&\leq&|z^{*}(t;(t,\xi))-z_{j}^{*}(t;(t,\xi))|+\Delta_{j}(t,\xi,\bar{\xi})\\
\\
&&+|z^{*}(t;(t,\bar{\xi}))-z_{j}^{*}(t;(t,\bar{\xi}))|<3\varepsilon,\end{array}

and the uniform continuity of
\xi\mapsto z^{*}(t;(t,\xi))
and
\xi\mapsto H(t,\xi)
follows for any fixed
t\geq 0
.

Step 6:
H
and
G
are continuous on
\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}.
By using the continuity of the solutions with respect to the initial time and initial conditions
[
64
, Ch.V]
, we note that for any
\varepsilon_{1}>0
there exists
\delta_{1}(t_{0},\xi_{0},\varepsilon_{1})>0
such that

(7.3.11)

|x(s,t,\xi)-x(s,t_{0},\xi_{0})|<\varepsilon_{1}\quad\textnormal{when}\quad|t-t_{0}|+|\xi-\xi_{0}|<\delta_{1}

for any
t
and
s
in a compact interval of
\mathbb{R}^{+}
containing
t_{0}
.

On the other hand, by using the continuity of the solutions with respect of the parameters
[
64
, Ch.V]
combined with a Palmer’s result
[
98
, Lemma 1]
restricted to
\mathbb{R}^{+}
, we know that for any
\varepsilon_{2}>0
there exists
\delta_{2}(t_{0},\xi_{0},\varepsilon_{2})>0
such that

(7.3.12)

|z^{*}(s;(t,\xi))-z^{*}(s;(t_{0},\xi_{0}))|<\varepsilon_{2}\quad\textnormal{when}\quad|t-t_{0}|+|\xi-\xi_{0}|<\delta_{2},

for any
t
and
s
in a compact interval of
\mathbb{R}^{+}
containing
t_{0}
.

Additionally, we know that for any
\varepsilon_{3}>0
there exists
\delta_{3}(\varepsilon_{3},t_{0})
such that

(7.3.13)

||X(t,s)-X(t_{0},s)||<\varepsilon_{3}\quad\textnormal{when}\quad|t-t_{0}|<\delta_{3}

for any
t
and
s
in a compact interval of
\mathbb{R}^{+}
containing
t_{0}
.

From now on, we will assume that
t
,
s
and
t_{0}
are in a compact interval
I\subset\mathbb{R}^{+}
and we denote

\omega_{1}(s,t,t_{0},\xi,\xi_{0})=f(s,x(s,t,\xi)+z^{*}(s;(t,\xi)))-f(s,x(s,t_{0},\xi_{0})+z^{*}(s;(t_{0},\xi_{0}))).

Without loss of generality, we will assume that
t>t_{0}
. Now, notice that

\begin{array}[]{rcl}H(t,\xi)-H(t_{0},\xi_{0})&=&\displaystyle\xi-\xi_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}X(t,s)f(s,x(s,t,\xi)+z^{*}(s;(t,\xi)))\,ds\\
\\
&&\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{t_{0}}X(t_{0},s)f(s,x(s,t_{0},\xi_{0})+z^{*}(s;(t_{0},\xi_{0})))\,ds\\
\\
&=&\xi-\xi_{0}\\
\\
&&\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t_{0}}\{X(t,s)-X(t_{0},s)\}f(s,x(s,t,\xi)+z^{*}(s;(t,\xi)))\,ds\\
\\
&&\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t_{0}}X(t_{0},s)\omega_{1}(s,t,t_{0},\xi,\xi_{0})\,ds\\
\\
&&\displaystyle+\int_{t_{0}}^{t}X(t,s)f(s,x(s,t,\xi)+z^{*}(s;(t,\xi)))\,ds.\end{array}

Let
C=\max\{||X(u,s)||\colon u,s\in I\}
. By using
(A3)
combined with (
7.3.11
),(
7.3.12
) and (
7.3.13
), we deduce that

\begin{array}[]{rcl}|H(t,\xi)-H(t_{0},\xi_{0})|&\leq&\displaystyle|\xi-\xi_{0}|+\mu\int_{0}^{t_{0}}||X(t,s)-X(t_{0},s)||\,ds\\
\\
&&+\displaystyle\mu\int_{t_{0}}^{t}||X(t,s)||\,ds\\
\\
&&\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t_{0}}||X(t_{0},s)||\,|\omega_{1}(s,t,t_{0},\xi,\xi_{0})|\,ds\\
\\
&\leq&|\xi-\xi_{0}|+\mu t_{0}\varepsilon_{3}+C|t-t_{0}|\mu+C\gamma(\varepsilon_{1}+\varepsilon_{2})t_{0},\end{array}

and we conclude that
H
is continuous in any
(t_{0},\xi_{0})\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Finally, the result follows by verifying that
G
is continuous in any
(t_{0},\eta_{0})\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
, which can be proved
in a similar way.
∎

In order to establish the main result of this subsection, we introduce the following additional condition:

(D4)

The function
f(t,x)
and its derivatives with respect to
x
up to order
r
–th are continuous functions of
(t,x)
.

A direct consequence of the above property (see
e.g.

[
36
, Chap.2]
) is that

\partial y(t,\tau,\eta)/\partial\eta
satisfies the matrix differential equation (
7.2.1
).

The following result provides sufficient conditions ensuring that the maps
\xi\mapsto H(t,\xi)
and
\eta\mapsto G(t,\eta)
satisfy the properties stated in Definition
7.3.2
.

Theorem 7.3.2
.

If
(A1)–(A4)
and
(D4)
are satisfied, then
(
6.1.1
)
and
(
6.1.2
)
are
C^{r}
continuously topologically equivalent on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
.

Proof.

The property (i) of Definition
7.3.2
is satisfied by Theorem
7.3.1
whereas the property (ii) will be established by cases.

Case
r=1
.
As
y\mapsto f(t,y)
is
C^{1}
for any
t\geq 0
, it follows that
y\mapsto Df(t,y)
and
\eta\mapsto\partial y/\partial\eta
are continuous.
This allows to calculate the first partial derivatives of the map
\eta\mapsto G(t,\eta)
for any
t\geq 0
and any
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
as follows

(7.3.14)

\frac{\partial G}{\partial\eta_{i}}(t,\eta)=e_{i}-\int_{0}^{t}X(t,s)Df(s,y(s,t,\eta))\frac{\partial y}{\partial\eta_{i}}(s,t,\eta)\,ds,

which implies that the partial derivatives exists and are continuous for any fixed
t\geq 0
, then
\eta\mapsto G(t,\eta)
is
C^{1}
.

By using the identity
X(t,s)A(s)=-\frac{\partial}{\partial s}X(t,s)
combined with
(
7.2.1
) we can deduce that for any
t\geq 0
, the Jacobian matrix is given by

(7.3.15)

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\displaystyle\frac{\partial G}{\partial\eta}(t,\eta)&=&\displaystyle I-\int_{0}^{t}X(t,s)Df(s,y(s,t,\eta))\frac{\partial y}{\partial\eta}(s,t,\eta)\,ds\\
\\
&=&I-\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\frac{d}{ds}\left\{X(t,s)\frac{\partial y}{\partial\eta}(s,t,\eta)\right\}\,ds\\
\\
&=&\displaystyle X(t,0)\frac{\partial y(0,t,\eta)}{\partial\eta},\end{array}

or, in a more concise manner

(7.3.16)

\frac{\partial G}{\partial\eta}(t,\eta)=X(t,0)\frac{\partial y(0,t,\eta)}{\partial\eta}.

In order to achieve our proof we will use a variation of the Hadamard’s Theorem
[
109
, Cor. 2.1]
which states that
\eta\mapsto G(t,\eta)\in C^{1}
is a diffeomorphism if and only if
\textnormal{Det}\frac{\partial G(t,\eta)}{\partial\eta}\neq 0

for any
\eta\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
|G(t,\eta)|\to+\infty
when
|\eta|\to+\infty
.

Notice that the right term of (
7.3.16
) is composed by two fundamental matrices
associated respectively to the linear systems (
7.1.1
) and (
7.2.1
). By applying the Liouville’s formula
(see Theorem
1.1.2
from Chapter 1 or Theorems 7.2 and 7.3 from
[
36
, Ch.1]
)
we can see that

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\textnormal{Det}\frac{\partial G}{\partial\eta}(t,\eta)&=&\textnormal{Det}X(t,0)\,\textnormal{Det}\frac{\partial y(0,t,\eta)}{\partial\eta}\\
\\
&=&\displaystyle\exp\left(\int_{0}^{t}\textnormal{Tr}A(s)\,ds\right)\exp\left(\int_{t}^{0}\textnormal{Tr}\{A(s)+Df(s,t,\eta)\}\,ds\right),\end{array}

and consequently
\textnormal{Det}\frac{\partial G(t,\eta)}{\partial\eta}>0
for any
t\geq 0
.

In addition, let us recall that

G(t,\eta)=\eta+w^{*}(t;(t,\eta)),

where
w^{*}(t;(t,\eta))
is given by (
7.3.3
), we can deduce that

|G(t,\eta)|\to+\infty
as
|\eta|\to+\infty
, due to
|w^{*}(t;(t,\eta))|\leq K\mu/\alpha
for any
(t,\eta)
. Therefore, we conclude that
\eta\mapsto G(t,\eta)
is a global diffeomorphism for any fixed
t\geq 0
.

Finally, by using again the property
(D4)
combined with Theorem 4.1 from
[
64
, Ch.V]
) we also can conclude that
(t,\eta)\mapsto\frac{\partial y(0,t,\eta)}{\partial\eta}
on
\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}

and by the identity (
7.3.16
) we have that
(t,\eta)\mapsto\frac{\partial G}{\partial\eta}(t,\eta)
is continuous on
\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
and this case follows.

Case
r=2
.
Due to
(D4)
we can verify that
the second partial derivatives
\partial^{2}y(s,\tau,\eta)/\partial\eta_{j}\partial\eta_{i}
satisfy
the system of differential equations

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\frac{\partial^{2}y}{\partial\eta_{j}\partial\eta_{i}}&=&\displaystyle\{A(t)+Df(t,y)\}\frac{\partial^{2}y}{\partial\eta_{j}\partial\eta_{i}}+D^{2}f(t,y)\frac{\partial y}{\partial\eta_{j}}\frac{\partial y}{\partial\eta_{i}}\\
\\
\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}y}{\partial\eta_{j}\partial\eta_{i}}&=&0,\end{array}\right.

for any
i,j=1,\ldots,n,
where
D^{2}f
is the formal second derivative of
f
and
y=y(t,\tau,\eta).
By using (
7.3.14
) we have

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}G}{\partial\eta_{j}\partial\eta_{i}}(t,\eta)&=&\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{t}X(t,s)D^{2}f(s,y(s,t,\eta))\frac{\partial y}{\partial\eta_{j}}(s,t,\eta)\frac{\partial y}{\partial\eta_{i}}(s,t,\eta)\,ds\\
\\
&&\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{t}X(t,s)Df(s,y(s,t,\eta))\frac{\partial^{2}y(s,t,\eta)}{\partial\eta_{j}\partial\eta_{i}}\,ds\\
\\
&=&\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{t}\frac{d}{ds}\left\{X(t,s)\frac{\partial^{2}y(s,t,\eta)}{\partial\eta_{j}\partial\eta_{i}}\right\}\,ds\\
\\
&=&\displaystyle X(t,0)\frac{\partial^{2}y(0,t,\eta)}{\partial\eta_{j}\partial\eta_{i}}.\par\end{array}

Thus, the map
\eta\mapsto G(t,\eta)
is
C^{2}
for any fixed
t\geq 0
. The identity
\xi=G(t,H(t,\xi))
for any fixed
t\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
, the Jacobian matrix of the identity map on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
can be seen as

DG(t,H(t,\xi))DH(t,\xi)=I\quad\textnormal{for any fixed $t\in\mathbb{R}^{+}$}.

By Case
r=1
, we have that
\eta\mapsto G(t,\eta)
is a diffeormorphism of class
C^{1}
for any fixed
t\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
, which implies that

(7.3.17)

DH(t,\xi)=[DG(t,H(t,\xi))]^{-1}\quad\textnormal{for any $t\in\mathbb{R}^{+}$}

is well defined. In addition, note that
(t,\xi)\mapsto DH(t,\xi)
is continuous since the maps
U\mapsto U^{-1}
and
(t,\xi)\mapsto DG(t,H(t,\xi))
are continuous for any
U\in Gl_{n}(\mathbb{R})
and
(t,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Now, differentiating again with respect to the second variable, we have the formal computation

D^{2}G(t,H(t,\xi))DH(t,\xi)DH(t,\xi)+DG(t,H(t,\xi))D^{2}H(t,\xi)=0

and the identity (
7.3.17
) implies that

(7.3.18)

D^{2}H(t,\xi)=-DH(t,\xi)D^{2}G(t,H(t,\xi))DH(t,\xi)DH(t,\xi).

It is easy to see that
D^{2}H(t,\xi)
is continuous with respect to
(t,\xi)
due to is a composition of maps that are continuous with respect to
(t,\xi).
Therefore
\eta\mapsto G(t,\eta)
is a global diffeomorphism of class
C^{2}
for any fixed
t\geq 0.

Case
r\geq 3
.
By using
(D4)
we can conclude that
\eta\mapsto y(0,t,\eta)
is
C^{r}
and the partial derivatives

(t,\eta)\mapsto\frac{\partial^{|m|}y(0,t,\eta)}{\partial\eta_{1}^{m_{1}}\cdots\partial\eta_{n}^{m_{n}}},\quad\textnormal{where $|m|=m_{1}+\ldots+m_{n}\leq r$},

are continuous for any
(t,\eta)\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Moreover, this fact combined with (
7.3.15
) shows that the partial derivatives up to order
r
–th of
G
with respect to
\eta

(t,\eta)\mapsto\frac{\partial^{|m|}G(t,\eta)}{\partial\eta_{1}^{m_{1}}\cdots\partial\eta_{n}^{m_{n}}}=X(t,0)\frac{\partial^{|m|}y(0,t,\eta)}{\partial\eta_{1}^{m_{1}}\cdots\partial\eta_{n}^{m_{n}}},

where
|m|=m_{1}+\ldots+m_{n}\leq r,

are continuous in
\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}
. Additionally, the higher formal derivatives of
H
up to order
r-
th and its continuity on
\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
can be deduced in a recursive way from (
7.3.17
) and (
7.3.18
).

The property (iii) of Definition
7.3.2
is immersed in the previous analysis. Therefore, the result follows.
∎

7.4.
Comments and References

1)
The differentiability results of the section 1 are inspired in the article
[
29
]
. In general, the section 1 improves the clarity of the presentation
and, in several intermediate steps, provides a clearer and simpler explanation. We also point out that, to the best of our knowledge, there are no previous works
devoted to the smoothness properties of the Palmer’s homeomorphism.

2)
The original motivation to study the smoothness of the Palmer’s homeomorphism is found in the framework of converse asymptotic stability results: note that

(A1)
–
(A4)
implies that (
7.1.1
) and (
7.1.2
) are uniformly asymptotically stable. On the other hand, a converse result from P. Monzón implies the existence
of a
Rantzer’s density function

r(t,x)
associated to (
7.1.1
) and an interesting achievement of the article
[
29
]
was the construction of a Rantzer’s density function
for (
7.1.2
) in terms of
r(t,x)
and the Palmer’s homeomorphism. We refer to Theorem 3 from
[
29
]
for details.

3)
The restrictive nature of hypotheses
(D1)
and
(D3)
prompted to restrict the time domain of the Palmer’s homeomorphism from

\mathbb{R}
to
[0,+\infty)
which was carried out in
[
30
]
and
[
31
]
and summarized in the section 2. The construction of homeomorphisms becomes much easier, while their differentiability properties are obtained with less restrictive hypotheses.

4)
In the context of Section 2, and under the additional assumption of existence of an equilibrium
\bar{y}
, namely

A(t)\bar{y}=f(t,\bar{y})=0\quad\textnormal{for any $t\geq 0$},

a byproduct of the global linearization result is
[
30
, Th.3.1]
:

Theorem 7.4.1
.

Assume that
(A1)–(A4)
are fulfilled, then

i)

If the system has an equilibrium
\bar{y}
, it is unique.

ii)

If
\bar{y}=0
, namely
f(t,0)=0
for any
t\geq 0
, then:

H(t,0)=G(t,0)=0\quad\textnormal{for any $t\geq 0$}.

iii)

If
\bar{y}\neq 0
, then

\lim\limits_{t\to+\infty}H(t,0)=\bar{y}\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\lim\limits_{t\to+\infty}G(t,0)=0.

iv)

The equilibrium
\bar{y}
is globally uniformly asymptotically stable for the system (
7.1.2
).

5)
In
[
32
]
the authors generalize the smooth linearization results from
[
30
]
by dropping
the assumption
(A2)
by allowing a family of unbounded perturbations
f
.

6)
When considering projections different from the trivial ones, a first result in terms of the differentiability of the linearization on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
was obtained by D. Dragičević
et al.
in
[
51
]
, its approach take in account nonuniform exponential dichotomy and the spectrum associated to this dichotomy.
Secondly in
[
74
]
, N. Jara proves, without using dichotomy spectrum, that if the linear system has a general nonuniform dichotomy and the nonlinear part has restrictive asymptotic assumptions; in compensation for avoiding the dichotomy spectrum, then the linearization is of class
C^{2}
on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
.

7.5.
Exercises

1.-

By using the methods of Theorems
7.2.1
and
7.2.2
, show that
y(t)\mapsto G[t,y(t)]
is a
C^{3}
preserving orientation diffeomorphisms.

2.-

Verify that
\eta\mapsto G(t,\eta)
satisfies (
7.2.9
).

4.-

Prove that the homeomorphism
\eta\mapsto G(t,\eta)
verifies the property (ii) of Definition
7.3.1
.

5.-

Prove that
(t,\eta)\mapsto G(t,\eta)
of Definition
7.3.1
, is continuous in any
(t_{0},\eta_{0})\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

6.-

By using (
7.3.17
) and (
7.3.18
),
obtain a formula for the higher formal derivatives of
H
up to order
r-
th and prove its continuity on
\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}.

7.-

In Theorem
7.3.1
, carry out the steps of the proof considering
J=\mathbb{R^{-}}.

8.-

In the condition
(A4)
change the projection
P=I
by
P=0.
What can you say about all theorems of this chapter?

9.-

Give explicit examples that verify the hypothesis of all theorems of this chapter.

Appendix A
Exponential Dichotomy in the scalar case (Pablo Amster)

A particularity of the scalar case is that the unique possible projectors are the
trivial ones. Limit cases are always special and provides new perspectives on the
already studied results. In this context,
we will be focused on the study of the scalar equation:

(A.0.1)

\dot{x}=a(t)x\quad\textnormal{where $t\in J$}

and, as it has been usual on this book,
J
can be the intervals

J=(-\infty,0]
,
J=[0,+\infty)
and
J=(-\infty,+\infty)
.

Definition A.0.1
.

The equation (
A.0.1
) has an exponential dichotomy on the unbounded interval
J
if and only if there exist constants
C\geq 0
and
\alpha>0
such that

\left|\int_{s}^{t}a(r)\,dr\right|\geq\alpha(t-s)-C\qquad\textnormal{for any $t\geq s$ with $t,s\in J$}.

By redefining the constant
C
if necessary, it is clear that Definition
A.0.1
always corresponds to one of the following situations

a)

There exist constants
C\geq 0
and
\alpha>0
such that:

(A.0.2)

\int_{s}^{t}a(r)\,dr\leq C-\alpha(t-s)\qquad\textnormal{for any $t\geq s$ with $t,s\in J$}.

b)

There exist constants
C\geq 0
and
\alpha>0
such that:

(A.0.3)

\int_{t}^{s}a(r)\,dr\geq\alpha(s-t)-C\qquad\textnormal{for any $s\geq t$ with $t,s\in J$}.

The above properties are equivalent to the standard definition of exponential dichotomy:

a’)

There exist constants
K\geq 1
and
\alpha>0
such that

(A.0.4)

e^{\int_{s}^{t}a(r)\,dr}\leq Ke^{-\alpha(t-s)}\quad\textnormal{for any $t\geq s$ with $t,s\in J$}.

b’)

There exist constants
K\geq 1
and
\alpha>0
such that

(A.0.5)

e^{-\int_{t}^{s}a(r)\,dr}\leq Ke^{-\alpha(s-t)}\quad\textnormal{for any $s\geq t$ with $t,s\in J$}.

In fact, inspired by the standard definition, the case a) corresponds to the exponential dichotomy with the
identity projector
while the case b) corresponds to
the exponential dichotomy with the
null projector
.

\bullet

If (
A.0.1
) has an exponential dichotomy on
J=[0,+\infty)
, the properties (
A.0.4
)–(
A.0.5
)
says that any solution either converges exponentially to zero (identity projector) or diverges exponentially (null projector) as
t\to+\infty
, since (
A.0.5
) is equivalent to
K^{-1}e^{\alpha(t-t_{0})}\leq e^{\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\mathfrak{a}(r)\,dr}
with
t\geq t_{0}\geq 0
.

\bullet

Similarly, if (
A.0.1
) has an exponential dichotomy on
J=(-\infty,0]
, the properties (
A.0.4
)–(
A.0.5
)
says that any solution either converges exponentially to zero (null projector) or diverges exponentially (identity projector) as
t\to-\infty
, since (
A.0.4
) is equivalent to
K^{-1}e^{\alpha(t_{0}-t)}\leq e^{\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\mathfrak{a}(r)dr}
with
t\leq t_{0}\leq 0
.

\bullet

If (
A.0.1
) has an exponential dichotomy on
J=(-\infty,+\infty)
then it also has an exponential dichotomy on
(-\infty,0]

and
[0,+\infty)
and inherits the above asymptotic behaviors at
\pm\infty
.

As we can see, if (
A.0.1
) has an exponential dichotomy on
J
, its solutions have
a
dichotomic
asymptotic behavior at
t\to+\infty
or
t\to-\infty
: they are either convergent or divergent at exponential rate. As we know, this qualitative asymptotic behavior is behind the name of exponential dichotomy.

Remark A.0.1
.

As a consequence of the previous discussion is that, if (
A.0.1
) has an exponential dichotomy on
(-\infty,+\infty)
then
the unique solution bounded on
(-\infty,+\infty)
is the trivial one. In fact, any other solution will be divergent
at
+\infty
or
-\infty
.

A noteworthy fact is that a linear equation (
A.0.1
) can have an exponential dichotomy simultaneously on
(-\infty,0]

and
[0,+\infty)
but does not necessarily has an exponential dichotomy on
(-\infty,+\infty)
,
we refer to Section from Chapter 3 for an illustrative example.

A.0.1.
Roughness, admissibility and spectra

The next result is known as the
roughness property
and shows that the set of bounded continuous functions such that an exponential dichotomy exists is open. More precisely,

Proposition A.0.1
.

Assume that (
A.0.1
) has an exponential
dichotomy on
[0,+\infty)
with constants
C
,
\alpha

and let

b\colon[0,+\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
be a continuous function such that

\delta_{0}:=\limsup\limits_{t\to+\infty}|b(t)|<{\alpha}.

Then the perturbed equation

\dot{x}=[a(t)+b(t)]x

also has an exponential dichotomy on
[0,+\infty)
with constants
\tilde{C},\alpha_{0}
where

\tilde{C}>C
and
\alpha_{0}
is any positive constant satisfying the restriction

\alpha_{0}+\delta_{0}<\alpha
.

Proof.

Fix
\alpha_{0}>0
such that
\alpha_{0}<\alpha-\delta_{0}
and
T\geq 0
such that
|\mathfrak{e}(t)|<\alpha-\alpha_{0}
for any
t\geq T
. The proof will consider
the three possible cases:
t\geq s\geq T
,
T\geq t\geq s
and
t\geq T\geq s
.

Firstly, when
t\geq s\geq T
it is verified that:

\left|\int_{s}^{t}[a(r)+b(r)]\,dr\right|\geq\alpha(t-s)-C-\int_{s}^{t}|b(r)|\,dr\geq\alpha_{0}(t-s)-C.

Secondly, when
s\leq t\leq T
, let us define
C_{0}:=\int_{0}^{T}|\mathfrak{a}(r)+\mathfrak{e}(r)|\ dr
. It is clear that

\left|\int_{s}^{t}[a(r)+b(r)]\,dr\right|\geq-C_{0}\geq\alpha_{0}(t-s)-C_{0}-\alpha_{0}\,T.

Finally, for
t>T\geq s
, we have that

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\displaystyle\left|\int_{s}^{t}[a(r)+b(r)]\,dr\right|&\geq&\displaystyle\left|\int_{T}^{t}[a(r)+b(r)]\,dr\right|-\int_{s}^{T}|a(r)+b(r)|\ dr\\
&\geq&\alpha_{0}(t-T)-C-C_{0}\\
&\geq&\alpha_{0}(t-s)-\alpha_{0}T-C-C_{0},\end{array}

then we have that the equation has an exponential dichotomy with constants
\alpha_{0}
and
\tilde{C}:=C+C_{0}+\alpha_{0}\,T
.
∎

We stress the beautiful and remarkable simplicity of the proof of the above lemma
compared with the general
n
–dimensional case which has been addressed in several
monographs as
[
12
]
,
[
41
, Ch.4]
and research articles as
[
76
,
95
]
.

Now, let
(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{D})
be a pair of function spaces, as we have seen in the Chapter 2,
this pair is said
to be
admissible
if for any
v\in\mathcal{D}
, the equation

(A.0.6)

\dot{y}=a(t)y+v(t),

has –at least– one solution in the space
\mathcal{B}
. The next result describes the equivalence between the exponential dichotomy on
[0,+\infty)
with the admissibility property
when
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{D}
is the Banach space of bounded continuous functions on

[0,+\infty)
with the supremum norm:

Proposition A.0.2
.

Let
t\mapsto a(t)
be a continuous function on
[0,+\infty)
. The following properties are equivalent:

a)

The equation (
A.0.1
) has an exponential dichotomy on
[0,+\infty)
.

b)

For any
bounded and continuous function
v\colon[0,+\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
, the problem

(A.0.7)

\dot{x}=a(t)x+v(t)

has
at least one bounded and continuous solution on
[0,+\infty)
.
More precisely, (
A.0.2
) holds if and only if
all solutions are bounded for each bounded
v(\cdot)
and
(
A.0.3
) holds if and only if
there exists exactly one bounded solution for each bounded
v(\cdot)
.

c)

The non homogeneous equation

\dot{z}=a(t)z+1

has at least a bounded solution on
[0,+\infty)
.

d)

There exists a bounded and continuous function
v\colon[0,+\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
with

\liminf\limits_{t\to+\infty}|v(t)|>0

such that the problem (
A.0.7
) has at least one bounded solution.

Proof.

Proof of
\textnormal{a)}\Rightarrow\textnormal{b)}
:
If (
A.0.2
) holds, then any solution of (
A.0.6
) has the form:

The first term of the right-hand side is bounded since it converges to
0
when
t\to+\infty
, while the second term satisfies, for
t\geq t_{0}
:

\left|\int_{t_{0}}^{t}v(s)e^{\int_{s}^{t}a(r)\,dr}\,ds\right|\leq\|v\|_{\infty}\int_{t_{0}}^{t}Ke^{-\alpha(t-s)}\,ds=\frac{K}{\alpha}\|v\|_{\infty}\left(1-e^{-\alpha(t-t_{0})}\right),

and the boundedness on
[0,+\infty)
of any solution of (
A.0.6
) is verified. Now, if (
A.0.3
) holds instead, then it is readily verified that the expression

x(t)=-\int_{t}^{+\infty}v(s)e^{-\int_{t}^{s}a(r)\,dr}\,ds

is well defined and gives an explicit description for the unique solution of (
A.0.6
)
which is bounded on
[0,+\infty)
.

On the other hand, observe that the statements c) and d) are particular cases of b), which implies that
\textnormal{a)}\Rightarrow\textnormal{c)}
and
\textnormal{a)}\Rightarrow\textnormal{d)}
are straightforwardly verified.

Proof of
\textnormal{d)}\Rightarrow\textnormal{c)}
: Let
t\mapsto x(t)
be a bounded solution of (
A.0.7
) such that

m\leq x(t)\leq M\quad\textnormal{for any $t\in[0,+\infty)$},

By hypothesis, we have the existence of
v_{0}>0
such that
|\mathfrak{v}(t)|>v_{0}

for any
t\geq t_{0}
with
t_{0}\geq 0
. Without loss of generality, we can suppose

t_{0}=0
.

Firstly, we will assume that
v(t)\geq v_{0}>0
for any
t\geq 0
,
then
the above solution
t\mapsto x(t)
also verifies:

x(t)=x_{0}e^{\int_{0}^{t}a(r)\,dr}+\int_{0}^{t}e^{\int_{s}^{t}a(r)\,dr}v(s)ds\geq x_{0}e^{\int_{0}^{t}a(r)\,dr}+v_{0}\int_{0}^{t}e^{\int_{s}^{t}a(r)\,dr}\,ds,

which allow us to deduce:

\frac{x(t)}{v_{0}}\geq\frac{x_{0}}{v_{0}}e^{\int_{0}^{t}a(r)\,dr}+\int_{0}^{t}e^{\int_{s}^{t}a(r)\,dr}\,ds:=z(t).

We can see that
t\mapsto z(t)
is solution of
\dot{z}=a(t)z+1
passing through
x_{0}/v_{0}
at
t=0
, which also verifies
z(t)\leq M/v_{0}
for any
t\geq 0
.

Now, let us observe that

\begin{array}[]{rcl}z(t)&=&\displaystyle\frac{x_{0}}{v_{0}}e^{\int_{0}^{t}\mathfrak{a}(r)\,dr}+\int_{0}^{t}e^{\int_{s}^{t}\mathfrak{a}(r)\,dr}\,ds\\
\\
&=&\displaystyle||v||_{\infty}^{-1}\left(x_{0}\frac{||v||_{\infty}}{v_{0}}e^{\int_{0}^{t}a(r)\,dr}+\int_{0}^{t}e^{\int_{s}^{t}a(r)\,dr}||v||_{\infty}\,ds\right)\\
\\
&\geq&\displaystyle||v||_{\infty}^{-1}\left(x_{0}\ e^{\int_{0}^{t}a(r)\,dr}+\int_{0}^{t}e^{\int_{s}^{t}a(r)\,dr}v(s)\,ds\right)\\
\\
&\geq&||v||_{\infty}^{-1}x(t),\end{array}

and we proved that

m||v||_{\infty}^{-1}\leq z(t)\leq v_{0}^{-1}M\quad\textnormal{for any $t\in[0,+\infty)$}.

Secondly, if we assume that
-v(t)\geq v_{0}>0
for any
t\geq 0
, we multiply (
A.0.7
) by
-1
and make the transformations
u=-x
and
w(t)=-v(t)
, leading to
\dot{u}=a(t)u+w(t)
, which also has a bounded solution
and the above proof can be carried out.

Proof of
\textnormal{c)}\Rightarrow\textnormal{a)}
: Let
t\mapsto z(t)
be a solution
of
\dot{z}=a(t)z+1
which is bounded on
[0,+\infty)
. Firstly, a noteworthy
property of the above equation is that: if
z(0)\geq 0
, the solution remains positive
for any
t>0
while, if
z(0)<0
the solution either remains negative or has a unique change of sign.

Secondly, let us verify that

(A.0.8)

\displaystyle\liminf_{t\to+\infty}|z(t)|>0.

In fact, this property is immediately verified when
z(\cdot)
is positive
after some value since
t\mapsto z(t)
is increasing at any value of
t
such that
|z(t)|\|a\|_{\infty}<1
. Now, if
z(t)<0
for any
t\geq 0
, we have to consider two cases:

Case i)

\limsup\limits_{t\to+\infty}z(t)<0
, which is equivalent to (
A.0.8
).

Case ii)

\limsup\limits_{t\to+\infty}z(t)=0
, which cannot be possible, because
z(\cdot)
is increasing after some finite time and, consequently,
z(t)=\textit{o}(1)
, which leads to
\liminf\limits_{t\to+\infty}z^{\prime}(t)>0
, obtaining a contradiction with the boundedness of
t\mapsto z(t)
and (
A.0.8
) follows.

In consequence, we can assume that
0<c\leq|z(t)|\leq M
for any
t\geq T
. In the case that
z(\cdot)
is positive for
t>s\geq T
it follows that:

C\geq\ln\left(\frac{z(t)}{z(s)}\right)=\int_{s}^{t}a(r)\,dr+\int_{s}^{t}\frac{1}{z(r)}\,dr,

which leads to:

\int_{s}^{t}a(r)\,dr\leq C-\frac{1}{M}(t-s).

Similarly, if
z(\cdot)
is negative for any
t\leq s
we will have that

\int_{t}^{s}a(r)\,dr\geq-C+\frac{1}{M}(s-t),

then the equation (
A.0.1
) has an exponential dichotomy on
[T,+\infty)

and the exponential dichotomy is verified on
[0,+\infty)
as a consequence of Lemma
3.2.1

from Chapter 3.

∎

We stress the remarkable simplicity of the proof of the above lemma
compared with the
general
n
–dimensional case which has been addressed from a functional analysis approach in

[
41
]
and variational methods in
[
27
]
.

The exponential dichotomy spectrum has a simpler characterization in the scalar case when

J=[0,+\infty)
.

Proposition A.0.3
.

If
t\mapsto a(t)
is bounded and continuous on
[0,+\infty)
then the exponential dichotomy spectrum of (
A.0.1
) is characterized as follows:

\Sigma^{+}(a)=[\beta^{-}(a),\beta^{+}(a)]

where
\beta^{-}(a)
and
\beta^{+}(a)
are the lower and upper Bohl exponents, which are
respectively defined by:

\beta^{-}(a):=\liminf\limits_{L,s\to+\infty}\frac{1}{L}\int_{s}^{s+L}a(r)\,dr\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\beta^{+}(a):=\limsup\limits_{L,s\to+\infty}\frac{1}{L}\int_{s}^{s+L}a(r)\,dr.

More precisely,

•

The case (
A.0.2
) holds for
a(\cdot)-\lambda
if and only if
\lambda<\beta^{-}(a)
.

•

The case (
A.0.3
) holds for
a(\cdot)-\lambda
if and only if
\lambda>\beta^{+}(a)
.

Proof.

We will prove an equivalent result, namely:

\mathbb{R}\setminus\Sigma^{+}(a)=(-\infty,\beta^{-}(a))\cup(\beta^{+}(a),+\infty).

If
\lambda\notin\Sigma^{+}(a)

it follows from Definition
5.2.2
from Chapter 5 that
\dot{x}=[a(t)-\lambda]x

has an exponential dichotomy on
[0,+\infty)
, that is either (
A.0.2
) or (
A.0.3
) holds for
a(\cdot)-\lambda
.

The proof will be a consequence of the following statements:

i)

The property (
A.0.3
) holds for
a(\cdot)-\lambda

if and only if
\lambda\in(-\infty,\beta^{-}(a))
,

ii)

The property (
A.0.2
) holds for
a(\cdot)-\lambda

if and only if
\lambda\in(\beta^{+}(a),+\infty)
.

Assume for example that (
A.0.3
) holds for
a(\cdot)-\lambda
, then for arbitrary
s>t
we have

\frac{1}{s-t}\int_{t}^{s}a(r)\,dr\geq\lambda+\alpha-\frac{C}{s-t}

for some
\alpha>0
. Taking lower
limit for
t\to+\infty
and
L:=s-t\to+\infty
, we deduce that
\beta^{-}(a)\geq\lambda+\alpha>\lambda
. Conversely, for
\beta^{-}(a)>\lambda
we may fix
\alpha\in(0,\beta^{-}(a)-\lambda)

and
t_{0},L_{0}>0
such that

\frac{1}{s-t}\int_{t}^{s}a(r)\,dr>\lambda+\alpha\quad\textnormal{with}\quad t>t_{0}\quad\textnormal{and}\quad s-t>L_{0},

that is

(A.0.9)

\int_{t}^{s}[a(r)-\lambda]\,dr>\alpha(s-t)\quad\textnormal{with}\quad s-t>L_{0}\quad\textnormal{and}\quad t>t_{0}

We have to study the cases where the conditions
s-t>L_{0}
and
t>t_{0}
are not satisfied, namely:

a)

0\leq s-t\leq L_{0}
,

b)

t_{0}\geq s-L_{0}>t
,

c)

s-L_{0}>t_{0}\geq t
.

Firstly, the cases a) and b) imply that
0\leq s-t\leq L_{0}
and
t\leq s\leq L_{0}+t_{0}
. Then if a) or b) are
satisfied we have that:

\int_{t}^{s}[a(r)-\lambda]\,dr\geq-\|a-\lambda\|_{\infty}(L_{0}+t_{0})\geq\alpha(s-t)-(\|a-\lambda\|_{\infty}+\alpha)(L_{0}+t_{0}).

Finally, if
t\leq t_{0}<s-L_{0}
, then by using (
A.0.9
) we have

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\displaystyle\int_{t}^{s}[a(r)-\lambda]\,dr&=&\displaystyle\int_{t_{0}}^{s}[a(r)-\lambda]\,dr+\int_{t}^{t_{0}}[a(r)-\lambda]\,dr\\
&>&\displaystyle\alpha(s-t_{0})-\|a-\lambda\|_{\infty}t_{0}\\
&\geq&\displaystyle\alpha(s-t)-(\|a-\lambda\|_{\infty}+\alpha)t_{0}.\end{array}

By gathering the above cases we have that (
A.0.3
) is verified with

\alpha>0
and
C=(\|a-\lambda\|_{\infty}+\alpha)t_{0}
, then the statement i) is verified.
The statement ii) can be proved in an analogous way.

∎

The above result is usually stated without proof in the literature
[
4
, p.30]
and
[
50
]
.
Last but not least, we refer the reader to
[
9
,
10
]
and
[
43
]
for more details about the Bohl exponents.

Appendix B
Matrix Norms

B.1 Basic facts

Given a matrix
A\in M_{n}(\mathbb{R})
, the matrix norm induced
by the euclidean vector norm
|\cdot|_{2}
is defined as follows:

||A||_{2}:=\sup\limits_{x\neq 0}\frac{|Ax|_{2}}{|x|_{2}}=\sqrt{\lambda_{\max}(A^{*}A)},

where
\lambda_{\max}(A^{*}A)
is the largest eigenvalue of
A^{*}A
.

B.2 Positive definite matrices

Definition B.0.1
.

A square hermitian matrix
A=A^{*}\in M_{n}(\mathbb{C})
is positive definite (semi–definite) if

x^{*}Ax>0
(
x^{*}Ax\geq 0
) for any
(n\times 1)
vector
x\neq 0
.

Definition B.0.2
.

A square symmetric matrix
A=A^{T}\in M_{n}(\mathbb{R})
is positive definite (semi–definite) if

x^{T}Ax>0
(
x^{T}Ax\geq 0
) for any
(n\times 1)
real vector
x\neq 0
.

The notation
A>0
(
A\geq 0
) is reserved to
denote a positive definite (semi–definite) matrix.

The positive definite (semi–definite) matrices have interesting properties:

1)

A
is positive (semi) definite if and only if all the eigenvalues of
A
are
positive (nonnegative).

2)

A
is positive (semi) definite if and only there exists a positive (semi) definite
matrix
B\in M_{n}(\mathbb{C})
such that
A=BB
, that is
B
is a square root of
A
.

3)

A
is a real positive (semi) definite if and only there exists a real positive (semi) definite
matrix
B\in M_{n}(\mathbb{R})
such that
A=BB
, that is
B
is a square root of
A
.

For properties 2) and 3) we refer the reader to Theorem 13 from
[
69
, Ch.9]
,
[
86
, p.155]
and
[
133
, p.37]
for details.

Appendix C

The next result has been introduced (1919) by the Swedish mathematician Thomas Hakon Grönwall
in
[
60
]
as a tool to prove the continuity of the solution of a differential equation
with respect to the initial conditions.

C.1 Gronwall’s Lemma

Lemma B.0.1
.

Let
J\subset\mathbb{R}
be an interval,
t_{0}\in J
and
c>0
. Let
u,v\in C(J,(0,+\infty))
such that for any
t\geq t_{0}
with
t\in J
it follows that;

(B.0.1)

u(t)\leq c+\int_{t_{0}}^{t}v(s)u(s)\,ds,

then for any
t\geq t_{0}
we have that

(B.0.2)

u(t)\leq c\,e^{\int_{t_{0}}^{t}v(s)\,ds}.

Proof.

Note that the positiveness of the functions and parameters imply that inequality (
B.0.1
) is equivalent to

\displaystyle\frac{u(t)v(t)}{c+\int_{t_{0}}^{t}v(s)u(s)\,ds}\leq v(t).

As the left side of the above inequality is a logarithmic derivative of
t\mapsto c+\int_{t_{0}}^{t}v(s)u(s)\,ds
. Now, by integrating between
t_{0}
and
t
and using again (
B.0.1
) we obtain

\ln(u(t))\leq\ln\left(c+\int_{t_{0}}^{t}v(s)u(s)\,ds\right)=\ln(c)+\int_{t_{0}}^{t}v(s)\,ds

and (
B.0.2
) can be deduced easily.
∎

An important variation of Gronwall’s Lemma is given by the following result extracted from
[
19
, p.19]
:

Lemma B.0.2
.

Let
J\subset\mathbb{R}
be an interval,
t_{0}\in J
and
c>0
. Let
u,v\in C(J,(0,+\infty))
such that for any
t\in J
it follows that;

(B.0.3)

u(t)\leq c+\left|\int_{t_{0}}^{t}v(s)u(s)\,ds\right|,

then for any
t\in J
we have that

u(t)\leq ce^{\left|\int_{t_{0}}^{t}v(s)\,ds\right|}

Proof.

If
t\geq t_{0}
, the assumption (
B.0.3
) is equivalent to (
B.0.1
) and by Gronwall’s Lemma we can deduce that

u(t)\leq c\,e^{\int_{t_{0}}^{t}v(s)\,ds}=c\,e^{\left|\int_{t_{0}}^{t}v(s)\,ds\right|}.

Now, let us assume that
t\in J
with
t<t_{0}
, let us note by (
B.0.3
) that

0<u(t)\leq w(t):=c+\left|\int_{t_{0}}^{t}v(s)u(s)\,ds\right|=c-\int_{t_{0}}^{t}v(s)u(s)\,ds

We derivate
w(t)
and obtain that

w^{\prime}(t)=-v(t)u(t)\geq-v(t)w(t),

that is equivalent to

w^{\prime}(t)+v(t)w(t)\geq 0

and also to

w^{\prime}(t)e^{\int_{t_{0}}^{t}v(s)\,ds}+v(t)e^{\int_{t_{0}}^{t}v(s)\,ds}w(t)\geq 0,

which can be written as

\frac{d}{dt}\left[w(t)e^{\int_{t_{0}}^{t}v(s)\,ds}\right]\geq 0.

Then, the function
t\mapsto\phi(t)=w(t)e^{\int_{t_{0}}^{t}v(s)\,ds}
is increasing for any
t\in J
with
t\leq t_{0}
. In consequence, as
t\leq t_{0}
we have that
\phi(t)\leq\phi(t_{0})=w(t_{0})=c
or

w(t)e^{\int_{t_{0}}^{t}v(s)\,ds}\leq w(t_{0})=c,

then

w(t)\leq ce^{-\int_{t_{0}}^{t}v(s)\,ds}.

As
w(t)\geq u(t)>0
we can conclude that

u(t)\leq w(t)\leq ce^{-\int_{t_{0}}^{t}v(s)\,ds}=ce^{\left|\int_{t}^{t_{0}}v(s)\,ds\right|}=ce^{\left|-\int_{t}^{t_{0}}v(s)\,ds\right|}=ce^{\left|\int_{t_{0}}^{t}v(s)\,ds\right|}

∎
